# The 5 Basic Crochet Stitches, in the Order to Learn Them

Source: src/content/posts/basic-crochet-stitches.md



Almost everything in crochet is five stitches. Shells, bobbles, clusters, and puffs are these five combined. Learn them shortest to tallest. Names are **US terms**.

Use medium worsted in a light solid color and a 5 mm (H/8) hook. Dark yarn and variegated yarn hide the structure you need to see.


![A cream crochet swatch showing basic stitches beside a hook](../../assets/articles/basic-crochet-stitches-result.jpg "Illustration. Five stitches build almost everything — learn them in height order.")
## The two moves

**Yarn over (`yo`)** — wrap the working yarn over the hook from back to front.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/yarn-over.svg" alt="Diagram of a yarn over. The working yarn wraps over the hook from back to front." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Yarn over moves from the back of the hook toward the front.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Pull through** — draw the hook, with yarn on it, back through a loop or a stitch.

The five stitches differ only in how many times you yarn over first, and how many loops you close at a time.

## The foundation chain

![Hands making a foundation chain in cream yarn](../../assets/articles/basic-crochet-stitches-1.jpg "Illustration. The foundation chain is where every flat piece starts.")

Every flat piece starts with a chain. The chain starts with a slip knot, which is not a stitch and does not count.

**Slip knot**

1. Cross the tail over the working yarn so you have a loop, with the tail hanging down.
2. Reach through that loop and pull a new loop of working yarn through it.
3. Put the new loop on the hook.
4. Pull the tail and the working yarn in opposite directions until the knot sits against the hook.

The knot should slide. If it will not move, the yarn hand is too tight. Loosen it before you chain. The loop on the hook is not a chain.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/slip-knot.svg" alt="Diagram of a slip knot in three steps: cross the tail, pull a loop through, and seat it on the hook so it still slides." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The slip knot is adjustable. The loop on the hook is not a chain.</figcaption>
</figure>

Then chain:

1. Yarn over.
2. Pull that yarn through the loop on the hook. That is one chain.
3. Repeat for the number the pattern asks for.

Do not count the slip knot or the loop on the hook. Only the finished V-shaped links count. Chain about 20, and keep it loose.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/foundation-chain.svg" alt="Diagram of a foundation chain. The front of each chain is a V, the back is a bump, and the loop on the hook is not counted." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Count the finished V links. Skip the slip knot and the loop still on the hook.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div class="tip">

The front of a chain is a row of Vs. The back has a raised bump. Work under one strand of the V, under both, or into the bump. All three are correct. The bump gives the neatest edge. Do the same thing all the way across.

</div>

## 1. Slip stitch (`sl st`)

The shortest stitch. It barely adds height. Use it to join rounds, move the hook sideways, or seam.

1. Insert the hook into the stitch.
2. Yarn over.
3. Pull through the stitch and the loop on the hook in one movement.

One loop remains.

## 2. Single crochet (`sc`)

![Close-up of single crochet stitches being formed](../../assets/articles/basic-crochet-stitches-2.jpg "Illustration. Single crochet — short, dense, and the stitch most beginners live in.")

Short, dense, and sturdy. Use it for amigurumi, bags, and dishcloths.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/insert-single-crochet.svg" alt="Diagram of the top of a stitch, with a front loop and a back loop, and the hook going under both." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Ordinary single crochet goes under both loops at the top of the stitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/single-crochet.svg" alt="Diagram of single crochet in three steps: insert under both loops, pull up a loop so two loops are on the hook, then pull through both." width="640" height="430" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Single crochet. Two loops on the hook, then yarn over and pull through both.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. Insert the hook into the next stitch.
2. Yarn over and pull up a loop. Two loops on the hook.
3. Yarn over and pull through both loops.

Into a foundation chain, start in the **second chain from the hook**.

## Practice: a 15-stitch rectangle

This is a counting exercise, not a finished object. No sample of it was measured for this page.

Chain 16. Count the finished V links only. The loop on the hook is not a chain.

**Row 1.** Single crochet in the second chain from the hook, then in each chain across. You skipped one chain, so 16 − 1 = **15 single crochet**.

**Later rows.** Chain 1 and turn. For this exercise that chain is height only. It does not count. Single crochet in the first stitch and in every stitch after it, including the last one. The row is 15 again.

Stop when four rows in a row are 15. Write the counts down: 15, 15, 15, 15. A 14 means an edge stitch was missed. A 16 means the turning chain was used as a stitch, or the hook went into one stitch twice. The [turning chain page](/crochet-turning-chain/) shows that wedge. A [stitch counter](/crochet-stitch-counter/) can hold the number while you look.

When you stop, cut the yarn, pull the tail through the last loop, and weave it through several stitches on the back. Do not cut it flush against the knot. Weaving is covered in [weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

## 3. Half double crochet (`hdc`)

Between single and double in height. Softer than single crochet, still fairly dense. Useful for hats and blankets.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/half-double-crochet.svg" alt="Diagram of half double crochet: yarn over before inserting, three loops on the hook, then one pull through all three." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Half double crochet starts with a yarn over. The closing pull goes through all three loops.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. Yarn over, then insert the hook into the next stitch.
2. Yarn over and pull up a loop. Three loops on the hook.
3. Yarn over and pull through all three loops at once.

You yarn over *before* entering the stitch. That extra wrap is the height. Into a chain, start in the **third chain from the hook**. On the next row, chain 2 to turn. Whether that chain counts as a stitch is the usual reason an edge slants, and it is covered on the [turning chain](/crochet-turning-chain/) page. Pick one rule and keep it for the whole swatch.

## 4. Double crochet (`dc`)

![Close-up of a double crochet stitch in progress](../../assets/articles/basic-crochet-stitches-3.jpg "Illustration. Double crochet — yarn over first, then work the loops off in pairs.")

The most-used stitch: tall, quick, and slightly open.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/double-crochet.svg" alt="Diagram of double crochet: three loops on the hook, yarn over and pull through two, then yarn over and pull through the last two." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Double crochet closes two loops at a time. It does not pull through all the loops at once.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. Yarn over, then insert the hook into the next stitch.
2. Yarn over and pull up a loop. Three loops on the hook.
3. Yarn over and pull through the **first two loops only**. Two loops remain.
4. Yarn over and pull through the last two loops.

You close the loops in two stages, not all at once. Into a chain, start in the **fourth chain from the hook**. The turning chain for the next row is usually 3. The [turning chain](/crochet-turning-chain/) page is where to decide whether that chain replaces the first double crochet.

## 5. Treble crochet (`tr`)

Also written triple crochet. Tall and lacy, for shawls, open patterns, and fabric you want finished fast.

1. Yarn over **twice**, then insert the hook.
2. Yarn over and pull up a loop. Four loops on the hook.
3. Yarn over, pull through the first two loops. Three left.
4. Yarn over, pull through the next two loops. Two left.
5. Yarn over, pull through the last two loops.

Close two loops at a time until one remains. Into a chain, start in the **fifth chain from the hook**. Wrap three times for a double treble, four for a triple treble, then close two loops at a time.

## The pattern behind them

| Stitch | Wraps before inserting | Chain to start in | Turning chain |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `sl st` | 0 | — | — |
| `sc` | 0 | 2nd | 1 |
| `hdc` | 1 | 3rd | 2 |
| `dc` | 1 | 4th | 3 |
| `tr` | 2 | 5th | 4 |

Swatch each stitch about 15 wide and 10 rows tall, then make a dishcloth in single crochet or a scarf in half double. A row is successful when the count matches the row before it. Ten single crochets, a chain-1 that you do not count, and ten single crochets on the way back is a straight edge. Eleven means the turning chain was used as an extra stitch.

## The mistakes everyone makes

**Chaining too tightly.** The foundation should be looser than the stitches. If row one is a fight, chain with a hook one size larger, then switch back.

**Gaining or losing stitches at the ends.** Nearly always the turning chain. The [full explanation](/crochet-turning-chain/) is its own page.

**The wrong part of the stitch.** Unless the pattern says otherwise, go under **both** top loops. Front loop only and back loop only are variations a pattern will name.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/front-back-loops.svg" alt="Diagram comparing a hook under both loops, under the front loop only, and under the back loop only." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Front loop is the one nearer you. Back loop is the far one. Ordinary stitches use both.</figcaption>
</figure>

**A hard grip on the yarn.** Tight, uneven stitches usually mean the yarn hand is too tense. Let it run more freely.

## Locate the next stitch

Lay the row flat and find the V at the top of each stitch. Put a marker under both loops of the first and last real stitch. On the next row, move those markers into the stitches you actually make, not into the turning chain. For a 15-stitch sc exercise, chain 16 and keep 15 real Vs after every row. Practice hdc separately with chain 17 for 15 hdc, starting in the third chain; ch 2 is not counted in that exercise. Do not switch stitch heights halfway through a swatch intended to measure gauge.

## Frequently asked

### Which stitch first?

Chain, then single crochet. Slip stitch is easier to make, but a fabric of slip stitches is very dense and slow to build, and it is hard to see where the next stitch goes. Single crochet teaches you to read the stitches.

### Why does my rectangle have holes?

Double crochet and taller stitches are open. That is the stitch. For solid fabric, use single crochet or half double, or drop a hook size.

### Do I need UK terms?

Only for British or Australian patterns. The same abbreviation is a different stitch in each system. The [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) has the conversion.


# Bat Amigurumi: Wings, Body and Ears

Source: src/content/posts/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears.md



Bats look complicated because of the wings. The body is a small oval and the ears are tiny triangles. The hard part is making a left wing and a right wing that are mirror images of each other, and sewing them on level so the bat does not look like it is shrugging.

The mirror problem has a simple answer, and it is the lesson of this page. You do not write a separate pattern for the left wing. You make two identical wings and turn one over. Single crochet looks almost the same on both sides, so the flipped wing reads as the opposite hand. The same trick works for any lopsided flat piece: a leaf, a foot, a pair of ears.

You should be comfortable [working in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/) and sewing pieces with a yarn needle. US terms. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations of the finished bat, which has not been made up in yarn here, so its size depends on your yarn and hook.

![A finished black crochet bat with matching wings and small triangular ears](../../assets/articles/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears-result.jpg "Illustration. The intended look.")

## What you need

- **Dark worsted-weight yarn**: black, charcoal, or deep purple. A scrap of white for fangs if you want them. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A hook one or two sizes smaller than the label**, so stuffing does not show through. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Stuffing, a yarn needle, a stitch marker, pins.

Dark yarn makes stitches hard to see. Work in good light, and count every round and row out loud.

## Step 1: the body

![Hands crocheting a small black sphere with a stitch marker in the round](../../assets/articles/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears-1.jpg "Illustration. The body first. Wings and ears are separate.")

A short oval: the same increase, even, decrease shape as the [pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/), with fewer increases and more even rounds, so it is taller than it is wide.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1 starts in a magic ring, pulled tight so the top has no hole.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Rounds 4–8:** Sc in each stitch. (18)

**Round 9:** (Sc, sc2tog) 6 times. (12)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/invisible-decrease.svg" alt="Three steps. 1: insert the hook into the front loop only of the next stitch. 2: without pulling through, insert into the front loop of the stitch after it, so three loops are on the hook. 3: yarn over and pull through the two front loops, then yarn over and pull through the two loops on the hook. Two stitches become one." width="480" height="752" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Through the front loops only, the decrease disappears into the fabric.</figcaption>
</figure>

Stuff firmly. A soft body lets the wings drag it out of shape.

**Round 10:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6) Fasten off and pull closed.

For a longer bat, add even rounds. For a stubbier one, remove them. The rest of the pattern does not change.

## Step 2: two ears

![Two matching small black crochet triangular ear pieces laid flat](../../assets/articles/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears-4.jpg "Illustration. Two matching triangles.")

Worked flat. Make two.

**Row 1:** Ch 2. 2 sc in the 2nd chain from the hook. Turn. (2)

**Row 2:** Ch 1, sc, 2 sc in last stitch. Turn. (3)

**Row 3:** Ch 1, sc 2, 2 sc in last stitch. Turn. (4)

**Row 4:** Ch 1, sc 4. Fasten off with a long tail.

Because each row turns, the increase at the end of a row falls on alternate sides. The ear widens on both edges, so the two ears match without flipping.

## Step 3: two wings

![Two flat black crocheted wing shapes and two small triangular ear pieces laid out separately](../../assets/articles/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears-2.jpg "Illustration. Two wings the same size. Count every row.")

Worked flat. Make two, exactly the same.

**Row 1:** Ch 5. Sc in the 2nd chain from the hook and each chain across. Turn. (4)

**Row 2:** Ch 1, sc 3, 2 sc in last stitch. Turn. (5)

**Row 3:** Ch 1, 2 sc in first stitch, sc 4. Turn. (6)

**Row 4:** Ch 1, sc 5, 2 sc in last stitch. Turn. (7)

**Row 5:** Ch 1, sc 7. Do not fasten off.

**Scallops:** Turn. Sl st in the first stitch. (Ch 3, skip 1, sl st in next) 3 times. Fasten off with a long tail.

The increases alternate between the end and the start of a row on purpose. Because the work turns, the end of row 2 and the start of row 3 sit on the same edge. All three increases land on one side, so that edge slants and the other stays straight. The scallops use all 7 stitches: 1 + 3 × 2 = 7.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bat-wing-mirror.svg" alt="A wing worked 4, 5, 6, 7, 7 stitches with every increase on one edge, so one side slants and the other is straight, and three chain-3 scallops along the 7-stitch edge. A second identical wing is turned over. On the body, both straight edges are sewn on and the slants and scallops point outward, with two ears on top." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Two identical wings. Turn one over, and the pair mirrors.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Step 4: make the pair mirror

Lay both wings flat, scallops at the bottom. Their straight edges point the same way. Turn one wing over, front to back. Now one straight edge faces left and the other faces right. Those straight edges go against the body.

Before sewing, lay the two wings on top of each other with the straight edges together. If one is larger, a row or an increase is missing. Redo it. Stretching the smaller wing to match makes it pucker.

## Step 5: assemble

![A tapestry needle sewing a flat black wing onto a rounded black body](../../assets/articles/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears-3.jpg "Illustration. Sew along the whole straight edge, or the wing flops.")

1. **Ears.** Pin both to the top of the body, slightly toward the front, a little apart. Pinch the base of each a little as you sew so it cups forward. Pin both before sewing either one, so you can check they are level.
2. **Wings.** Pin the straight edge of each wing to a side of the body, starting about two rounds below the top, scallops pointing down. Check from the front that they sit at the same height.
3. **Sew.** Whipstitch along the full straight edge through the wing and into the body. Add a second pass at the top of the edge, where the weight pulls.

## Step 6: the face

![A tapestry needle embroidering small eyes and fangs onto a black crochet bat](../../assets/articles/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears-5.jpg "Illustration. Two eyes and a small white V for fangs are enough.")

Two French knots for eyes, in a color that shows against dark yarn, and a small V of white for fangs. Less is better on a small face.

## Finish the body before attaching the wings

After round 10, cut a sewing tail, thread it through the six remaining front loops, pull closed, and secure it before hiding the tail. For the ears, the two-stitch end is the tip and the four-stitch last row is the base: sew row 4 to the body, leaving row 1 pointing upward. Mark the front center with a scrap strand. Pin one ear on each side of it and mirror the wing seams from the same body round. The straight five-row wing edge is the attachment edge; the seven-stitch scalloped edge points downward. Remove every pin before finishing.

## Troubleshooting

**Both wings slant the same way.** The second one was not turned over.

**One wing is bigger.** A missing increase or row. Count against the list.

**The wings droop.** Sewn only at one point. Sew the whole edge and reinforce the top.

**Ears sit on the sides.** They belong inside the width of the top, not around its widest part.

**The bat tips forward.** The stuffing is uneven. Stuff the lower half as firmly as the top.

## Frequently asked

### Can I crochet the wings in the round?

You can, but flat wings sew on more cleanly at this size.

### What if I only have chunky yarn?

The same counts make a larger bat with a larger hook. Keep the proportions by changing nothing else.

### How do I make a bat garland?

Make several, leave a long tail at the top of each body, and tie them along a cord.

### What should I make next?

A larger ghost with a skirt is the [ghost amigurumi](/crochet-ghost-amigurumi-for-beginners/). The [spider and web](/how-to-crochet-a-spider-and-web/) uses the same small stuffed body with legs.


# Black Cat Crochet Wallet: Free Pattern With Ears and Gold Eyes

Source: src/content/posts/black-cat-crochet-wallet.md



A black cat is the Halloween wallet people recognize from across the room, and this one is built so the pocket has no side seams at all. You work around both sides of a starting chain, which turns the chain into the bottom fold. Then a flap grows from the back half, two small triangles become ears on the fold, and a snap keeps it shut.

This page is one of five patterns in the [Halloween crochet wallet collection](/halloween-crochet-wallets/). The skill it teaches is working into both sides of a foundation chain, which is also how many pencil cases and amigurumi bodies start.

![A black crochet cat pouch with pink inner ears, gold eyes, and a zipper](../../assets/articles/black-cat-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

The finished size and card fit here are worked out from the example gauge rather than measured from a finished wallet, so make a quick gauge swatch and slip a real bank card in at round 10 to check the fit. A standard US bank card is 3.370 by 2.125 inches, the ISO/IEC 7810 ID-1 size.

## Materials

- **Worsted weight (CYC 4) yarn**, black. Cotton or a cotton blend holds a pocket's shape better than soft acrylic, but acrylic works. See the [yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **Scraps** of gold or yellow for the eyes, pink for the nose, and white or light gray for the whiskers. Worsted or a thinner yarn both work for embroidery.
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or whichever size gives the gauge below. That is smaller than most worsted ball bands suggest, on purpose. A wallet needs firm fabric. [Hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One sew-on snap**, 3/8 in (10 mm), and black sewing thread.
- Tapestry needle, scissors, stitch marker, and one card to test the fit.

**How much yarn.** Not weighed from a made wallet. The wallet is about 27 square inches of fabric. Make your gauge swatch, measure it, and weigh it. A 4 by 4 inch swatch is 16 square inches, so the wallet uses about 1.7 times its weight. Add 20 percent for seams and tails. The scrap colors use a yard or two each.

## Abbreviations and stitches

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. Full list in the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/).

| Abbreviation | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| ch | chain |
| sc | single crochet |
| sl st | slip stitch |
| sc2tog | single crochet two together: pull up a loop in each of 2 stitches, yarn over, pull through all 3 loops. One stitch fewer. |
| sc3tog | single crochet three together: the same with 3 stitches. Two stitches fewer. |
| rnd | round |
| st(s) | stitch(es) |

**Working the other side of a chain.** After the last stitch along the top of the chain, rotate the work so the chain's underside faces up. Each chain has a single free loop there. Insert the hook under that loop for each stitch. See [basic stitches](/basic-crochet-stitches/) if single crochet is new.

**Joined rounds.** At the end of each round, slip stitch into the first single crochet. Chain 1, which does not count as a stitch, and make the first single crochet of the next round in the same stitch as the join. Do not turn.

## Size and gauge

**Gauge:** 16 sc and 20 rows (or rounds) = 4 in in single crochet. Make a swatch about 5 inches square and measure the middle, as in the [gauge guide](/crochet-gauge/).

The pocket is a flat tube 16 stitches across each side. At 4 stitches to the inch, that is 4.0 inches wide outside. The two side folds take a little, so the inside is about 3.8 inches. An ID-1 card is 3.37 inches wide, which leaves about 0.4 inch of ease for a few cards. Twelve rounds at 5 to the inch make 2.4 inches outside, about 2.3 inside, so a 2.125 inch card sits just below the rim.

<div class="warning">

If 16 stitches measure less than 3.75 inches, the card will be tight. Go up a hook size, or chain 18 so each side has 17 stitches. All flap counts then go up by 1 (17, 15, 13), and the face and ear placements must be recentered on the new flap width. Do not copy the 16-stitch placement chart onto a wider flap.

</div>

## The pattern

### Stage 1: the seamless pocket

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cat-wallet-tube.svg" alt="Diagram. A chain of 17 with the chain nearest the hook skipped. Round 1 works 16 single crochet along the top from right to left, rotates, and works 16 along the other side from left to right, 32 stitches. Rounds 2 to 12 go around without turning, making a flat tube 16 stitches wide and 12 rounds tall." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Both sides of the chain make round 1. The chain becomes the bottom fold.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The black pocket with a card started inside, before the ears and face](../../assets/articles/black-cat-crochet-wallet-pocket.jpg "Illustration.")

With black:

**Rnd 1:** Ch 17. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and in each ch across. (16 sc) Rotate the work. Sc in the free loop on the other side of each ch across, starting in the same ch as the last stitch. (16 sc) Join with a sl st to the first sc. (32)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cat-wallet-turn.svg" alt="The slip-knot end of the chain of 17, with single crochet on top and underneath. Correct: the last single crochet on top and the first one underneath go into the same chain, the one next to the slip knot, giving 16 on each side and 32 in the round. Common mistake: the first underside stitch goes into the next chain instead, leaving 15 underneath and 31 in the round. Working the skipped chain next to the hook gives the opposite error, 33." width="480" height="711" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The turn at the end of the chain. The last sc on top and the first sc underneath share one chain.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rnds 2–12:** Ch 1 (does not count), sc in the same st as the join and in each st around. Join with a sl st to the first sc. (32)

Place a marker in the first stitch of round 2 and move it up each round. Count at the end of every round. If you have 31 or 33, the error is almost always at one of the two ends of the chain.

**Test the card at round 10.** Flatten the tube with the join at one side fold and slide a card in. It should go in flat with a little room at each side. Too tight: start again on a larger hook. Too loose: a smaller one.

Do not fasten off.

### Stage 2: the flap

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cat-wallet-flap.svg" alt="Diagram. Side view of the pocket with the flap rising from the back and folding over the front, and a snap on the front. Flap rows as worked: rows 1 to 7 are 16 stitches, row 8 is 14 and row 9 is 12, each with a decrease at both ends." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The flap is worked in rows over half of round 12. Decrease at both ends of rows 8 and 9.</figcaption>
</figure>

Flatten the pocket without stretching and mark both side folds so there are 16 rim stitches on each face. The 16 stitches between the markers on the chosen back form the flap; the front stays unworked. If the round join is not at a fold, fasten off and rejoin at the first marked back stitch. Otherwise continue from the join.

**Row 1:** Ch 1 (does not count), sc in the same st as the join and in the next 15 sts. Turn. (16)

**Rows 2–7:** Ch 1, sc in each st across. Turn. (16)

**Row 8:** Ch 1, sc2tog, sc in the next 12 sts, sc2tog. Turn. (14)

**Row 9:** Ch 1, sc2tog, sc in the next 10 sts, sc2tog. (12)

Fasten off and leave a 6 inch tail. Each decrease row uses the whole row below: 2 + 12 + 2 = 16, then 2 + 10 + 2 = 14.

Fold the flap over the opening. It should cover the front from the rim down about 1.6 inches, leaving roughly the bottom third of the front showing.

### Stage 3: the ears

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cat-wallet-ears.svg" alt="Diagram. One ear: row 1 is 5 single crochet, row 2 is 3, row 3 is a single stitch. Placement on the 16-stitch fold: 1 stitch, an ear over 5, a gap of 4, an ear over 5, 1 stitch." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. 1 + 5 + 4 + 5 + 1 = 16, so the ears sit symmetrically.</figcaption>
</figure>

![Two small black ears on the top fold of the wallet](../../assets/articles/black-cat-crochet-wallet-ears.jpg "Illustration.")

Make 2, in black, leaving a 10 inch tail at the start of each.

**Row 1:** Ch 6. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and in each ch across. Turn. (5)

**Row 2:** Ch 1, sc2tog, sc in the next st, sc2tog. Turn. (3)

**Row 3:** Ch 1, sc3tog. (1)

Fasten off. Each ear is about 1.25 inches across the base and 0.6 inch tall.

### Stage 4: the face

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cat-wallet-face.svg" alt="Diagram. The closed flap as a grid 16 stitches across and 9 rows tall, row 1 at the top fold. Gold eyes on rows 3 and 4 over stitches 4 to 5 and 12 to 13, with black vertical pupils. A pink nose on rows 5 and 6 over stitches 8 and 9. Three white whiskers on each side across rows 5 to 7. The snap is under row 8 at the center." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Count stitches and rows on the flap before the first stitch of embroidery.</figcaption>
</figure>

![Close view of the gold eyes, pink nose, and three whiskers on each side](../../assets/articles/black-cat-crochet-wallet-face.jpg "Illustration.")

Close the flap first. Row 1 is now at the top fold. If you embroider with the flap standing up, the face ends up upside down.

1. **Eyes.** With gold, fill an oval over stitches 4 and 5 on rows 3 and 4 with four or five close straight stitches, side by side. Repeat over stitches 12 and 13.
2. **Pupils.** With black, one vertical straight stitch down the middle of each eye, from the top of row 3 to the bottom of row 4.
3. **Nose.** With pink, a small triangle pointing down, centered between stitches 8 and 9 on rows 5 and 6. Three or four stitches that fan from one bottom point.
4. **Whiskers.** With white, three straight stitches on each side of the nose, fanning outward across rows 5 to 7 to about stitch 2 and stitch 15. Whiskers longer than half an inch snag. Tack each one down at its middle with a tiny stitch over it.

Work through the flap only, and keep the stitches on the back short. The underside of the flap rubs against the front of the wallet every time it opens.

## Assembly and finishing

1. **Ears.** Hold each ear upright with its row 1 edge along the top fold, where the flap meets the back. Place them as the diagram shows: 1 stitch in from each side, 4 stitches apart. Whipstitch the base to the fold through both loops, then go back along the base a second time.
2. **Snap.** Close the flap and push a pin through to the front at the center of row 8. Sew the ball half of the snap to the underside of the flap at row 8, between the two middle stitches of that 14-stitch row, local stitches 7 and 8. Sew the socket half to the outside of the front at the pin, which is usually near round 5. Use black thread doubled, four or five passes through each hole, and do not go through to the inside of the pocket.
3. **Ends.** Weave the tails inside the tube along a fold, changing direction once, as in [weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cat-wallet-finished.svg" alt="Diagram to scale. The closed wallet about 4 inches wide and 2.4 inches tall with ears above. The open pocket with an ID-1 card drawn inside, about 3.8 inches wide and 2.3 inches deep inside on paper." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The card fits on paper with a little room. Your gauge decides whether it fits in yarn.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Make it yours

- **Wider or deeper.** One more chain adds one stitch to each side, about 0.25 inch of width. Each extra round adds about 0.2 inch of depth. Change the flap counts by the same number of stitches.
- **Eye color.** Green or amber eyes read as cat just as well. Avoid white eyes on black; they read as a ghost.
- **A gray or orange cat.** Black embroidery for pupils, a darker nose, and the same counts.
- **Lining.** A fabric lining takes up the ease the card needs. Chain 19 instead of 17, then hand-sew a fabric tube inside with the raw edge folded under at the rim.
- **Reinforcement.** Sew a small felt circle behind the socket half of the snap on the inside of the front, so the snap pulls on felt rather than on two stitches.

## Check the flap and closures before sewing

Number embroidery positions across the closed flap, from the same physical edge each time. Rows 1–7 are 16 stitches, row 8 is 14 and row 9 is 12: the narrowed rows have different local center numbers. Temporarily tack the ears to the back-fold edge, open the flap fully, and check that the ear bases do not bridge the opening. Place the loaded wallet on the table to mark the snap; use a clip or removable thread marker instead of pushing a pin toward your hand. Open and close it several times before securing sewing tails. Wider versions need recentered decoration and a new closure mark.

## Common mistakes and fixes

**Round 1 has 31 or 33 stitches.** The skipped chain was worked, or the first chain on the other side was skipped. The first single crochet on the underside goes into the same chain as the last one on top.

**The join leans.** A slight lean can occur even with correct joined-round counts. Count 32 stitches, exclude the joining slip stitch and ch 1, and work the first sc into the same stitch as the join. Set the flap position from the actual side folds, not from a seam that has drifted.

**The flap is off center.** Row 1 started in the wrong place. The flap should start right at the marked side fold. Rejoin there if the round join has drifted.

**The face is upside down.** It was embroidered with the flap standing up. Pick it out and work with the flap closed.

**The ears flop.** Loose fabric. Make the ears on a hook one size smaller, and sew the base twice. A drop of fabric stiffener inside the ear is an option, but test it on a swatch.

**The snap will not meet.** The flap was closed loosely when you marked it. Mark with a card inside the wallet.

## Care

Empty the wallet. Hand wash in cool water and dry flat. Gold and white yarn next to black can pick up dye in the first wash, so test a scrap of the black first if it is a new yarn: wet it, press it in a white paper towel, and look for color.

## Frequently asked

**Will a bank card fit?** On paper, yes: the inside is about 3.8 by 2.3 inches against a 3.37 by 2.125 inch card, so test with a real card at round 10 to be sure.

**How many cards?** It depends on your gauge and the cards. No card-stack capacity has been measured. Test your intended stack before placing the snap; a thicker stack needs additional width and flap reach.

**Can it carry coins?** It is not designed for loose coins. The flap covers the opening but the corners are open, and a snap pops under weight.

**Can I use safety eyes?** Their backs press into the pocket and catch cards. Embroidery lies flat.

**Is it left-handed friendly?** Yes. The rounds travel the other way, but the counts and placement do not change. See [left-handed crochet](/left-handed-crochet/).

**Can I sell what I make?** Yes, the finished wallets. Please link to this page rather than reposting the pattern.

## More Halloween wallets

Each wallet in the set teaches a different construction:

- [Ghost crochet wallet](/ghost-crochet-wallet/): two shaped panels and a scalloped hem
- [Pumpkin crochet wallet](/pumpkin-crochet-wallet/): sideways back-loop ribbing and a leaf tab
- [Witch hat crochet wallet](/witch-hat-crochet-wallet/): an inner divider and a worked buttonhole
- [Spiderweb crochet wallet](/spiderweb-crochet-wallet/): a pointed flap and an embroidered web

To size a flap wallet for any card stack, see the [Halloween card wallet](/halloween-crochet-card-wallet/). Decreases are explained in [increases and decreases](/crochet-increase-decrease/).

The cat wallet comes down to one round worked around both sides of a chain. Count 32 at the end of every round, test the card at round 10, and embroider with the flap closed.


# How to Change Yarn Color in Crochet Cleanly

Source: src/content/posts/changing-yarn-color-crochet.md



Stripes look clean or messy for one reason. **Change color on the final yarn-over of the last stitch in the old color**, not at the start of the new row.

That is the difference between a straight color line and a dot of the old color at the edge of every row.


![A crochet swatch with a clean cream to terracotta color change](../../assets/articles/changing-yarn-color-crochet-result.jpg "Illustration. Change on the last yarn-over so the new color starts clean.")
## The basic color change

![Hook joining terracotta yarn on the last cream yarn-over](../../assets/articles/changing-yarn-color-crochet-1.jpg "Illustration. Finish the old stitch with the new color.")

For single crochet or double crochet, work the last stitch in the old color until two loops remain on the hook. For half double crochet, three loops remain before the final yarn-over; pull the new color through all three.

1. Stop before the final yarn-over. Two loops are still on the hook for sc or dc; three remain for hdc.
2. Drop the old color. Yarn over with the new color.
3. Pull the new color through all remaining loops to complete the stitch.

The stitch body stays the old color. The loop on the hook is the new color. Leave both tails about six inches, and keep going.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/color-change-last-yarn-over.svg" alt="Diagram in three steps. Two old-color loops on the hook, a yarn-over with the new color, then a finished old-color stitch with a new-color loop on the hook." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The new color goes in on the yarn-over that closes the last old stitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

The same rule holds for every stitch height. The new color always goes in on the yarn-over that closes the stitch.

## Between rows

If you finish the row in the old color, then chain up in the new color, the last stitch of the old row sits at the edge of the new stripe. It reads as a mistake.

Complete the last stitch of the old row with the new color, using the steps above. Turn. The turning chain is already the new color, and the line at the edge is straight.

<div class="tip">

Work four rows of single crochet. Change the wrong way once, at the start of a row, and the right way once, in the last stitch of the previous row. Hold the swatch next to a window. The difference is obvious.

</div>

## Mid-round

![Hands joining terracotta yarn mid-round on a cream amigurumi](../../assets/articles/changing-yarn-color-crochet-2.jpg "Illustration. Mid-round changes use the same last-yarn-over rule.")

Same yarn-over. On a joined round, make the change at the join and the seam hides it.

A spiral has no join, so the color change steps diagonally. That is built into spiral rounds. For stripes, joined rounds avoid it.

You can change in the middle of a row the same way. A clean vertical block of color means managing two yarns at once. That is intarsia, not a simple stripe.

## Carry it, or cut it?

**Carry the unused color up the side** when the stripe is narrow, about four rows or fewer. Leave it at the edge and pick it up when you need it. Catch it lightly against the edge stitches so it does not hang.

**Cut it** when the stripe is wider, about five rows or more. A long float up the side snags, and it pulls the edge in. Cut, leave a six-inch tail, and weave it in.

Do not carry a float across the back of ordinary stripes. That is a different colorwork method, and in a simple stripe it makes a mess.

Even-numbered stripes keep the color you need waiting at the same edge, so you can carry the narrow ones instead of cutting every row.

## Planning stripes before you start

Where each color finishes decides whether you can carry it. Work it out on paper first.

| Plan, worked flat in rows | Where the idle color waits | Carry or cut |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Two colors, 2 rows each | At the edge where you need it | Carry. Each color sits out 2 rows. |
| Two colors, 3 rows each | At the opposite edge | Cut, or the yarn has to cross the whole row |
| Three colors, 2 rows each | At the edge where you need it | Carry. Each color sits out 4 rows, the limit. |
| Two colors, 6 rows each | At the edge where you need it | Cut. A 6-row float is too long. |

The rule behind the table: an even number of rows brings you back to the edge you started from, so the waiting yarn is right there. An odd number leaves you at the other edge.

Joined rounds have no far edge. Every round ends where it began, so the idle color is always at the join. Carry it up the inside at the join for narrow stripes, as you would up the side of flat work.

## A new ball of the same color

Join the new ball on the final yarn-over, the same way. It disappears because both yarns match.

Do not knot it. Knots work to the surface with washing and show as a bump. Weave both tails. Join at the end of a row when you can, so the tails hide in the edge.

The magic knot and the Russian join splice two strands into one continuous yarn. The magic knot is faster and leaves a very small hard point. The Russian join is bulkier, smooth, and secure. Neither suits slippery yarn, or baby items, where woven ends are safer.

The color change sits in the top of the last old stitch. On the wrong side, and on tall stitches, the new color can look as if it bleeds down a little. That is normal. It shows most in double crochet and taller stitches, and least in single crochet, which is why graphic colorwork is usually single crochet.

## Practice a color change in two stitch heights

Make ten sc, completing the tenth with color B on its final pull-through; ch 1 in B, turn and work ten sc. On a separate hdc swatch, stop the last hdc with three loops on the hook and pull B through all three; ch 2, turn and retain the original stitch count. Leave the idle yarn slack enough that the edge remains straight. Changing color does not add a stitch. If a new-color loop looks loose, snug the working yarn gently after making the next stitch, then weave both tails into matching-color stitches.

## Common mistakes

**Changing at the start of the row.** Change in the last stitch of the previous row.

**Pulling the new color tight.** The first stitches of a new color get yanked, and the edge pinches. Keep the first two stitches loose on purpose.

**Cutting the tail too short.** Six inches is the minimum. Longer if you will seam with it. A three-inch tail will not stay woven.

**Weaving a tail along the color boundary.** A dark tail goes through dark stitches. A light tail goes through light stitches. Navy through a cream stripe shows, especially after washing.

**Saving every end for the end.** Weave as you go, or a striped blanket becomes a long finishing job.

## Frequently asked

**Can I change color in the middle of a row?** Yes. Same technique. A clean vertical edge means two yarns in play at once, which is intarsia rather than striping.

**How do I avoid a pile of ends on stripes?** Use even-numbered stripes so the next color is waiting at the same edge, and carry the narrow stripes up the side instead of cutting them.

**Why does the color change look like a step?** You are in a spiral. Joined rounds give stripes a straight join.


# Christmas Granny Square Variations

Source: src/content/posts/christmas-granny-square-variations.md



A granny square is the same move over and over: clusters of three double crochets worked into the **gaps**, never into the tops of the stitches. Once that is in your hands, the interesting part is color. Christmas is where those choices go muddy. Four layouts below. None of them changes the stitch. The [Halloween granny squares](/halloween-granny-square-variations/) use this same cluster. Their problem is a candy-corn order and a dark join. This page's problem is red sitting against green.

US terms throughout. The rounds are the standard granny square. The pictures are illustrations of the finished look, so treat the color layouts as a plan you can change to suit your yarn.


![A set of Christmas granny squares in red green and cream](../../assets/articles/christmas-granny-square-variations-result.jpg "Illustration. The finished look these color variations build toward.")
## Starting every granny round consistently

After round 1, travel with slip stitches across the next two dc and into the next corner space. In that corner, ch 3 (counts as the first dc), then work 2 dc, ch 2, and 3 dc. This is the first complete corner. Work the other corners and side clusters as described, separating adjacent groups with ch 1, and join to the top of the beginning ch 3. When changing colors, fastening off and rejoining the new color in a corner is an alternative to traveling. Never make ch 3 plus three dc for the first half-corner: that would be four dc instead of three.

## What you need

- Worsted-weight yarn ([yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/)) in red, green, white, and optionally a metallic gold
- A hook that matches the yarn, often a US H/8 (5.0 mm)
- A yarn needle. There will be a lot of ends

US abbreviations throughout.

## The classic square, one color

![Hook working a granny cluster in seasonal yarn](../../assets/articles/christmas-granny-square-variations-1.jpg "Illustration. Clusters go into the gaps, never into the stitch tops.")

**Round 1.** Magic ring, then ch 3 (counts as the first dc), 2 dc into the ring, ch 2. (3 dc into the ring, ch 2) three times. Join to the top of the beginning ch-3. Four clusters, four ch-2 corners.

**Round 2.** Slip stitch across to the first corner space. In each corner space work (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc), with ch 1 between corners. Join.

**Every round after.** In each corner space work (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc). In each ch-1 space along the sides, work 3 dc. Put ch 1 between every pair of groups. Join at the end of the round.

Some granny patterns skip the ch 1 and put the clusters straight into the gaps between groups. Both are standard. Pick one and use it for every square in a set, or the squares will not match.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-square-anatomy.svg" alt="A granny square in three rounds. Corners are 3 double crochet, chain 2, 3 double crochet in the chain-2 space. Sides are 3 double crochet in each chain-1 space with chain 1 between groups. Round 1 has 1 cluster per side, round 2 has 2, round 3 has 3." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round N has N clusters along each side, counting the corner halves. That is the count to check.</figcaption>
</figure>

There is a count, and it is easy to check. Count the clusters along one side, including the half of each corner on that side. Round 3 has three, round 7 has seven. A side that comes up short has a missed corner or a skipped space.

<div class="tip">

**Work into the spaces, not the stitches.** The hook goes into the gap between clusters, not into the top of a double crochet. A square that goes stiff and loses its holes is what happens when you work into the stitch tops.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-side-space.svg" alt="One side of a granny square, round 3 worked over round 2. Correct: the side cluster of round 3 sits in the chain-1 space between round 2's two corner halves, with a chain-1 on each side of it. Common mistake: the cluster goes into the top of a double crochet instead. The gap closes, the side goes stiff, and the square starts to cup." width="480" height="721" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>A side cluster in the chain-1 space, and the same cluster pushed into a stitch top.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

## Variation 1: candy-cane rings

![A granny square with cream center and red outer rounds](../../assets/articles/christmas-granny-square-variations-2.jpg "Illustration. Change color between rounds for candy-cane rings.")

Change color by round, alternating red and white, each color for one round or for two. Keep the bands even all the way out. Uneven bands read as a mistake.

Finish on white. A white outer round separates each square from the next. Without it, a field of red-and-white squares turns into one busy cloth.

## Variation 2: star center, quiet border

Work the first two rounds in gold or white, then the rest in a single deep green. The eye lands on one bright center against a calm field. The accent is only two rounds, so a metallic that fights you only has to be fought briefly.

If the center is metallic, hold it with a strand of ordinary worsted rather than working it alone.

## Variation 3: the half-and-half square

Two colors split the square on the diagonal, red on two adjacent sides and green on the other two. The split runs through two opposite corners, so each round starts in one of them:

1. In the starting corner, with red: ch 3, 2 dc. That is the second half of the corner.
2. Work the next two sides and the corner between them in red.
3. In the opposite corner, work 3 dc in red, changing to green on the last yarn-over. Then ch 2, 3 dc in green.
4. Work the next two sides and the corner between them in green.
5. Back in the starting corner, work 3 dc, ch 2 in green. Join to the top of the ch 3, changing to red on the join.

Carry the color you are not using inside the stitches. Lay it along the top of the round below and work the double crochets over it, so it is hidden inside the clusters. Do not let it float across an open chain space, because a float on the front snags. If carrying feels clumsy, use a separate small ball for each half and twist the two yarns at each change.

Complete the last yarn-over of the stitch before the change with the new color. That step is covered in [changing yarn color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/).

## Variation 4: the Nordic square

Three rounds of cream, one round of red, three more rounds of cream, one round of green. Narrow stripes on a pale ground. It uses very little of the accent colors, so it is the best layout for leftovers, and it looks the least like a novelty project.

## Joining

**Match the edges.** Lay two squares so each cluster faces a cluster. Join without aligning and the corners drift.

**Use one join for the whole set.** Whip stitch through the back loops is flattest. A slip stitch join leaves a ridge, which looks intentional in a contrasting color. Joining as you go is faster and much harder to undo.

**Join in the color you finished on.** White on white disappears. Red on white becomes a grid. Either is fine if you chose it.

Weave ends when you finish each square. Saving them for the end of a blanket is how the project stalls. The method is in [weaving in ends and blocking](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

## Count clusters before changing the palette

A cluster on this page is three complete dc in one space, not dc3tog. Round 1 has four clusters. Round 2 has eight, round 3 has twelve and round 4 has sixteen. Along one side, count the corner-half cluster at each end: a four-round square has four clusters per side. The starting ch 3 replaces the first dc of the first cluster. If you add a full three dc after it, that cluster has four dc and will distort the count. Use the numbered four-round square in the granny-square tote tutorial when learning the basic square, then apply the color sequences here.

## Mistakes that muddy the color

**Red against green with nothing between them.** The two colors are close in value, so the boundary goes muddy at a distance. One round of white or cream between them fixes it.

**Too many colors, or a dark outer round on every square.** Four colors is the ceiling, and three is better. Dark green edges make the whole blanket read as dark green.

**Squares that do not match, or a missed corner.** A metallic or a different brand can work up at a different size from the rest. Check one square of each colorway before you make a stack. Every corner needs (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc). Miss one and the square goes off-square on the next round.

## Frequently asked

### How many squares for a blanket?

Make one, measure it, then divide your blanket size by that measurement. Deciding on a square count first is how the finished piece comes out an awkward rectangle.

### Can I mix the four layouts?

Yes, if they share an outer-round color. Mixed squares all edged in cream read as one set. The same squares edged in their own colors read as samples.

### Why is my square curling?

Too few stitches, usually a missed corner. A granny square should lie flat off the hook. Count the clusters on each side and find the short one.


# Crochet Abbreviations Chart: Every Term Explained (US & UK)

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-abbreviations-chart.md



A line like `2 hdc in next st, sk 1, rep from *` is ordinary pattern shorthand. The charts below are US terms, so print the page and keep it with the project.

<div class="warning">

**If the fabric is the wrong height, check US versus UK before you rip more rows.** The same letters name different stitches. Half as tall as the photo is usually this. The conversion is [further down the page](#us-vs-uk-terms-the-one-that-catches-everyone).

</div>

![A crochet abbreviations reference card beside yarn and a hook](../../assets/articles/crochet-abbreviations-chart-result.jpg "Illustration. Keep this chart open until the common abbreviations stick.")

## The stitches

![Cream crochet fabric beside a small stitch reference note](../../assets/articles/crochet-abbreviations-chart-1.jpg "Illustration. Same abbreviations show up in almost every US pattern.")

| Abbreviation | Stitch | In plain English |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ch` | chain | The starting loop-chain. Also used to make gaps and spaces. |
| `sl st` | slip stitch | The shortest stitch. Used to join rounds and travel across without adding height. |
| `sc` | single crochet | The short, dense, basic stitch. |
| `hdc` | half double crochet | Between an `sc` and a `dc` in height. |
| `dc` | double crochet | The tall workhorse stitch. Fast and slightly open. |
| `tr` or `trc` | treble (triple) crochet | Taller than `dc`, noticeably lacy. |
| `dtr` | double treble crochet | Taller again. Mostly seen in lace and shawls. |
| `trtr` (sometimes `ttr`) | triple treble crochet | Very tall. Rare outside decorative work. The Craft Yarn Council writes it `trtr` and uses `ttr` for Tunisian treble, so check the pattern's key. |

## Where to put the stitch

| Abbreviation | Meaning | In plain English |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `st` / `sts` | stitch / stitches | The loops along the top of your work. |
| `sp` / `sps` | space / spaces | The gap made by a chain, not a stitch. You work *into the hole*. |
| `lp` / `lps` | loop / loops | The strands on your hook, or the two strands at the top of a stitch. |
| `BLO` | back loop only | Insert the hook under the *far* loop only. Creates a ridged, stretchy fabric. |
| `FLO` | front loop only | Insert the hook under the *near* loop only. |
| `blo` / `flo` | back / front loop | Lowercase versions of the same instruction. Same meaning. |
| `fp` / `bp` | front post / back post | Work around the body of the stitch below rather than into its top. Gives you `fpdc`, `bpdc`, and ribbing. |
| `tch` / `t-ch` | turning chain | The chain(s) at the start of a row that give it height. |
| `ch-sp` | chain space | A space created by a chain. `ch-2 sp` means the gap made by two chains. |

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/front-back-loops.svg" alt="Diagram comparing a hook under both loops, under the front loop only, and under the back loop only." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Front loop is the strand nearer you. Back loop is the far strand. A plain stitch uses both.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Actions and counts

| Abbreviation | Meaning | In plain English |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `yo` | yarn over | Wrap the yarn over the hook. |
| `sk` | skip | Pass a stitch without working into it. Usually paired with a chain. |
| `inc` | increase | Add a stitch, normally by working two stitches into one. |
| `dec` | decrease | Remove a stitch by joining two into one. |
| `tog` | together | Worked together as one, as in `dc2tog`. |
| `sc2tog` | single crochet 2 together | A decrease. Two `sc` become one. |
| `dc2tog` | double crochet 2 together | The same idea with double crochet. |
| `inv dec` | invisible decrease | A neater decrease used in amigurumi. Hides the join. |
| `rep` | repeat | Do the marked section again. |
| `beg` | beginning | The start of the row or round. |
| `rnd` / `rnds` | round / rounds | Worked in a spiral or a joined circle, not back and forth. |
| `row` | row | Worked flat, turning at the end. |
| `RS` / `WS` | right side / wrong side | The public face of the fabric versus the back. |
| `pm` | place marker | Put a stitch marker here. You will need it later. |
| `sm` | slip marker | Move the marker up as you pass it. |
| `FO` | fasten off | Cut the yarn and pull it through to secure. |
| `MC` / `CC` | main color / contrast color | Which yarn to use. |
| `MR` | magic ring | An adjustable starting loop for working in the round. |
| `fsc` / `fdc` | foundation single / double crochet | Makes the chain and the first row at the same time. |

## Textured stitches

Designers define these differently. A special-stitches note in the pattern wins over this chart.

| Abbreviation | Name | Roughly |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `cl` | cluster | Several stitches joined at the top. |
| `sh` | shell | Several stitches worked into one stitch, fanning out. |
| `bo` | bobble | A cluster that pops forward. |
| `pc` | popcorn | A rounder, chunkier bobble. |
| `puff` | puff stitch | Several loops drawn up and closed together. |
| `pico` / `p` | picot | A tiny decorative chain bump, common on edgings. |

## US vs UK terms: the one that catches everyone

![Dense single crochet swatch beside taller double crochet fabric](../../assets/articles/crochet-abbreviations-chart-2.jpg "Illustration. US and UK reuse the same letters for different stitch heights.")

The names are shifted by one. What the US calls a single crochet, the UK calls a double crochet. A UK `dc` is the short, dense stitch. A US `dc` is the tall one.

| US term | UK term |
| --- | --- |
| slip stitch (`sl st`) | slip stitch (`ss` or `sl st`) |
| single crochet (`sc`) | double crochet (`dc`) |
| half double crochet (`hdc`) | half treble (`htr`) |
| double crochet (`dc`) | treble (`tr`) |
| treble crochet (`tr`) | double treble (`dtr`) |
| double treble (`dtr`) | triple treble (`trtr`) |

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/us-uk-terms.svg" alt="Chart pairing US stitch names with UK names. US single crochet matches UK double crochet. US double crochet matches UK treble." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The letters move one stitch taller in UK terms. A UK dc is the short stitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

**`sc` means US terms.** UK terminology has no single crochet. **`htr` means UK terms.** `ss` and `yoh` are also used by some US designers, so neither proves that a pattern uses UK terminology. "Colour", "tension" instead of "gauge", and grams instead of yards point to a UK or Australian designer. If you still cannot tell, work a small swatch both ways and compare it with the photo. A fabric half as tall as the picture is the giveaway.

## Reading the punctuation

Parentheses put stitches in one place: `(2 dc, ch 1, 2 dc) in next st`. At the end of a row they are the count, `(12 sts)`. Some patterns also use them for a repeat, `(sc, inc) 6 times`, so read what follows the closing parenthesis. Brackets repeat: `[sc, ch 1] x 6`. An asterisk starts a repeat that runs to the end. A number before a stitch is how many to make (`3 dc` is three double crochets). A number inside the name is how many stitches one decrease uses (`dc3tog` is one stitch made from three). If the previous row has 24 stitches, `*2 dc in next st, sk 1 st, rep from * to end (24 sts)` finishes at 24: each repeat uses 2 stitches and makes 2.

## Read the placement words along with the abbreviation

“Sc 3” means one sc in each of three successive stitches unless the instruction specifies “in the same stitch.” “3 sc in next st” means three sc all in one stitch. For (sc 2, inc) six times, each repeat uses three old stitches and makes four new stitches: start with 18 and finish with 24. “Ch-2 space” is the gap under the chain bridge; “each of the two chains” means the individual links. For special stitches, use the pattern-specific definition even if another designer uses the same abbreviation differently. Consult the [Craft Yarn Council list](https://www.craftyarncouncil.com/standards/crochet-abbreviations) for standard US terms.

## Mistakes

Some designers count the starting `ch 3` on a double-crochet row as the first stitch. Others do not. A stitch gained or lost every row is usually this. `ch-2 sp` means the hole, not the chain links. Check US or UK again when you change designers.

## Frequently asked

**Is there an official list?** The Craft Yarn Council publishes a master list of US abbreviations, which this chart was checked against. This chart also includes common ones that list does not cover, such as `MR`, `FO`, and `inv dec`. A pattern's own special-stitches key overrides both.

**Why symbols instead of words?** Stitch diagrams, common in Japanese patterns and lace, draw the motif.

**Do I need to memorize this?** No. About fifteen show up in the first month. Look the rest up.


# 12 Crochet Bag Charm Ideas from Scrap Yarn

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-bag-charm-ideas.md



A bag charm is a scrap project that has to survive a zipper pull. If the loop is thin, it saws through in a week. If the charm is heavy, it knocks the bag while you walk.

Below are 12 shapes worth making from leftover worsted cotton. Only the tiny heart has a full stitch pattern. The other eleven are ideas: color, size, and what to watch. They are not tutorials. Do not treat the group photo as proof that each shape has been written out. The loop that goes on a zipper should be a short chain of the same cotton as the charm, closed with a slip stitch, and thick enough that it does not saw through. Test the loop on the zipper pull before you weave the end. A loop that barely fits will cut itself.

US terms. Photos are illustrations. The heart has not been physically tested.

![A group of small crochet charms on linen](../../assets/articles/crochet-bag-charm-ideas-result.jpg "Illustration. A set of charm ideas. Only the heart pattern on this page is written in full.")

## Shared rules

- Cotton scraps. Worsted makes a charm of about 2 inches. Sport or fingering weight with a smaller hook makes a smaller one. Amounts have not been weighed. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- A hook a size smaller than the label suggests, so the fabric stays firm. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- A split ring, plus a chain of about 12 stitches sewn on as the loop.
- Stuff very lightly, or not at all. Keep a charm small and light, about 2 inches or less, so it does not knock against the bag.

Sew the loop to a solid part of the charm, weave the tail through the chain three times, and clip the ring on after. Do not crochet around the metal.

![Four small charms: strawberry, heart, daisy, pumpkin](../../assets/articles/crochet-bag-charm-ideas-1.jpg "Illustration. Close crops of four ideas. Stitch patterns for these, except the heart, are not on this page.")

## The twelve

**Strawberry.** Red body, green cap. Beginner if you already make small balls. The seeds are embroidered after, with shallow stitches, so the back stays flat against a bag.

**Cherry.** Two tiny red balls and a green chain stem. The stem is the weak point. Chain it a little thicker by holding two strands, or it kinks and snaps.

**Mini flower.** Five petals around a small center. A full-size flower with a different construction is [how to crochet a simple flower](/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower/). That pattern is not a charm. Scale is the whole problem: a flower that looks right at 4 inches looks sparse at 1 inch.

**Tiny heart.** Full pattern below. Beginner. Rows, then two short lobes.

**Bow.** Two loops and a center wrap. Intermediate only because the tails twist. Pin the loops before you wrap the center or the bow collapses into a knot.

**Mini mushroom.** A short white stem and a red cap. Beginner shapes, fiddly sewing. The cap wants to slide off a skinny stem. Sew through the center, not just the edge.

**Daisy.** White petals, yellow center. Same warning as the mini flower. Keep petal counts low or the charm becomes a disk.

**Star.** Five points. The three-round flat star in [Christmas ornament ideas](/easy-crochet-christmas-ornaments/) works as a charm in a thinner yarn. Stiffen it lightly or the points flop.

**Pumpkin.** Orange ball, brown seam lines, short stem. A stuffed pumpkin with a full write-up is [how to crochet a pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/). Use that if you want a bowl-sized pumpkin. A charm is the same idea at scrap size, and it is not written out here.

**Mini ghost.** White blob and two embroidered eyes. A pouch-sized ghost with a drawstring is a [drawstring bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/) with a face. A charm has no opening.

**Tiny purse.** A flat rectangle folded and seamed, with a strap of chain. Beginner. It is a charm, not a coin purse. Do not expect it to hold anything.

**Mini Christmas tree.** Green triangle, small trunk. A standing tree pattern is [how to crochet a Christmas tree](/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-tree/). That one is an ornament-scale project, not this charm.

## Tiny heart pattern

Work in rows. Ch 1 at the end of a row does not count as a stitch.

![Hands working a small pink heart](../../assets/articles/crochet-bag-charm-ideas-2.jpg "Illustration. The heart is rows of single crochet, split into two lobes at the top.")

Chain 2.

**Row 1:** sc in the 2nd chain from the hook. Ch 1, turn. (1)

**Row 2:** inc. Ch 1, turn. (2)

**Row 3:** inc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (4)

**Row 4:** inc, sc 2, inc. Ch 1, turn. (6)

**Row 5:** inc, sc 4, inc. Ch 1, turn. (8)

**Row 6:** sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (8)

**Row 7, first lobe:** sc in the next 4 stitches. Ch 1, turn. Leave the other 4 unworked. (4)

**Row 8:** dec, dec. (2)

Fasten off.

**Second lobe:** join the yarn in the first unworked stitch of Row 6. Sc in that stitch and the next 3. Ch 1, turn. (4)

**Next row:** dec, dec. (2)

Fasten off. Weave the ends.

Row 5 is 8 because 6 stitches plus 2 increases equal 8. Each lobe takes 4 of those 8. Two decreases on 4 stitches equal 2.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/heart-charm-rows.svg" alt="A heart worked from the point up. Rows 1 to 5 grow 1, 2, 4, 6, 8 stitches, row 6 is 8 even, and each lobe uses 4 of the 8 stitches, worked as 4 then 2. A dashed outline shows the optional single crochet edging with 3 sc at the point and a slip stitch in the dip." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Rows grow from the point, then the top splits into two lobes. The edging rounds off the steps.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Edging (optional).** The rows leave a stepped outline. To smooth it, join yarn at the bottom point and sc evenly all the way around, one sc in each row end and each stitch, 3 sc in the bottom point, and a sl st into the dip between the lobes. Join to the first sc and fasten off. The edging adds a stitch of width all round.

At 4 sc to the inch, 8 stitches are about 2 inches across, a little more with the edging. For a smaller charm, use a thinner yarn and hook rather than fewer rows. Fewer rows change the shape.

Chain 12 for the hanger. Sew both ends between the lobes, on the back, so the point hangs down.

![A small heart on a brass ring](../../assets/articles/crochet-bag-charm-ideas-3.jpg "Illustration. The ring goes through a sewn chain. It is not stitched into the heart.")

## On the bag

One charm on a zipper is enough. Three on the same pull tangle. If you want a set, use separate rings on separate hardware.

![Charms hanging from a handbag](../../assets/articles/crochet-bag-charm-ideas-4.jpg "Illustration. Separate rings. The photo is a styling idea, not twelve finished patterns.")

## Complete one heart before making a set

For the heart, inc means two sc into one stitch and dec means sc2tog. Body counts are 1, 2, 4, 6, 8, 8. On row 7, work only four stitches, turn and decrease twice; the other four body stitches must remain untouched for the second lobe. Mark that first unworked stitch before fastening off. The lobes finish at two stitches each. After weaving tails, sew a reinforced hanger through several stitches on the back between the lobes, keeping the center dip visible. Test with the charm alone. The other listed shapes remain ideas, not eleven additional complete patterns.

## Mistakes

**A loop of one strand.** It wears through. Use the chain, then sew it down so the ring cannot saw one fiber.

**Copying a designer's charm from a photo.** A shape idea is not a license to redraw someone else's pattern. The heart above is the pattern on this page. For the rest, write your own counts or use a project you already have the rights to make.

**Heavy stuffing.** The charm should not thunk against the bag.

Wash a new charm before it lives on a pale bag. Cotton can still drop a little dye, especially red and orange. Rinse it, let it dry, and then clip it on.

## Care

Take the ring off. Hand wash cool. Dry flat. Do not iron. A pressed heart becomes a felted lump if the yarn is wool. Cotton is the safer scrap.

## Frequently asked

**Are all 12 patterns on this page?**

No. The heart is complete. The list is there so you can pick a scrap project and see the attachment method. A missing pattern is not an invitation to invent stitch counts and call them tested.

**Can I sell the hearts?**

Finished hearts, yes. Do not republish the row instructions.

**What yarn should I avoid?**

Eyelash and mohair. The shape disappears, and the loop grabs every sweater you walk past.

## Next

A useful scrap that is not a charm is the lip-balm holder in the [device case guide](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/). If you want a Halloween piece that holds something, use a [drawstring treat bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/) instead of a mini pumpkin charm.


# Crochet Christmas Gift Ideas

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-christmas-gift-ideas.md



The useful Christmas question is not "what can I invent in December." It is which finished object you can actually complete before the date on the calendar. Every project below already has a tutorial on this site. Yarn that behaves in small holiday stitches is covered in [yarn for Christmas crochet](/yarn-for-christmas-crochet/).

<div class="warning">

This is a guide to existing tutorials rather than a new pattern. Pick the project you can finish, not the one with the newest date.

</div>

![Crochet holiday gifts: a holly ornament, a red mug cozy with a tree, a gingerbread keychain, and a green snowflake pouch](../../assets/articles/crochet-christmas-gift-ideas-thumb.png)

## If you have one evening

- [Christmas gift card holder](/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-gift-card-holder/). A flat pocket. One of the few gifts that is finished when the sewing is done.
- [Mug cozy](/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners/). Measure the mug first or it will not close.
- [Snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/). Three rounds, then stiffening. Allow a day for it to dry hard.
- [Scrunchie](/how-to-crochet-a-scrunchie/). Sized from a hair elastic, so you do not need the person's measurements.
- [Simple flower](/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower/). Sew it to a plain gift or to the mug cozy.
- [Fall leaf](/how-to-crochet-a-fall-leaf/). A small appliqué. The stitch count is on paper, not from a finished leaf.
- [Halloween coasters](/free-pattern-halloween-coasters/). A flat pumpkin disc, plus a granny set. Not a hot pad.

The [ornament ideas](/easy-crochet-christmas-ornaments/) page lists fifteen shapes and writes one flat star in full. Use that star if you want a written pattern, and use the links on that page for the tree and the snowflake.

## If you have a weekend

- [Chunky beanie](/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie/). Bulky yarn finishes faster than worsted. Measure the head.
- [Ear warmer](/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer/). A good gift when you do not know the hat size.
- [Fingerless gloves](/how-to-crochet-fingerless-gloves/). Measure the hand first. With the thumb gap opposite the round join, both gloves are worked the same.
- [Book or e-reader sleeve](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/). Measure the device. The same folded shape fits many e-readers if you measure it instead of guessing a brand size.
- [Wine bottle bag](/how-to-crochet-a-wine-bottle-bag/). The bottle is the form. Crochet to that bottle.

## If you are starting in October

- [Gnome](/how-to-crochet-a-gnome/). Four small pieces. The hat length is what makes it read as a gnome.
- [Christmas tree](/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-tree/). A cone, not a blanket.
- [Beginner scarf](/how-to-crochet-a-beginner-scarf/). One stitch, repeated. Swatch before you trust the width.
- [Hooded scarf](/how-to-crochet-a-hooded-scarf/). Long. Start it before December.
- [Baby blanket](/how-to-crochet-a-baby-blanket/). A plain rectangle. Wash a swatch before you buy all the yarn. No buttons and no appliqués.
- [Slippers](/how-to-crochet-slippers/). A sole that is pure yarn is slippery on wood floors. Read the finishing notes before you gift them.

## People who do not want another hat

- [Drawstring bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/) if Halloween yarn is still out, or the [makeup pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch/) and [pumpkin zipper pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-zipper-pouch/).
- [Laptop sleeve](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/) or [phone case](/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case/) only if you can measure the device. A guessed phone size is a gift that does not fit. A phone with a strap is the [phone crossbody](/how-to-crochet-a-strap-bag/).
- [Market bag](/how-to-crochet-a-market-bag/) for someone who actually carries one.
- [Bowl cozy](/how-to-crochet-a-bowl-cozy/) only if you read the heat section first. It is cotton, it is not a hot pad, and it does not claim to be microwave-safe.

## Sizing a gift without asking

Half the projects above need a measurement, and asking spoils the surprise. Measure something the person already owns instead.

- **A hat they wear.** Lay it flat, measure across the brim, and double it. That is the hat's relaxed size, which already includes the ease they like. Use it as the target in the [chunky beanie](/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie/).
- **A phone or e-reader.** Measure the device in the case they use, because that is what has to fit. The [phone case](/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case/) explains why thickness matters as much as width.
- **Gloves.** Measure around the palm of a glove that fits them, lying flat, and double it.
- **Slippers or shoes.** Measure the inside length of a shoe they wear, heel to toe. That is close to the foot length, though a shoe usually has a little extra room. [Slippers](/how-to-crochet-slippers/) also need a measurement over the top of the foot, which only the foot can give, so they are hard to make as a surprise.

If you cannot measure anything, pick a project that does not need it: the scrunchie, an ornament, the gift card holder, or the ear warmer, which has some stretch.

## Put a care card in the box

A crochet gift is easy to ruin in its first wash. Write a small card with three facts and tuck it in:

- **The fiber**, copied from the yarn label: acrylic, cotton, wool, or a blend.
- **How to wash it.** Copy the label's symbols or write them out. Hand wash and dry flat is the safe default for anything with stuffing or stiffener.
- **What it is not.** A cotton cozy is not a hot pad. Slippers slide on hard floors. A stiffened ornament dissolves if it gets wet. Say so on the card, because the person opening it did not read the tutorial.

Keep the ball band yourself, pinned to a leftover length of yarn, so you can repair the gift later in the same yarn.

## What not to promise

Do not tell someone a project is done when the ends are still hanging out. [Weaving ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/) is part of the gift. Do not stuff a new amigurumi pattern into the same week as a blanket. The [Santa](/crochet-santa-amigurumi-for-beginners/) tutorial is a technique page for that shape, separate from the gnome.

## Choose a tutorial you can size and finish

Open the linked pattern before buying yarn. Write down its required skills, measurements, accessories, and drying time. For a hat, ear warmer, or gloves, obtain a measurement or a suitable reference item; stretch does not remove the need to size the gift. Time one repeat or ten rows in your own yarn, estimate the remaining crochet, then add assembly, end-weaving, and any blocking or stiffener drying. An ornament that needs to dry overnight is not ready when the last stitch is made. This page is a project selector; the full instructions are on the linked pages.

## Frequently asked

### Which of these is the best first Christmas project?

The gift card holder or the flat star on the ornament page. Both are small, and both tell you quickly whether your tension will cooperate.

### Can I sell these?

The tutorials explain techniques and original project notes for this site. Selling a finished object you made is ordinary. Copying another designer's published pattern is not, and this page does not grant that.


# Crochet Gauge: How to Measure It and Fix It

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-gauge.md



Gauge is the number of stitches and rows that fit into a set measurement, almost always 4 inches. Two people can use the same yarn, hook, and instructions and come out two inches apart, because one of them holds the yarn tighter.


![A cream crochet gauge swatch measured with a metal ruler](../../assets/articles/crochet-gauge-result.jpg "Illustration. Measure stitches and rows over inches, not by counting the whole swatch.")
## How to read the gauge line

A pattern will say something like:

> **GAUGE:** 14 sc and 16 rows = 4 inches

Fourteen single crochet stitches across, and sixteen rows tall, should measure four inches. **Stitch gauge** is the width. **Row gauge** is the height.

Sometimes the line is in pattern repeats: "3 shell patterns = 4 inches." Count repeats instead of single stitches.

## Making the swatch

**Make it bigger than 4 inches.** A swatch that is exactly 4 inches includes the edge stitches, and edges are a different tension than the middle. Six inches square lets you measure the center.

**Use the stitch the project uses.** Shell gauge is not single crochet gauge.

**Use the yarn the project uses.** Fiber and twist both change the number.
