**Block the swatch if you will block the item.** Cotton and wool change when they are wet and dried flat. Acrylic mostly does not.

Keep the swatch. Pin the ball band to it. A swatch is awkward to put back into the project, and over time the set becomes a record of how each yarn behaves for you.

## Measuring

![Hands counting stitches across a gauge swatch with a ruler](../../assets/articles/crochet-gauge-1.jpg "Illustration. Count inside the fabric, away from the edges.")

Lay the swatch flat. Do not hold it up, pin it, stretch it, or smooth it. Crochet will stretch an inch if you ask it to, and then the measurement is a lie.

Put a ruler across the middle. Count the stitches inside 4 inches, including a partial stitch as a fraction. 14½ is a real answer. Turn the ruler and count rows the same way.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gauge-swatch.svg" alt="Diagram of a crochet swatch with a marked window across the middle labeled 4 inches. The edges of the swatch sit outside that window." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Measure inside the fabric. The edge stitches are a different tension and stay outside the window.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div class="tip">

A gauge window is a stiff card or metal plate with a 4-inch square hole. Lay it on the swatch and count what shows through.

</div>

## Adjusting

![Two cream gauge swatches compared with a metal ruler](../../assets/articles/crochet-gauge-2.jpg "Illustration. Too many stitches per inch means go up a hook size.")

**More stitches than the pattern in 4 inches** means each stitch is smaller. You crochet tightly. Go **up** a hook size and swatch again.

**Fewer stitches** means each stitch is bigger. You crochet loosely. Go **down** a hook size.

More stitches in the same space means each one is smaller, so a bigger hook makes them bigger. Change one size at a time. How far one hook size moves the gauge depends on the yarn and on your hands, so there is no fixed amount. Swatch again and measure.

Match **stitch gauge** first. Width is what determines fit, and most patterns tell you to work length to a measurement rather than to a row count. If the stitches are right and the rows are off, follow the inch measurements. Where a pattern insists on a row count for shaping, such as armholes or sleeve caps, try another hook and see whether both numbers get closer.

## When you can skip it

An exact gauge is optional only when the size can vary and you are working to a measured length or width. Swatch whenever a blanket must meet a target size, a coaster must support a particular mug, or a bag must fit specific contents. A wallet and a device sleeve need a fit check even though they are not garments.

Do not skip it for hats, sweaters, mittens, gloves, socks, or any fitted garment. Do not skip it for amigurumi either. A loose gauge leaves gaps, and stuffing shows through. Toys are worked tight on purpose.

If being two inches off would bother you, swatch.

## Working to your own gauge

If you cannot hit the pattern gauge, or you prefer the fabric you have, you can plan from your own numbers on a simple shape.

> Your gauge: 12 sc = 4 inches, so 3 stitches per inch.
> You want a 20-inch-wide blanket.
> 3 × 20 = **60 stitches** to start.

The same arithmetic shows why a tight swatch makes a small garment even when the stitch count is right.

> The pattern asks for 16 sc over 4 inches.
> Your swatch has 20 sc over 4 inches.
> Each of your stitches is 4 ÷ 20 = 0.2 inch wide. The pattern's stitch is 4 ÷ 16 = 0.25 inch wide.
> At the pattern's stitch count, your fabric is 16 ÷ 20 = 0.8 times as wide. That is 20 percent narrower.

Go up a hook size and measure again. Do not keep the tight swatch and hope the finished piece relaxes into the missing width.

The same sum is on the [gauge calculator](/crochet-gauge-calculator/). It predicts a width from your swatch. It does not measure the piece in your hands, and it does not know your row gauge.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/hook-gauge-choice.svg" alt="Diagram. More stitches than the pattern in four inches means try a larger hook. Fewer stitches means try a smaller hook." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. More stitches in four inches means the fabric is narrower. Fewer stitches means it is wider.</figcaption>
</figure>

The same arithmetic works for length, using your row gauge. It is reliable for rectangles: blankets, scarves, dishcloths, simple shawls. On a fitted garment, every increase and decrease sits on the original numbers, so changing the count means redrafting the shaping.

## Record both directions and the fabric condition

Write the stitch, hook in millimeters, yarn, measured width, measured height, and whether the swatch was washed and dried. Divide counted stitches by width for stitches per inch and counted rows by height for rows per inch. For a 20-inch rectangle at 3.5 stitches per inch, start with 70 stitches plus the pattern's foundation-chain allowance. At four rows per inch, a 12-inch length needs about 48 rows. A swatch worked in turned sc rows does not necessarily predict spiral-round height or BLO row gauge: measure the fabric used by the actual project.

## Common mistakes

**Measuring the whole swatch, edges included.** Count the middle.

**Stretching the fabric while you measure.** It will tell you a number you cannot repeat.

**Letting gauge drift.** Tension tightens when you are stressed and loosens when you are relaxed. That evens out on a large piece. A different brand of the same millimeter hook can still shift the gauge, because the head and the shaft taper differ. Finish on the hook you started with. A new dye lot or a different color of the same yarn occasionally behaves differently, because dye affects the fiber. Beginners often start tight and loosen as they relax. If the swatch is from your first week, make another one.

**Stopping between sizes without choosing.** Try that size in another brand, or take the closer hook and adjust the stitch count with the arithmetic above.

## Frequently asked

**Do I have to swatch every time?** For garments, yes. Once you know you usually run tight or loose, one swatch is enough to confirm the hook.

**My gauge matches and the item is still the wrong size.** Check the pattern's finished measurements against the person. A correct gauge can still be a size meant for someone else.

**What if only my row gauge is off?** Keep the stitch gauge and work the length in inches. Recalculate only where the pattern shapes from a row count.


# Crochet Ghost Amigurumi for Beginners

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-ghost-amigurumi-for-beginners.md



A crochet ghost is a forgiving first stuffed toy. You need a rounded head, a body that hangs or stands, a hem that looks a little ragged on purpose, and two eyes. There is one real problem to solve, and most simple ghost patterns skip it: an open-bottomed ghost has nowhere to keep its stuffing, and a soft single crochet hem cannot hold the ghost upright.

This page gives two answers. The **hanging ghost** stuffs only the head and cinches a round below it, which holds the stuffing and makes a neck at the same time. The **standing ghost** closes the bottom with a flat base and works the wavy skirt separately, into loops left free for that purpose. Both start the same way.

If magic rings still feel shaky, start with [crochet in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/). US terms. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations of the finished ghost, which has not been made up in yarn here, so its size depends on your yarn, hook, and stuffing.

![A finished white crochet ghost with a wavy hem and two simple embroidered eyes](../../assets/articles/crochet-ghost-amigurumi-for-beginners-result.jpg "Illustration. The intended look.")

## Two builds

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ghost-two-builds.svg" alt="Left, a hanging ghost with a loop on top, a stuffed head held by a yarn cinch through round 10, and an open body that flares into a wavy hem. Right, a standing ghost whose round 17 is worked in back loops, with decrease rounds folding under into a flat stuffed base, and a skirt worked into the front loops of round 16 that hangs over the base." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The hanging ghost holds its stuffing with a cinch. The standing ghost holds it with a closed base, and its skirt is added afterward.</figcaption>
</figure>

## What you need

- **White or cream worsted-weight yarn**, plus black yarn or embroidery floss for the face. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A hook one or two sizes smaller than the label**, so the fabric is tight enough to hide the stuffing. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Polyester stuffing, a yarn needle, a stitch marker.

## Step 1: the head and body (both builds)

![Hands crocheting a rounded white dome in the round](../../assets/articles/crochet-ghost-amigurumi-for-beginners-1.jpg "Illustration. The head and body are one piece, started at the top.")

Start at the top of the head and work down in a spiral. Mark the first stitch of each round.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1 starts in a magic ring, pulled tight so the top has no hole.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 4:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)

**Round 5:** (Sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (30)

**Rounds 6–16:** Sc in each stitch. (30)

On an even round, the count must stay at 30. If it grew, you probably worked two stitches into the last stitch of a round. The hem can look ragged. The count cannot.

**Mark the eyes now.** Put a scrap of contrasting yarn through a stitch on about round 8, the middle of the head. It is much easier to place them before stuffing.

## Step 2: the face

![A tapestry needle with black yarn stitching an oval eye onto white crochet fabric](../../assets/articles/crochet-ghost-amigurumi-for-beginners-3.jpg "Illustration. Embroidered eyes, not plastic ones.")

Embroider two small ovals in satin stitch, or two French knots, about 4 stitches apart at the marked round. Use floss or yarn thinner than the body yarn, or the eyes look heavy. A tiny mouth is optional. Weave the ends inside.

Embroidered eyes are safer than glued or plastic ones for anything a child might chew.

## Step 3A: the hanging ghost

**Stuff the head.** Fill rounds 1 to 9 firmly.

**Close the stuffing compartment.** Run white yarn through every stitch around round 10. Pull the circumference closed, not merely finger-wide, so no stuffing can migrate into the skirt. Secure it with a small knot tucked inward, then weave both tails through nearby stitches in different directions. Do not force a large amount of stuffing against the closure. This cinch changes the silhouette and must be checked on a sample. For a separate compartment, sew a small lining closed below the stuffed head instead.

**Round 17:** (Sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (36)

**Round 18, the wave:** (Sc, hdc, dc, dc, hdc, sc) 6 times. (36)

There are no increases in the wave. The different stitch heights make it ripple. Six repeats of 6 use all 36 stitches. Fasten off and weave the end in.

**Hanging loop:** Thread a doubled strand through the top of the head, near the magic ring, and knot it into a loop. Pass it through twice so it does not pull out.

## Step 3B: the standing ghost

**Round 17:** Sc in the back loop only of each stitch. (30)

This leaves the front loops of round 16 free for the skirt, and makes a fold line so the base turns under flat.

**Round 18:** (Sc 3, sc2tog) 6 times. (24)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/invisible-decrease.svg" alt="Three steps. 1: insert the hook into the front loop only of the next stitch. 2: without pulling through, insert into the front loop of the stitch after it, so three loops are on the hook. 3: yarn over and pull through the two front loops, then yarn over and pull through the two loops on the hook. Two stitches become one." width="480" height="752" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Through the front loops only, the decrease disappears into the fabric.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 19:** (Sc 2, sc2tog) 6 times. (18)

Stuff the head and sides evenly, but keep the closing base from bulging. Overstuffing the base makes it dome; a BLO fold does not guarantee a flat bottom. Set it on the table during closing and redistribute stuffing if it rocks.

**Round 20:** (Sc, sc2tog) 6 times. (12)

**Round 21:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6) Fasten off and pull the hole closed.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ghost-profile.svg" alt="Standing ghost, round by round, as bars sized by stitch count. Round 1: 6, Round 2: 12, Round 3: 18, Round 4: 24, Round 5: 30, Rounds 6 to 16: 30, Round 17: 30, Round 18: 24, Round 19: 18, Round 20: 12, Round 21: 6. Rounds 1 to 5 add 6 each. Rounds 6 to 16 stay at 30. Round 17 (terracotta) is back loops only, then rounds 18 to 21 remove 6 each." width="480" height="538" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The standing build by stitch count. The back-loop round 17 is the fold where the base turns in.</figcaption>
</figure>

**The skirt:** Turn the ghost upside down. Join white in any free front loop of round 16.

- **Skirt round 1:** Working into the front loops, (sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (36)
- **Skirt round 2:** (Sc, hdc, dc, dc, hdc, sc) 6 times. (36)

Fasten off. The skirt hangs over the base, so the ghost looks open but stands.

## Edge options

Both builds end with a 36-stitch round. Any edge whose repeat divides 36 will work.

- **Wave:** (sc, hdc, dc, dc, hdc, sc), 6 repeats.
- **Picots:** (sc 3, ch 2, sl st in the 2nd chain from the hook), 12 repeats.
- **Plain:** one round of sc. Quiet, and still clearly a ghost.

A five-stitch scallop will not fit: 36 is not a multiple of 5.

## Choose the bottom treatment before round 17

For the hanging build, the stuffing belongs above the closed cinch and the skirt remains hollow. For the standing build, round 17 goes into the back loops of round 16, leaving the front loops of that same round available for the skirt. Run a contrasting marker strand through those free loops before continuing, without catching it into the base. After closing the base, check it on a flat table before adding the skirt. To pull a six-stitch closing hole shut, run the tail through each remaining front loop in order, pull gently, secure, and bury it. Neither version is a tested child's toy.

## Troubleshooting

**Stuffing falls out of the hanging ghost.** The cinch is too loose. Pull it tighter, or tuck in a little more stuffing so it fills the space.

**The standing ghost tips over.** The base is under-stuffed or domed. Add stuffing at the bottom before round 21.

**The skirt flares like a tutu.** Too many increases. Skip skirt round 1 and work the wave straight into the 30 front loops: 30 is also a multiple of 6.

**Eyes too low.** Eyes belong on the head, above the neck.

**A diagonal wobble.** The marker was lost. Count back.

## Frequently asked

### Can I use joined rounds instead of a spiral?

Yes. Slip stitch to the first stitch and ch 1 at each round. The ch 1 does not count as a stitch.

### What colors besides white?

Gray, pale blue, or glow-in-the-dark acrylic. Keep strong contrast for the face.

### Can I add weight to the base?

Some makers put plastic pellets in a sealed fabric pouch inside the base. Loose pellets and small parts are a choking hazard, so do not do this for a toy that might reach a small child.

### What should I make next?

The same increase, even, decrease shape with grooves is the [pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/). Flat pieces sewn to a small body are the [bat](/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears/).


# Crochet Hook Size Chart: US Letters to Millimeters

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart.md



Your pattern calls for a 4 mm hook. Your hook says `G/6`. Are those the same thing?

Yes. **The millimeter measurement is the real size.** Letters and numbers are a US convention manufacturers have never fully agreed on, so a size H from one brand can differ slightly from another. Where a pattern gives both, trust the millimeters.


![A row of aluminum crochet hooks in graduated sizes with a gauge plate](../../assets/articles/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart-result.jpg "Illustration. Letter, metric, and steel sizes are different rulers — convert before you buy.")
## Standard hook sizes

![Aluminum crochet hooks checked in a metal gauge plate](../../assets/articles/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart-2.jpg "Illustration. A gauge plate beats trusting the letter stamped on the hook alone.")

These are the aluminum and plastic hooks you use with ordinary yarn, from sock weight up to chunky.

| Millimeters | US size | Legacy UK size | Typical yarn |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2.25 mm | B/1 | 13 | Lace, thread, fine sock |
| 2.75 mm | C/2 | 11 | Sock, fingering |
| 3.25 mm | D/3 | 10 | Sock, fingering, sport |
| 3.5 mm | E/4 | 9 | Sport, baby |
| 3.75 mm | F/5 | — | Sport, DK |
| 4 mm | G/6 | 8 | DK, light worsted |
| 4.5 mm | 7 | 7 | DK, worsted |
| 5 mm | H/8 | 6 | Worsted |
| 5.5 mm | I/9 | 5 | Worsted, aran |
| 6 mm | J/10 | 4 | Aran, bulky |
| 6.5 mm | K/10½ | 3 | Bulky |
| 8 mm | L/11 | 0 | Bulky, chunky |
| 9 mm | M/N-13 | 00 | Bulky, super bulky |
| 10 mm | N/P-15 | 000 | Super bulky |
| 11.5 mm | P-16 | — | Super bulky |
| 15 mm | P/Q | — | Super bulky, jumbo |
| 15.75 mm | Q | — | Jumbo |
| 16 mm | Q | — | Jumbo |
| 19 mm | S | — | Jumbo |
| 25 mm | T/U/X | — | Jumbo |
| 30 mm | T/X | — | Jumbo |

The millimeter and US columns follow the Craft Yarn Council's hook size table. The Council also lists in-between sizes that some brands sell, including 2.5 mm, 3.125 mm (D), 4.25 mm (G), 5.25 mm (I), 5.75 mm (J), 7 mm, and 12 mm. That is why two hooks marked "I" can be different sizes.

<div class="tip">

**About the UK column.** Those numbers run backwards from the metric size, and modern British and Australian patterns give millimeters instead. The column is for vintage patterns and inherited hooks. Old UK sizes were never set by a body like the Craft Yarn Council, and published charts disagree by a size at several points. Treat the column as approximate, and measure an old hook in a gauge rather than trusting the stamp.

</div>

## Steel hooks for thread crochet

Steel hooks are a finer, separate system, and **the numbers run in the opposite direction**: a higher number is a smaller hook. Use them for doilies, thread lace, and fine edgings.

| Millimeters | US steel size |
| --- | --- |
| 3.5 mm | 00 |
| 3.25 mm | 0 |
| 2.75 mm | 1 |
| 2.25 mm | 2 |
| 2.1 mm | 3 |
| 2 mm | 4 |
| 1.9 mm | 5 |
| 1.8 mm | 6 |
| 1.65 mm | 7 |
| 1.5 mm | 8 |
| 1.4 mm | 9 |
| 1.3 mm | 10 |
| 1.1 mm | 11 |
| 1 mm | 12 |
| 0.85 mm | 13 |
| 0.75 mm | 14 |

A steel size 2 and a standard B/1 are both 2.25 mm. The difference is the shape of the shaft and the head, not the diameter.

Steel sizes are less standardized than regular hooks. The table follows common US labeling, but brands differ by a tenth of a millimeter or so at the same number, and the Craft Yarn Council's yarn chart pairs steel 6 to 8 with a range of 1.6 to 1.4 mm. When a thread pattern gives a millimeter size, use that and ignore the number.

## Which hook does your yarn want?

The hook on a yarn label is a starting point, not a rule.

| Yarn weight | Common names | Suggested hook |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0 — Lace | fingering, 10-count crochet thread | Steel 6 to 8 (1.6–1.4 mm), or a regular B/1 (2.25 mm) |
| 1 — Super fine | sock, fingering, baby | 2.25–3.5 mm (B/1 to E/4) |
| 2 — Fine | sport, baby | 3.5–4.5 mm (E/4 to 7) |
| 3 — Light | DK, light worsted | 4.5–5.5 mm (7 to I/9) |
| 4 — Medium | worsted, afghan, aran | 5.5–6.5 mm (I/9 to K/10½) |
| 5 — Bulky | chunky, craft, rug | 6.5–9 mm (K/10½ to M/13) |
| 6 — Super bulky | super chunky, roving | 9–15 mm (M/13 to Q) |
| 7 — Jumbo | jumbo, arm-weight | 15 mm and larger (Q and up) |

**Amigurumi.** Go one or two sizes smaller than the label so stuffing does not show through. Worsted on a 3.5 mm or 4 mm hook, rather than 5.5 mm, makes a firm toy.

**Blankets and shawls.** Go one size larger. At the label's gauge, a blanket often feels stiff.

## Use the millimeter size to resolve conflicting labels

A hook marked G can be 4.0 or 4.25 mm depending on the maker. Select the printed millimeter size first, then make the pattern's gauge swatch. Steel labels also vary, so do not substitute by number alone. The “typical yarn” column is a starting suggestion, not an interchangeability rule: two yarns in category 4 may need different hooks to produce the same fabric. Compare the pattern with the current [Craft Yarn Council hook chart](https://www.craftyarncouncil.com/standards/hooks-and-needles) and [steel-hook chart](https://www.craftyarncouncil.com/standards/steel-crochet-hook-crochet-thread-sizes). Keep the hook you swatched with for the project.

## Common mistakes

![Two crochet hook tip shapes compared side by side](../../assets/articles/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart-1.jpg "Illustration. Tip shape and true millimeter size both matter.")

**Trusting the letter on the handle.** There is no enforced standard. A size I is usually 5.5 mm, but the Craft Yarn Council's own table lists a 5.25 mm "I" that some brands sell, and a 5.75 mm "J" beside the usual 6 mm J/10. The Council lists F/5 as 3.75 mm and G/6 as 4 mm, so a hook marked only "F" or "G" is worth checking. Japanese hooks, and many ergonomic sets, are labeled only in millimeters. A hook gauge — a small metal plate with graduated holes — settles this for every hook you own, including unlabeled ones.

**Using the pattern's hook without checking gauge.** That hook size exists to get you to the pattern's **gauge**. If your tension is tighter than the designer's, the item comes out smaller. If your tension is loose, it comes out larger.

1. Start with the hook the pattern asks for.
2. Work the gauge swatch it specifies, usually a 4-inch square.
3. Too small means your stitches are tight, so go **up** a hook size. Too large means go **down**.
4. Swatch again with the new hook.

Skip this on a coaster. On a sweater, it saves a project that does not fit. If you crochet for hours or your hands ache, an ergonomic handle helps, covered in the [hooks and yarn guides](/gear/).

## Frequently asked

**Can I use a different hook than the pattern says?** Yes, if you hit the gauge. For scarves, blankets, and dishcloths, use what feels good.

**What does the ½ mean in K/10½?** It is a size name, not a fraction of anything measurable. K/10½ is 6.5 mm.

**Which hook should I learn on?** A 5 mm (H/8) with a medium worsted yarn in a light, solid color. Big enough to see what your hands are doing, and light yarn shows the stitch so you can tell when something has gone wrong.


# How to Crochet in the Round: Magic Ring, Spirals and Joined Rounds

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-in-the-round.md



Working in the round means crocheting in a continuous circle instead of turning at the end of a row. How you close the center, whether you join or spiral, and how fast you increase decide whether you get a hat, a toy, or a flat circle.

![A flat cream crochet circle worked in the round with a stitch marker](../../assets/articles/crochet-in-the-round-result.jpg "Illustration. Circles start in the center — magic ring or chain ring — then grow with even increases.")

## Do not turn

The right side faces you the whole time, and you keep traveling the same direction. Every round is a right-side round. If a pattern wants you to turn, for texture, it will say so.

## The magic ring

![Hands forming a magic ring with cream yarn](../../assets/articles/crochet-in-the-round-1.jpg "Illustration. The magic ring closes tight — pull it shut after the first round.")

The magic ring, also called a magic circle or adjustable ring, is a loop you stitch into and then pull shut. Use it when the center must be solid: amigurumi, crown-first hats, closed motifs.

Chain four or five and slip stitch to the first chain when a small hole is fine: doilies, open motifs, most granny squares. On a stuffed toy that hole shows the stuffing.

**1. Make the loop.** Lay the yarn across your palm, tail down about six inches. Wrap the working yarn around two fingers so the working strand crosses **over** the tail. Where they cross, the ring is two strands.

**2. Insert and pull up a loop.** Slide the hook under both strands, catch the working yarn, and draw it back through. Chain one. That chain anchors the ring and does not count as a stitch.

**3. Work the first round over both strands,** the loop and the tail together. The round feels floppy because you are stitching around a loop, not into stitches. That is normal.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The round goes over the loop and the tail together. Pulling the tail closes the center.</figcaption>
</figure>

**4. Close it last.** When the round is finished, pull the tail until the center disappears.

**5. Join or continue.** Slip stitch into the first stitch for joined rounds. For a spiral, put a marker in the first stitch and keep going.

<div class="warning">

**Do not pull the tail until the round is finished.** Closing early locks the ring at a size you cannot stitch into, and you will undo the round.

</div>

Taller stitches need more of them in the first round. Use the pattern's number. This table is only a typical start.

| First round | Typical count | Common use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| single crochet | 6 | Amigurumi, toys |
| half double crochet | 8 | Denser hats, small motifs |
| double crochet | 12 | Granny squares, hats, circular motifs |
| treble crochet | 16 | Open lace motifs |

## Joined rounds or a spiral

**Joined rounds.** Slip stitch into the first stitch, chain up, and start again. The joins stack into a seam. Use them for granny squares, a new color each round, striped hats, and lace. Motifs join because each round starts in a set place, often a corner. On a plain hat the seam shows.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/join-round-slst.svg" alt="Rounds shown unrolled as strips, with the join of each round marked. Correct: slip stitch into the top of the first sc, ch 1, and work the first sc of the next round into that same stitch; the joins stack in a straight line. Mistake: the first sc goes into the next stitch instead, so every join moves one stitch sideways and the seam slants." width="480" height="584" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The join goes into the first real stitch of the round, not the chain that started it.</figcaption>
</figure>

The join steps up. For a last round, cut the yarn, thread it through the first stitch, and pull until the close looks like a stitch. To skip the chain-up, put a slip knot on the hook and work the first stitch directly. That removes the gap when every round is a new color.

**Spirals.** Do not join. Keep going into the first stitch. No seam. Usual for amigurumi, plain hats, and socks, and simpler to learn. A hat can spiral, then finish the brim with one joined round.

Mark the first stitch and move the marker each round. A split ring, safety pin, paperclip, yarn scrap, or bobby pin works. The rounds look the same, so memory fails.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/stitch-marker.svg" alt="Diagram of a crochet circle with a marker clipped into the first stitch of the round. The marker is not counted as a stitch." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. In a spiral, the marker sits in the first stitch. Move it up when you get back to it.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div class="warning">

**A spiral ends on a small step.** The last stitch sits one stitch-height above the fabric around it. On a toy the step hides in a seam. On a hat brim, work one or two slip stitches down into the previous round to taper it, or finish with a single joined round.

</div>

## Circle or tube? The increases decide

![A growing cream crochet circle with even increases](../../assets/articles/crochet-in-the-round-2.jpg "Illustration. Six increases per round keep a circle flat in single crochet.")

**Keep increasing** and the fabric stays flat. In single crochet, add as many stitches each round as the first round had: 6, then 12, 18, 24, 30. After the round that should be 18, stop and count. The repeat is one single crochet, then two in the next stitch, six times: 6 × 3 = 18. **Stop increasing** and it becomes a tube. A hat stops once the flat circle matches the crown, then works even for the depth. A toy body is that tube, decreased later. The mechanics are in [increases and decreases](/crochet-increase-decrease/).

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/flat-circle.svg" alt="A flat circle in single crochet, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 1: 6 sc in a magic ring, 6 stitches. Round 2: 2 sc in each st, 12 stitches. Round 3: (sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 18 stitches. Round 4: (sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 24 stitches. Round 5: (sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 30 stitches. Round 3 uses the 12 stitches of round 2: 6 plain and 6 increases make 18." width="480" height="563" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The flat circle in single crochet. Each round adds the 6 stitches round 1 started with.</figcaption>
</figure>

Cupping means too few increases: a hat or a toy, and a problem only if you wanted a coaster. Ruffling means too many. The edge cannot hold the extra fabric flat.

Insert under **both** top loops unless the pattern says otherwise. Those loops are in [basic stitches](/basic-crochet-stitches/). `BLO`, back loop only, in unturned rounds makes horizontal ridges. A stretchy vertical ribbed brim, like the beanie on this site, is worked sideways in turned BLO rows.

## Use a three-round practice circle

For a spiral, make six sc in the ring, mark the first sc, and tighten the ring. Round 2: two sc in each of the six stitches (12); move the marker into the first new stitch. Round 3: (sc, inc) six times (18). There is no joining slip stitch and no ch 1 between these spiral rounds. For a separate joined-round exercise, join each round to its first sc, ch 1 without counting it, and work the first new sc into the same stitch as the join. Never treat the join as a thirteenth or nineteenth stitch.

## Mistakes

**The ring will not close.** The hook caught one strand. Undo it.

**It closed, then loosened.** Weave the tail through the back before you stuff.

**The first round looks like a hexagon.** Six single crochets look angular for two or three rounds. If it stays that way, stagger the increase positions between rounds without changing the total. For a 24-to-30 round, work sc 1, inc, (sc 3, inc) 5 times, sc 2: this uses all 24 old stitches and makes 30.

**The start disappeared.** That was a spiral with no marker.

**A seam up the side.** Joined rounds. A spiral has no seam.

**A circle that became a bowl.** Too few increases. Right for a hat.

**A gap at every join.** The chain-up is thinner than a stitch. Use a standing stitch, or chain one fewer than the stitch height.

**A center too tight to stitch into.** Leave the starting ring about the size of a dime.

## Frequently asked

**Is a magic ring the same as a magic circle?** Yes. Same method, along with "adjustable ring." Patterns use all three names.

**Do I have to start with a magic ring?** No. A chain ring is correct wherever a small center hole does not matter.

**Does the chain-up count as a stitch?** It depends on the pattern. Check the round's count and stay consistent. More is in [turning chains](/crochet-turning-chain/).


# Crochet Increases and Decreases: Shaping Explained

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-increase-decrease.md



Hats, toys, garments, circles, anything that is not a rectangle: you add stitches in some places and remove them in others. The two moves work the same way at every stitch height.


![A cream crochet piece showing increases and decreases shaping the fabric](../../assets/articles/crochet-increase-decrease-result.jpg "Illustration. Increases add stitches; decreases remove them — that is all shaping is.")
## Increasing

![Close-up of a crochet increase two stitches in one](../../assets/articles/crochet-increase-decrease-1.jpg "Illustration. Two stitches in one stitch = an increase.")

**An increase is two stitches worked into the same place.** The extra stitch makes the fabric wider.

1. Work a stitch into the next stitch.
2. Work a second stitch of the same kind into that same stitch.

Your count has gone up by one. This is the same for single, half double, double, and treble crochet. Patterns write `2 sc in next st` or `2 dc in next st`. `inc` means the same thing.

`3 dc in next st` adds two stitches. Shells and fans are this move, used as a texture instead of as shaping.

## Decreasing

![Close-up of a crochet decrease joining two stitches](../../assets/articles/crochet-increase-decrease-2.jpg "Illustration. sc2tog pulls two stitches into one.")

**A decrease turns two stitches into one.** You work each stitch until the last step, leave those loops on the hook, and close them together.

Patterns write `sc2tog`, `hdc2tog`, `dc2tog`. `dc3tog` turns three stitches into one. `dec` is the older, vaguer word for the same idea. The longer name tells you the stitch and how many.

### Single crochet (`sc2tog`)

1. Insert the hook, yarn over, pull up a loop. Two loops on the hook.
2. Insert the hook into the next stitch, yarn over, pull up a loop. Three loops on the hook.
3. Yarn over and pull through all three.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/single-crochet-decrease.svg" alt="Diagram of a single crochet decrease. Pull up a loop in the first stitch, pull up a loop in the next, then yarn over and pull through all three loops. The count drops by one." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. sc2tog uses two stitches and leaves one. The count falls by one.</figcaption>
</figure>

### Half double (`hdc2tog`)

1. Yarn over, insert the hook, yarn over, pull up a loop. Three loops.
2. Yarn over, insert the hook into the next stitch, yarn over, pull up a loop. Five loops.
3. Yarn over and pull through all five.

### Double crochet (`dc2tog`)

1. Yarn over, insert the hook, yarn over, pull up a loop, yarn over, pull through two loops. Two loops remain.
2. Repeat in the next stitch, stopping at the same point. Three loops remain.
3. Yarn over and pull through all three.

That is the rule at any height, including trebles: stop one step short on each stitch, then finish them together.

## The invisible decrease

Standard `sc2tog` leaves a small bump. On a stuffed toy those bumps sit in plain sight. The invisible decrease lies flatter and uses the same stitch count.

1. Insert the hook under the front loop only of the next stitch.
2. Insert the hook under the front loop only of the stitch after it.
3. Yarn over and pull through both front loops.
4. Yarn over and pull through the two loops left on the hook.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/invisible-decrease.svg" alt="Three steps. 1: insert the hook into the front loop only of the next stitch. 2: without pulling through, insert into the front loop of the stitch after it, so three loops are on the hook. 3: yarn over and pull through the two front loops, then yarn over and pull through the two loops on the hook. Two stitches become one." width="480" height="752" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Front loops only. The back loops stay on the outside, so the decrease does not show.</figcaption>
</figure>

If a decrease leaves a hole, snug the last yarn-over a little as you close it. On amigurumi, use the invisible decrease instead.

## Where to put them

**One stitch in from the edge** on garments. Decreasing in the first or last stitch makes a stepped edge that is hard to seam. Work one stitch, then the decrease: `sc in first st, sc2tog`. The edge stays a straight column.

**Spaced evenly** on circles and crowns. Six increases bunched in one place make corners. `[sc in next 3 sts, 2 sc in next st] 6 times` spaces them so the fabric curves.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/increase-stagger.svg" alt="Correct: the increase in each round is moved, for example by working half the plain stitches before the first increase on alternate rounds, so the edge stays round. Mistake: every increase lands directly above the last one, which builds six corners and the circle becomes a hexagon." width="480" height="679" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Shifting where each round's increases fall keeps a circle round instead of hexagonal.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Stacked on the same stitch each row** when you want a line you can see, such as a raglan or the panels of a beret.

<div class="tip">

Count at the end of every shaped row. The number in parentheses is there so one lost stitch does not throw off every round after it. On a hat crown, it will.

</div>

## A flat circle

In single crochet, each round adds as many stitches as the first round had. Start with 6 and add 6 every round: 6, 12, 18, 24, 30.

The third round is the one worth counting out loud. The previous round has 12 stitches. The instruction `(sc, 2 sc in the next st)` six times uses 2 stitches per repeat and makes 3. Six repeats use all 12 stitches and make 6 × 3 = 18. A count of 17 or 19 means one increase was skipped or worked twice. Fix that round before you continue. On the next round the repeat becomes `(sc in next 2 sts, 2 sc in the next st)`. Each repeat uses 3 stitches and makes 4, and six repeats use all 18: 6 × 4 = 24.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/flat-circle.svg" alt="A flat circle in single crochet, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 1: 6 sc in a magic ring, 6 stitches. Round 2: 2 sc in each st, 12 stitches. Round 3: (sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 18 stitches. Round 4: (sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 24 stitches. Round 5: (sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 30 stitches. Round 3 uses the 12 stitches of round 2: 6 plain and 6 increases make 18." width="480" height="563" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Six increases a round, and one more plain stitch between them each time.</figcaption>
</figure>

Taller stitches need a larger start. A double crochet circle typically starts at 12 and adds 12 each round.

**The circle cups like a bowl** when there are too few increases. That is what you want for a hat or a toy. Once the flat circle matches the crown, stop increasing and work even. The fabric turns into a dome, then a tube. Keep increasing and the hat stays a disc.

**The edge ruffles** when there are too many increases. There is more fabric than the circumference needs.

## Check consumed stitches as well as the result

At 18 stitches, (sc 2, inc) six times uses all 18 and produces 24. At 24 stitches, (sc 2, sc2tog) six times uses all 24 and produces 18. Mark each repeat boundary if you keep losing your place. Do not count the two loops of a stitch as two stitches, and do not add a plain sc after the last repeat unless the pattern asks for it. A repeat can have the right arithmetic and still shape differently at your gauge: lay a flat circle on the table and check for cupping or waves.

## Common mistakes

**A ragged edge.** The decrease landed on the first or last stitch. Move it in by one.

**Corners on a circle.** The increases are bunched. Spread them.

**A hat that will not dome.** The increases went on too long. Stop when the diameter matches the crown, then work even rounds.

**A hole in the decrease.** The closing yarn-over is loose, or a visible `sc2tog` is sitting on a curved toy. Tighten the close, or switch to the invisible decrease.

## Frequently asked

**Is `sc2tog` the same as `dec`?** Usually. `sc2tog` and `dc2tog` name the stitch. `dec` does not, and many amigurumi patterns use `dec` to mean the invisible decrease. Check the pattern's stitch key.

**Can I increase and decrease in the same row?** Yes. Add on one side and remove on the other, and the width stays the same while the shape moves. That is a dart, a waist, or a curve.

**Do increases and decreases work the same in rounds?** The stitch is identical. In rows they usually sit near the edges. In rounds they are spread around.


# Crochet Mug Cozy for Beginners (Button Closure)

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners.md



A mug cozy is a strip of single crochet that wraps the mug from one side of the handle to the other. A chain loop on one end passes inside the handle and closes over a button on the other end. The stitches are the easiest on the site. Three things trip beginners up: which gauge sets the length, what the handle does to the shape, and mugs that are not straight.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). If your [single crochet](/basic-crochet-stitches/) is even, you are ready. The pictures are illustrations. No cozy for this page was crocheted or fitted on a mug. The 10 inch mug below is an example for the arithmetic.

![A handmade crochet mug cozy in cream and sage wrapped around a ceramic mug with a wood-button closure](../../assets/articles/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners-result.jpg "Illustration. It draws a textured stripe and a round button tab. This page makes plain single crochet with a chain loop.")


## How the cozy is built

The foundation chain is the cozy's height. The rows travel around the mug. You wrap the strip as it grows and stop when the two short ends sit on either side of the handle. That is why it is worked sideways: the height is decided once, by the chain, and the length is decided at the mug, by adding rows.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/mug-cozy-strip.svg" alt="A long horizontal strip. An arrow on the left edge marks the chain as the height. An arrow along the bottom says the rows travel around the mug. Notes say 12 single crochet at 4 per inch is 3 inches tall from a chain of 13, and 9 inches at 5 rows per inch is 45 rows, not 9 times 4, which uses the stitch gauge in the row direction." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Stitches set the height. Rows set the length around the mug. Each uses its own gauge.</figcaption>
</figure>

The direction has a consequence. Because rows go around the mug, the length comes from your **row** gauge, not your stitch gauge. The earlier version of this page multiplied 9 inches by 4 stitches per inch and called it 36 rows. At 5 rows per inch, 9 inches is 45 rows. Nine extra rows is more than an inch and a half of cozy.

It also changes stripes. A color change at the start of a row makes a stripe that runs up the mug, not around it.

## Measure the mug

![A tape measure around a ceramic mug beside a crochet cozy rectangle](../../assets/articles/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners-1.jpg "Illustration. The mug is the pattern. Measure before you chain.")

Write down four things:

1. **Around the mug** at the height where the cozy will sit. If the mug flares, measure near the top and near the bottom of that band.
2. **Cozy height:** from just above where the base curves in, to about half an inch below the rim, so lips never touch yarn. The half inch is a choice.
3. **Handle base:** the width of the handle where it meets the wall, side to side.
4. **Handle gap:** the space between the handle and the wall at mid-height. The loop has to pass through it.

The example mug is **10 inches** around with a **1 inch** handle base, and the cozy is **3 inches** tall. The body is 10 − 1 = **9 inches**, because the cozy stops on either side of the handle instead of covering it.

If the top and bottom measurements differ, a straight strip can only match one of them. Sized to the top, it slides down. Sized to the bottom, it gaps at the top. Shaping the strip is a different construction, not covered here. For a tapered tumbler, see the [tumbler sleeve](/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve/).

## Yarn and tools

- **Worsted cotton.** It is the usual choice next to hot drinks, it washes well, and it holds its shape on a mug. Acrylic can soften or melt with high heat. If you give an acrylic cozy, say so. [Yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A hook matched to the yarn**, often US G/6 (4.0 mm) or H/8 (5.0 mm). [Hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One button**, about 5/8 to 3/4 inch across. That size is a choice. The loop is made to fit whichever button you pick.
- Yarn needle, scissors, and the mug.

## Gauge and the worked example

Swatch in single crochet and measure both directions. [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/) shows how. The example uses 4 stitches and 5 rows per inch. Neither number came from a finished cozy.

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Height | 3 in × 4 sts per inch | 12 sts, chain 13 |
| Length | (10 − 1) in × 5 rows per inch | 45 rows |
| Same length at 4 rows per inch | 9 × 4 | 36 rows |

## Stage 1: chain the height

![Hands chaining the foundation for a mug cozy height](../../assets/articles/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners-2.jpg "Illustration. Chain the cozy height, not the mug circumference.")

Chain 13.

**Row 1:** Sc in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Ch 1, turn. (12)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/mug-cozy-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 13. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 12 sc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. The chain sets the cozy's height: chain 13 for 12 sc, 3 inches at 4 sc per inch.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Checkpoint:** hold the row upright against the mug. It should reach from just above the base curve to about half an inch below the rim. If it does not, change the chain now. This is the only point where the height is cheap to fix.

## Stage 2: rows around the mug

![A growing single-crochet rectangle for a mug cozy held against a mug](../../assets/articles/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners-3.jpg "Illustration. Wrap the strip as it grows until the ends reach the sides of the handle.")

**Row 2 and on:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (12)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The usual way a 12-stitch cozy grows: a stitch worked into the ch 1.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/mug-cozy-step-2.svg" alt="Rows around the mug. Rows of 12 sc drawn one symbol per stitch and worked back and forth, each starting with a turning chain (ch 1, not a st). Row 45 is highlighted. Every row: 12 sc. About 45 rows in the example. Stop when the strip reaches from one side of the handle to the other, relaxed." width="480" height="591" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. Rows build the length around the mug. Each row is 12 sc, and the ch 1 is not a stitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

Count the stitches every 10 rows. A gained stitch makes one long edge longer than the other, and the ends will not line up beside the handle.

Wrap the strip around the mug every 10 rows. Stop when the two short ends sit against the two sides of the handle base. That is 45 rows in the example. Your row count is whatever reaches.

**Checkpoint:** both short ends sit beside the handle, not on top of it, and the strip lies flat against the wall all the way round.

## Stage 3: the loop and the button

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/mug-cozy-handle.svg" alt="A mug seen from above. The cozy wraps the wall and stops on either side of the handle, which sticks straight out. A dashed line shows the strap passing under the handle, against the wall, to a button on the other end. Notes say the body is around the mug minus the handle base, 10 minus 1 is 9 inches, and that a sewn tube cannot get past the handle because it is fixed to the wall at top and bottom." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The loop goes through the gap between the handle and the wall. That is the only way across.</figcaption>
</figure>

![Sewing a wood button onto the flap of a crochet mug cozy](../../assets/articles/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners-4.jpg "Illustration. The button goes on the first-row end. The chain loop goes on the last row.")

1. **Button.** On the foundation-chain end, sew the button at mid-height, about half an inch in from the edge. Go through several stitches of the cozy, not through a single strand.
2. **Loop.** Wrap the cozy on the mug. On the last-row end, join yarn at mid-height, between stitches 6 and 7 of the 12. Chain until the chain passes through the handle gap and reaches around the button. Slip stitch back into the same place to close the loop, and fasten off.
3. **Test it before you weave the ends.** Pass the loop through the handle and over the button. It should close with a slight stretch. A loop that slides off easily is too long. Pull it out and chain fewer.

Do not sew the ends into a tube on a mug with a handle. The handle is fixed to the wall at the top and at the bottom, so a closed tube cannot slide past it. You would have to sew the cozy onto the mug and cut it off to wash it. The earlier version of this page suggested seaming "below and above the handle", which is where the handle is attached. For a cup with no handle and straight sides, a seamed tube works.

## Finish and fit check

Weave the ends in on the inside. If the long edges curl, steam cotton lightly. See [blocking](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

1. Slide the cozy on and close the loop over the button.
2. The ends sit beside the handle, and the rim is clear of yarn.
3. Hold a warm mug, not one straight off the boil. The cozy should stay put, not slide.

**Too loose:** take the loop off, pull out rows from the last-row end, and make the loop again. **Too tight:** take the loop off and add rows. That is why the loop goes on the last row. **Ends over the handle:** too many rows.

<div class="warning">

**Do not microwave a mug with the cozy on.** Take the cozy off first. Yarn and buttons do not belong in a microwave.

A button is a small part that can come loose. Check its thread before you give the cozy, and keep it away from small children.

</div>

## Check button placement with the mug empty

Mark the chosen button and loop positions with the strip wrapped around an empty, cool mug. The loop must travel through the handle opening and around the button without pulling either end over the handle attachment. Remove the strip before sewing. Use several passes through multiple crochet stitches and leave no loose thread that could catch a finger. If shortening the strip, undo the loop first and remove rows from the last-row end; then remake the loop. The foundation end holds the button and remains unchanged. Try it again before weaving the final tails.

## Troubleshooting

**The cozy is an inch short.** You worked the length from the stitch gauge. Count your rows per inch and add the difference.

**One long edge is longer than the other.** Gained or lost stitches. Count back to the row where you still had 12.

**There is a gap at the top or the bottom.** The mug tapers. A straight strip only fits one circumference.

**The cozy slides down.** It is loose, or the mug narrows toward the base.

**The loop slips off the button.** The loop is too long or the button too small. Chain fewer.

**The button pulls through.** It was sewn through one strand. Sew it through several stitches.

## Frequently asked

### How long does this take?

The example is 45 rows of 12, about 540 single crochet. Time your first 10 rows and multiply by 4.5.

### Can I make a set?

Yes, if the mugs are identical. For different mugs, measure each one. Mugs that look the same often are not.

### Does it work on tumblers?

On a straight-sided tumbler with no handle, yes, and you can seam it into a tube. On a tapered one, see the [tumbler sleeve](/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve/).

### Can I add a pocket for a tea bag?

Yes. Crochet a second, smaller rectangle and sew three sides of it onto the outside before you add the button. Keep it flat so the mug still sits steady.

### What should I make next?

A [scrunchie](/how-to-crochet-a-scrunchie/) as a small gift, a [snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/) for the tree, or [coasters](/free-pattern-halloween-coasters/) for under the same mug.


# Crochet Santa Amigurumi for Beginners

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-santa-amigurumi-for-beginners.md



A Santa is the first amigurumi where the face shows. A [gnome](/how-to-crochet-a-gnome/) hides its eyes under a hat, and a [pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/) has no face, so both forgive a lot. On Santa, an eye one stitch off is the first thing anyone sees.

The body is easy: one piece, with no neck seam to sew. The skill is placement, and there is a reliable way to do it. Pick one stitch at the center front, mark it, and place every feature by counting stitches and rounds out from that marker, never by eye. A spiral leans slightly, so placing by eye is how faces end up crooked.

US terms. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations of the finished Santa, which has not been made up in yarn here, so no finished height is given.

![A finished beginner crochet Santa amigurumi with red hat](../../assets/articles/crochet-santa-amigurumi-for-beginners-result.jpg "Illustration. The intended look.")

## What you need

- **Worsted-weight yarn** in red, a skin tone, white, and a scrap of black. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A hook one or two sizes smaller than the label**, so stuffing does not show. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Stuffing, a yarn needle, stitch markers, pins.
- Black yarn for embroidered eyes, or 6 mm safety eyes if the Santa will not go to a small child.

<div class="warning">

Safety eyes are not safe for children under three. The name refers to the locking washer, not the age range. A plastic eye that works loose is a choking hazard. For a baby or toddler, embroider the eyes.

</div>

## Step 1: body and head in one piece

![Hands crocheting a red Santa hat tube](../../assets/articles/crochet-santa-amigurumi-for-beginners-1.jpg "Illustration. Pieces start in the round.")

Red. Work in a spiral and mark the first stitch of each round.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1 starts in a magic ring, pulled tight so the top has no hole.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 4:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)

**Round 5:** (Sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (30)

That flat circle is the base. Press it on a table. It must sit flat, or Santa rocks.

**Rounds 6–13:** Sc in each stitch. (30)

**Round 14:** (Sc 3, sc2tog) 6 times. (24) The narrowing makes the shoulders.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/invisible-decrease.svg" alt="Three steps. 1: insert the hook into the front loop only of the next stitch. 2: without pulling through, insert into the front loop of the stitch after it, so three loops are on the hook. 3: yarn over and pull through the two front loops, then yarn over and pull through the two loops on the hook. Two stitches become one." width="480" height="752" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Through the front loops only, the decrease disappears into the fabric.</figcaption>
</figure>

Change to the skin tone at the end of round 14. [Changing yarn color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/).

**Rounds 15–20:** Sc in each stitch. (24)

Stuff the body firmly now, firmer than the head will be. A soft base slumps under the head.

**If you are using safety eyes**, fit them now, before the head closes. See the placement in Step 2.

**Round 21:** (Sc 2, sc2tog) 6 times. (18)

**Round 22:** (Sc, sc2tog) 6 times. (12) Stuff the head.

**Round 23:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6) Fasten off. Thread the tail through the front loops of the last 6 stitches and pull closed.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/santa-profile.svg" alt="Santa body and head, round by round, as bars sized by stitch count. Round 1: 6, Round 2: 12, Round 3: 18, Round 4: 24, Round 5: 30, Rounds 6 to 13: 30, Round 14: 24, Rounds 15 to 20: 24, Round 21: 18, Round 22: 12, Round 23: 6. Body: rounds 6 to 13 at 30. Round 14 drops to 24 for the shoulders. Head: rounds 15 to 20 at 24, then close." width="480" height="541" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Body and head as one piece. Round 14 is the only shaping in the middle, and it makes the shoulders.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Step 2: place the face by counting

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/santa-face-placement.svg" alt="A grid of stitches on the front of the head. A marker runs up the center front stitch. The eyes are on the same round, about two thirds of the way down the head, each 2 stitches out from the marker, so 4 apart with the marker between. The nose is on the marker one round lower. The beard starts under the nose." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>One marker, and every feature counted from it.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. **Pick the front.** The color-change jog is at the back, so choose the stitch opposite it. Thread a strand of contrasting yarn up that column of stitches.
2. **Pick the eye round.** The head is rounds 15 to 23. About two thirds of the way down from the top is round 18 or 17. Lower than instinct says looks friendlier.
3. **Count out.** Each eye sits 2 stitches out from the marker on the same round, so the eye positions are 4 stitch steps apart, with three positions strictly between them including the marker. Pin them.
4. **Nose position.** On the marker, one round below the eyes.

Step back and look from a few feet away before you sew anything.

## Step 3: the eyes and nose

![Embroidering eyes on a crochet Santa](../../assets/articles/crochet-santa-amigurumi-for-beginners-3.jpg "Illustration. Simple embroidered eyes suit a small face.")

**Eyes.** Embroider each one over a single stitch with black, two or three wraps, or a French knot. Use the same number of wraps on both.

**Nose.** Skin tone:

- **Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)
- **Round 2:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (9)
- **Round 3:** Sc in each stitch. (9)
- **Round 4:** (Sc, sc2tog) 3 times. (6)

Stuff lightly with a pinch of stuffing, fasten off with a tail, and sew it on at the pinned spot.

## Step 4: the beard

Two options. **Fringe** is faster: cut white strands about twice the finished length, fold each in half, pull the fold through a stitch with the hook, then pull the ends through the fold. Work a curve from one side of the face, under the nose, to the other side, then trim. **A crocheted beard** uses the four numbered rows below.

Either way, the beard starts just under the nose and covers the bottom of the face.

### Crocheted beard option, row by row

For a compact flat triangular beard, use white and the body hook. Row 1: ch 8, sc in the second chain and across (7). Row 2: ch 1, turn, sc2tog, sc 3, sc2tog (5). Row 3: ch 1, turn, sc2tog, sc 1, sc2tog (3). Row 4: ch 1, turn, sc3tog (1). Fasten off with a sewing tail. With the seven-stitch edge just under the nose and the point toward the body, pin it without covering the eyes. Sew the top edge and tack each side through the beard and body surface only. This replaces the unspecified half-circle option; check its proportions on your Santa before sewing.

## Step 5: the hat

Red. Worked from the tip down.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

**Rounds 2–3:** Sc in each stitch. (6)

**Round 4:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (9)

**Round 5:** Sc in each stitch. (9)

**Round 6:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (12)

**Round 7:** Sc in each stitch. (12)

Keep going the same way, adding 3 stitches every other round, until the hat has 27 stitches. The head is 24 around, so 27 slips over it.

**Round 8:** (Sc 3, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (15)

**Round 9:** Sc in each stitch. (15)

**Round 10:** (Sc 4, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (18)

**Round 11:** Sc in each stitch. (18)

**Round 12:** (Sc 5, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (21)

**Round 13:** Sc in each stitch. (21)

**Round 14:** (Sc 6, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (24)

**Round 15:** Sc in each stitch. (24)

**Round 16:** (Sc 7, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (27)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/santa-hat-profile.svg" alt="Santa hat, tip to brim, as bars sized by stitch count. Rounds 1 to 3: 6, Round 4: 9, Round 5: 9, Round 6: 12, Round 7: 12, Round 8: 15, Round 9: 15, Round 10: 18, Round 11: 18, Round 12: 21, Round 13: 21, Round 14: 24, Round 15: 24, Round 16: 27. Three stitches added every other round from round 4. The white brim adds two rounds of 27." width="480" height="603" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The hat grows 3 stitches every other round, so it stays a slim cone and ends 3 wider than the 24-stitch head.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Brim:** Change to white. Two rounds of sc. (27) Fasten off with a tail.

**Pom-pom:** Make a second nose in white and sew it to the tip. Do not stuff the hat. Sew it on at a slight angle, all the way round the brim.

## Step 6: the belt

Black. Chain until the chain wraps the body at about round 9, then sc back along the chain. Sew it around the body. A small square of yellow, either a few stitches of embroidery or a tiny crocheted square, makes a buckle at the center front, on the marker line.

## Mark the face before closing the head

At the end of round 20, mark the front and the eye positions while the head is still open. Eyes two stitches to either side of the center are four stitch steps apart; there are three positions strictly between them. Use that center marker to place the nose one round lower. Close the head only after any plastic eye washers are fitted; for embroidered eyes, keep the tails inside and secure them before closure if possible. Make the beard and hat, pin both, then check that the hat brim leaves the eyes visible and the beard does not cover them.

## Troubleshooting

**The eyes are uneven.** They were placed by eye. Count from the marker.

**Too high.** Eyes near the top look surprised. Move them down a round.

**He leans backward.** The base is domed or the body is softer than the head. Press the base flat and stuff the body harder.

**Stuffing shows through.** Hook too large. Go down a size.

**The hat perches on top.** Too few stitches. Add one more increase round.

## Frequently asked

### Can the head be a separate piece?

It can, but joining two stuffed balls so the head stays straight is a separate skill. One piece avoids it.

### How do I hide the color-change jog?

Put it at the back. The belt and the hat brim cover most of it.

### Is it safe for a toddler?

With embroidered eyes and firmly sewn parts it has no small parts, but it has not been safety tested. Treat it as decoration.

### What should I make next?

A cone with no face at all is the [gnome](/how-to-crochet-a-gnome/). A flat motif for the tree is the [snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/).


# The Turning Chain: Why Your Edges Slant

Source: src/content/posts/crochet-turning-chain.md



If your rectangle is coming out as a trapezoid, or you started with 20 stitches and now have 17, the turning chain is the usual reason.

It is a small thing patterns explain badly, and it accounts for more ripped-out rows than any other single mistake.


![Close-up of a turning chain at the start of a crochet row](../../assets/articles/crochet-turning-chain-result.jpg "Illustration. The turning chain lifts the row to the right height — and may or may not count as a stitch.")
## What it is

![Hands making a turning chain at the end of a row](../../assets/articles/crochet-turning-chain-1.jpg "Illustration. Chain, turn, then work the next row.")

When you finish a row and turn, the hook sits at the bottom of the new row, and the next stitches are taller than that. A chain lifts the hook to the height of the stitch you are about to work. That chain is the turning chain. Without it, the first stitch of every row squashes and the edge pulls in.

## How many chains

| Stitch | Turning chain | Counts as a stitch? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| single crochet (`sc`) | 1 | No — height only |
| half double crochet (`hdc`) | 1 or 2, as specified | Follow the pattern; the hdc rectangles on this site use ch 2 that does not count |
| double crochet (`dc`) | 3 | Usually yes |
| treble (`tr`) | 4 | Usually yes |
| double treble (`dtr`) | 5 | Usually yes |

For single crochet, the answer is consistent: **the chain-1 does not count as a stitch.** It is there for height. After chaining 1 and turning, work the first single crochet into the very first stitch of the row.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turning-chain.svg" alt="Diagram of a single crochet row. Four stitches are numbered, and the chain-1 at the end is marked as not a stitch. An arrow points at the first stitch." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. For single crochet, the first stitch of the new row is the first stitch of the old row. The chain-1 is only height.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/count-stitches.svg" alt="Diagram of four chain-top Vs numbered 1 through 4, and a loop still on the hook marked as not a stitch." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Count the V on top of each finished stitch. The loop on the hook is not one of them.</figcaption>
</figure>

A short check: start with 10 single crochet. Chain 1, turn, and work 10 again. The row is still 10. If you also put a stitch into the chain, the row is 11. Do that for five rows and the count is 15. The fabric is a wedge, and the extra stitches cannot be eased out later.

Write the next check down. Chain 11. Single crochet in the second chain and across: that is 10. Work three more rows of 10, each starting with a chain-1 that you do not count. The list should read 10, 10, 10, 10. A 9 is a missed edge stitch. An 11 is an extra insertion, usually the turning chain or the first stitch worked twice. This is arithmetic, not a swatch measured for this page. The [stitch counter](/crochet-stitch-counter/) is there if you lose the number while you look at the edge.

For everything taller, it depends on the designer.

**If the turning chain counts,** skip the first stitch of the previous row. The chain stands in for it. Work the last stitch of the row into the top of the previous row's turning chain.

**If it does not count,** work into the first stitch as usual, and ignore the previous turning chain at the end of the row.

Both are correct. Mixing them in one project is what slants the edge.

<div class="tip">

**How to tell which your pattern means.** `Ch 3 (counts as dc)` or `Ch 3 (counts as first dc)` means it counts. `Ch 3 (does not count as a stitch)` means it does not. If the pattern says nothing, check the stitch count at the end of the row and see which reading gives you that number. When it is genuinely ambiguous, pick one and keep it for the whole project.

</div>

In joined rounds you do not turn. A starting chain replaces the turning chain, with the same heights and the same counting question. The right side faces you the whole time.

Spiral rounds, common in amigurumi, have no chain. Keep going, and mark the first stitch of each round. There is nothing else to show where the round begins.

## Mark both row ends to make the rule visible

On a ten-sc swatch, put markers in the first and last sc. Ch 1, turn, make the first sc into the marked first stitch and the last sc into the marked last stitch. Move both markers into the new stitches. You still have ten. For a counted ch-3 dc row, mark the top of the ch 3 as the first stitch; skip the stitch directly under it and finish the following row in the marked chain top. These are two different counting conventions. The pattern's stated rule decides which markers you use.

## Common mistakes

### The work gets narrower

You are losing a stitch each row.

**The last stitch is missing.** In double crochet, the previous turning chain looks like part of the edge. If your pattern counts it, that chain is the last stitch. Work into the top of it.

**The first stitch is skipped when it should not be.** If the turning chain does not count, the first real stitch goes into the first stitch of the row.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>In single crochet the ch 1 never counts, so the first stitch of the row goes into the first stitch, at the base of the chain.</figcaption>
</figure>

### The work gets wider

You gained a stitch. Either you worked into the first stitch and also counted the turning chain, or you worked into a turning chain the pattern does not count.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/uneven-edges.svg" alt="Diagram of a straight fabric with the same stitch count each row, beside a wedge that gains one stitch every row." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The same count each row stays straight. Gaining one stitch a row makes a wedge.</figcaption>
</figure>

### The edge slants, but the width is right

You are counting the turning chain on some rows and not on others. Count at the end of every row for the next ten rows and the pattern of the mistake will show. You cannot repair a slanted edge afterward. Undo to the last row where the count was right.

Flip the work the same way every row, toward you or away from you. Alternating adds a twist that shows on long pieces such as scarves.

### A gap at the start of double crochet rows

![Cream double crochet fabric showing a gap beside the turning chain](../../assets/articles/crochet-turning-chain-2.jpg "Illustration. A loose turning chain leaves a gap at the start of dc rows.")

A `ch 3` standing in for a double crochet leaves a small hole, because a chain is thinner than a real double crochet. This is normal.

**Chain 2 instead of 3.** Slightly shorter, and the gap closes. Very common in modern patterns.

**Stacked single crochet used as a dc-height start.** Work one sc in the first stitch. Insert the hook under the left vertical strand of that sc (right vertical strand for left-handed work), yarn over and pull up a loop, then yarn over and pull through both loops to stack a second sc above it. This combined start replaces one dc; do not add another dc in the same base stitch. Practice it on a swatch before substituting it into a pattern.

Neither changes your stitch count. Both stand in for the `ch 3` and count as the first double crochet.

## Frequently asked

**Can I skip the turning chain?** For single crochet, many people skip the chain-1, turn, and work into the first stitch. The edge is tighter and it works. For double crochet and taller, no. Without the height, the edge pulls in.

**Why do my patterns disagree?** There is no enforced standard. Read the first row of each new pattern rather than assuming it matches the last one.

**Does the turning chain count in my stitch total?** Only if the pattern says it counts as a stitch. If it does, include it. This is why a count can look wrong when it is actually right.


# 15 Easy Crochet Christmas Ornament Ideas

Source: src/content/posts/easy-crochet-christmas-ornaments.md



A crochet ornament has one job: hang flat enough that it does not curl into a tube by New Year. The hanging loop matters more than the color.

This page lists 15 shapes. The flat star is a full pattern. Two other shapes already have patterns on this site, and those links are the instructions. The rest are ideas for planning yarn and difficulty. They are not stitch-by-stitch tutorials, and the group picture is an illustration of the shapes. For the star that is written out, the hanging loop has to pass over the hook you will actually use. Chain it, slip stitch it to the top, and check the hook before you weave the tail. If the star cups, block it flat before it hangs. A cupped star twists on the tree.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/).

![A group of small crochet Christmas ornaments on a table](../../assets/articles/easy-crochet-christmas-ornaments-result.jpg "Illustration. Ornament ideas in one photo. Only the star pattern is written in full on this page.")

## What every ornament needs

- Worsted cotton. A flat ornament uses a small scrap. A stuffed ball uses more. Amounts have not been weighed. [Yarn for Christmas](/yarn-for-christmas-crochet/) covers sparkle yarn and why it behaves badly in small stitches.
- US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- A hanging loop: chain 12, sew both ends to the top, weave the tail through the chain. A single unsewn strand will pull out.

Stuffing is optional and usually a mistake on a flat ornament. It makes the piece heavy for a thin branch.

## The fifteen

**Mini Christmas tree.** The flat triangle in [how to crochet a Christmas tree](/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-tree/) hangs flat. Use a smaller hook or start on 11 stitches for a smaller one, and sew the hanging loop at the tip.

**Star.** Full pattern below. Beginner. Flat, five points, joined rounds.

**Snowflake.** Use [how to crochet a snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/). That page deals with keeping points flat. This page does not repeat it.

**Mini stocking.** Beginner idea. A short rectangle folded and seamed, with a cuff row in a second color. The heel is the part people fake with a lump of stitches. If you want a real heel, that is a sock technique, not a one-round charm.

**Candy cane.** Beginner idea. A tight chain in red, then a second pass of white on one side, then a pipe cleaner or a length of wire inside if you need it to keep the hook shape. Yarn alone often uncurls. Say so if you sell them.

**Bell.** Beginner idea. A small cone worked in the round. The clapper is a bead on a strand, and the bead must be too wide to pull back through the stitches.

**Heart.** The row-by-row heart in [bag charm ideas](/crochet-bag-charm-ideas/) can hang as an ornament if you add the same chain loop. That pattern is a charm, not a stuffed pillow.

**Gingerbread-inspired ornament.** Idea only. A brown flat shape with a white embroidered edge. Do not copy a specific designer's gingerbread set. Embroidery should stay shallow so the back is not a knot.

**Mini wreath.** Beginner idea. A short tube joined into a ring, with a chain bow. The ring collapses if the tube is only 4 stitches around. Plan on a firmer hook.

**Snowman.** Intermediate idea. Three balls stacked and sewn. The existing [gnome](/how-to-crochet-a-gnome/) is a different toy. A snowman needs its own counts, which are not on this page.

**Gift box.** Beginner idea. Six squares seamed into a cube is a lot of ends for an ornament. A flat pocket that holds a card is the [Christmas gift card holder](/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-gift-card-holder/), and that one is not meant to hang.

**Mini mitten.** Beginner idea. Same construction warning as the stocking. A thumb added as an afterthought looks like a bump. Plan the thumb as a few stitches split off the round, or skip the thumb and call it a flat mitten shape.

**Bauble.** Beginner idea if you can make a ball: increase to a circle, work even, then decrease in mirror. The [pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/) gives a counted ball; work six even rounds instead of four for a round bauble and skip the grooves. Leave a loop before you close it. Do not hang a heavy stuffed ball on a weak branch.

**Angel-inspired ornament.** Idea only. A circle head and a triangle body read as an angel at a distance. Wings are where copied patterns usually live. Keep wings as two simple triangles you design yourself, or skip them.

**Festive bow.** Beginner idea. Two loops, a center wrap, a hanging chain. Pin the loops before you wrap or the bow becomes a knot. This is not a full bow pattern.

![A star, a small tree, and a round ornament](../../assets/articles/easy-crochet-christmas-ornaments-1.jpg "Illustration. Three shapes from the list. The tree pattern is on its own page.")

## Flat star pattern

Joined rounds. The slip stitch that joins does not count as a stitch. Ch 1 at the start of a round does not count.

![Hands crocheting a small cream star](../../assets/articles/easy-crochet-christmas-ornaments-2.jpg "Illustration. The points are groups of stitches worked into chain spaces.")

**Round 1:** 5 sc in a magic ring. Join with a sl st to the first sc. (5)

**Round 2:** Ch 1, (sc in the next stitch, ch 5) 5 times. Join with a sl st to the first sc. (5 sc and 5 chain-5 spaces)

Each repeat uses one of the 5 stitches from Round 1.

**Round 3:** In each chain-5 space, work sc, hdc, dc, ch 3, sl st in the 3rd chain from the hook (a picot), dc, hdc, sc. Join to the first sc. Fasten off. (30 stitches and 5 picots)

That is 6 stitches and a picot in each of 5 spaces. They sit in the chains, not in the single crochets of Round 2. If you also stitch into those single crochets, you get extra fabric between the points and the star looks like a flower.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/star-ornament.svg" alt="A five-point star.
