Round 1 is 5 single crochet in a ring, round 2 is single crochet and chain 5 five times, and round 3 works single crochet, half double, double, a chain-3 picot, double, half double, single crochet into each loop. A dashed outline shows that without the picot each point is a rounded petal." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Stitch heights rise to the middle of each loop, and the picot sharpens the tip. Without it the points look like petals.</figcaption>
</figure>

The picot is what makes it a star. A loop filled with sc, hdc, dc, hdc, sc rises and falls in a smooth arch, which reads as a flower petal. A small chain loop at the top of the arch gives it a sharp tip.

Weave the ring tail. Chain 12, sew both ends behind one point.

![A yarn loop sewn to the top of a star](../../assets/articles/easy-crochet-christmas-ornaments-3.jpg "Illustration. The loop is sewn. It is not a loose tail.")

## Hanging

Use a branch that can take the weight. A stuffed bauble needs a sturdier loop than this flat star. Space ornaments so the loops do not share one twig.

![Crochet ornaments on a small tree](../../assets/articles/easy-crochet-christmas-ornaments-4.jpg "Illustration. Display idea. The photo is not fifteen separate patterns.")

## Separate five star points from the joining stitches

Round 2 makes five sc anchors and five ch-5 loops. After its join, slip stitch into the first loop before starting round 3. Work the full six-stitch point and its picot into that loop, then move directly to the next loop without adding sc into the intervening anchors. Finish all five loops and join to the first sc. Count thirty sc/hdc/dc stitches plus five picots; connecting slip stitches are not additional points. A hanger is made separately and sewn behind one point. The remaining ornament ideas need their own patterns or the explicitly linked tutorial.

## Mistakes

**Skipping the join count.** If Round 1 has 6 stitches, you will get 6 points. The pattern asks for 5.

**A wire you cannot see.** If you use a pipe cleaner in a candy cane, the ends must be bent in and covered. A raw wire tip is a bad ornament.

**Blocking with a hot iron on acrylic.** It melts. Pin a damp cotton star and let it dry, which is the method in [weaving ends and blocking](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

## Care

Dust them. Wash only if you must, by hand, with the hanger still secure. Dry flat. Store them where the loops cannot tangle into a knot you have to cut.

## Frequently asked

**Are all 15 patterns here?**

No. The star is complete. The tree and the snowflake are on their own pages. The other shapes are planning notes.

**Can I sell the stars?**

Finished stars, yes. Do not republish the three rounds.

**Will the star stay flat?**

At this size, cotton and a normal tension usually lie flat because there is no stuffed center. If it cups, the ring was pulled too tight. Redo Round 1 rather than blocking a curled center into submission.

## Next

If you are making gifts that are not ornaments, the [gift card holder](/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-gift-card-holder/) is a flatter project with a real measurement to hit.


# Halloween Coasters: Pumpkin Circle and Granny Square Set

Source: src/content/posts/free-pattern-halloween-coasters.md



<div class="warning">

The sizes here come from gauge rather than a finished sample, so set your mug on the disc before you fasten off.

</div>

A coaster has one job: lie flat under a mug. The pumpkin coaster on this page is a flat circle of single crochet, which is also the shape that starts most crochet worked in the round. Learning why it stays flat, and what to do when it does not, is most of the skill. The granny squares further down do the same job in a different shape, and the same square grows into a dishcloth. For color changes, see [Halloween Granny Square Variations](/halloween-granny-square-variations/) and [Changing Yarn Color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/).

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. US double crochet is UK treble. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/).

![Four Halloween crochet coasters: a spiderweb, a ghost, a jack-o'-lantern, and a black cat square](../../assets/articles/free-pattern-halloween-coasters-thumb.png)


## Why a flat circle adds six stitches a round

Each round of single crochet moves the edge outward by about one stitch height. The distance around a circle is 2 × π × the radius, so every round has to be longer than the last by about 2 × π, or 6.28, stitch heights. If a single crochet is about as tall as it is wide, that is about six extra stitches per round. That is where "start with 6, add 6 each round" comes from.

It is a good rule, not a law. The DK *Crochet: The Complete Step-by-Step Guide* starts its practice circle with 8 single crochet and doubles to 16, and starts its circular pillow with 6. Both lie flat for some people. Double crochet circles commonly start with 12 for the same reason: a taller stitch moves the edge further each round, so the round needs more new stitches.

That is also why the same written circle cups for one person and ruffles for another. If your single crochet is taller than it is wide, six new stitches are too few and the edge pulls up into a bowl. If your stitches are short and wide, six are too many and the edge waves.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/coaster-increases.svg" alt="A circle divided into six wedges with eight rings. A table lists round 1 as 6 stitches in the ring, round 2 as 12, then 18, 24, 30, 36, 42, and 48 at round 8, with one more plain stitch between increases each round. Round 9 is 48 with no increases. At 4 stitches per inch, 48 stitches are 12 inches around and about 3.8 inches across." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>One increase in each of six wedges per round. The number in brackets is how many plain stitches sit between increases.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Materials

- **Worsted cotton:** orange for the disc and a short length of green for the stem. Cotton soaks up condensation from a cold glass. Next to a warm mug it is the safer fiber, because acrylic can melt near heat. That is a fiber choice, not a heat rating. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives you a flat circle. A smaller hook than the label suggests makes a denser coaster that hides the table. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **A stitch marker.** In a spiral you cannot see where a round ends without one.
- Yarn needle, scissors.

To estimate yarn for a set, weigh your first finished coaster and multiply.

## Size: wider than the mug, not a fixed number

At the planning gauge of 4 single crochet to the inch, 48 stitches are 48 ÷ 4 = 12 inches around. Across is 12 ÷ 3.14 = about **3.8 inches**. That is arithmetic, not a measured disc.

Measure the base of the mug you will use. The coaster should be wider than that by whatever margin you like. Each extra round adds 6 stitches, which at 4 per inch is 1.5 inches of edge, or 1.5 ÷ 3.14 = about **half an inch** across. That makes adding a round an easy fix.

## The pumpkin disc, round by round

Work in a spiral: do not join rounds. Put the marker in the first stitch of each round and move it up as you go. [Working in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/) covers the spiral and the magic ring.

**Rnd 1.** Magic ring, 6 sc in the ring. Pull the tail until the hole closes. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Pull the tail until the center closes. An open center is the first thing a mug finds.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rnd 2.** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Rnd 3.** (Sc in the next stitch, 2 sc in the next) 6 times. (18)

**Rnd 4.** (Sc in the next 2, 2 sc in the next) 6 times. (24)

**Checkpoint:** lay it flat. At 24 stitches a small disc should already lie flat or nearly so. A bowl shape here will only get worse.

**Rnd 5.** (Sc in the next 3, 2 sc in the next) 6 times. (30)

**Rnd 6.** (Sc in the next 4, 2 sc in the next) 6 times. (36)

**Rnd 7.** (Sc in the next 5, 2 sc in the next) 6 times. (42)

**Rnd 8.** (Sc in the next 6, 2 sc in the next) 6 times. (48)

**Rnd 9.** Sc in each stitch. (48)

**Checkpoint:** measure across and set your mug on it. Add a round of increases, (sc in the next 7, 2 sc in the next) 6 times for 54, if it is too small. Then work one plain round.

**Finish.** A spiral ends half a step higher than it started. Slip stitch into the next stitch, fasten off, and weave the tail along the edge for an inch to smooth the step.

### Keeping it round instead of six-sided

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/increase-stagger.svg" alt="Correct: the increase in each round is moved, for example by working half the plain stitches before the first increase on alternate rounds, so the edge stays round. Mistake: every increase lands directly above the last one, which builds six corners and the circle becomes a hexagon." width="480" height="679" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Move where the increases fall from round to round, and the six corners never form.</figcaption>
</figure>

Written as above, each round's increases sit just past the last round's, and they stack into six lines that can turn the circle into a soft hexagon. To break the lines, start alternate rounds partway through the repeat. Round 5, for example, becomes: sc in the next stitch, 2 sc in the next, (sc in the next 3, 2 sc in the next) 5 times, sc in the last 2. That is 1 + 2 + 25 + 2 = 30, the same count with the increases moved.

## Stem

With green, ch 7. Slip stitch in the second chain from the hook and in each chain back. (6 sl st) Fasten off, leaving a tail. Sew the tail end to the edge of the disc so the chain sticks out like a stem. Sew through the disc and back through the chain, then weave the tail. A knot on the back is not a finish.

## Reading a circle that is not flat

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/coaster-cup-ruffle.svg" alt="Three side views of a crochet circle on a table. Flat lies on the table. Cupped curves up at both edges like a bowl, labeled too few increases or tall tight stitches. Ruffled waves up and down, labeled too many increases or a loose wide stitch. A note says to count the round first, because a missed increase also cups." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Hold the disc at eye level on the table. The edge tells you which way to adjust.</figcaption>
</figure>

**It cups.** Count first: a missed increase is the usual cause. If the count is right, go up a hook size. If it still cups, your single crochet is tall for six increases. Add a seventh increase on alternate rounds and count carefully.

**It ruffles.** Count first: an extra increase does this too. If the count is right, go down a hook size, or work one plain round and look again.

**It looks six-sided.** Stacked increases. Stagger them as shown above.

**There is a hole in the middle.** The magic ring was not pulled closed. Pull the tail again and weave it through the first round.

**It is smaller than the mug.** Add rounds. Each one adds about half an inch across at the example gauge.

## Granny square coaster (make 4)

The granny square is a second shape for the same job. It shows more of the table through its spaces, so it suits a mug more than a sweating glass.

**Color order suggestion.** Rnd 1 A (orange), Rnd 2 B (black), Rnd 3 C (purple), Rnd 4 B (black). Or follow Variation 1 on the granny tutorial. In this square the beginning ch-3 counts as a double crochet.

**Rnd 1.** With the first color, ch 4, join to form a ring. Ch 3, 2 dc in the ring, ch 2, (3 dc in the ring, ch 2) 3 times, join to the top of the ch-3. Fasten off if changing. (12 dc, 4 corners)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-round-1.svg" alt="Granny square round 1. Four clusters of three double crochet worked into the ring, 12 double crochet in all, with a chain-2 corner between each pair. There are no side spaces yet." width="480" height="639" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1: four clusters in the ring, with a ch-2 corner between each pair.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rnd 2.** Join the next color in any ch-2 corner. Ch 3, (2 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the same corner, ch 1, *(3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the next corner, ch 1; rep from * twice more, join. Fasten off if changing. (24 dc, 4 ch-2 corners, 4 ch-1)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-round-2.svg" alt="Granny square round 2. 24 double crochet in all. Each of the four corners holds 3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc. Each side has 1 chain-1 space, still empty; round 3 fills them." width="480" height="667" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 2: every cluster goes into a corner, and the ch 1 between corners makes the first side spaces.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rnd 3.** Join the next color in any corner. Ch 3, (2 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the corner, ch 1, 3 dc in the next ch-1 space, ch 1, *(3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the corner, ch 1, 3 dc in the next ch-1 space, ch 1; rep from * around, join. (36 dc, 4 ch-2 corners, 4 side clusters)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-side-space.svg" alt="One side of a granny square, round 3 worked over round 2. Correct: the side cluster of round 3 sits in the chain-1 space between round 2's two corner halves, with a chain-1 on each side of it. Common mistake: the cluster goes into the top of a double crochet instead. The gap closes, the side goes stiff, and the square starts to cup." width="480" height="721" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>From round 3 on, side clusters go into those ch-1 spaces, never into a stitch top.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rnd 4.** Join in any corner. Ch 3, (2 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the corner, ch 1, 3 dc in the next space, ch 1, 3 dc in the next space, ch 1, *(3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the next corner, ch 1, 3 dc in the next space, ch 1, 3 dc in the next space, ch 1; rep from * around, join. (48 dc, 4 corners, 2 side clusters on each side)

Each round adds 12 double crochet: 12, 24, 36, 48. The corners do the growing. Every round puts a new corner shell on each side of each corner and one more cluster along each side. If a round comes out at a number other than a multiple of 12, a cluster went into a stitch top instead of a space. No size is promised for this square. Measure yours after blocking.

## Dishcloth

Work as the coaster through Rnd 4, then keep going in the same pattern through **Rnd 7**, one more cluster per side each round. Round 7 has 7 × 12 = 84 dc. Stop earlier or later if the cloth is the size you want. Suggested colors: alternate B and A each round with a C round in the middle.

**Optional border.** One round of single crochet in B: 1 sc in each dc and each ch-1 space, and 3 sc in each corner space. On a 7-round square that is 27 along each side plus 3 at each corner, 120 in all. Count the first side before you go round.

## Finishing

Change color on the last yarn-over of the join. Weave the ends in as you go, because black ends show through orange if left loose. Block all pieces to the same measurement before calling the set done. Steam- or wet-block cotton as described in the [blocking notes](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/). Label the back of one coaster with the fiber content if you are giving the set away.

## Check the outer circle before adding a plain round

A flat coaster must keep enough increases to remain flat at its actual gauge. Round 9 worked even after a 48-stitch circle can start turning up into a wall; it is optional, not a guaranteed flat border. If it cups, undo it and finish round 8 with a loose slip stitch taper, or increase to 54 if a larger coaster is wanted. Set an empty, cool mug on it: the base must rest fully on flat fabric, not on the stem or a raised join. Compare all four finished squares after washing and drying before joining or gifting a set.

## Frequently asked

### Can this go in the microwave?

No. It is a coaster. Cotton is the safer fiber next to a warm mug, but a coaster is not a hot pad, and nothing on this page was heat-tested.

### Why does my circle look like a hexagon?

The increases are stacked in six lines. Stagger the start of each repeat on alternate rounds.

### Do I need the granny squares too?

No. The round pumpkin stands on its own. The squares are a second set if you want a matching cloth.

## What to make next

A stuffed [pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/), which starts with this same circle and then stops increasing so the sides rise. Or the [granny square tote](/how-to-crochet-a-granny-square-tote/), which joins eight of these squares, or the [mug cozy](/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners/) if you would rather wrap the cup than sit under it.


# How to Crochet a Ghost Mini Wallet

Source: src/content/posts/ghost-crochet-mini-wallet.md



This is a fixed-count pattern for a small white wallet with a ghost face on its flap. It differs from the [Halloween card wallet](/halloween-crochet-card-wallet/), which teaches you to size a pocket from any cards, in two ways. The pocket here is deep enough for a dollar bill folded in half as well as a card. And the flap is shaped: three decrease rows narrow it from the full width to a rounded tab with a button loop. Shaping a flap with paired decreases is the skill this page teaches.

A standard ID-1 card, the size of most US bank cards and licenses, is **3.370 × 2.125 inches** (85.60 × 53.98 mm) under ISO/IEC 7810. US paper currency is **2.61 × 6.14 inches**, per the Bureau of Engraving and Printing. Folded in half, a bill is about 3.07 × 2.61 inches.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No wallet for this page was crocheted or measured. Lay a card on the fabric before you seam.

![A white crochet ghost mini wallet with a button flap and a card at the pocket](../../assets/articles/ghost-crochet-mini-wallet-result.jpg "Illustration. A white flap wallet with a face.")


## How the wallet is built

One rectangle, 16 stitches wide. Rows 1 to 14 fold up to become the front of the pocket. Rows 15 to 28 are the back. Rows 29 to 34 are the flap: two straight rows, three decrease rows, and a row with a chain loop for the button. Seam the pocket sides, sew on a button, and embroider a face on the flap.

### Why the pocket is 14 rows

Fourteen rows at 5 per inch is 2.8 inches. That is deeper than a card needs, on purpose: a bill folded once is 2.61 inches tall, and it has to fit under the flap too. The cost is that a card alone sits about two-thirds of an inch below the opening. To take it out, press up on it from the bottom of the pocket, through the fabric.

If you will never carry cash, see the card-only variation below. Its pocket is 2.4 inches, so the card sits nearer the top.

### How the flap narrows

A single crochet decrease (sc2tog) turns two stitches into one. Work one at the start and one at the end of a row, and the row is two stitches shorter, one from each side. Both edges step inward by a stitch, a quarter inch at this gauge. Three such rows take the flap from 16 stitches to 10, from 4 inches to 2.5, and the corners slope evenly on both sides.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ghost-wallet-flap.svg" alt="The flap rows stacked: rows 29 and 30 at 16 stitches, row 31 at 14, row 32 at 12, row 33 at 10, each with a decrease marked at both ends, and row 34 as 3 single crochet, a chain-5 loop over 4 skipped stitches, and 3 single crochet. Notes: each decrease row moves both edges in a quarter inch; the pocket is 2.8 inches deep for a 2.125 inch card or a bill folded to 2.61 inches." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Decrease at both ends of the same row, or the flap leans to one side.</figcaption>
</figure>

## What you need

![White yarn, black scrap yarn, a hook, a button, a needle, and a blank card](../../assets/articles/ghost-crochet-mini-wallet-1.jpg "Illustration. Materials. The card is there to check the width.")

- **Worsted cotton**, white, and a scrap of black. Cotton holds a pocket's shape better than fuzzy acrylic. [Yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or whatever gives 4 stitches per inch. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One button**, about 1/2 inch across.
- Yarn needle, scissors, one card to test the fit.

Abbreviations used: ch, sc, sc2tog (single crochet two together, written "dec" below).

## Gauge check

Work rows 1 to 4 and measure. Sixteen stitches should be close to 4 inches. Seamed, that leaves about 3.75 inches inside for a 3.37 inch card.

<div class="warning">

If 16 stitches measure under 3.6 inches, the card will jam after seaming. Go up a hook size, or work 18 stitches: chain 19, and every count below goes up by 2 (18, 16, 14, 12), with row 34 as sc 4, ch 5, skip 4, sc 4.

</div>

## Stage 1: the rectangle

**Row 1:** Ch 17. Sc in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Turn. (16)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/mini-wallet-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 17. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 16 sc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Chain 17 for 16 sc, about 4 inches at the example gauge.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rows 2–30:** Ch 1, sc in each stitch across. Turn. (16)

The ch 1 does not count as a stitch. Count every five rows. Single crochet edges tend to gain a stitch at the turn.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The gained stitch this page warns about. The first sc of each row goes into the edge stitch, not the ch 1.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Stage 2: the flap

**Row 31:** Ch 1, dec, sc in the next 12 stitches, dec. Turn. (14)

**Row 32:** Ch 1, dec, sc in the next 10 stitches, dec. Turn. (12)

**Row 33:** Ch 1, dec, sc in the next 8 stitches, dec. Turn. (10)

**Row 34:** Ch 1, sc in the next 3 stitches, ch 5, skip 4, sc in the last 3. (6 sc and a ch-5 loop)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/mini-wallet-step-2.svg" alt="Buttonhole row over 10 stitches: sc 3, ch 5 over 4 skipped stitches, sc 3. 6 sc and a ch-5 loop. Row 33 has 10 stitches: 3 + 4 skipped + 3. If the loop misses your button, redo this row with ch 6 or ch 7 over the same 4 stitches." width="480" height="465" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. The loop row works over the 10 stitches left after the three decrease rows.</figcaption>
</figure>

Fasten off.

Each decrease row uses every stitch of the row below: 2 + 12 + 2 = 16, then 2 + 10 + 2 = 14, then 2 + 8 + 2 = 12. Row 34 is 3 + 4 skipped + 3 = 10. If the loop misses your button, redo row 34 with ch 6 or ch 7 over the same four stitches.

## Stage 3: fold and seam

Lay the rectangle with the foundation chain at the bottom. Fold it up so the fold falls between rows 14 and 15. The chain edge now lies against row 28. Rows 29 to 34 stand above as the flap.

Slide the card in before you sew. It should go in flat and sit below the opening. Then try the folded bill behind it.

Whipstitch each side from the fold to the opening, through both layers, catching only the outer loop of each edge stitch. Going deeper takes width from the inside. Go back along each side a second time. A card corner works at a single seam line until it opens. Weave the tails inside the seam.

## Stage 4: button and face

Close the flap with the card inside. Mark where the loop lands on the front. Sew the button there, through the front layer only.

With black, embroider the face on the outside of the flap, above the loop: two short stitches for eyes, about 4 stitches apart, and a small wide V or oval a row below them for the mouth. Shallow stitches. Deep ones leave a bump on the inside of the flap that catches the card.

## Mark the three zones before folding

On the original 34-row version, place removable markers at rows 14 and 28. Fold between 14 and 15; the foundation edge meets row 28, while rows 29–34 remain the free flap. Pair side row ends from the fold upward: front 14 with back 15, front 13 with back 16, continuing to front 1 with back 28. Sew each pair without catching the flap. The loop in row 34 replaces four base positions with five chains and is intentionally not a ten-sc straight row. Test the actual button before fastening off that row.

## Variations

**Card only.** Work row 1 and rows 2–26 at 16 stitches. Mark the fold between rows 12 and 13: front 1–12, back 13–24, straight flap 25–26. New rows 27, 28, and 29 use the original decrease instructions for rows 31, 32, and 33, finishing at 14, 12, and 10. New row 30 is sc 3, ch 5, skip 4, sc 3. The foundation edge meets row 24 when folded; diagrams numbered through row 34 show only the original cash-and-card version.

**A colored flap.** Work rows 31 to 34 in a second color, changing on the last yarn-over of row 30. See [changing yarn color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/).

**No face.** A plain white wallet works the same.

## Troubleshooting

**The flap leans.** A decrease was missed at one end. Count the stitches on rows 31 to 33.

**The flap edges look stepped.** That is normal for single crochet decreases. A round of slip stitch around the flap smooths it.

**The card will not go in.** The seams went too deep, or the gauge is tight. Resew through the outer loops, or remake with a larger hook.

**The bill lifts the flap off the button.** Two bills, or a bill folded in quarters. One bill folded once.

**The pocket sags.** Loose gauge. Drop to an F/5 (3.75 mm) hook and redo it. Check the card again before seaming.

## Care

Empty it. Hand wash cool, dry flat, and do not iron the face. Cotton can shrink. If it does, the card will tell you, and the fix is the wider foundation, not stretching.

## Frequently asked

**Will a US driver's license fit?**

Most are ID-1 size, the same as a bank card. Test yours.

**Can I add a lining?**

Not at this width. A fabric lining takes up the ease the card needs.

**Can I sell finished wallets?**

Yes. Do not repost the pattern.

**Left-handed?**

Same rows. The fold and face do not change. See [left-handed crochet](/left-handed-crochet/).

## Next

The method for sizing a flap pocket to any cards is the [Halloween card wallet](/halloween-crochet-card-wallet/). A drawstring pouch for coins is a [drawstring bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/). Decreases are explained in [increase and decrease](/crochet-increase-decrease/). A ghost-shaped wallet with a scalloped hem and no flap is the [ghost crochet wallet](/ghost-crochet-wallet/), part of the [Halloween crochet wallet collection](/halloween-crochet-wallets/).


# Ghost Crochet Wallet: Free Pattern With a Scalloped Hem

Source: src/content/posts/ghost-crochet-wallet.md



A ghost wallet only works if it still looks like a ghost with cards in it. This one keeps the face above the pocket, so the cards sit behind the ghost's middle and the face never stretches. Two flat panels, one edging round that joins them and scallops the hem, and a snap you cannot see from outside.

It is one of five patterns in the [Halloween crochet wallet collection](/halloween-crochet-wallets/). If you want a smaller, simpler ghost with a fold-over flap, the [ghost mini wallet](/ghost-crochet-mini-wallet/) is a different design worth a look. This one teaches shaping with decreases and joining two layers with an edging.

![A cream ghost-wallet concept illustration; follow the snap-closure construction diagram](../../assets/articles/ghost-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

The finished size and card fit here are worked out from the example gauge rather than measured from a finished wallet, so swatch first and lay a real bank card on the front panel before you join. A standard US bank card is 3.370 by 2.125 inches, the ISO/IEC 7810 ID-1 size.

## Materials

- **Worsted weight (CYC 4) yarn** in cream, ivory, or white. Cream hides grime better than bright white. [Yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **Scraps** of black for the eyes and mouth and soft pink for the cheeks.
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives the gauge. Firm fabric matters more than the number. [Hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One sew-on snap**, 3/8 in (10 mm), with cream or white thread. A clear plastic snap disappears best.
- Tapestry needle, two stitch markers, scissors, one card.

**How much yarn.** Not weighed from a made wallet. The two panels and edging come to about 33 square inches, about 2 times a 4 inch swatch. Weigh your swatch, double it, and add 20 percent.

## Abbreviations and stitches

US terms. US half double crochet is UK half treble. See the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/).

| Abbreviation | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| ch | chain |
| sc | single crochet |
| hdc | half double crochet |
| sl st | slip stitch |
| sk | skip |
| sc2tog | single crochet two together: pull up a loop in each of 2 stitches, yarn over, pull through all 3 loops. One stitch fewer. |
| st(s) | stitch(es) |
| RS / WS | right side / wrong side |

**Turning chain rule.** Every row begins: ch 1, turn. The ch 1 does not count as a stitch. Work the first single crochet into the first stitch.

**Single crochet through two layers.** Insert the hook through the front panel and then the back panel, matching the edges, and work the stitch as normal.

## Size and gauge

**Gauge:** 16 sc and 20 rows = 4 in. Make and measure a swatch first, as in the [gauge guide](/crochet-gauge/).

Each panel is 17 stitches, 4.25 inches wide. The side edging takes a little from each side, which leaves the pocket about 3.9 inches wide inside, against a 3.37 inch card. The front panel is 14 rows, 2.8 inches. After the hem join, the pocket is about 2.7 inches deep. A 2.125 inch card leaves room above it for the snap at row 13. The back panel is 22 rows, 4.4 inches, and the edging adds the scallops below and a round on top: about 4.6 by 4.9 inches overall.

## The pattern

### Stage 1: the two panels

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ghost-wallet-panels.svg" alt="Diagram. Two stitch grids side by side. The back panel is 17 stitches wide, 18 straight rows, then rows 19 to 22 decrease to 15, 13, 11, and 9 stitches for a rounded head. The front panel is 17 stitches by 14 rows. A label marks where the front panel ends on the back." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Both panels start the same way. The back keeps going and rounds off.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The two cream panels before joining: a taller back with a stepped top, and a shorter front](../../assets/articles/ghost-crochet-wallet-panels.jpg "Illustration.")

**Back panel**, cream:

**Row 1:** Ch 18. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and in each ch across. (17)

**Rows 2–18:** Ch 1, turn. Sc in each st across. (17)

**Row 19:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 13 sts, sc2tog. (15)

**Row 20:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 11 sts, sc2tog. (13)

**Row 21:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 9 sts, sc2tog. (11)

**Row 22:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 7 sts, sc2tog. (9)

Fasten off. Weave the tails in now; they are hard to reach after joining.

**Front panel**, cream:

**Row 1:** Ch 18. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and in each ch across. (17)

**Rows 2–14:** Ch 1, turn. Sc in each st across. (17)

Fasten off and weave in.

Lay the front on the back with the hems matched. The front should end with 8 rows of the back showing above it. If their widths differ, count both panels first. Equal stitch counts can still measure differently if your tension changed; remake the mismatched panel rather than stretching it to fit.

### Stage 2: the face, while the back is flat

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ghost-wallet-face.svg" alt="Diagram. The back panel from row 14 to row 22, 17 stitches across with stitch 9 at the center. Black oval eyes on rows 17 and 18 at stitches 6 and 12. A small black mouth on row 16 at stitch 9. Pink cheeks on row 16 at stitches 5 and 13. A dashed line at the top of row 14 marks the pocket opening." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Stitch k mirrors stitch 18 minus k, so the face stays centered.</figcaption>
</figure>

![Black oval eyes, a small mouth, and pink cheeks on cream crochet, above the pocket opening](../../assets/articles/ghost-crochet-wallet-face.jpg "Illustration.")

Pick the side of the back panel you like better. That is its right side, and it faces forward, above the front panel. Count stitches from the right-hand edge with row 1 at the bottom.

1. **Eyes.** With black, a vertical oval over rows 17 and 18 at stitch 6: three or four close straight stitches, side by side. Repeat at stitch 12.
2. **Mouth.** With black, a small oval or a short horizontal stitch on row 16 at stitch 9. An oval reads as "boo," a line as calm.
3. **Cheeks.** With pink, two or three short stitches stacked into a soft dot on row 16 at stitches 5 and 13. Use a single ply of the yarn if it looks heavy.

Work through the back panel only and keep the strands on the wrong side short. The wrong side becomes the outside back of the wallet, so finish each color with a few running stitches under the backs of the embroidery, not with a knot.

### Stage 3: the snap, before joining

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ghost-pocket-orientation.svg" alt="The two pocket-facing surfaces and the edge that must remain open." width="800" height="480" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The two pocket-facing surfaces and the edge that must remain open.</figcaption>
</figure>


<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ghost-wallet-snap.svg" alt="Diagram. Left, a side view: the tall back panel and shorter front panel joined at the hem, with the two halves of a snap facing each other inside the pocket at row 13. Right, the wallet to scale with an ID-1 card in the pocket, about 3.9 inches wide and 2.7 inches deep inside on paper, the snap above the top of the card." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The snap closes the top of the pocket from inside, so nothing shows on the ghost.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. Sew the **socket** half to the right side of the back panel at row 13, stitch 9. This side faces into the pocket.
2. Sew the **ball** half to the wrong side of the front panel at row 13, stitch 9. This side also faces into the pocket.
3. Lay the panels together and press. The two halves should meet. If they miss by more than a stitch, move one now. Placement is easier while the panels are flat, although the pocket remains accessible after joining.

Sew with doubled thread, four or five passes per hole, catching only the panel you are sewing on.

### Stage 4: the joining round

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ghost-wallet-join.svg" alt="Diagram. The front panel on the back panel with one edging round. Along the hem through both layers: slip stitch in stitch 1, then skip 1, 5 half double crochet in the next, skip 1, slip stitch in the next, four times. Up the first side 14 single crochet through both layers, 4 through the back only, 5 along the head slope. 9 across the top. 23 down the other side." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. One round joins the panels, scallops the hem, and leaves the front's top edge open.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The hem edging, four scallops across the bottom](../../assets/articles/ghost-crochet-wallet-hem.jpg "Illustration. The pattern's repeat makes four scallops.")

Place the front panel on the right side of the back panel, wrong side of the front against the face side of the back, hems and side edges matched. Clip the sides with markers. Join cream yarn at a bottom corner in stitch 1 of the hem, working through both layers.

**Mark the join before you begin.** Lay both foundation edges together and number their 17 chain positions from the starting corner. Mark row ends 1, 14, and 18 on each side of the back. Match front row end 1 to back row end 1, through row end 14. Below row 14 the hook must pass through a row-end opening in each panel. Above it, pass through the back only. A row-end opening is in the edge of the completed row, not the top V of a stitch and not the turning-chain loop. Spread the work on the table as you insert the hook so you can see both layers.

**Snap orientation.** Below the opening, the decorated side of the back is the pocket interior; above the opening the same side is visible with the face. The front panel's undecorated back faces it. Hold the snap halves on those facing surfaces, clip the sides, insert your actual card stack, and close the snap before permanently sewing. Row 13 is a starting location: move both halves upward together if the hardware touches the cards.

**Slope placement.** The five sc cover the four shaped row ends: one in row 19, one in row 20, two spread within row 21's edge, and one in row 22. Mirror this placement on the return side. Avoid reusing the top corner stitch when starting the nine top stitches. Check the outline lies flat; redistribute the slope stitches if it puckers or waves. This edging still needs a yarn sample to confirm its shape.

**Hem:** Sl st in st 1. (Sk 1 st, 5 hdc in the next st, sk 1 st, sl st in the next st) 4 times. (17 hem sts used: 1 + 4 x 4)

**First side:** Ch 1. Working into the row ends, 14 sc through both layers, then 4 sc through the back only, then 5 sc evenly along the slope of the head. (23)

**Top:** Sc in each of the 9 sts of row 22. (9)

**Second side:** 5 sc along the slope, 4 sc through the back only, 14 sc through both layers. (23)

Sl st in the first sl st. Fasten off and weave the tail inside the edging.

The diagram starts at the lower left. Right-handed crocheters with the front facing them will start at the lower right instead. The counts are the same either way.

<div class="warning">

The top edge of the front panel is the pocket opening. The edging passes it at the 14th row end on each side and keeps going up the back only. Do not work across it.

</div>

## Assembly order, at a glance

1. Back panel, then front panel. Weave in all tails.
2. Face on the back panel.
3. Snap halves on both panels. Test the meeting point.
4. One joining round.
5. Test a card and the snap.

## Make it yours

- **A wider pocket with a plain hem.** Chain 20 for each panel (19 stitches). Work the same straight-row totals. Back rows 19–22 are: sc2tog, sc 15, sc2tog (17); sc2tog, sc 13, sc2tog (15); sc2tog, sc 11, sc2tog (13); sc2tog, sc 9, sc2tog (11). Use 19 sc through both layers across the hem, not the 17-stitch scallop repeat. Work 11 sc across row 22. Center the snap at stitch 10 and shift every face position one stitch toward the higher number: eyes 7 and 13; mouth 10; cheeks 6 and 14. Side-row counts stay unchanged. This variation is not shown in the 17-stitch diagrams.
- **No scallops.** Work 17 sc along the hem through both layers instead of the scallop repeat.
- **A glow-in-the-dark ghost.** Glow yarns are often thicker or stiffer. Swatch first; the stitch counts only work at the gauge.
- **Lining.** Cut fabric to the front panel's size plus a 1/4 inch fold on every edge and hand-sew it inside the front before joining. The pocket loses a little width, so check with a card first.

## Keep the pocket opening visible while joining

Clip the two panels together and slide a card in before stitching. Mark both ends of the front's row 14 in a contrasting color. During the joining round, count paired row ends 1–14 up the first side, then switch to the taller back alone. On the other side, work the exposed back first and switch to both panels when you reach the other row-14 marker. The horizontal top of the front remains completely unworked. After the first side and hem are joined, check that the card still slides in before completing the second side.

## Common mistakes and fixes

**The scallops end at the wrong place.** The repeat uses exactly 16 stitches after the first slip stitch. Count the hem stitches before starting. If the hem has 16 or 18, the repeat cannot land.

**The front's top edge got crocheted shut.** The 14th row end was missed. Pull back to the side and restart counting at the hem.

**The head looks pointed.** The slope got too few stitches. Five sc along four decrease rows rounds it; three pulls it into a point.

**The face drifts toward a side.** Stitches were counted from different edges. Count from the right-hand edge every time, or mark stitch 9 first.

**The snap is visible.** It was sewn through both layers of a panel's stitches. Catch only the back half of each stitch.

**The pocket gapes above the snap.** Normal with one snap. A second snap at row 13, stitches 4 and 14, can help, but it makes cards harder to reach.

## Care

Hand wash in cool water. Squeeze, do not wring, and dry flat with the hem scallops pinned. Black embroidery on cream is the risk in the first wash: test a scrap of the black on a white paper towel first, or use a colorfast cotton.

## Frequently asked

**Will a card fit?** On paper, yes, with about 0.5 inch of width and room above the card. Test before joining by laying a card on the front panel; it should be narrower and shorter.

**Why is the snap inside instead of on a flap?** There is no flap. The face is the outside, so the closure stays hidden behind the front panel.

**Can a beginner make it?** If you can single crochet and decrease, yes. The joining round is the intermediate part, mostly because of the counting.

**How is it different from the ghost mini wallet?** That design is a folded rectangle with a tapered flap. This one is two shaped panels and no flap.

**Can I sell the finished wallets?** Yes. Please link here instead of reposting the pattern.

## More Halloween wallets

- [Black cat crochet wallet](/black-cat-crochet-wallet/): a seamless tube with ears
- [Pumpkin crochet wallet](/pumpkin-crochet-wallet/): sideways back-loop ribs
- [Witch hat crochet wallet](/witch-hat-crochet-wallet/): two pockets and a buttonhole
- [Spiderweb crochet wallet](/spiderweb-crochet-wallet/): embroidery on a pointed flap

For decreases, see [increases and decreases](/crochet-increase-decrease/). For hiding tails in a cream fabric, see [weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

The ghost comes down to doing things in the right order: face and snap while the panels are flat, then one carefully counted round. Count the hem before you start it, and leave the front's top edge alone.


# How to Crochet a Gothic Bat Earbuds Pouch

Source: src/content/posts/gothic-bat-earbuds-pouch.md



A bare earbud case disappears in a bag. A small black bat with purple wings is easy to spot, but only if the case still fits and the flap still closes once the wings are on. Decorations sewn on the outside take up room and shift the balance. Put them in the wrong place and the pouch either tips over or will not shut.

The pouch itself is a cup sized to the case. The round-pouch method in the [device case guide](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/) explains in detail how to size one by measuring around the case, so this page keeps the sizing short. What this page adds is the appliqué: shaping a flat triangle with decreases, adding picots to an edge, and sewing parts on where they stay out of the way.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No pouch for this page was crocheted. Inches are estimates from the example gauge.

![A small black crochet bat pouch with purple wings, ears, a wood button, and a yarn loop](../../assets/articles/gothic-bat-earbuds-pouch-result.jpg "Illustration. A black body, purple wings, ears, button flap, loop.")


## How it is built

A flat circle, then straight rounds, makes a cup. A short flap is worked in rows over part of the rim and closes with a chain loop and a button. Two ear triangles sit on top. Two wing triangles with picot edges sit on the sides. A chain loop between the ears takes a split ring.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bat-appliques.svg" alt="An ear triangle worked 5, 3, 1 stitches and a wing triangle worked 9, 7, 5, 3, 1 with picots on the long sides. A front view of the pouch shows ears at the top behind the flap, a loop between them, and wings sewn by their wide edge along the middle of the sides, above the base and below the flap." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The wings go on the middle of the tube: above the base so the pouch stands, below the flap so it closes.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Yarn and hook

- **Worsted cotton**, black and purple. Cotton gives the wing points a crisp edge. Fuzzy yarn blurs the picots. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives firm fabric. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- A button about 1/2 inch across, a yarn needle, a stitch marker, an optional split ring.

## Size the body to the case

Measure around the case at its widest point. At 4 single crochet per inch, multiply by 4, then round up to the nearest multiple of 6 for a flat circle. A small round case about 7 inches around needs 28, so 30. A larger case needs 36. Make a swatch first and use your own stitches per inch, not the example's.

The cup is deep enough when the top of the case sits just below the rim.

## Stage 1: the body

Black. Work in a spiral, without joining, and move a marker up each round. [Working in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/).

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1 starts in a magic ring. The ch 1 that closes the first loop is not a stitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 4:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)

**Round 5:** (Sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (30)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bat-pouch-step-1.svg" alt="Body base: rounds 1 to 5, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 1: 6 sc in a magic ring, 6 stitches. Round 2: 2 sc in each st, 12 stitches. Round 3: (sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 18 stitches. Round 4: (sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 24 stitches. Round 5: (sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 30 stitches. Each round adds 6. A 36-stitch body adds round 6: (sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times." width="480" height="563" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Six increases a round to 30, the example body for a small case. Your case may need round 6.</figcaption>
</figure>

For a 36-stitch body: **Round 6:** (Sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (36)

**Even rounds:** Sc in each stitch until the case sits just inside the rim. At 5 rounds per inch, about 10 rounds for a 2 inch case.

![A short black crochet cylinder still on the hook](../../assets/articles/gothic-bat-earbuds-pouch-3.jpg "Illustration. Even rounds, no increases.")

## Stage 2: the flap

Worked in rows over one third of the rim.

**Row 1:** Sc in the next 10 stitches. Turn. (10) For 36 stitches, use 12.

**Rows 2–5:** Ch 1, sc in each stitch. Turn. (10)

**Row 6:** Ch 1, sc 4, ch 4, skip 2, sc 4. (8 sc and a 4-chain loop) For 12, sc 5, ch 4, skip 2, sc 5.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bat-pouch-step-2.svg" alt="A flap of 6 rows worked over 10 of the 30 stitches on the last round, with a chain loop on the top row. Rows 1 to 5: 10 sc. Row 6: sc 4, ch 4, skip 2, sc 4. For a 36-stitch body the flap is 12 wide: sc 5, ch 4, skip 2, sc 5." width="480" height="449" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. The flap uses 10 of the 30 stitches. The other 20 stay open as the mouth of the cup.</figcaption>
</figure>

Fasten off. Close the flap over the case and mark where the loop lands. Sew the button there.

## Stage 3: the ears (make 2)

Black. A triangle loses one stitch at each edge every row.

**Row 1:** Ch 6. Sc in the 2nd chain from the hook and each chain across. Turn. (5)

**Row 2:** Ch 1, sc2tog, sc, sc2tog. Turn. (3)

**Row 3:** Ch 1, sc3tog. (1)

Fasten off with a sewing tail. The chain edge is the base.

## Stage 4: the wings (make 2)

Purple. The same shaping, made larger.

**Row 1:** Ch 10. Sc in the 2nd chain from the hook and each chain across. Turn. (9)

**Row 2:** Ch 1, sc2tog, sc 5, sc2tog. Turn. (7)

**Row 3:** Ch 1, sc2tog, sc 3, sc2tog. Turn. (5)

**Row 4:** Ch 1, sc2tog, sc, sc2tog. Turn. (3)

**Row 5:** Ch 1, sc3tog. (1) Do not fasten off.

Each row uses every stitch of the one below: 2 + 5 + 2 = 9, 2 + 3 + 2 = 7, 2 + 1 + 2 = 5, then all 3.

**Picot edge:** Slip stitch down one long side, working into the row ends. At three evenly spaced points, work (sl st, ch 3, sl st in the same place). Slip stitch along the chain edge without picots, because that edge gets sewn down. Work the second long side the same way. Fasten off with a sewing tail.

A picot is a small chain loop that stands off the edge. Three per side read as the scalloped edge of a bat wing.

## Stage 5: placing and sewing

![A purple wing being sewn to the side of the black pouch](../../assets/articles/gothic-bat-earbuds-pouch-5.jpg "Illustration. Wings on the sides, ears on top.")

1. **Case in first.** Put the case in the pouch and close the flap. Everything is placed with the pouch full, because an empty pouch is narrower.
2. **Ears.** Sew the chain edge of each ear to the rim just beside the flap, one on each side of where the flap starts. They must not sit on the flap, or they stop it folding.
3. **Wings.** Pin each wing's wide edge vertically along a side, centered on the middle of the wall. Too low and the wing touches the table and tips the pouch. Too high and it catches the flap.
4. **Sew loosely.** Whipstitch through both layers with easy tension. Pulled tight, the seam puckers the wall and makes the pouch smaller inside.
5. **Loop.** Join black on the back wall, one round below the middle of the flap's base. Ch 16, slip stitch into the same stitch. For a sturdier loop, slip stitch back along the chain.
6. Weave the ends in on the inside. [Weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

Check the case one last time. It should drop in and the flap should close without pulling.

![A white earbud case sitting in the open bat pouch](../../assets/articles/gothic-bat-earbuds-pouch-6.jpg "Illustration. The case drops in. The flap closes over it.")

## Use markers to keep the decoration out of the opening

Before the flap, flatten the filled pouch and mark the center back and the intended flap span. Rejoin at its first stitch if the spiral marker is elsewhere. For 30 rim stitches the flap is ten stitches; for 36 it is twelve. Work only that span and leave the rest open. Pin ears beside the flap base, then pin each wing's nine-stitch foundation edge vertically along one wall. Sew through wing and that wall only, with the device removed. Check the lid and flap movement after temporarily tacking each piece. Do not assume circumference alone guarantees a round base matches a box-shaped case.

## Variations

**All black.** The wing shape still reads as a bat.

**Eyes.** Embroider two small eyes rather than sewing on beads. Beads come off and are a choking risk for small children.

**Larger wings.** Start the wing with ch 12 (11 sc). Decrease the same way: 11, 9, 7, 5, 3, 1. Larger wings stick out further and catch on things in a bag.

## Troubleshooting

**The pouch tips over.** The wings were sewn too low. Move them up.

**The flap will not close.** The ears are on the flap, or the case is too tall. Move the ears, or add rounds.

**The side puckers.** Sewn too tight. Redo with easier tension.

**The wing has no point.** Row 5 was skipped, or a decrease was missed. Count each row.

**The picots disappear.** The yarn is too fuzzy, or the picot chain is too short. Use ch 4.

## Care

Hand wash cool. Press out in a towel. Dry flat. Machine drying can shrink cotton enough to trap the case.

## Frequently asked

**Will it fit every earbud case?**

Only if you size it to yours. Measure around the case and use the stitch count that covers it.

**Is the loop strong enough for keys?**

It has not been tested. A chain loop is weaker than a slip-stitch-reinforced one. For a heavy key ring, use a metal clip sewn on firmly, not a yarn loop.

**Can I sell finished pouches?**

Yes. Do not republish the pattern.

## Next

Sizing a case by its perimeter, in more detail, is the round-pouch method in the [device case guide](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/). Another flat piece with a closure and a decoration is the [card wallet](/halloween-crochet-card-wallet/).


# Halloween Crochet AirPods Case: Black Cat Pattern

Source: src/content/posts/halloween-crochet-airpods-case.md



An AirPods case rides loose in a bag, picks up lint in the hinge, and then the lid stops seating cleanly. A snug cozy keeps the grit out and makes a small case easy to find by feel. This one is a black cat for Halloween: a deep black sleeve with two ears, an embroidered face, and a small pumpkin by the hem. It is a cover, not armor. It does not cushion a drop and it does not keep out a spill.

Most AirPods case patterns hand you one stitch count and hope your case fits it. This one is sized from the case. The number that matters is the distance around the case, its perimeter, not its width. The worked example is planned for the AirPods Pro 2 USB‑C charging case — the MagSafe Charging Case (USB‑C) that ships with the AirPods Pro 2 — and a short table below retargets it for the AirPods 4 and AirPods 3 cases.

US crochet terms throughout. US single crochet is UK double crochet; see the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The case dimensions here are Apple's own published figures, linked below. This is an independent accessory. It is not made, sold, or endorsed by Apple, and "AirPods" is used only to say which case it fits.

![A black crochet cat cover on an open AirPods case, with pink inner ears, gold eyes, and a pom-pom](../../assets/articles/halloween-crochet-airpods-case-thumb.png)

## What you are making

An open-top black sleeve that slides over the charging case. The base is a small oval worked flat to match the case footprint, and the walls rise straight up to just below the lid seam. Two triangular ears sit on the top corners, and a flap rises from the back, folds over the top to hold the case in for carrying, and unbuttons so the lid can open. A short chain loop clips to a bag. The face is embroidered, so nothing can pull off and be swallowed. To charge, you slide the case out, which keeps the honest answer simple: this tutorial requires removing the sleeve before charging; it does not claim wireless compatibility.

## Which AirPods case this fits

Measure your own case with the lid closed, because that is the only reliable guide. If you want to check your figures against Apple's, here are the three cases this pattern targets, with the starting stitch count worked out from each footprint at the example gauge of 4 single crochet to the inch.

| Charging case | Height × Width × Depth (Apple) | Footprint perimeter | Start count |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AirPods Pro 2 — MagSafe Charging Case (USB‑C) | 45.2 × 60.6 × 21.7 mm (1.78 × 2.39 × 0.85 in) | 6.48 in | 26 sts — the worked example |
| AirPods 4 | 46.2 × 50.1 × 21.2 mm (1.82 × 1.97 × 0.83 in) | 5.60 in | 22 sts |
| AirPods 3 | 46.40 × 54.40 × 21.38 mm (1.83 × 2.14 × 0.84 in) | 5.96 in | 24 sts |

Apple publishes these figures for the [AirPods Pro 2 USB‑C charging case](https://support.apple.com/en-us/111834), the [AirPods 4](https://support.apple.com/en-us/121203), and the [AirPods 3](https://support.apple.com/en-us/111863). The footprint perimeter is 2 × (width + depth): the case stands with the lid up, so its standing height becomes the wall and its width and depth become the loop the wall has to close around.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/airpods-measure-map.svg" alt="Front and side view of the AirPods Pro 2 USB‑C charging case. Width 2.39 inches or 60.6 millimeters, height 1.78 inches or 45.2 millimeters, side depth 0.85 inches. The lid seam runs across the upper part and the charging port is on the bottom. Footprint perimeter is 2 times 2.39 plus 0.85, which is 6.48 inches." width="480" height="520" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Measure width, depth, and height with the lid closed. The sleeve is built around the footprint, so the perimeter is the number that decides the stitch count.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Measure the actual perimeter.** The 2 × (width + depth) column treats the rounded case as a rectangle and therefore overestimates its outline. Wrap a narrow paper strip around the widest part, mark the overlap, and use that measured length for your stitch calculation. The listed start counts are planning examples only. At this coarse gauge, changing two stitches changes the circumference by 0.5 inch, so a smaller yarn may give finer control. A correct perimeter does not by itself establish the oval's length and depth; check its footprint before building the wall.

## What you need

- **Smooth worsted (medium / weight 4) cotton in black.** Cotton holds a crisp edge and does not shed into the speaker mesh or port the way a fuzzy acrylic can. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A scrap of orange** for the nose and pumpkin, and a short length of gold or white for the eyes and whiskers.
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or whatever size gives you a firm fabric that holds its shape. [Hook sizes](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One small button**, about 3/8 inch (10 mm) across.
- Stitch marker, yarn needle, scissors.

Worsted cotton on a G hook gives a close fabric a case will not poke through. If yours comes out loose and gappy, drop to a 3.75 mm or 3.5 mm hook rather than changing the counts.

## Before you start: gauge and fit

Two gauges matter here, and they do different jobs. **Stitch gauge** (single crochet per inch) sets how many stitches it takes to close around the case, so it controls the fit. **Row gauge** (rounds per inch) sets how fast the wall grows, so it controls the height. A pattern that only quotes one of them cannot tell you both, which is why a tube that fits around can still come out too short or too tall.

The worked example uses 4 sc to the inch and 5 rounds to the inch. From there the arithmetic is short:

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Footprint perimeter (Pro 2) | 2 × (2.39 + 0.85) | 6.48 in |
| Starting stitches | 6.48 × 4, rounded to even | 26 |
| Foundation chain | straight side = (26 − 12) ÷ 2 = 7, so ch 7 + 3 | ch 10 |
| Wall height to the lid seam | about 1.2 in on the example case | about 6 rounds |

Two things keep this honest. First, crochet stretches, so pick the count that lands **at or just under** the perimeter rather than over it; a cozy that measures a hair small grips, while one worked loose slides off. Second, the number of wall rounds is set by where your case's lid seam sits, which you find by fitting, not from a spec sheet, so treat "about 6 rounds" as the example and stop at your own seam.

The oval base uses more than the perimeter. Its stitch count comes from the perimeter and your stitch gauge, its two straight sides follow the case **width** and its rounded ends follow the case **thickness** (depth), and the wall stops below the lid seam for **lid clearance** set by your row gauge. So all four measurements shape the piece, not the perimeter alone.

These finished figures are a planning example, worked out on paper from Apple's published dimensions and the example gauge rather than measured from a crocheted sample. They are a starting point, not a promise of fit: measure against your own case at each checkpoint and change the stitch count or hook if the cozy runs large or small.

If your gauge is different, keep the method and change the numbers: count the single crochet across one inch of your own flat base, multiply the perimeter by that figure, round to the nearest even number, and use the base count formula below.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/airpods-gauge-fit.svg" alt="A gauge and fit table at an example 4 single crochet per inch. 24 stitches measure 6.0 inches around and are snug. 26 stitches measure 6.5 inches and are the worked example. 28 stitches measure 7.0 inches and are roomy. The Pro 2 footprint perimeter is 6.48 inches, so 26 sits closest." width="480" height="500" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Pick the row whose measurement is closest to your case's perimeter, leaning snug. The fabric gives; an open count does not grip.</figcaption>
</figure>

## How the case is built

The sleeve is a flat-bottomed tube with trimmings. An oval base worked around a short foundation chain matches the oblong footprint better than a round circle would. Its two straight sides run along the case's width and the rounded ends curve around its thickness, so the base is shaped by both measurements while the perimeter sets how many stitches it takes to close around them. Once the base is the right size around, the increases stop and the walls go straight up — the same turn-up you see in [crochet in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/). The ears are small flat triangles, the flap is a few rows worked on the back stitches, and the face is embroidered on at the end. If you want the plain version without a Halloween face, the round-pouch method in the [device case guide](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/) is the same construction with a round base, and a [drawstring bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/) closes the same tube with a cord.

## The full pattern

Work in a spiral without joining, and keep a marker in the first stitch of each round. The base count follows one rule: **round 2 total = 2 × (straight side) + 12.** For the Pro 2's 26 stitches the straight side is 7; for 24 use 6, and for 22 use 5, adjusting the foundation chain to straight-side count + 3.

### Stage 1: the oval base

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/airpods-oval-corrected.svg" alt="The exact chain positions and stitch totals for the revised base." width="800" height="480" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The exact chain positions and stitch totals for the revised base.</figcaption>
</figure>


Work both long sides of the foundation chain and put three stitches in each end to turn the corner.

**Foundation:** ch 10. Label the chains 1–10 from the hook toward the slip knot; chain 1 is skipped.

**Round 1:** 3 sc in chain 2 (first rounded end; mark the first sc), sc in chains 3–9 (7 sc), 3 sc in chain 10 (second end). Rotate without turning the fabric over. Work one sc into each unused underside loop of chains 9–3 (7 sc). Stop before chain 2; it already contains its complete three-stitch end group. (20 = 3 + 7 + 3 + 7)

**Round 2:** From the marker, inc in each of the first 3 sc, sc in the next 7, inc in each of the next 3, sc in the last 7. Move the marker into the first new sc. (26 = 6 + 7 + 6 + 7)



**Checkpoint:** lay the base flat. A gentle cup is fine, since it becomes the wall. A ruffled, wavy edge can mean too many increases or a gauge mismatch — the extra stitches belong only at the two ends. Then set it against the bottom of the case: the straight sides should run the length of the footprint (the case width) and the ends should sit around its thickness. If the oval is clearly longer or wider than the case bottom, change the straight-side count or drop a hook size before you build the wall.

### Stage 2: the body

**Rounds 3–8:** sc in each stitch around. (26 each round)

With no increases, the oval turns up and the walls climb straight. Stand the case in every couple of rounds and stop when the lid seam clears the rim, so the lid stays free to open. Six rounds reach it on the example case; your count is whatever covers your case to its seam.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/airpods-body-rise.svg" alt="Side view of the sleeve. The 26-stitch oval base lies flat, and once increases stop, rounds 3 to 8 work even in 26 single crochet so the fabric turns up into a straight wall. No stitches are added or removed in the wall." width="480" height="470" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Stage 2. Even rounds with no increases stand the tube up. Stop at the lid seam, not at a fixed round number.</figcaption>
</figure>

### Stage 3: the flap and buttonhole

The flap is worked back and forth on 7 stitches at the back of the rim — the stitches that will sit behind the hinge. The ch 1 at the end of each row does not count as a stitch.

**Row 1:** join yarn at the back, sc in the next 7 stitches. Ch 1, turn. (7)

**Rows 2–5:** sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (7)

**Row 6 (buttonhole):** sc 2, ch 2, skip 2, sc 3. (5 sc worked over the 7‑stitch span, with a ch‑2 buttonhole)

Fasten off, leaving a tail. The flap folds over the top of the case; mark where the buttonhole lands on the front and sew the button there through several stitches. Buttoned, it holds the case in for carrying; unbuttoned and folded back, it leaves the lid free.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/airpods-lid-clearance.svg" alt="Side view showing lid clearance. The sleeve rim ends just below the lid seam, so the case lid sits above the rim and still flips open. The flap folds over the top to carry and folds back to open the lid. The case is taken out to reach the charging port." width="480" height="470" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Stage 3. The rim stops below the lid seam so the lid clears it. The flap carries the case; it is not a charging door.</figcaption>
</figure>

### Stage 4: the ears

Make two. Each ear is a small triangle worked flat, losing a stitch at each edge until it points.

**Row 1:** ch 7, starting in the 2nd ch from the hook, sc 6. Ch 1, turn. (6)

**Row 2:** sc2tog, sc 2, sc2tog. Ch 1, turn. (4)

**Row 3:** sc2tog, sc2tog. Ch 1, turn. (2)

**Row 4:** sc2tog. (1)

Fasten off, leaving a long tail to sew with.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/airpods-ear.svg" alt="Cat ear worked flat as a triangle. Row 1 has 6 single crochet. Row 2 decreases one stitch at each edge to 4. Row 3 decreases to 2. Row 4 is a single decrease to 1, the point. Make two." width="480" height="470" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Stage 4. Each middle row loses one stitch at each edge, so the sides slope evenly to the point.</figcaption>
</figure>

### Stage 5: assembly and the cat face

Sew one ear near each end of the top front, angled slightly outward, using the long tails. With orange, embroider a small triangle nose near the center and a stitched mouth below it. With gold or white, work two eyes above the nose and a few straight whisker stitches to each side. Keep the face flat and sewn down; no plastic eyes, which can pull off and are a choking hazard around children and pets. For the carrying loop, ch 12, join into a ring with a slip stitch, and sew it to the back below the flap. Add a small pumpkin by the hem if you like: a few orange stitches gathered into a bump with a green top.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/airpods-assembly.svg" alt="Assembly. Two ears sit on the top front, one near each end of the oval. The flap rises from the back, folds over the top, and buttons to the front, with a buttonhole on its last row dropping over a sewn button. A carrying loop sits at the back. To charge, the case comes out." width="480" height="500" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Stage 5. Ears on the corners, face embroidered, flap over the back, loop behind. The front stays clear for the cat.</figcaption>
</figure>

![A black crochet AirPods case cozy with the plain charging case lifting out of the open top, showing the carrying loop and the covered port](../../assets/articles/halloween-crochet-airpods-case-1.jpg "Illustration. The open top lets the case lift straight out; the base covers the port, so the case comes out to charge.")

## Check the fit

Three checkpoints catch almost every problem. **The base** should lie flat with at most a gentle cup and sit close to the case bottom — straight sides along the width, ends around the thickness; a ruffle is too many increases. **The body** should hug the case with light tension; if the case drops in without touching the sides, go down two stitches (a shorter straight side) or a hook size. **The rim** should sit just below the lid seam so the lid opens without fighting the fabric. When all three are right, the buttoned flap holds the case for a bag and releases in a second to open.

## Make it your own

- **Other cases.** Use the table above: measure, find the perimeter, and set the base count with round 2 = 2 × straight + 12. The AirPods 4 case wants 22 stitches (ch 8), the AirPods 3 case 24 (ch 9).
- **Snug or roomy.** For a tighter grip, drop to the next even count down; for a looser slide, go one up. At four stitches per inch, two stitches change the perimeter estimate by half an inch.
- **Not a cat.** Skip the ears and face for a plain sleeve, or restyle the front as a pumpkin or ghost using the scraps. Color changes are worked the usual way; see [changing yarn color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/).
- **A sturdier loop.** For a keychain clip, work the loop as a short chain of single crochet instead of a plain chain so it does not stretch out.

## When it goes wrong

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/airpods-mistakes.svg" alt="Four common mistakes. A twisted oval from incorrect chain-loop placement, a ruffled wavy base from too many increases, a flap that jams the lid unless folded back, and a covered charging port that needs the case removed to charge." width="480" height="470" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The four problems that actually come up, and the fix for each.</figcaption>
</figure>

**The case will not go in.** The count is under the perimeter, or the fabric is too tight. Add two stitches to the base or go up a hook size.

**The case rattles and slips out.** Too much ease. Drop two stitches, or work the same count on a smaller hook.

**The base ruffles before the wall.** The increases are stacking up. They belong only at the two ends; the straight sides get one stitch each, no more.

**The base twists.** This oval is worked around a chain, not formed by joining a foundation-chain ring. Check that the return side uses the seven unused chain loops, that the skipped chain was left alone, and that no chain was worked twice outside its end group.

**The lid will not open.** The rim is too tall, or the flap is buttoned. Unbutton and fold the flap back; if the rim still covers the seam, your wall is a round or two too high for this case.

## Finishing and care

Weave the tails in on the inside and give the sleeve a light steam or press so the base sits flat. Take the case out before washing; hand wash cool and dry flat, since cotton can shrink and turn a snug cozy too small. Leave charging to the bare case — the base covers the port and wireless charging compatibility is unverified, so remove the case to charge.

## Fit the flap before making the buttonhole

The seven-stitch flap is a starting width, not a fit measurement for every case. Remove the case for sewing, but use it as a form while marking: center the flap behind the hinge and work plain rows until it crosses the lid and reaches the intended front button position without tension. Work the buttonhole row only then. Keep the ears out of that path. For a carrying loop, make a separate reinforced strip: ch 13, sc in the second chain and across (12), fasten off with tails and sew both ends to several back-wall stitches. No load rating is established.

## Frequently asked

**Can it charge while it is in the cozy?**

Remove it for charging. The base covers the wired port, and this sleeve has not been checked for wireless charging. Yarn alone does not establish whether wireless charging works.

**Will it fit AirPods Max or the older AirPods?**

No. This is sized for the small charging cases in the table. AirPods Max are over-ear headphones with a different case entirely, and very old cases have their own dimensions — measure and resize if yours is not listed.

**Is this an official Apple case?**

No. It is an independent handmade accessory. "AirPods" only describes which charging case it is cut to fit, and nothing here is made or endorsed by Apple.

**Do I need to swatch?**

Make the base and measure around its edge before you commit to the wall — that is your gauge check and it takes two rounds. If you want the longer explanation, see [crochet gauge](/crochet-gauge/).

## Related tutorials

For the plain version of this tube, see the round-pouch method in [how to crochet a case for any device](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/). For a spookier earbud case with wings, try the [gothic bat earbuds pouch](/gothic-bat-earbuds-pouch/). And for a flat sleeve that follows the same around-not-across rule, there is [how to crochet a phone case](/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case/).


# Halloween Crochet Card Wallet with Ghost (Easy Flap + Button)

Source: src/content/posts/halloween-crochet-card-wallet.md



A card wallet is one rectangle with three jobs. The bottom part folds up to become the front of the pocket. The middle is the back. The top is a flap that folds over and buttons. Once you see the rectangle as those three zones, you can size it for any card, and the same method works for a gift-card pocket, a transit-card holder, or a slim wallet for two cards and a license.

This page works the method through one example in pumpkin orange, with a black button and a small flat ghost on the flap. The ghost is decoration. The pocket is the skill.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/), and [basic stitches](/basic-crochet-stitches/) if rows are new. The pictures are illustrations. No wallet for this page was crocheted or fitted to a card.

![A close-up of a pumpkin-orange crochet card wallet with a black button closure and a small white ghost applique, held in front of warm Halloween candles and pumpkins](../../assets/articles/halloween-crochet-card-wallet-result.jpg "Illustration. A flap wallet with a button and a ghost.")


## How the rectangle becomes a wallet

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/card-wallet-layout.svg" alt="A rectangle 16 stitches wide in three zones: rows 1 to 12 are the front, folded up; rows 13 to 24 are the back; rows 25 to 31 are the flap, with a loop on row 31 and a ghost on the flap. Width is the card plus stack, ease, and seams, about 3.9 inches. The pocket is 12 rows, 2.4 inches deep." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Fold on the first dashed line to make the pocket. Fold on the second to close the flap.</figcaption>
</figure>

The foundation chain sets the width, and it does not change. The rows build the three zones one after another. After the last row, fold the front up against the back, seam the sides of the pocket only, and leave the flap free.

## Size it from your cards

Stack every card the wallet will hold and measure the stack. An ID-1 card is 0.030 inch thick, so two cards are about 0.06 inch and a license plus two cards about 0.09.

**Width.** The fabric has to cover the card's width and its stack thickness, plus some ease so the cards slide, plus the side seams, which take up about one stitch.

**Pocket depth.** The card's height plus a little ease. The flap covers the top, so the card can sit fully inside.

**Flap.** It has to fold over the top of the back, past the opening, and reach a button on the front. About 1.4 inches suits a 2.4 inch pocket.

## The worked example

Two ID-1 cards, at 4 stitches and 5 rows per inch.

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Width | 3.37 + 0.06 stack + 0.25 ease + 0.25 seams | 3.93 in, 16 sc, chain 17 |
| Pocket depth | 2.125 + 0.03 + about 0.25 ease | about 2.4 in, 12 rows |
| Front | same as the pocket | rows 1–12 |
| Back | same as the pocket | rows 13–24 |
| Flap | about 1.4 in | rows 25–31 |
| Loop row | 6 + 4 + 6 | sc 6, ch 4, skip 4, sc 6 |

## What you need

- Worsted yarn in **pumpkin orange**, plus scraps of **white** and **black**.
- A hook that gives firm fabric, often US G/6 (4.0 mm). A soft pocket lets cards slip.
- **One black button**, about 3/4 inch (20 mm) across.
- Yarn needle, clips or stitch markers.
- **The cards the wallet will hold.**

## Stage 1: chain and check

Chain 17. Sc in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Ch 1, turn. (16)

Lay a card on the first row. The fabric should be wider than the card by about the width of two stitches. If it is not, start again with more stitches or a larger hook. Measuring now saves seaming a pocket the card cannot enter.

<div class="tip">

**If the first row cups,** the chain was too tight. Redo it looser, or work the chain on a hook one size larger.

</div>

## Stage 2: the front and back

**Rows 2–24:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (16)

The ch 1 does not count as a stitch. Count every few rows.

**Checkpoint at row 24:** fold the first 12 rows up against the next 12. Slide the cards in. They should go in flat and sit just inside the opening. If the top of a card shows, both pocket zones need more rows. Undo to the planned fold, extend the front, then work an equally deep back before starting the flap. Adding rows only at the current end lengthens the back, not the front. Write down the new fold and flap-start row numbers.

![Hands crocheting a flat orange single-crochet rectangle that will become the card wallet body and flap](../../assets/articles/halloween-crochet-card-wallet-1.jpg "Illustration. One tall rectangle. The pocket and flap are both in it.")

## Stage 3: the flap

**Rows 25–30:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (16)

For a black stripe at the flap edge, [change color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/) on the last yarn-over of row 29 and work rows 30 and 31 in black.

**Row 31:** Sc 6, ch 4, skip 4, sc 6. (12 sc and a ch-4 loop)

Fasten off. Six, four skipped, and six is sixteen, so the loop sits centered and the edge stays the same width. Four skipped stitches span about an inch, which suits a 3/4 inch button. For a 1/2 inch button, work sc 7, ch 3, skip 2, sc 7.

## Stage 4: fold and seam

<figure class="diagram">
