<img src="/diagrams/folded-wallet-row-pairs.svg" alt="Front and back row ends are paired outward from the bottom fold." width="800" height="480" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Front and back row ends are paired outward from the bottom fold.</figcaption>
</figure>


Fold rows 1 to 12 up against rows 13 to 24. Clip the sides. Whipstitch both sides from the fold to the opening, through the outer loop of each edge stitch. **Stop at the opening.** Sewing into the flap makes a closed pouch. Add an extra stitch at the top of each seam, where cards pull. Weave the ends inside the seams, not loose in the pocket.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/card-wallet-fold.svg" alt="Drawn to scale. A flat piece 16 sc, about 4 in wide and 31 rows, 6.2 in tall, folded at the dashed line into a pocket with the flap above. Whipstitch both sides from the fold to the opening. Stop there. Front: rows 1 to 12. Back: rows 13 to 24. Flap: rows 25 to 31, about 1.4 in. The width includes about 0.25 in for the seams." width="480" height="577" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Measure the rectangle flat before seaming. It should be about 4 inches wide at the example gauge, or the card will not fit.</figcaption>
</figure>

![A tapestry needle whipstitching one side seam of the folded orange card wallet, stopping below the flap](../../assets/articles/halloween-crochet-card-wallet-4.jpg "Illustration. Seam the pocket sides only. Stop before the flap.")

## Stage 5: the button

Put the cards in. Fold the flap down and mark where the loop lands on the front. Sew the button there, **through the front wall only**. If the needle goes through both layers, the pocket is sewn shut at that point, and the cards hit the button's shank.

## Stage 6: a flat ghost

A ghost worked as a little tube flattens to about an inch and a half wide, which crowds a 4 inch flap. This one is flat and about an inch tall.

With white:

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. Slip stitch to the first sc. (6) This is the head, about half an inch across.

**Row 1:** Ch 1, sc in the next 3 stitches. Turn. (3)

**Row 2:** Ch 1, sc in each stitch. Turn. (3)

**Row 3:** Sl st in the first stitch, 3 hdc in the next, sl st in the last. (A wavy hem)

Fasten off with a tail for sewing. Embroider two French-knot eyes in black. Sew the ghost to the flap, to one side of the loop, so it shows when the flap is closed. Sew through the flap only.

## Fit check

Slide the cards in, close the flap, and tip the wallet upside down.

**Too tight:** next time add a stitch or two of width. **Too loose:** a round of slip stitch around the opening firms it. **Flap will not reach:** add flap rows before row 31. **The loop fights the button:** rework row 31 with one chain more or less.

## Same pocket, different face

![A set of Halloween crochet card wallets with ghost, pumpkin, bat, spider, and cat fronts laid out on a wooden table with pumpkins and candles](../../assets/articles/halloween-crochet-card-wallet-variations.jpg "Illustration. Same three-zone rectangle, different appliqués. Not made samples.")

| Look | Base color | Appliqué idea |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Ghost (this page) | Orange or black | The small flat ghost above |
| Pumpkin | Orange or gray | A 6-stitch circle in orange, a few black face stitches, a green scrap stem |
| Bat | Gray or black | Two small black triangles in rows for wings, a 6-stitch head |
| Spider | Gray or cream | A 6-stitch black circle with eight embroidered legs |
| Cat | Black | Two ear triangles at the flap edge, embroidered whiskers |

## Match the folded rows before sewing

For the 31-row example, front rows 1–12 fold against back rows 13–24. From the fold upward, pair row 12 with 13, row 11 with 14, continuing to row 1 with 24. Rows 25–31 remain outside the side seams as the flap. Mark each pair with a clip if the edges are hard to read. Work the little ghost with ch 1 that does not count, and sew it through the flap alone while the pocket is open. Check the button through its loop before permanently sewing the button to the loaded wallet's marked position.

## Troubleshooting

**Cards go in but bow.** The width only covers the card, not the stack. Add a stitch.

**The flap pops open.** Too many cards for the flap length, or a loop that is too long. Fewer cards, or one more flap row.

**The pocket sags open at the top.** Loose gauge. Go down a hook size and redo it.

**The ghost wrinkles the flap.** It is sewn too tightly, or it is too big. Sew it loosely through the back of its stitches.

**The seams pull open at the top.** One stitch at the stress point. Add a second.

## Frequently asked

**Will a license and two cards fit?**

At this size, by the arithmetic, yes. Stack yours and check before you seam.

**Can I skip the ghost?**

Yes. Orange and a black button are enough.

**Do I need to know amigurumi?**

No. The body is flat rows. The ghost is one round and three rows.

## Next

A fixed-count version with a deeper pocket that also takes a folded bill is the [ghost mini wallet](/ghost-crochet-mini-wallet/). A pocket with no flap is the [Christmas gift card holder](/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-gift-card-holder/). Five fixed-count designs, a black cat, ghost, pumpkin, witch hat, and spiderweb, are in the [Halloween crochet wallet collection](/halloween-crochet-wallets/).


# Halloween Crochet Wallets: 5 Free Card Wallet Patterns

Source: src/content/posts/halloween-crochet-wallets.md



A Halloween wallet is a quick project, a good gift, and a reason to use up the scraps of black, orange, and purple you bought last October. The trouble with most "wallet" patterns is that nobody checked whether a card goes in. These five were sized on paper against a standard bank card, and each one uses a different construction, so making all five teaches you five techniques rather than one rectangle five times.

![Five Halloween crochet coin purses: a black cat, a ghost, a jack-o'-lantern, a spiderweb, and a witch hat](../../assets/articles/halloween-crochet-wallets-thumb.png)

All five are written for worsted yarn and a US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook. A plain single-crochet swatch checks the gauge the cat, ghost, witch hat, and spiderweb assume. It does not prove a card will fit, and it does not measure the pumpkin, which is worked in the back loop only. Each pattern names the row where you slide a real card in.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/halloween-wallets-compare.svg" alt="Diagram. The five wallets side by side. Black cat: a seamless tube with ears and a snap. Ghost: two shaped panels joined by one edging round. Pumpkin: sideways ribs, folded, with a leaf tab. Witch hat: three zones, an inner divider, and a buttonhole. Spiderweb: a pointed flap with embroidery and a loop." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Five constructions. The pumpkin's ribs are back loop only, so its row gauge differs.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Pick a pattern

**[Black cat crochet wallet](/black-cat-crochet-wallet/).** Beginner. A seamless pocket worked in rounds around both sides of a chain, a 9-row flap with gold eyes, two triangle ears, and a snap.

![A black crochet cat pouch with pink inner ears, gold eyes, and a zipper](../../assets/articles/black-cat-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

**[Ghost crochet wallet](/ghost-crochet-wallet/).** Intermediate. Two shaped panels, a soft embroidered face with pink cheeks, and one edging round that joins the layers and scallops the hem. The snap hides inside.

![A cream crochet ghost pouch with a zipper, black eyes, and pink cheeks](../../assets/articles/ghost-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

**[Pumpkin crochet wallet](/pumpkin-crochet-wallet/).** Beginner. One sideways piece in back-loop ribs, folded and seamed, with a jack-o'-lantern face, a rolled stem, and a green leaf tab that snaps shut.

![An orange crochet pumpkin pouch with a green stem, a black face, and a zipper](../../assets/articles/pumpkin-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

**[Witch hat crochet wallet](/witch-hat-crochet-wallet/).** Intermediate. One purple rectangle with a worked buttonhole, an inner divider for two pockets, and a black and orange hat appliqué.

![A black crochet witch-hat pouch with a purple band, an orange buckle, and a zipper](../../assets/articles/witch-hat-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

**[Spiderweb crochet wallet](/spiderweb-crochet-wallet/).** Intermediate. A black wallet with a pointed flap, a backstitched white web, a tiny purple spider, and a button loop.

![A black crochet wallet with a white spiderweb, a small spider, and a side button](../../assets/articles/spiderweb-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

The diagrams further down are the counted construction.

## How they compare

| Wallet | Level | Outside size, about | Closure | New technique |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Black cat | Beginner | 4.0 x 2.4 in, plus ears | Snap | Working both sides of a chain |
| Ghost | Intermediate | 4.6 x 4.9 in | Hidden snap | Shaping and joining two layers with an edging |
| Pumpkin | Beginner | 4.25 x 2.5 in | Leaf tab and snap | Back-loop ribbing worked sideways |
| Witch hat | Intermediate | 4.25 x 2.4 in | Button | Buttonhole and a three-layer seam |
| Spiderweb | Intermediate | 4.25 x 2.6 in | Button loop | Backstitch embroidery |

Sizes are worked out from a gauge of 16 sc and 20 rows to 4 inches. The pumpkin's back-loop row gauge may differ, which is why that pattern works to a length instead of a fixed row count.

**What you need for all five.** Worsted weight (CYC 4) yarn in black, cream, orange, and purple, scraps of gold, pink, green, and white, a US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook, three 3/8 inch sew-on snaps, two 5/8 inch flat buttons, and a tapestry needle.

## Which one to make first

- **Never made anything but a swatch:** the pumpkin. Straight rows, one fold, two seams.
- **Comfortable with rounds:** the black cat. It has no seams on the pocket at all.
- **Want two pockets:** the witch hat. Cards on one side, cash on the other.
- **Like embroidery more than crochet:** the spiderweb. The crochet is plain; the web is drawn on.
- **Want the most character:** the ghost. It takes longest because of the joining round.

If you need a wallet for a specific number of cards, or want to work out your own size, start with the sizing method in the [Halloween card wallet](/halloween-crochet-card-wallet/) instead. For a smaller, flap-style ghost, see the [ghost mini wallet](/ghost-crochet-mini-wallet/).

Every pattern gives sizes worked out from the example gauge of 16 sc and 20 rows to 4 inches rather than from a finished wallet, so swatch first. A standard US bank card is 3.370 by 2.125 inches, the ISO/IEC 7810 ID-1 size, and each pattern tells you where to test the fit with a real card before you commit to the finishing. That test is the one step not to skip.

## Gauge, then a real card

Make a single crochet swatch about 5 inches square on the G/6 hook and measure the middle 4 inches, as in the [gauge guide](/crochet-gauge/). If you get 16 stitches and 20 rows, the cat, ghost, witch hat, and spiderweb are at the gauge those patterns assume. If you get 17 or 18 stitches, go up a hook size. If you get 14 or 15, go down one.

The pumpkin is a different fabric. It is worked in the back loop only, and its row gauge must be measured separately from plain single crochet. Make a second small swatch in back loops, and follow that pattern's instruction to work until the piece measures 4.25 inches.

A matching swatch only means the stitch size is in range. Seams, joins, and the flap still change the opening. Slide a real card in at the step each pattern names before you sew the closure.

Weigh the plain swatch too. Each pattern gives its fabric area as a multiple of a 4 inch square, which is how you estimate yarn without guessing.

## Choose the closure as well as the shape

The collection contains five featured designs, with two additional flap-wallet tutorials linked separately. None of the five is a sealed coin purse. For each project, check its own fabric: the pumpkin uses BLO rows, the cat uses rounds, and the other three use flat rows. All five together require three snaps and two buttons, plus appropriate sewing thread. Make one complete wallet before preparing a batch. Record the hook, measured gauge, actual pocket size, closure position, and card-stack fit so you can reproduce your own result rather than assume the illustration establishes those measurements.

## Mistakes these patterns share

**Skipping the card test.** Every pattern names a point to test. A pocket that is 0.2 inch too narrow will not stretch enough.

**Embroidering in the wrong orientation.** The cat and witch hat flaps are decorated with the flap closed. The ghost face goes on before the panels are joined.

**Sewing closures through both layers.** A snap or button sewn through the whole wallet stitches the pocket shut. Sew through one layer.

**Loose fabric.** Wallets need firmer fabric than a hat or blanket, which is why the hook is smaller than most worsted labels suggest.

## Frequently asked

**Are these patterns tested?** The counts and sizes are worked out from the example gauge rather than from a finished wallet, so each pattern gives you a point to test the fit with a real card as you work.

**Do they hold coins?** None of them is designed for loose coins. They are card sleeves with closures.

**Can I use acrylic yarn?** Yes. Cotton holds the shape better and shows stitch detail, but any worsted yarn that gets the gauge works.

**Can I sell what I make?** Yes, the finished wallets. Please link to the pattern rather than reposting it.

**Where do I learn the basics first?** Start with [basic crochet stitches](/basic-crochet-stitches/) and the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). Every pattern here uses US terms.

Pick the pattern that matches your skills, swatch the fabric that pattern actually uses, and test a real card where it tells you to. The rest is single crochet.


# Halloween Granny Square Variations

Source: src/content/posts/halloween-granny-square-variations.md



Halloween granny squares are ordinary granny squares with a deliberate palette: pumpkin orange, black, purple, yellow, and white. The stitch is the same cluster-of-double-crochet motif as the [Christmas granny squares](/christmas-granny-square-variations/). That page solves a different color problem: red and green need a light round between them or the boundary goes muddy. This page is high-contrast rings, including a yellow-orange-white candy-corn order. The stitch does not change. What changes is **when** you switch colors and **how** you keep the joins from looking muddy.

If you have not changed colors cleanly before, read [How to Change Yarn Color in Crochet](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/) first.

US terms throughout. The rounds are the standard granny square. The pictures are illustrations of the finished look, so treat the color layouts as a plan you can change to suit your yarn.

![A set of finished Halloween granny squares in candy-corn, orange, and dark seasonal colors](../../assets/articles/halloween-granny-square-variations-result.jpg "Illustration. The finished look these color variations build toward.")

## Starting every granny round consistently

After round 1, travel with slip stitches across the next two dc and into the next corner space. In that corner, ch 3 (counts as the first dc), then work 2 dc, ch 2, and 3 dc. This is the first complete corner. Work the other corners and side clusters as described, separating adjacent groups with ch 1, and join to the top of the beginning ch 3. When changing colors, fastening off and rejoining the new color in a corner is an alternative to traveling. Never make ch 3 plus three dc for the first half-corner: that would be four dc instead of three.

## What you need

- Worsted yarn in orange, black, purple, plus yellow and white for candy corn (scraps are fine)
- Hook matched to the yarn ([hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/), [yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/))
- Yarn needle

Abbreviations: [chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). Classic granny uses dc clusters and ch spaces.

## Step 1: classic granny move (one color)

![A hook working a cluster of three double crochets into the chain space of an orange granny square](../../assets/articles/halloween-granny-square-variations-1.jpg "Illustration. Step 1 — clusters go into the gaps, never into the stitch tops.")

**Rnd 1.** Ch 4, join to form a ring (or magic ring). Ch 3 (counts as dc), 2 dc in ring, ch 2, *3 dc in ring, ch 2; repeat from * twice more, join to top of beginning ch-3. (4 clusters, 4 corner spaces)

**Rnd 2.** Slip to next corner space (or start with a standing dc). *(3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in corner, ch 1; repeat from * around, join. (24 dc, 4 ch-2 corners, 4 ch-1)

**Later rounds.** Corners always get (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc). Each ch-1 space along the sides gets 3 dc, with ch 1 between groups. Some patterns leave out the ch 1. Either works, but use the same style on every square, or they will not match when you join them.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-side-space.svg" alt="One side of a granny square, round 3 worked over round 2. Correct: the side cluster of round 3 sits in the chain-1 space between round 2's two corner halves, with a chain-1 on each side of it. Common mistake: the cluster goes into the top of a double crochet instead. The gap closes, the side goes stiff, and the square starts to cup." width="480" height="721" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The one rule every variation on this page keeps: clusters go into spaces, never into stitch tops.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-square-anatomy.svg" alt="A granny square in three rounds. Corners are 3 double crochet, chain 2, 3 double crochet in the chain-2 space. Sides are 3 double crochet in each chain-1 space with chain 1 between groups. Round 1 has 1 cluster per side, round 2 has 2, round 3 has 3." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round N has N clusters along each side, counting the corner halves. Check it before each color change.</figcaption>
</figure>

Count before you change colors. A side with too few clusters is much easier to fix before the next color covers it.

## Step 2: candy-corn rings

![A flat granny square worked in concentric rounds of yellow, orange and white](../../assets/articles/halloween-granny-square-variations-2.jpg "Illustration. Step 2 — candy corn from the middle out: yellow, then orange, then white.")

Work each round in a different color: golden yellow in the center, then pumpkin orange, then soft white (or cream) on the outer rounds.

Change color on the join: finish the last yarn-over of the joining slip stitch (or the last dc) with the new color. Cut the old color if you will not need it soon; carry only when the next round needs it immediately and the carry can hide along a seam edge.

<div class="tip">

**Plan the stack before you hook.** Sticky note: R1 yellow, R2–3 orange, R4–5 white. Guessing mid-square is how you reverse the candy-corn order.

</div>

## Changing color at the join, step by step

Every variation on this page changes color between rounds, and the join is where a square either looks clean or shows a lump of the wrong color.

1. Work the round to the end in the old color, finishing with the last cluster.
2. Insert the hook into the top of the beginning ch 3 to join. Drop the old color to the back.
3. Pick up the new color and pull it through both loops on the hook. That slip stitch is now in the new color, so no old color shows above the join.
4. Slip stitch across to the next corner space in the new color, or cut the yarn and start with a standing double crochet in a corner. A standing start leaves no slip stitches across the top of the cluster, so the join is almost invisible.
5. Leave tails of about 6 inches on both colors. Weave each one into the round of its own color, so a black tail never crosses orange.

If the joins form a diagonal line up the square, start each new color in a different corner. That spreads them out.

## Step 3: spooky center, quiet border

![A granny square with a dark black center and bright pumpkin-orange outer rounds](../../assets/articles/halloween-granny-square-variations-4.jpg "Illustration. Step 3 — dark center, bright border. The outer rounds hide scrap yarn.")

Keep rounds 1–2 in black, then switch to orange or purple for outer rounds. The dark center reads as a void; the bright outer rounds keep a blanket from looking like a hole punched in fabric.

This variation is forgiving with scrap yarn because the outer rounds use the most yardage.

**Optional harder variation:** split each round on the diagonal, two sides orange and two black. The color changes happen halfway through two opposite corners. The step-by-step version is the half-and-half square in [Christmas granny square variations](/christmas-granny-square-variations/). Swap the colors and it works the same way.

**Optional denser coaster:** work dc in every stitch and chain-space around, still placing `(2 dc, ch 2, 2 dc)` or `(3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc)` in corners.

## Step 4: join squares into a set

![Two granny squares held edge to edge while a needle joins them stitch by stitch](../../assets/articles/halloween-granny-square-variations-3.jpg "Illustration. Step 4 — match the two edges stitch for stitch or the corners will drift.")

Block each square to the same measurement before joining — see [weaving in ends and blocking](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/). Join with slip stitch or whip stitch in black so the seams disappear into the darkest color.

For a small wall hanging, join three squares in a column with purple joins and add a black border round around the whole piece.

**How many squares.** Finish and block one square, measure it, and divide the size you want by that measurement. Four squares across and four down make a 16-square cushion front. A blanket is the same arithmetic with bigger numbers. Decide the count after the first square, not before, or the finished piece comes out an awkward rectangle.

**Mixing the variations.** Candy-corn, dark-center, and plain orange squares sit together as one set if they all finish on the same outer color. Edge them all in black, and the different centers read as a pattern rather than leftovers.

## Make one counted square before colorwork

Use the complete four-round square in the [granny-square tote tutorial](/how-to-crochet-a-granny-square-tote/) as your first swatch. Counts are 12, 24, 36, and 48 dc, including each round's beginning ch 3 as one dc. Each round has four ch-2 corners; the side ch-1 spaces total 0, 4, 8, and 12. Fasten off one color and rejoin the next in a corner if traveling slip stitches confuse you. Keep all four corners and all side spaces visible while counting. Apply the candy-corn sequence to those rounds; changing colors does not change the counts.

## Mistakes that muddy Halloween colorwork

**Changing color at the start of a round after the chain-up.** Change on the last yarn-over of the previous stitch or join.

**Too many colors in one small square.** Three is plenty.

**Tight chain spaces in black yarn.** Check drape in daylight.

**Skipping end-weaving.** Black ends that poke through orange show forever.

## Frequently asked

### Do I need glow-in-the-dark yarn?

No. Fun as a one-round accent; scratchy as the main fabric.

### Can I mix acrylic and cotton in one square?

For practice, yes. For a set you care about, keep fiber content consistent.

### What size should a coaster be?

Large enough for the mug base plus a little margin. Make one square, set the mug on it, then decide.

### What to make next

A [pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/), a [ghost](/crochet-ghost-amigurumi-for-beginners/), or the [Halloween card wallet](/halloween-crochet-card-wallet/).


# How to Crochet a Baby Blanket

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-baby-blanket.md



A baby blanket on this page is a rectangle of [half double crochet](/basic-crochet-stitches/), the same stitch as the [beginner scarf](/how-to-crochet-a-beginner-scarf/). The scarf can be an inch off and still be a scarf. A blanket planned at 30 inches can come out a different size after it is washed. The other rule is what you leave off the surface.

US terms. The chain-2 does not count. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). No blanket for this page was crocheted, washed, or measured.

![A folded cream, blush, and blue crochet blanket with a scalloped edge](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-baby-blanket-thumb.png)

<div class="warning">

Nothing on this page was safety-tested with a baby. Do not sew on buttons, beads, flowers, or ties. A piece that can come off does not belong on a blanket a child can mouth. Follow the yarn band for washing. The stitch counts below are planning arithmetic.

</div>


## Step 1: Write the size, then wash a swatch

Write the width and the length you want before you chain. Thirty inches wide and 36 inches long is the example. A toddler lap blanket is a different pair of numbers.

Use washable worsted in a light solid color so a dropped stitch shows. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/). Read the band. Work a swatch in half double, then wash and dry it the way the blanket will be washed. Cotton often grows. Some acrylic relaxes. Measure after it is dry.

You need two numbers from that dry swatch: stitches per inch, and rows per inch. They are not interchangeable.

## Step 2: Two widths for the same 30 inches

Planning gauge, unverified: **3 hdc = 1 inch** and **2.5 rows = 1 inch**, with a US H/8 (5.0 mm) or whatever hook matches the swatch. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/). [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/).

At 3 stitches per inch, 30 × 3 = **90 stitches**. The chain is 92, because you start in the third chain.

A second swatch, so the multiply is visible: 14 half doubles in 4 inches is 14 ÷ 4 = **3.5 stitches per inch**. Then 30 × 3.5 = **105 stitches**, and the chain is 107.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/blanket-width.svg" alt="Diagram of two cases for a 30 inch blanket. At 3 half doubles per inch the count is 90 and the chain is 92. At 3.5 per inch the count is 105 and the chain is 107." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Ninety stitches is only the first case. Use the washed swatch.</figcaption>
</figure>

Length uses rows per inch. At 2.5 rows per inch, 36 × 2.5 = **90 rows**. If the dry swatch is 3 rows per inch, 36 inches is 108 rows. The 90-row figure is not a partner to the 105-stitch figure. Each direction has its own multiply.

Yardage is the swatch scaled up. Weigh a known number of stitches, then scale to stitches times rows. Buy an extra skein of the same dye lot before you are close to the end. A new lot in the middle of a solid blanket shows as a stripe. This page does not name a yardage.

## Step 3: One stitch, the same count, a loose chain

The foundation chain on a blanket is long, and a tight chain makes the first edge shorter than the rows above it. Chain loosely, or use one hook size larger for the chain only, then return to the swatch hook for row 1.

These rows are the **90-stitch example**. Substitute your count.

**Row 1.** Chain 92. Half double in the third chain from the hook and in each chain across. (90)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/blanket-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 92. The 2 chains nearest the hook are skipped and the first hdc goes into the 3rd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 90 hdc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Chain 92 for 90 hdc at the example washed gauge. The 2 skipped chains do not count.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Row 2.** Chain 2, turn. Half double in the first real stitch and in each stitch across. (90)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/scarf-first-stitch.svg" alt="The chain-2 is turned and does not count. The first half double of the new row goes into the first real stitch, at the base of that chain." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>On 90 stitches a single gained stitch per row is easy to miss. The first hdc goes into the first real stitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

The chain-2 is not a stitch. [Turning chain](/crochet-turning-chain/). Repeat row 2 until the blanket matches the length you wrote down, measured flat, not stretched.

Checkpoint every 10 rows: the count is still 90, or your number, and the side is not walking. A blanket that gains a stitch on one edge becomes a trapezoid. It will not lie flat on a mattress. On a piece this wide you will not see the gain in the first foot. Count anyway.

Fasten off. Weave ends for several inches inside the fabric. A knot on the corner is a hard lump. [Weaving ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

## What stays off the blanket

No buttons, beads, sewn flowers, or long ties. Open mesh looks light and catches fingers. If you want a softer fabric, go up one hook size, wash a new swatch, and recount. Do not switch to a hole stitch to get drape.

## Finish and measure the washed rectangle

After reaching the planned length, fasten off with enough tail to weave through several stitch posts and reverse direction. Wash and dry according to the yarn label, then measure width and length again. Check each edge for protruding tails and loose stitches; no sewn decoration is added. This is a crochet blanket tutorial, not approval for infant sleep. Keep loose blankets out of an infant's crib or bassinet; use it only in an appropriate supervised setting. The [American Academy of Pediatrics safe-sleep guidance](https://www.healthychildren.org/English/ages-stages/baby/sleep/Pages/A-Parents-Guide-to-Safe-Sleep.aspx) explains a clear sleep space.

## Mistakes

**The first edge is tighter than the rest.** The chain was tight. The next blanket gets a looser chain or a larger hook for that chain only. Pulling the finished edge will not add the missing length.

**The sides bow outward.** The count crept up. Rip back to the last correct row.

**One skein looked like enough.** The band's yards were never multiplied by the stitch count. A second dye lot is now a stripe.

## Frequently asked

### Is the plain stitch enough for a newborn?

It is a solid fabric without holes and without sewn-on parts. That is the construction this page can stand behind. It is not a safety test. Use the yarn the band says you can wash, and skip anything that can come loose.

### Can I use single crochet?

Yes. It is denser, slower, and it uses a different gauge. Swatch single crochet, wash it, and recount. Do not reuse 90.

## Next

The small version of this rectangle, where an inch of error matters less, is the [beginner scarf](/how-to-crochet-a-beginner-scarf/). If the chain edge keeps losing the first stitch, the repair is the [turning chain](/crochet-turning-chain/) page, worked on a swatch before you chain 92.


# Easy Crochet Scarf for Beginners

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-beginner-scarf.md



A first scarf is a rectangle of [half double crochet](/basic-crochet-stitches/). The chain decides the width. The rows decide the length. Nothing is shaped, seamed, or fitted. If the edges slant, the stitch count changed.

US terms. US half double crochet is UK half treble. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). No scarf for this page was crocheted and measured.

![A rust crochet scarf coiled on a wood table](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-beginner-scarf-thumb.png)


## Step 1: Pick a width and a length with a tape

Decide the width you want against your coat, and the length from one end to the other the way you will wear it. Eight inches wide and 60 inches long is the example. A taller person, or someone who tucks the ends into a coat, needs a longer tape, not a longer guess.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/half-double-crochet.svg" alt="Diagram of half double crochet: yarn over before inserting, three loops on the hook, then one pull through all three." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Yarn over once before the hook goes in. Three loops, then one pull through all three.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Step 2: Turn a swatch into a stitch count

The planning gauge is **3 hdc = 1 inch** and **2.5 rows = 1 inch** in worsted with a US H/8 (5.0 mm) hook. It was not measured on a finished scarf. [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/). [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).

Work about 15 half doubles for several rows. Measure 10 stitches in the middle. Two cases:

- Those 10 stitches are 4 inches. Each stitch is 4 ÷ 10 = **0.4 inch**. An 8 inch width needs 8 ÷ 0.4 = **20 stitches**.
- The planning gauge holds: 8 × 3 = **24 stitches**.

Half double on this page starts in the third chain, so the chain is the stitch count plus 2. Twenty stitches start with chain 22. Twenty-four stitches start with chain 26.

The same 24 stitches are not 8 inches if your stitch is 0.4 inch wide. 24 × 0.4 = **9.6 inches**. That is the usual surprise: the pattern count was copied, and the scarf came out wide.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/scarf-width.svg" alt="Diagram comparing 24 half double crochet at 3 per inch, which is 8 inches, with the same 24 stitches at 0.4 inch each, which is 9.6 inches. An 8 inch scarf at that looser gauge needs 20 stitches." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Use the count from the swatch. Twenty-four is only the 3-per-inch case.</figcaption>
</figure>

Measure rows in that same swatch, separately. Stitches per inch and rows per inch are not the same number. A 60 inch scarf at 2.5 rows per inch is 60 × 2.5 = **150 rows**. If you get 3 rows per inch, 60 inches is 180 rows. Do not treat 150 as required.

## Materials

Worsted yarn with some drape. Acrylic and a washable wool blend both work if the swatch feels like a scarf and not a mat. Cotton is usually stiffer. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).

The hook is the one that made the swatch, not automatically an H. Yarn needle and a tape. Weigh the skein before you start and again when you stop. This page has no weighed yardage.

## Step 3: Keep the same count every row

Chain-2 does not count as a stitch. [Turning chain](/crochet-turning-chain/).

These rows are the **24-stitch example**. If step 2 gave you another number, use that number. The shape of the row does not change.

**Row 1.** Chain 26. Half double in the third chain from the hook and in each chain across. (24)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/hooded-scarf-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 26. The 2 chains nearest the hook are skipped and the first hdc goes into the 3rd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 24 hdc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Chain 26 for 24 hdc. The 2 skipped chains next to the hook are the turning chain, not a stitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Row 2.** Chain 2, turn. Half double in the first stitch, the one at the base of the chain, and in each stitch across. (24)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/scarf-first-stitch.svg" alt="Diagram of a row of stitches with a chain-2 at the end that is skipped. The first half double of the next row goes into the first real stitch." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The chain is turned out of the way. The first half double uses the first stitch of the row you just finished.</figcaption>
</figure>

Repeat row 2 until the scarf matches the tape. Count the stitches every 10 rows. You want 24, or your own number.

Checkpoint after row 2: if you have 25, the hook went into the turning chain. If you have 23, the first stitch was skipped. Fix it before the scarf is a wedge. One extra stitch every row, over 150 rows, is 149 extra stitches at the far end.

Fasten off. Weave the ends along the side for an inch or more, not straight out the short end. [Weaving ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

## A wider scarf, or stripes

Add stitches before you chain, using your own stitch width. At the planning gauge only, 30 stitches are 10 inches and the chain is 32. That is a preference, not a tested drape.

Change color on the last yarn-over of the last half double of a row, so the new color starts cleanly on the next row. [Changing color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/).

## Make a short sample with finished edges

Before the full scarf, work four rows at your chosen width and count every row. The 24-stitch example starts with ch 26; row 1 begins in the third chain. Every subsequent row starts with ch 2 that does not count and hdc into the first real stitch. Mark both end stitches so the final hdc does not go into the turning chain. Measure width once this sample lies flat. For yarn planning, weigh a washed sample of known area and scale to the chosen scarf area, leaving extra for swatching and tails. Do not wait until the last skein to check supply.

## Mistakes

**The long edges slant.** The count is changing at the turn. Pick up the rule in the diagram and count.

**The scarf is stiff.** The hook is small for the yarn, or the fiber has no give. Go up a hook and remeasure. Do not steam acrylic with an iron. The yarn band is the heat limit.

**The ends pop out.** They were woven straight off the short edge. Take them back along the side.

## Frequently asked

### Is half double harder than single crochet?

It is one yarn-over before the insert, then one pull through three loops. The stitch is taller than single crochet, so a scarf needs fewer rows for the same length. Recalculate if you switch stitches. Do not keep the half-double numbers.

### Do I block it?

Only if the edges curl and the yarn band allows water or steam. A hot iron can flatten acrylic for good.

## Next

A band that goes around the head, with the chain and the rows swapped in meaning, is the [ear warmer](/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer/). The same rectangle, made much wider, is the [baby blanket](/how-to-crochet-a-baby-blanket/). A hood on the scarf is a different fold, and the old hood counts on that page do not fit an adult: [how a hooded scarf is folded](/how-to-crochet-a-hooded-scarf/).


# How to Crochet a Bowl Cozy

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-bowl-cozy.md



A bowl cozy is a cotton square made of two layers, with the four corners sewn so the square can stand up around a bowl. The stitches are ordinary [single crochet](/basic-crochet-stitches/). The part that needs thinking is the square itself: how wide it has to be, how to join the layers without a ruffle, and how long a corner pinch can be before the bowl no longer sits flat.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No cozy for this page was crocheted, fitted on a bowl, or held against heat. The 12 inch square below is arithmetic for one example bowl. It is not a size to copy onto a different bowl.

![A pink and cream crochet bowl cozy with side handles around a white bowl](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-bowl-cozy-thumb.png)

<div class="warning">

Cotton only. Acrylic can melt and can burn. Do not use metal thread, metallic yarn, wire, sequins, or metal buttons. Do not put this cozy in a microwave, on a stove, or in an oven. It has not been heat-tested, and it is not protection from a hot bowl. A thin cotton layer can scorch. A bowl that is too hot to hold can burn you through it.

</div>


## Step 1: Measure three places on the bowl

![A tape laid across a plain bowl](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-bowl-cozy-2.jpg "Illustration. Measure your bowl. The marks in the drawing are not the example size.")

Write down three inches, not two:

1. Rim diameter, across the top, outside the bowl.
2. Wall height, from the table to the rim.
3. Base diameter, where the bowl actually sits on the table.

The rim and the base are different on a bowl that flares. The square formula below uses the rim. The pinch check later uses the base. If you skip the base, you can sew a cozy that looks large enough and still rocks.

The planning side is the rim plus the wall twice. Treat that as a starting estimate for the flat square, not a formula that fits every bowl. A flare, a thick join, and the pinches all change the finished shape. The middle of the square is standing in for the bottom. Each side of that middle has to climb a wall. The corners of a square stick out past a round bowl, and those corners are what you will pinch.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bowl-cozy-plan.svg" alt="Diagram of a square whose side is the rim diameter plus the wall height on the left and again on the right. The corners are marked as extra fabric to pinch. The example is 6 plus 3 plus 3 equals 12 inches." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Planning sketch. The dashed middle is the rim diameter, not the base. The corners are surplus.</figcaption>
</figure>

Example, for a bowl **6 inches across the rim and 3 inches tall**. The base is not in that sentence, so this example cannot prove a fit.

6 + 3 + 3 = **12 inch** side.

At the unverified gauge of 4 single crochet and 5 rows to the inch:

- 12 × 4 = **48 stitches**
- The chain is 49, because you single crochet into the second chain and the chain-1 turn does not count
- 12 × 5 = **60 rows**

Twelve inches is that multiplication. It is not a cozy anyone has set a bowl into. A wider rim or a taller wall replaces the 6 and the 3 before you chain anything.

## Step 2: Swatch and turn inches into stitches

**The example gauge is 4 sc = 1 inch and 5 rows = 1 inch.** It was not measured on a finished cozy. [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/) is the whole difference between a 12 inch square and a square that misses the rim.

Chain about 20, work single crochet for about 10 rows with the cotton and the hook you will use, then measure the middle. If 4 stitches are not 1 inch, do not keep the 48. Multiply 12 by your own stitches per inch, and multiply 12 by your own rows per inch. A hook change is the usual fix when the stitch width is off. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).

Checkpoint before the real square: you can state your stitches per inch and your rows per inch from a swatch, and both numbers came from the middle of the fabric, not from a stretched edge.

## Materials

![Cream cotton yarn, a hook, and a plain bowl](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-bowl-cozy-1.jpg "Illustration. Cotton only. Yardage is not a weighed cozy.")

- Worsted **cotton**. Read the label. If it says acrylic, a blend, metallic, or it does not say cotton, use it on a different project. Acrylic can melt. Metallic thread can get hot. Wool can scorch. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- The hook that produced your swatch. The example hook is a US G/6 (4.0 mm) only if that hook actually gives you 4 single crochet to the inch.
- Yarn needle and scissors. If you mark a corner, take the marker out when you finish. Metal does not belong in the cozy. No buttons, beads, wire, or sequins.

The example stitch count is 48 × 60 = **2,880 single crochets in one square**, and **5,760 in two**. The joining round adds more, about 200 if you follow the 4-for-5 rate below (4 sides × 48, plus 3 stitches in each of 4 corners). That is a count, not a yardage. Weigh the yarn in your swatch, count the stitches in that swatch, and scale up. An older line on this page said about 300 yards. That figure was not weighed off a cozy. Do not shop from it.

The fabric is solid single crochet. Some drawings of this shape show holes. Those holes are not in these rows.

## Step 3: Make two squares that match

Chain-1 does not count as a stitch. Put the first and last stitch of every row in the last real stitch, not in the turning chain. If the count drifts, the [turning chain](/crochet-turning-chain/) is the usual place it happens.

These rows are the **12 inch example**. If your bowl or your swatch changed the count, use your count. The shape of the row does not change: one single crochet in each stitch, same number every row.

Chain 49.

**Row 1:** Single crochet in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Chain 1, turn. (48)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bowl-cozy-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 49. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 48 sc." width="480" height="439" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Chain 49 for 48 sc, 12 inches at 4 sc per inch. Make two squares from the same count.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rows 2–60:** Single crochet in each stitch across. Chain 1, turn. (48)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Two squares only match if both keep 48. The first stitch of each row goes in the edge stitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

Checkpoint every 10 rows: the row still has 48 stitches, and the piece is still a rectangle. At the example row gauge, 10 rows are 2 inches and 60 rows are 12. If your rows per inch are different, 60 rows will not match the width. Stop at your own row count, then measure both sides. They should match each other before you join.

Make the second square to the same count. If it comes out wider, the tension changed or a row gained a stitch. Rip it back. A lopsided pair gathers into a cozy that tips. One square is not a shortcut. A single layer gathers and flops. Two layers make the fabric thicker. They do not make it a hot pad.

## Step 4: Join so the sides do not ripple

![Two cream squares stacked, edges aligned](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-bowl-cozy-3.jpg "Illustration. Both squares need the same stitch count before the join.")

Hold the squares together, wrong sides in, if you can tell a wrong side. Join the cotton at a corner. Single crochet through both layers.

Along the top and the bottom, put one single crochet in each stitch. That is 48 on the example.

Along the sides, do not put one single crochet in every row end. The example has 5 rows in the same inch that holds only 4 stitches. One stitch per row end puts **60** stitches on an edge whose top has **48**. Those extra 12 stitches have nowhere to go, so the side is likely to ripple. Matching the counts removes that difference on paper. It does not promise a smooth join in yarn, because a row end is shaped differently from the top of a stitch. If you put in too few, the side can pull in and the square can cup. Look at the square after the join and move stitches if it waves.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bowl-cozy-join.svg" alt="Diagram comparing 48 single crochets along the top of a 12 inch square with 60 single crochets if every row end is used. Four single crochets for every five row ends brings the side back to 48." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Worked for the example gauge only. The equal count is arithmetic, not a promise that the join lies flat. Recount the groups if your swatch is not 4 stitches and 5 rows to the inch.</figcaption>
</figure>

The rate is 4 single crochets for every 5 row ends. The example side has 60 row ends, and 60 ÷ 5 = 12 groups, and 12 × 4 = 48. Mark every fifth row end and work four single crochets before the next mark.

In each corner, work 3 single crochet so the join can turn. Those three are the corner. They are not part of the 48.

Checkpoint after the join: each side, between corner stitches, has the same count, and the square still measures the same on the width and the height. Fasten off, weave the ends, and look through the fabric for a marker or a needle. Then look again.

## Step 5: Pinch with the bowl sitting in the square

Put the bowl in the center while the square is still flat. You are trying to get four walls and a flat bottom the base can sit on.

At each corner, pinch the two edges together so they lie on top of each other, starting at the corner and running toward the middle of the side. Sew through both layers of both edges.

A pinch removes fabric from the flat center. For a first look, the flat center left on one side is about the side length minus one pinch at each end.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bowl-cozy-pinch.svg" alt="Diagram of a square with a pinch at each corner. The flat center is about the side minus two pinches. The example is 12 minus 2 minus 2 equals 8 inches." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The 8 inches is arithmetic from a 2 inch pinch. It is the check against the base, not a length to sew without the bowl.</figcaption>
</figure>

On the example, a 2 inch pinch gives 12 − 2 − 2 = **8 inches** of planning center. Two inches was an old estimate taken from a 3 inch wall. Set next to the wall, it is already shorter than the wall, so the rim can stand above the fabric. Set next to an unknown base, 8 inches may be wider than the base, or it may be narrower. If the base is wider than the flat center, the bowl can sit on the darts and rock. If the pinch is shorter than the wall, the rim can end up outside the cozy. Neither result has been sewn and checked. Both can be true for this example, which is why **2 inches is not an instruction**.

Sew two opposite corners first, set the bowl in, and adjust the last two. If the bowl will not sit down, the pinches are too long. Take one out. If the rim is above the fabric, the pinches are too short, or the square was too small before you sewed. Shortening a pinch gives the center back. It does not add yarn to a square that was never tall enough. In that case, undo the pinches and add rows.

![A corner pinched and sewn so the side stands up](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-bowl-cozy-4.jpg "Illustration. The pinch length comes from your bowl. Two inches is the example that fails the 3 inch wall.")

The bottom should stay wide enough for the base you measured in step 1.

![The cozy on a table with a plain bowl set inside](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-bowl-cozy-5.jpg "Illustration. A table holder in the drawing. Heat behavior was not tested.")

## Heat

Do not microwave this cozy. Do not use it as a pot holder, an oven mitt, or a trivet for a bowl you cannot hold with your bare hands. Cotton can scorch and can burn. Acrylic, if someone substitutes it, can melt onto skin. A metal stitch marker, a needle, or metallic thread does not belong in the fabric.

Nothing on this page is evidence that the cozy protects you from a hot dish. Two layers are not that evidence. If the bowl is too hot to hold, leave it on a trivet you already trust and wait. The cozy is a shape for a bowl that is cool enough to handle.

## Another bowl

1. Measure rim, wall, and base.
2. Side = rim + wall + wall.
3. Multiply by your stitches per inch and your rows per inch.
4. Make two squares. Join them at the rate your gauge requires, so each side gets the same number of stitches the top got.
5. Pinch with the bowl in the square until the walls clear the rim and the base still sits on flat fabric.

The 12 inch example is step 2 for a rim of 6 and a wall of 3. It never had a base measurement, so it stops being useful at step 5.

## Baste the darts before making permanent seams

Label the four corners A–D. At A, fold the corner diagonally so its two adjacent outer edges meet. Mark the same measured distance from the corner along both edges, then baste the paired edges from the corner to the marks. Repeat at opposite corner C and set the empty, cool bowl inside. Adjust both equally until its base rests flat. Then baste B and D and try again. Only replace basting with permanent stitches after the bowl sits without rocking. Do not trim away the dart fabric during fitting. This is a shaped holder for a cool bowl, not tested heat protection.

## Mistakes

**The side ripples after joining.** The row ends got more stitches than the top. On the example gauge, that means you used one stitch per row instead of four stitches per five row ends. Take the join out and recount.

**The second square is wider.** A row gained a stitch, usually at the start. Count 48, or your own number, at the end of every tenth row.

**The bowl rocks.** The flat center is smaller than the base. The pinches are too long. Undo one pair and try a shorter pinch with the bowl sitting there.

**The rim sticks out.** The pinch is shorter than the wall, or the square was short to begin with. A shorter pinch will not add height. Add rows if the flat square, before any pinch, is already shorter than rim + wall + wall at your gauge.

**Acrylic, a blend, or metallic thread.** Acrylic can melt. Metal does not belong in this project. If the skein is not cotton, choose a different project.

**One layer.** It gathers and flops. Make two squares.

**A metal marker left in the corner.** Take it out after the sewing. Metal does not belong in this object.

**Using it on a bowl you cannot hold.** That is a burn risk. The thickness of the cotton is not a heat test.

## Care

Wash off food. Hand wash cool. Dry flat, fully, before it is used again. Do not iron it. Do not store it wet. The heat warning still stands after washing.

## Frequently asked

### Can I microwave it?

No. This page does not recommend microwaving the cozy. It has not been heat-tested, and cotton can scorch. Use the cozy only with a bowl that is already cool enough to hold.

### Why not acrylic if that is the yarn I have?

Acrylic can melt. Use it for a different project. This one is cotton.

### Will the 12 inch square fit my bowl?

Only as a paper plan for a rim of about 6 inches and a wall of about 3 inches, and only after your swatch matches the example gauge. It still ignores the base. Measure all three and redo the side. Do not sew the 2 inch pinch just because the example used it.

### Why not one single crochet in every row end?

On the example gauge the side would have 60 stitches and the top would have 48. The difference is what ruffles the join. Match the stitch count, then look at the square.

## Next

A cotton wrap for a cup you have measured, with the same rule that the cup decides the size, is the [tumbler sleeve](/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve/). If the row count keeps changing, the fix is the swatch in [crochet gauge](/crochet-gauge/), worked before the 60 rows and not after.


# How to Crochet a Cable Organizer

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-cable-organizer.md



A charging cable in a bag turns into a knot because nothing holds the coil. A buttoned strap does. This is about the smallest useful thing you can crochet, and it still has one measurement that decides whether it works: the distance around the coiled cable where the strap will sit. Make the strap to a fixed row count and it will either not reach the button or flap loose around a thin cable.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No strap for this page was crocheted or buttoned around a cable. The 4 inch coil in the example is invented so the arithmetic has numbers.

![An oatmeal crochet strap around a coiled white cable](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-cable-organizer-result.jpg "Illustration. A buttoned strap around one coil. Not a strap measured on a cable.")


## How the strap works

The strap is a narrow rectangle in rows. A button is sewn near the first row. The last row has a buttonhole made by chaining over skipped stitches. Wrapped around the coil, the buttonhole meets the button.

So the length is not a style choice. The distance from the button to the buttonhole has to equal the distance around the coil. Everything else on the strap is a small margin at each end. The earlier version of this page gave 36 rows with the button 4 rows up. That puts 32 rows, about 6.4 inches, between them. It fits a fat laptop coil and leaves a loose loop around a thin phone cable.

## Measure the coil

Coil the cable the way it already wants to sit. Forcing it the other way puts a twist in it, and the strap will hold that twist just as well.

Wrap a strip of paper around the bundle where the strap will go, snug but not squeezing. Mark the overlap and measure the strip flat. In the example, it is **4 inches**.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cable-strap-length.svg" alt="A coiled cable with a paper strip around the bundle, 4 inches in the example. Below, the strap laid flat with a button 3 rows from the start and a buttonhole on the last row. The distance between them is 4 inches. The total is 0.6 plus 4 plus 0.2, which is 4.8 inches, or 24 rows at 5 rows per inch." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The strap's working length is the button-to-buttonhole distance. The rest is margin.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Yarn and hook

![Oatmeal yarn, a hook, and a wood button](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-cable-organizer-2.jpg "Illustration. Yarn, hook, and button.")

- **Smooth worsted cotton**, from scraps. It is firm and does not stretch much once buttoned. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or one that makes firm fabric. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **A button about 1/2 inch across.**
- Yarn needle, scissors.

Crochet three or four rows and measure stitches and rows per inch. The example uses 4 stitches and 5 rows per inch.

## The worked example

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Width | 2 in × 4 sts | 8 sts, chain 9 |
| Margin below the button | 3 rows | 0.6 in |
| Button to buttonhole | the coil measurement | 4 in, 20 rows |
| Buttonhole row | 1 row | 0.2 in |
| Total | 0.6 + 4 + 0.2 = 4.8 in | 24 rows |
| Buttonhole | 3 + 2 + 3 | sc 3, ch 3, skip 2, sc 3 |

The buttonhole spans 2 skipped stitches, about half an inch at this gauge, to suit a half-inch button. For a larger button on the same eight-stitch strip, work sc 2, ch 4, skip 3, sc 3; test before fastening off.

Why 8 stitches wide: in worsted cotton, a strip much narrower than 2 inches tends to roll into a cord. If you want a narrow strap, use a finer yarn and keep the stitch count.

## Stage 1: the strip

Chain 9.

**Row 1:** Sc in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Ch 1, turn. (8)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cable-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 9. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 8 sc." width="480" height="439" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Chain 9 for an 8-stitch strap, about 2 inches wide at 4 sc per inch.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rows 2–23:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (8)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>A narrow strap shows a gained stitch at once. Start each row in the edge stitch, not the ch 1.</figcaption>
</figure>

The ch 1 does not count as a stitch. Count the eight stitches every few rows. A ninth makes the end wider than the buttonhole row.

**Checkpoint at row 20:** wrap the strip around the coil. The far end should reach about 0.6 inch past where row 3 lands. If it does not, keep going. If it overshoots, stop early.

## Stage 2: the buttonhole

**Row 24:** Sc 3, ch 3, skip 2, sc 3. (6 sc and a ch-3 space)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cable-step-2.svg" alt="Buttonhole row over 8 stitches: sc 3, ch 3 over 2 skipped stitches, sc 3. Rows 1 to 23 are 8 sc. The button goes on row 3, centered." width="480" height="415" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. The ch 3 spans 2 skipped stitches. One extra chain keeps the hole from pinching the button.</figcaption>
</figure>

Fasten off. Three plus two skipped plus three is eight, so the end keeps its width, and the chain lies flat across the gap.

## Stage 3: the button

Sew the button on row 3, centered across the eight stitches, through several stitches. Wrap the strap around the coil and button it. The button should meet the buttonhole without stretching the strap and without bending the cable tighter than it sat.

## Optional: keep the strap on the cable

A strap that comes off every time you use the cable gets lost. To keep it attached, chain 10 with a new length of yarn and sew both ends to the back of the strap near row 1. Pass one end of the cable through that small loop before you plug it in. The strap stays on the cable when you unbutton it.

## Measure between the closures, not the strip ends

Place a removable marker at row 3 where the button will sit. Wrap the finished strip around the relaxed coil and mark where that location meets the buttonhole end; do not stretch the cable or compress it to force a match. The 24-row example is only a starting count. For a three-stitch buttonhole on eight base stitches, use sc 2, ch 4, skip 3, sc 3: 2 + 3 + 3 = 8 base positions. Test the button and adjust the chains before fastening off. A retaining loop must be large enough for the connector; do not force a plug through a fixed ten-chain loop.

## Troubleshooting

**It will not reach the button.** The coil is fatter than the strip. Add rows before the buttonhole row.

**It buttons loose and the coil slides out.** Too long for the coil. Pull out rows and rework the buttonhole row.

**The strip rolls into a tube.** Too narrow for the yarn, or the tension is tight. Add two stitches or go up a hook size.

**The button pushes through the hole.** The hole is too big for the button. Chain fewer, or skip fewer.

**The end flares at the buttonhole.** The chain is longer than the skipped stitches can hold flat. Chain one fewer.

## Use and care

One cable per strap. Two cables in one strap make a tangle inside the strap. Leave the plug ends outside so you can see which cable it is. To tell straps apart, tie a short tail of colored yarn through the button end. Hand wash cool, dry flat, and button it only when dry.

## Frequently asked

**Will this fit a laptop charger?**

The cable, yes, once you measure that coil. Laptop cables are thicker, so the coil is fatter and the strap longer. Do not strap the power brick. It makes a lump that will not button.

**Does it stop the cable twisting?**

No. You still coil it by hand. The strap holds the coil you made.

**Can I sell them?**

Finished straps, yes. Do not republish the rows.

## Next

A pouch for the earbud case that goes with the cable is the round-pouch method in the [device case guide](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/). The same buttonhole on a wider flap closes a [strap bag](/how-to-crochet-a-strap-bag/).


# How to Crochet a Christmas Gift Card Holder

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-gift-card-holder.md



A gift card in a plain envelope looks like an afterthought. A small crochet pocket gives it a front. It also has to do two practical things: let the card in without bending it, and let the person you give it to get the card back out. The first is about width. The second is about where you fold, and that is the part most card-pocket patterns get wrong.

Most US gift cards follow the ID-1 size used for bank cards: **85.60 × 53.98 mm**, which is **3.370 × 2.125 inches**, under ISO/IEC 7810. Some store cards are smaller. Measure the card you are giving.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No pocket for this page was crocheted or fitted to a card.

![A green crochet pocket with a blank card and a pine sprig](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-gift-card-holder-result.jpg "Illustration. A green pocket with a red edge and a blank card. Not a pocket fitted to a card.")


## How the pocket is built

One rectangle of single crochet, 16 stitches wide and 24 rows long. It is folded up from the bottom so the first 9 rows become the front and the remaining 15 rows become the back. The sides of the front are sewn to the back. The top of the front stays open. A row of red along the front edge is the only decoration.

### Why fold off center

The earlier version of this page folded the rectangle in half, 12 rows to a side. At 5 rows per inch that is a pocket 2.4 inches deep. A 2.125 inch card drops right in and sits about a quarter of an inch below the opening, with nothing to grab. The page then said the card should "stop before it disappears", which it would not do.

Folding after row 9 makes the front 1.8 inches tall, shorter than the card, so about a third of an inch of card stands above it. The back is 15 rows, 3.0 inches, so it rises behind the card and gives the front of the gift something to frame.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gift-card-offset-fold.svg" alt="The flat rectangle, 16 stitches by 24 rows, with a fold after row 9. Folded, the front is 9 rows or 1.8 inches, the back is 15 rows or 3.0 inches, and a 2.125 inch card shows about a third of an inch above the red front edge. A note says folding in the middle gives 2.4 inches per side and the card sinks 0.27 inch below the opening." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The front is shorter than the card on purpose. The visible strip is how the card comes out.</figcaption>
</figure>

### Why 16 stitches

Sixteen stitches at 4 per inch is 4 inches. Whipstitched side seams take up roughly one stitch, leaving about 3.75 inches inside for a 3.37 inch card. That is about three-eighths of an inch of ease, enough for the card to slide in flat. If your gauge is 5 stitches per inch, 16 stitches is 3.2 inches and the card will not go in at all. Keep the count and go up a hook size.

## Yarn and hook

![Green and red yarn, a hook, and a blank card](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-gift-card-holder-1.jpg "Illustration. The card is a size check.")

- **Worsted cotton** in green, and a scrap of red. Cotton keeps the pocket flat. Fuzzy yarn catches the card's smooth face. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives 4 stitches per inch. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- A gift card, or a piece of card stock cut to 3.370 × 2.125 inches.
- Yarn needle, scissors.

## The worked example

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Width | 16 sts ÷ 4 per in | 4.0 in fabric, about 3.75 in inside |
| Front | 9 rows ÷ 5 per in | 1.8 in |
| Card showing | 2.125 − 1.8 | about 0.33 in |
| Back | 15 rows ÷ 5 per in | 3.0 in |
| Body stitches | 16 × 24 | 384 sc |

## Stage 1: the rectangle

Chain 17.

**Row 1:** Sc in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Ch 1, turn. (16)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gift-card-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 17. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 16 sc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Chain 17 for 16 sc, about 4 inches wide.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rows 2–24:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (16)

The ch 1 does not count as a stitch. Count every few rows. A 15 or a 17 makes the sides bow, and the seams will not line up.

**Checkpoint:** after row 4, lay the card on the fabric. The fabric should be wider than the card by about the width of two stitches.

Fasten off.

## Stage 2: the red edge

The foundation chain becomes the front edge. With red, join in the first chain and work 1 sc in each of the 16 chains along the foundation. Fasten off.

Doing this before the fold keeps the edge stitches out of the side seams. It also firms the front edge, which takes all the wear from the card going in and out.

## Stage 3: fold and seam

Fold the rectangle so the foundation edge sits 9 rows up, with the right sides out. The fold is the bottom. The 9-row front lies against rows 10 to 18 of the back. Rows 19 to 24 stand above it.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gift-card-step-2.svg" alt="Drawn to scale. A flat piece 16 sc, 4 in wide and 24 rows, 4.8 in tall, folded at the dashed line into a pocket. Right sides out. Seam both sides through both layers for the 9 front rows. Front: rows 1 to 9, 1.8 in. Back: rows 10 to 24, 3.0 in. A 2.125 in card shows about 0.33 in above the front." width="480" height="506" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. Drawn to scale. The fold is off center: 9 rows in front, 15 behind, so the card shows above the front.</figcaption>
</figure>

Slide the card in before you sew. The red sc edging adds height beyond the nine-row front, so the exposed card margin will be smaller than the plain-front estimate. Check the actual card: enough must remain visible to grip it. If almost none shows, reduce the front to eight rows by shifting the fold one row toward the foundation edge. The longer back does not need to be shortened.

Whipstitch each side from the fold to the top of the front, through both layers and through the outer loop of the edge stitches only. A seam deeper in the stitch narrows the pocket. Make a second stitch at the top of each seam, where the card puts the most strain. Weave the ends in on the inside of the back, not inside the pocket.

## Pair the nine front rows with the back

With a fold between rows 9 and 10, pair row ends 9/10, 8/11, 7/12, 6/13, 5/14, 4/15, 3/16, 2/17, and 1/18. Sew only those nine pairs on each side. Rows 19–24 stay exposed above the opening. Along the foundation, work the red edge into the sixteen chain positions already used by row 1, not the skipped chain nearest the hook. The red edging adds to front height, so test the card's visible margin after edging and before seaming. Adjust the fold if the recipient cannot grip the card.

## Variations

**A hanging loop.** Chain 12 at the center top of the back, slip stitch back to make a loop, and hang the pocket on the tree with the card in it.

**Embroidery.** The back stands 0.875 inch above the card. A small star in backstitch fits there. Keep the stitches shallow.

**No red.** The pocket still fits without the edge, but the foundation chain will wear faster.

## Troubleshooting

**The card will not go in.** The fabric is tighter than 4 stitches per inch, or the seams went too deep. Go up a hook size and keep 16 stitches.

**The card disappears into the pocket.** The front is taller than the card. Refold lower.

**The card falls out when the pocket is tipped.** The pocket is loose, or the front is very short. Make the next one with one fewer stitch, or fold one row higher.

**The sides bow outward.** The stitch count drifted. Count back to where it was 16.

**The red edge ruffles.** You worked more than one stitch in some chains. Sixteen chains, sixteen stitches.

## Giving it

Tuck the pocket under the ribbon on a wrapped gift, or hand it over on its own. Yarn does not protect a card from bending. Do not fold the pocket with the card inside. One card per pocket. A second card stretches the seams.

Write the amount on a separate note. Embroidering a dollar figure makes the pocket single-use.

## Frequently asked

**Will a credit card fit?**

Yes, it is the same ID-1 size. This is still a gift pocket. It is not a daily wallet.

**What about a smaller store card?**

Measure it. If it is shorter than 1.8 inches, it will vanish into this pocket. Fold after row 7 or 8 so the front stays shorter than the card.

**Can I sell finished holders?**

Yes. Do not republish the rows.

## Next

A card pocket with a flap and a button is the [Halloween card wallet](/halloween-crochet-card-wallet/), which also shows how to size a pocket from any card. December yarn choices are in [yarn for Christmas crochet](/yarn-for-christmas-crochet/).


# How to Crochet a Christmas Tree, Step by Step

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-tree.md



A tree for a mantel has to stand. A tree for a garland has to lie flat. This page gives three builds, and one rule that controls the shape of two of them: **how fast you add or remove stitches sets the slope.** Add stitches slowly and the cone is slim. Add them quickly and it is squat. Add six a round and it lies flat as a coaster.

A standing cone is worked from the tip down, so it gets wider as you go, and its flat base is made last. Some patterns start at the base and increase upward, which makes a cup that is widest at the top.

US terms. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations of the finished tree, which has not been made up in yarn here, so no finished size is given.

![A finished green crochet Christmas tree standing on a wooden table](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-tree-result.jpg "Illustration. The intended look.")

## Which one do you want

| You want | Build it as |
| --- | --- |
| A tree that stands on a shelf | Tiered cone |
| A tree for a garland, card, or appliqué | Flat triangle |
| A chunky tree with visible layers | Stacked rounds |

## The slope rule

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/cone-slope.svg" alt="Three cones worked from the tip down. About 1.5 stitches added per round makes a slim cone, about 2 makes the tree on this page, about 3 makes a squat cone, and 6 lies flat. Below, two flat triangles: decreasing both ends every row makes a low triangle, and every other row makes a taller tree shape." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Average stitches added per round decide the cone. Rows between decreases decide the triangle.</figcaption>
</figure>

A flat circle of single crochet needs about six new stitches each round to stay flat. Fewer than that, and the edge turns up into a cone. The cone below goes from 6 to 36 stitches over 18 rounds, about two stitches a round on average. For a slimmer tree, add more even rounds between increases. For a squatter one, remove them.

## What you need

- **Worsted-weight yarn** in green, or any color. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A hook one or two sizes smaller than the label** for the stuffed versions. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Stuffing, a stitch marker, a yarn needle, scraps for decoration.
- **A weight in a sealed bag** for a standing tree: plastic pellets or clean gravel. Loose grains escape through the stitches.

## Method 1: the tiered cone

![Hands crocheting a green cone in the round for a Christmas tree](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-tree-1.jpg "Illustration. Work in a spiral and mark the first stitch of every round.")

Work in a spiral from the tip. Mark the first stitch of each round.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1 starts in a magic ring, pulled tight so the top has no hole.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** Sc in each stitch. (6)

**Round 3:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (9)

**Round 4:** Sc in each stitch. (9)

**Round 5:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (12)

**Round 6:** Sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 7:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

Push small pieces of stuffing into the tip now with the end of a pencil. Once the cone is wider it is hard to reach, and an empty tip flops.

**Round 8:** Sc in each stitch. (18)

**Round 9:** Sc in the back loop only of each stitch. (18)

**Round 10:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)

**Round 11:** Sc in each stitch. (24)

**Round 12:** Sc in the back loop only of each stitch. (24)

**Round 13:** (Sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (30)

**Round 14:** Sc in each stitch. (30)

**Round 15:** Sc in the back loop only of each stitch. (30)

**Round 16:** (Sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (36)

**Rounds 17–18:** Sc in each stitch. (36)

The back-loop rounds 9, 12, and 15 leave the front loops standing as horizontal ridges, the tiers. Three ridges are enough. A back-loop round every round makes a ribbed cone instead.

**Fuller branches (optional).** Join green in any free front loop on a ridge and work 2 sc in each front loop around, then fasten off. That makes a small ruffle at each tier.

Stuff the cone firmly as you go.

### The base

**Round 19:** Sc in the back loop only of each stitch. (36) This is a fold line so the base sits flat.

**Round 20:** (Sc 4, sc2tog) 6 times. (30)

**Round 21:** (Sc 3, sc2tog) 6 times. (24)

Lay the weight bag flat and centered on the stuffing.

**Round 22:** (Sc 2, sc2tog) 6 times. (18)

**Round 23:** (Sc, sc2tog) 6 times. (12)

**Round 24:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6) Fasten off and pull the hole closed.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/tree-cone-profile.svg" alt="Tiered cone tree, round by round, as bars sized by stitch count. Rounds 1 to 2: 6, Round 3: 9, Round 4: 9, Round 5: 12, Round 6: 12, Round 7: 18, Round 8: 18, Round 9: 18, Round 10: 24, Round 11: 24, Round 12: 24, Round 13: 30, Round 14: 30, Round 15: 30, Round 16: 36, Rounds 17 to 18: 36, Round 19: 36, Round 20: 30, Round 21: 24, Round 22: 18, Round 23: 12, Round 24: 6. Rounds 9, 12 and 15 are back loops only, one per tier. Round 19 is the back-loop fold for the base, then rounds 20 to 24 close it." width="480" height="868" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The cone by stitch count. Each terracotta round is worked in back loops, leaving the ledge that makes a tier.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Method 2: the flat triangle

For garlands, cards, and appliqués. Worked from the bottom in rows. Ch 1 does not count as a stitch.

The start needs an **odd** number of stitches. Decreasing two per row from an odd number goes 15, 13, 11 and so on down to 3. From an even number it ends at 2, with nothing tidy to finish on.

**Row 1:** Ch 16. Sc in the 2nd chain from the hook and in each chain across. Turn. (15)

**Row 2:** Ch 1, sc in each stitch. Turn. (15)

**Row 3:** Ch 1, sc2tog, sc to the last 2, sc2tog. Turn. (13)

**Row 4:** Ch 1, sc in each stitch. Turn. (13)

Repeat rows 3 and 4: 11, 11, 9, 9, 7, 7, 5, 5, 3.

**Row 14:** Ch 1, sc3tog. (1) Fasten off.

Decreasing on every other row gives a tree shape. Decreasing on every row from 15 is finished in 8 rows and makes a low, wide triangle. If the sides are wavy, the turning chain is being counted as a stitch. [The turning chain](/crochet-turning-chain/).

On a card, a small brown rectangle sewn to the base reads as a trunk. On a garland, skip it.

## Method 3: stacked rounds

Make three flat circles, increasing six stitches a round as in rounds 1 to 5 of the [pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/): 6, 12, 18, 24, 30.
