Stop at 30 for the largest, 24 for the middle, and 18 for the top. Fold a small pleat into each circle, from the edge to the center, and sew it down. Each circle becomes a shallow cone. Stack them on a dowel, a chopstick, or a tight tube of brown crochet, largest at the bottom. The layered look is the point.

## Making it stand

![A green crochet Christmas tree being stuffed](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-tree-2.jpg "Illustration. Stuff firmly and weight the base.")

- **Weight at the bottom**, centered, before the base closes.
- **A flat base.** The back-loop fold round is what makes it sit.
- **Stuff the lower half firmly.** A hard upper half raises the center of gravity.
- **Offset the increases** by a stitch or two from round to round. Stacked increases make a seam that pulls the cone crooked.

## Decorating

![Sewing a brown trunk onto a crochet Christmas tree](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-tree-3.jpg "Illustration. Add decorations after the shape is stable.")

French knots in a contrasting color are the tidiest ornaments. Beads and buttons are a choking hazard on a gift for a small child, so embroider instead. A small flat star sewn against the tip stays put. Six ornaments look decorated. Thirty looks like a blob.

## Count the triangle rows explicitly

For the flat triangle, rows 1–14 finish at 15, 15, 13, 13, 11, 11, 9, 9, 7, 7, 5, 5, 3, and 1. Row 13 is the final two-edge decrease; row 14 is sc3tog. Sew a separate trunk to the fifteen-stitch foundation edge if wanted; the one-stitch end points upward. For the standing cone, free tier loops belong to rounds 8, 11, and 14, because the following rounds are worked BLO. Mark those loops before continuing. Keep any weighted insert fully enclosed and treat the dowel-based version as a display decoration.

## Troubleshooting

**It is a cup, not a cone.** Worked from the base up. Work from the tip down.

**It is nearly flat.** Too many increases per round. Add even rounds between them.

**The tip flops.** Not stuffed early enough.

**The triangle ends on 2 stitches.** It started on an even count.

**It leans.** Off-center weight, or stacked increases.

## Frequently asked

### Can I skip the stuffing?

Yes, by working the cone to fit over a craft foam cone instead of stuffing it. It stands evenly.

### How do I make a matching set?

Same hook, same yarn, same counts, same stuffing. The counts match exactly. Inches vary with how firmly you stuff.

### What should I make next?

A cone with a hat and beard is the [gnome](/how-to-crochet-a-gnome/). A flat motif in rounds is the [snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/).


# How to Crochet a Chunky Beanie

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie.md



A winter hat fails in one of two ways. It slides off because the brim is too loose, or it leaves a red line because the brim is too tight. Chunky yarn hides uneven stitches and works up fast. It does not hide a brim that was never measured, or a body that flares out above the brim like a chef's hat.

That flare is the mistake this page is built around. The brim is worked sideways in rows, then the body is picked up along its edge. The brim's length is counted in rows, and the body is counted in stitches, and the two are almost never the same number per inch. Pick up one stitch per row and the body comes out the wrong size. Below is how to work out the right count.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No hat for this page was crocheted or worn. The example gauges are invented to show the arithmetic.

![An oatmeal chunky crochet beanie with a folded brim](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie-result.jpg "Illustration. A bulky hat with the brim folded once.")


## Measure the head

Measure around the head just above the ears and across the forehead, where the hat will sit. A hat stretches, so it is made smaller than the head. **About 2 inches smaller** is a common starting point. With very stretchy yarn that may be too little. With firm yarn, 1 inch is enough.

Then measure the depth. Run the tape from the bottom of one ear, over the top of the head, to the bottom of the other ear, and halve it. That is roughly how far the hat needs to reach from the brim edge to the crown, with the brim folded the way it will be worn. Treat it as a guide, not a rule, because a slouchy hat is deeper on purpose.

Children's heads are not small adult heads. Measure the child.

The example: a **22 inch** head, so a **20 inch** hat.

## Yarn and hook

![A skein of bulky oatmeal yarn and a large hook](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie-1.jpg "Illustration. Start with the hook on the label, then swatch.")

- **Bulky yarn**, weight 5 on the [yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **The hook printed on the label**, often in the US L to N range (8.0 to 10.0 mm). [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Yarn needle, scissors, tape measure.

## Two swatches, two gauges

You need two numbers, so make two small swatches or one swatch with two parts.

1. **Body gauge:** 12 sc for several rows. Measure stitches per inch. The example uses **2.5**.
2. **Brim gauge:** 8 sc wide, about 15 rows in back-loop single crochet. Measure rows per inch, relaxed. The example uses **3**.

Back-loop rows squash together into ridges, so the rib's row gauge can be very different from the body's stitch gauge. That difference is the whole reason for the next section. [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/).

## The worked example

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/beanie-brim-pickup.svg" alt="A sideways ribbed brim is counted in rows and the body in stitches. The example brim is 60 rows for 20 inches at 3 rows per inch. The body needs 50 stitches at 2.5 stitches per inch, so work 5 single crochet over every 6 row ends, skipping one. One stitch per row would make 60 stitches, a 24 inch body that flares." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Work out the body count from the body gauge, then spread it along the brim's row ends.</figcaption>
</figure>

| Step | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hat | 22 in head − 2 in | 20 in |
| Brim rows | 20 × 3 | 60 rows |
| Body stitches | 20 × 2.5 | 50 sts |
| Pick-up ratio | 60 rows for 50 sts | 5 sts over every 6 rows |
| Crown | 50 − 5 per round | 9 rounds to 5 |

The crown decreases in groups of five, so a body count divisible by 5 makes it neat. If yours is not, round to the nearest multiple of 5.

## Stage 1: the brim

Ch 1 does not count as a stitch.

![A ribbed oatmeal crochet band](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie-2.jpg "Illustration. Back-loop single crochet makes the rib. The band is flat.")

Chain 9.

**Row 1:** Sc in the 2nd chain from the hook and across. Ch 1, turn. (8)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/beanie-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 9. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 8 sc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Chain 9 for an 8-stitch brim. The chain is the brim's height, not the hat's width.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Row 2:** Sc in the back loop only of each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (8)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/blo-rib.svg" alt="Two steps. 1: the top of each stitch has a front loop, nearer you, and a back loop, farther away. For the rib, insert the hook under the back loop only. 2: the front loops left behind form a raised ridge on every row. The fabric stretches across the ridges, in the direction the rows stack up, so a band worked sideways stretches around the head." width="480" height="593" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Back loop only on every row from row 2 makes the stretchy rib.</figcaption>
</figure>

Repeat row 2 until the band, relaxed, measures the hat size. In the example, about 60 rows. Then wrap it around the head. It should feel snug and stay put without pressing. Add or remove rows until it does, and use that row count.

Eight stitches at 2.5 per inch make a band about 3.2 inches tall, about 1.6 inches folded.

Bring the first row up to the last without twisting the band. Seam the short ends. That seam marks the back.

## Stage 2: pick up the body

Join yarn at the seam on the row-end edge that will sit above the brim. The opposite row-end edge remains the lower brim; if the brim is folded, the fold runs halfway across its eight-stitch depth, not along the pickup edge.

![The hat body worked upward from the brim](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie-3.jpg "Illustration. The body is worked in the round from the band.")

**Round 1:** Work the body count evenly into the row ends. In the example: (sc in each of the next 5 row ends, skip 1 row end) 10 times. Join. (50)

If your numbers do not divide as neatly, work out how many row ends you have for each stitch you need and skip as evenly as you can. Count at the end. The count matters more than perfect spacing.

**Even rounds:** Sc in each stitch. (50) Join each round, or spiral with a marker.

Try it on with the brim folded. Measure your round gauge too. The nine crown rounds add about 9 divided by your rounds-per-inch to the fabric length. Estimate that allowance, then start decreasing before the overall brim-to-top target is reached. Crown fabric curves inward, so baste the last opening and try the hat on before fastening off; if it is too deep, undo some even rounds. Do not use a fixed one-inch allowance for every gauge.

## Stage 3: the crown

Each round removes 5 stitches, which keeps the crown nearly flat in single crochet.

![The crown of the hat decreasing inward](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie-4.jpg "Illustration. Decreases pull the top closed.")

**Round 1:** (Sc 8, sc2tog) 5 times. (45)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/beanie-step-2.svg" alt="Crown round 1: 50 to 45, drawn as rings from the center out. Body: undefined, 50 stitches. Crown 1: (sc 8, sc2tog) 5 times, 45 stitches." width="480" height="509" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 3. Five decreases a round, spaced evenly, keep the crown flat.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** (Sc 7, sc2tog) 5 times. (40)

**Round 3:** (Sc 6, sc2tog) 5 times. (35)

**Round 4:** (Sc 5, sc2tog) 5 times. (30)

**Round 5:** (Sc 4, sc2tog) 5 times. (25)

**Round 6:** (Sc 3, sc2tog) 5 times. (20)

**Round 7:** (Sc 2, sc2tog) 5 times. (15)

**Round 8:** (Sc, sc2tog) 5 times. (10)

**Round 9:** Sc2tog 5 times. (5)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/beanie-step-3.svg" alt="The crown by stitch count, as bars sized by stitch count. Round 0: 50, Round 1: 45, Round 2: 40, Round 3: 35, Round 4: 30, Round 5: 25, Round 6: 20, Round 7: 15, Round 8: 10, Round 9: 5. R0 is the last even body round. Each crown round removes 5." width="480" height="458" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 4. The crown by stitch count: 50 down to 5 in nine rounds, then the gap is closed with the tail.</figcaption>
</figure>

For another count that is a multiple of 5, start with the round whose repeat matches: 55 stitches starts with (sc 9, sc2tog), 60 with (sc 10, sc2tog). Thread the tail through the last 5 stitches, pull closed, and weave it down inside. A knot on the crown shows through bulky yarn.

## Choose one round convention and keep it

For joined body and crown rounds, ch 1 does not count, start in the same stitch as the join, and join to the first sc after each round. For a spiral, omit both joins and ch 1 and move a marker into the first new stitch. Choose before picking up the body; do not switch midway without noting it. The example pickup is fifty stitches across sixty row ends, five stitches per six-row group. Mark ten groups to distribute it. Body stitch gauge, brim row gauge, and body round gauge are separate measurements; all three affect fit.

## Troubleshooting

**The body flares out above the brim.** One stitch per row end. Pick up by the body gauge.

**The body pulls the brim in.** Too few stitches picked up. Recount.

**The rib spirals.** The band was twisted before seaming. Open the seam and resew.

**The top stays a tube.** The crown decreases were skipped.

**Loose on the head.** The brim was measured stretched. Measure it relaxed.

**Covers the eyes.** Too many even rounds, or the brim is not folded. Stop sooner.

## Care

Bulky hats hold water. Squeeze, do not wring. Dry flat with the brim unfolded. Check the label before machine washing or drying.

## Frequently asked

**Will one count fit every adult?**

No. Measure, swatch, then calculate.

**Can I add a pom-pom?**

Yes, after the crown is closed. Sew it on firmly. Glue lets go.

**Can I skip the fold?**

Yes. Stop the even rounds sooner so the hat does not cover the eyes.

**Can I use worsted with these counts?**

No. Worsted has more stitches per inch. Swatch and redo the arithmetic.

## Next

A band with no crown is the [ear warmer](/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer/). Picking up stitches along an edge also comes up in the [fingerless gloves](/how-to-crochet-fingerless-gloves/).


# How to Crochet a Case for Any Device (Sleeve or Pouch)

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-device-case.md



Almost every "crochet case" pattern hands you one stitch count and hopes your phone, your earbuds, or your paperback happens to match it. They rarely do. This guide works the other way round: you measure your device, pick the construction that suits its shape, and size the piece from your own gauge. Two constructions cover nearly everything small enough to carry.

US terms throughout. US single crochet is UK double crochet. See the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) if a term is new. The sizes are worked out from an example gauge rather than from a finished sample, so swatch first and slide your device in before you sew anything shut. None of these counts is a promise about your yarn.

![A beige crochet flap sleeve closed with a wood button](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-device-case-thumb.png)

## Which construction does your device need

The shape of the object decides the method. Do not pick by what the object is called.

- **Flat and rigid** — a laptop, a tablet, an e-reader, a paperback. Fold a rectangle and seam the sides. Jump to [Method 1](#method-1-a-folded-sleeve-for-flat-devices).
- **Small and bulky, with a round-ish footprint** — an earbud case, a lip balm, a small power bank. Work a round base and a straight tube. Jump to [Method 2](#method-2-a-round-pouch-for-small-gear).
- **A phone.** A phone is flat, so it uses Method 1, but it is the most common case people want and it has its own worked page: [how to crochet a phone case](/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case/).
- **A tapered travel cup** that is wider at the top than the bottom needs shaping a straight tube cannot give. That is a [tumbler sleeve](/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve/).
- **A coiled cable** needs a strap and a buttonhole, not a pocket: the [cable organizer](/how-to-crochet-a-cable-organizer/).
- **A drawstring makeup or coin pouch** where the opening gathers shut is a different closure again: the [drawstring pouch guide](/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch/).

<div class="warning">

None of these is waterproof, and none is drop protection. Single crochet has gaps, and yarn does not absorb a fall. A crochet case is a soft cover that keeps lint, scratches, and loose keys off your gear. Say so to anyone you give one to.

</div>

## The one thing both methods share: gauge

Everything below is arithmetic on top of your gauge, so the gauge has to be yours, not the example's. Crochet a flat swatch about 4 inches square in the yarn and hook you will use, then measure the middle. The example gauge in this guide is **4 single crochet and 5 rows to the inch** in smooth worsted cotton on a US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook. If your swatch comes out at 3.5 stitches to the inch, your counts change, and that is the point. [Crochet gauge](/crochet-gauge/) walks through measuring it.

A quick note on yarn: smooth worsted cotton is the easiest choice here. It is firm, it holds a stitch shape, and it does not shed into a charging port or a speaker mesh the way a fuzzy acrylic does. Pick a hook a size or two smaller than the yarn label suggests, because a case wants denser fabric than a scarf. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/) and the [hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/) have the ranges.

## Method 1: a folded sleeve for flat devices

A flat sleeve is a single rectangle, folded in half, seamed up both sides, open at the top. The stitch count sets the width of the opening. The row count, folded in half, sets how deep the device sits. The fold becomes the bottom.

### Measure the device, then add its thickness to the width

Close the device and measure three things: its **width**, its **height**, and its **thickness** at the thickest point. The mistake that ruins a folded sleeve is sizing the width to the flat width only. The fabric has to wrap around the edges, so each face of the sleeve must cover the width *plus* the thickness, plus a little ease and a stitch for the seams.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/laptop-sleeve-plan.svg" alt="A long rectangle 53 stitches wide and 90 rows long, folded after row 45. For a 12 by 8.5 by 0.6 inch laptop: width 12 plus 0.6 plus 0.25 plus 0.25 is 13.1 inches, 53 stitches; depth per side 8.5 plus 0.3 plus 0.2 is 9 inches, 90 rows. A note says to measure flat, because a long cotton piece grows when it hangs." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The thickness is added to the width, not left out. On a thin tablet that is a quarter inch; on a laptop, more.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Worked example, a 12 x 8.5 x 0.6 inch laptop**, at 4 stitches and 5 rows to the inch:

- Width: 12 + 0.6 thickness + 0.25 seams + 0.25 ease = 13.1 in, so 53 single crochet, chain 54.
- Depth per side: 8.5 + 0.3 at the fold + 0.2 ease = 9.0 in. The fabric is twice that before folding, so 18 in, which is 90 rows.

**Worked example, a 5 x 8 inch paperback**, same gauge:

- Width: for a 0.5-inch-thick paperback, 5 + 0.5 thickness + 0.25 seams + 0.25 ease = 6.0 in, so 24 single crochet, chain 25. Measure the spine of any book; thickness is part of the calculation, not an optional hardcover allowance.
- Depth: choose how much of the cover stays visible. Three quarters of 8 in is 6 in of sleeve, so 12 in of fabric before the fold, which is 60 rows.

An e-reader or small tablet is the same sum with its own three numbers. Measure, do not guess from the screen size on the box — that is a diagonal, not the width.

### Work the rectangle, keeping the count honest

Chain your width-plus-one. For the paperback example that is chain 25.

**Row 1:** Single crochet in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Chain 1, turn. (24)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/book-sleeve-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 23. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 22 sc." width="480" height="439" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Chain 23 for 22 single crochet, 5.5 inches at 4 stitches per inch.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Every following row:** Single crochet in each stitch to the end. Chain 1, turn. The chain 1 is not a stitch.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Start every row in the edge stitch, not the turning chain. Over sixty or ninety rows a single gained stitch becomes a wedge, and the fold stops meeting.</figcaption>
</figure>

Count across every ten rows. On a long piece, measure it lying flat, not hanging off your lap — cotton stretches lengthwise under its own weight and reads long while it hangs.

### Fold, seam, and try the device before you weave in ends

Fold so the first row meets the last row, right sides together. The fold is the bottom. Single crochet or whipstitch through both layers up each side, matching row ends, and leave the top open. For a sewn seam, make one whipstitch per matching pair without drawing it tight. For a crocheted sc seam, distribute stitches to your stitch-to-row gauge: at 4 sc and 5 rows per inch, work 4 sc over each 5 paired row ends. Check it lies flat; seam strength depends on yarn, stitch placement, and reinforcement, not the seam name alone.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/sc-seam-two-layers.svg" alt="Two steps. 1: hold the pieces wrong sides together with the edges lined up stitch for stitch. 2: insert the hook through both layers at once, matching stitch to stitch, and work one sc in each pair; work 3 sc in a corner stitch to turn a corner flat." width="480" height="559" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>One stitch per row end, through both layers, from the fold to the top.</figcaption>
</figure>

Slide the device in before you weave the ends in, while the seam can still be redone. It should enter without bending and stop where you planned. If it will not go in, the opening is too narrow — seams do not add fabric, so the next rectangle needs more stitches, not a harder tug.

To keep a loose device from sliding out, sew a button to the front and chain a loop on the back long enough to reach over it with the device inside. Place the button only once the device is in the sleeve, not on the empty sleeve.

## Method 2: a round pouch for small gear

A small bulky item wants a closed round bottom and a straight tube, not a fold. Six stitches in a magic ring grow into a flat circle; when the circle is wide enough to go around the item, you stop increasing and the walls turn up on their own.

![A small round crochet pouch with a button flap beside an earbud case](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-an-earbud-pouch-result.jpg "Illustration. A round pouch with a button flap, sized to a charging case.")

### Size the tube from the perimeter, not the width

The number that matters is the distance **around** the item, because that is what the tube has to stretch over. For a boxy earbud case, measure width and depth and work the perimeter: 2 x (width + depth). For a round lip balm, measure across and multiply by 3.14. Add roughly half an inch to an inch of ease so the item drops in.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/earbud-pouch-sizing.svg" alt="A case footprint of 1.9 by 0.9 inches with a perimeter of 5.6 inches, shown inside a round pouch tube. A table at 4 single crochet per inch: 24 stitches is 6.0 inches around and snug, 27 is 6.75 inches and the example choice, 30 is 7.5 inches and roomy." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Choose the stitch count from the perimeter plus ease. A round tube around an oblong case leaves a little space at the sides, which soft fabric takes up.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Worked example, a 1.9 x 0.9 x 2.4 inch earbud case:** perimeter 2 x (1.9 + 0.9) = 5.6 in, plus about an inch of ease is 6.6 in, which at 4 stitches to the inch rounds to **27 stitches**. The wall is the case height, 2.4 in plus a little, at 5 rounds to the inch, about **13 rounds**.

**Worked example, a 0.65 inch lip balm:** around is 0.65 x 3.14 = 2.04 in, so **9 stitches** gives about 0.2 in of ease, snug enough to hold by friction. Ten even rounds clears the cap.

### Work the base, then the walls

Work in a spiral without joining, and move a stitch marker up into the first stitch of each round. [Working in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/) explains spirals.

**Round 1:** 6 single crochet in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The magic ring closes the center completely, so the base has no hole for the item to push through.</figcaption>
</figure>

Then increase six stitches each round until the circle is as wide as your target, slowing to fewer increases on the last round if you need to land on an exact count:

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)
**Round 3:** (sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)
**Round 4:** (sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)
**Round 5, to reach 27:** (sc 7, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (27)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/earbud-step-1.svg" alt="Base: rounds 1 to 5, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 1: 6 sc in a magic ring, 6 stitches. Round 2: 2 sc in each st, 12 stitches. Round 3: (sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 18 stitches. Round 4: (sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 24 stitches. Round 5: (sc 7, 2 sc in next) 3 times, 27 stitches. Rounds 1 to 4 add 6 each. Round 5 adds 3, for the 27 the case needs." width="480" height="563" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>A flat circle adds six stitches a round. A partial last round nudges you onto the exact count the item needs.</figcaption>
</figure>

For the lip balm, round 2 adds only three — (sc, 2 sc in next) 3 times for 9 — so the base cups on purpose and becomes the bottom of a narrow tube. [Increases and decreases](/crochet-increase-decrease/) covers the shaping if it is new.

Then stop increasing and work even rounds for the wall:

**Even rounds:** Single crochet in each stitch, same count every round, until the item sits just below the rim.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/earbud-step-2.svg" alt="The whole pouch by stitch count, as bars sized by stitch count. Round 1: 6, Round 2: 12, Round 3: 18, Round 4: 24, Round 5: 27, Rounds 6 to 18: 27. Base: rounds 1 to 5. Wall: 13 even rounds of 27, about 2.6 in tall at 5 rounds per inch." width="480" height="391" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>With no more increases, the fabric turns up into the wall by itself. Stand the item in every few rounds and stop when it is covered.</figcaption>
</figure>

### Close it with a flap or a drawstring

A **button flap** suits a case you open often. Work a flap in rows over about a third of the rim stitches, five or six rows tall, and put a chain loop on the last row to catch a sewn-on button.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/earbud-step-3.svg" alt="A flap of 6 rows worked over 9 of the 27 stitches on the last round, with a chain loop on the top row. Rows 1 to 5: 9 sc, ch 1 and turn. Row 6: sc 3, ch 4, skip 3, sc 3. The other 18 stitches stay open." width="480" height="421" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The flap is worked on part of the rim; the rest stays open as the mouth. The ch loop on the top row catches the button.</figcaption>
</figure>

A **drawstring** suits a pouch you reach into. It needs an even stitch count: work an eyelet round of (sc, ch 1, skip 1) around, then thread a crocheted cord through and knot it. The [drawstring pouch guide](/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch/) has the full eyelet-and-cord sequence.

A **hang loop** turns a lip-balm tube into a keychain. Sew the loop to the open rim, not the closed base, so the item hangs cap-up and gravity keeps it seated.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/lip-balm-loop.svg" alt="Two sleeves hanging from rings. Left: loop on the closed base, sleeve upside down, tube sliding out of the rim. Right: loop at the rim, base hanging down, cap up. Note: 9 stitches at 4 per inch is 2.25 inches around, and a 0.65 inch tube is 2.04 inches around." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>A sleeve hangs from the end the loop is on. Put it at the rim so the closed base hangs down.</figcaption>
</figure>

If you want to decorate a round pouch — bat wings, ears, a face — shape the appliqués separately and sew them on with the item inside so the flap still closes. The [bat earbuds pouch](/gothic-bat-earbuds-pouch/) works through triangle and picot shaping in detail.

## Fit the base footprint before building a tube

A measured perimeter sets the wall circumference but does not ensure a round base covers a rectangular case bottom. Place the item upright on the base: its corners must be supported without stretching the fabric into a different shape. If a round circle leaves corners outside, choose an oval or rectangular construction, or the folded sleeve. For a 27-stitch tube with drawstring closure, first work (sc 8, inc) three times to reach 30, then use fifteen (sc, ch 1, skip 1) repeats and restore thirty positions on the following round. Do not work an even-count eyelet repeat over twenty-seven stitches.

## Troubleshooting

**The device will not go in.** The width (Method 1) or the stitch count around (Method 2) left out the thickness. Start wider; seams and stretching will not add real fabric.

**One side of the sleeve is longer than the other.** A stitch was gained on one edge. Count back to the last row that was right.

**The folded piece came out long and grew longer after a week.** Cotton relaxes under its own weight. Measure flat and stop a little short next time.

**The round base ruffles instead of lying flat.** Too many increases for the count. Ruffling means the circle is growing faster than it should.

**The round base cups into a bowl too early.** The ring or the first increases are tight. Loosen, or add one more increase round.

**The flap pulls into a point and will not reach the button.** The loop is too short or the button is too low. Lengthen the chain before you move the button.

## Care

Take the device out first. Hand wash cool, press the water out in a towel, and dry flat. Put the device back only when the cotton is fully dry — damp cotton marks metal and cockles paper. Heat can shrink cotton enough that a snug case no longer fits, so keep it out of the dryer.

## Frequently asked

**Will one pattern fit every device?**

No, and that is the whole point of sizing from your own measurements. Two cases that share a width can differ by half an inch around, which is the difference between dropping in and not fitting.

**Which method is easier for a first project?**

The folded sleeve. It is straight rows and two seams, with no shaping. The round pouch adds a magic ring and increases, which the [working in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/) page covers.

**Can I sell what I make?**

Finished cases, yes. Do not republish these instructions as your own pattern.

## Next

For the single most-requested flat case, see the worked [phone case](/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case/). For a cup that is wider at the top, the [tumbler sleeve](/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve/) adds the taper. For an opening that gathers shut, the [drawstring pouch guide](/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch/) teaches the eyelet-and-cord closure. If your counts keep drifting on a long piece, the fix is upstream in [crochet gauge](/crochet-gauge/).


# How to Crochet a Drawstring Bag (Any Size)

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag.md



A drawstring bag is the same four pieces every time, whatever you are putting in it: a flat round base, a straight tube, one row of holes, and a cord. Learn it once at coin-pouch size and you can work it at candy-bag size, add a pumpkin belly, or swap a thin cord for a thick one, without a new pattern. This page teaches the method and then shows three worked sizes so you can see the choices.

US terms throughout. US single crochet is UK double crochet. See the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) if a term is new. The inch figures are worked out from an example gauge, not from a finished bag, so swatch first and drop the real contents in before you fix the cord length.

![A sage crochet drawstring bag with wooden beads on the cords](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag-thumb.png)

If your opening gathers shut, this is your page. If instead you want a flat pouch that folds and seams with a drawstring across a straight top, that is the [makeup pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch/). If you want a zipper rather than a cord, that is the [pumpkin zipper pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-zipper-pouch/).

## The method, start to finish

1. **A flat circle** grows from a magic ring, six stitches added every round, until it is as wide as you want the bottom.
2. **A straight tube** forms on its own the moment you stop increasing and work even rounds.
3. **An eyelet round** near the top makes the holes by chaining over skipped stitches.
4. **A cord** threads in and out of the holes; pulling both ends gathers the top shut.

Everything after that — a face, a stem, a belly, a thicker cord — is a variation on those four pieces.

## Shared setup: gauge and yarn

The counts below are at an example gauge of **4 single crochet and 5 rounds to the inch** in smooth worsted cotton on a US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook. Your first five rounds of the base are your swatch: measure across the flat circle and compare. [Crochet gauge](/crochet-gauge/) explains the measuring. Smooth cotton holds the round shape and does not grab wrappers the way a fuzzy acrylic does; pick a hook a size or two down from the label so the fabric is firm enough to stand up. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/) and the [hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/) have the ranges.

## Stage 1: the base, sized to the bottom you want

Work in a spiral without joining, and move a stitch marker up into the first stitch of each round. [Working in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/) covers spirals.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The magic ring closes the center so nothing falls out of the bottom.</figcaption>
</figure>

Each round adds six, with one more plain stitch between the increases each time:

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)
**Round 3:** (sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)
**Round 4:** (sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)
**Round 5:** (sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (30)
**Round 6:** (sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (36)
**Round 7:** (sc 5, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (42)
**Round 8:** (sc 6, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (48)
**Round 9:** (sc 7, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (54)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/candy-bag-step-1.svg" alt="Base: rounds 1 to 9, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 1: 6 sc in a magic ring, 6 stitches. Round 2: 2 sc in each st, 12 stitches. Round 3: (sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 18 stitches. Round 4: (sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 24 stitches. Round 5: (sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 30 stitches. Round 6: (sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 36 stitches. Round 7: (sc 5, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 42 stitches. Round 8: (sc 6, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 48 stitches. Round 9: (sc 7, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 54 stitches." width="480" height="509" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Stop when the circle is as wide as the bottom you want. The stitch count you land on is the count the whole tube and the eyelet round work from.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Stop where your bag wants to stop.** A small coin pouch ends the base at round 5 (30 stitches, about 2.4 in across). A candy bag runs to round 9 (54 stitches, about 4.3 in across). Lay the circle flat each time you add a round: flat is right, a ruffle means too many increases, and a bowl means the ring or your stitches are too tight, which makes the finished bag tip over.

<div class="tip">

End the base on a count the eyelet round can divide. Small eyelets (two stitches each) need an even number. Large eyelets (six stitches each) need a multiple of six, like 48 or 54. Choosing 30, 48, or 54 keeps both options open.

</div>

## Stage 2: the tube

**Even rounds:** Single crochet in each stitch, the same count every round, until the wall is as tall as you need. The fabric turns up into a tube by itself once the increases stop.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/coin-pouch-step-2.svg" alt="The whole pouch by stitch count, as bars sized by stitch count. Round 1: 6, Round 2: 12, Round 3: 18, Round 4: 24, Rounds 5 to 15: 30, Round 16: 15+15, Round 17: 30, Round 18: 30. Round 16 is the eyelet round: (sc, ch 1, skip 1) 15 times. Round 17 puts 1 sc in each sc and each space, back to 30." width="480" height="451" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The coin-pouch profile: a short base, ten even rounds of tube, then the eyelet round and a short frill above it.</figcaption>
</figure>

Drop the real contents in every few rounds and stop when they sit well below where the holes will go — the cord needs fabric above the contents to gather, or the bag will not close over a full load.

### Optional: shape a belly without starting over

You can widen a straight tube in the middle and bring it back in, which is how a plain orange cylinder becomes a pumpkin. Work one round of six evenly spaced increases where you want the belly to start, some even rounds, then one round of six evenly spaced decreases to return to the neck count.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/pumpkin-bag-profile.svg" alt="Side view of the pumpkin bag. A flat base of 48 stitches, rounds 9 to 12 straight at 48, round 13 increases to 54, rounds 14 to 21 straight at 54, round 22 decreases to 48, rounds 23 to 28 straight at 48, then the eyelet round 29 and two rounds above it. The belly is about a quarter inch wider on each side." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Six stitches out, six stitches back in. Keep the belly gentle so the bag still stands when it is full.</figcaption>
</figure>

For a 48-stitch neck: at the belly, **(sc 7, 2 sc in next) 6 times** widens to 54; work the belly rounds; then **(sc 7, sc2tog) 6 times** brings it back to 48. The same move shapes a vase, a bottle cozy, or a toy body.

## Stage 3: the eyelet round

The eyelet round repeats one idea: single crochet into some stitches, then chain over a skipped stitch or two. The chain bridges the gap so the round keeps its length, and the opening under each chain is a hole. The repeat must divide evenly into your stitch count, or the last hole lands short and the cord bunches there.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/eyelet-spacing.svg" alt="Two eyelet rounds compared on 54 stitches. Single crochet, chain 1, skip 1 makes 27 small holes for a thin chain cord and many small gathers. Single crochet 4, chain 2, skip 2, nine times, makes 9 larger holes 1.5 inches apart for a thicker cord and fewer, deeper folds. The next round puts 2 single crochet in each chain-2 space to return to 54." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Small close holes gather tightly and take a thin cord. Large spaced holes take a thick cord and close in fewer, deeper folds.</figcaption>
</figure>

- **Small holes:** (sc, ch 1, skip 1) all the way around. Each repeat is two stitches, so the count must be even. Thirty stitches make fifteen holes.
- **Large holes:** (sc 4, ch 2, skip 2) around. Each repeat is six stitches, so the count must be a multiple of six. Fifty-four stitches make nine holes.

After the eyelet round, work one round putting 1 sc into each single crochet and 1 sc into each ch-1 space, or 2 sc into each ch-2 space, to restore the full count, then one or two plain rounds. That short frill above the holes stops the cord riding up over the top edge when you pull it.

## Stage 4: the cord

The cord goes once around the opening and leaves two tails to tie. Measure the opening circumference and add about 4 inches per tail: a 7.5-inch opening wants roughly 7.5 + 4 + 4 = 15.5 inches.

- **A chain cord** is quickest. Chain to length, then slip stitch back along the chain so it cannot stretch open every time you pull it.
- **A twisted cord** is rounder, firmer, and easier for small hands. Cut two strands about three times the finished length, knot the ends, twist one end until the strands kink, then fold in half and let them ply around each other. Knot and trim.

Thread the cord in and out of the holes with a yarn needle, starting and finishing at the front so the bow sits where you want it, and knot each end so it cannot pull back through.

## Three worked sizes

**Coin pouch** — base to round 5 (30 stitches), ten even rounds, small eyelets (15 holes), a thin chain cord, a small embroidered face. About 2.4 inches across. The full-size walkthrough with the eyelet close-up is below in the earlier sections; this is the smallest useful version.

**Candy bag** — base to round 9 (54 stitches), about 23 even rounds, large eyelets (9 holes), a thick twisted cord, two embroidered eyes. About 4.3 inches across and 4.6 inches tall. The larger holes and thick cord are easier for a child to pull.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/candy-bag-step-2.svg" alt="Round 33: nine large eyelets, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 32: 54 stitches. Round 33: (sc 4, ch 2, skip 2) 9 times: 36 sc, 36 stitches. Each open circle is one ch-2 space over 2 skipped stitches." width="480" height="563" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The candy bag's nine large eyelets, spaced six stitches apart around a 54-stitch tube.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Pumpkin treat bag** — base to round 8 (48 stitches), a belly widened to 54 and brought back to 48, small eyelets (24 holes), a chain cord, and a short green stem sewn beside the rim; for the 48-stitch version, work three even rounds at 48, the increase round to 54, four even rounds at 54, the decrease round to 48, then three even rounds at 48 before the eyelets. The belly is the optional shaping from Stage 2.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/treat-bag-step-1.svg" alt="Round 13: widen to 54, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 12: 48 stitches. Round 13: (sc 7, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 54 stitches." width="480" height="509" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The pumpkin's belly: six spaced increases to 54, even rounds, then six spaced decreases back to 48.</figcaption>
</figure>

To decorate, embroider the face after you have gathered the top once to find the front, and keep the stitches shallow so contents do not catch on lumps inside. A stem is 5 sc in a magic ring worked as a short tube and sewn between two eyelets, not through one.

## Restore every skipped stitch after the eyelets

At fifty-four stitches, the large-hole round has nine repeats: sc 4, ch 2, skip 2. The next round must make four sc in the four sc and two sc in the ch-2 space for each repeat, returning to fifty-four. One sc in that space would reduce the round to forty-five and narrow the rim. For the small-hole version, thirty positions become fifteen sc and fifteen ch-1 spaces, then thirty sc again. Before threading a cord, count the holes and check the packed bag has fabric above the contents to gather. An odd number of holes may need a second pass through the starting eyelet to return both cord ends to the outside.

## Troubleshooting

**One spot will not gather.** A missing hole: the count did not divide by the repeat, or a repeat was skipped. Count the holes before you cord it.

**The cord will not pass through.** The cord is thicker than the holes. Use larger (ch-2) eyelets, or a thinner cord.

**The cord stretches and the top reopens.** A bare chain cord. Slip stitch back along it, or use a twisted cord.

**The bag tips over.** The base cupped instead of lying flat. Rework rounds 1 to 5 more loosely.

**A small item falls through the side.** A stitch was skipped in the tube, or the fabric is a taller stitch than single crochet, which leaves gaps.

## Care

Empty it and shake out any wrappers or crumbs. Hand wash cool and dry flat, pushing a shaped bag back into its form. Keep cotton out of the dryer; it can shrink enough to stiffen the drawstring.

## Frequently asked

**How do I make it bigger or smaller?**

Change where you stop the base. A wider base needs more increase rounds; a narrower one, fewer. Everything downstream works from whatever count you land on.

**Which cord for a child's bag?**

The thick twisted cord through large eyelets. It grips better and does not cut into small fingers. Knot the ends, and never put a cord around a child's neck.

**Can I sell what I make?**

Finished bags, yes. Do not republish these instructions as your own pattern.

## Next

For a flat pouch that folds and seams instead of working in the round, see the [makeup pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch/). For a firm case rather than a soft bag, the [device case guide](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/) covers folded sleeves and round pouches with flaps. If spirals still feel new, start with [crochet in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/).


# How to Crochet a Fall Leaf

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-fall-leaf.md



A fall leaf is a small flat appliqué. Sew it to a [pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/), a [drawstring treat bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/), or a [mug cozy](/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners/), or string a few into a garland.

It also teaches a technique that shows up in a lot of motifs: **shaping with stitch height alone.** There are no increases or decreases. You chain a short line, then work one row of stitches up one side of the chain and back down the other. Short stitches at the ends and tall stitches in the middle make a pointed oval. Change the order of heights and you change the shape.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet, and US double crochet is UK treble. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations of the finished leaf, and the inch sizes come from gauge, so measure as you go.

![A small rust-orange crochet leaf on linen](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-fall-leaf-result.jpg "Illustration. The flat leaf this page describes.")

## How the shape works

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/leaf-stitch-heights.svg" alt="A chain of 9 with the first chain from the hook skipped. Above the chain, stitches rise and fall: single crochet, half double, four double crochet, half double, single crochet. At the far end, 3 single crochet in one chain make the tip. Below, the return side mirrors the heights in 7 chains: half double, four double, half double, single crochet. 17 stitches make a pointed oval." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Heights rise and fall along each side of the chain. Three stitches in the end chain turn the corner and make the tip.</figcaption>
</figure>

Each stitch height is roughly a step: a single crochet is short, a half double is taller, a double taller again. Laid along a chain, they make a curve. Work the same curve on the other side of the chain and you get a symmetrical leaf. Three stitches crowded into the end chain turn the work around the corner and make the point.

## What you need

- **Worsted-weight yarn** in rust, gold, or olive, plus a scrap of brown or green for the stem if you want contrast. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook.** A smaller hook makes a neater, smaller leaf. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Yarn needle.

Gauge does not change the shape, only the size. For a matching set, make one leaf, then copy the hook and the chain tension.

## The small leaf

Work along one side of the chain, around the tip, and back along the other side. Do not turn.

**Chain 9.**

**First side.** Starting in the 2nd chain from the hook: sc, hdc, dc in each of the next 4 chains, hdc, sc. (8)

That uses 8 chains: 1 + 1 + 4 + 1 + 1 = 8. The chain closest to the hook is skipped, and the last stitch lands in the first chain you made. That first chain is the tip.

**Tip.** Work 2 more sc into that same chain, so it holds 3 sc. (10 so far)

**Return side.** Rotate the work. Working into the free loops on the underside of the chain: hdc, dc in each of the next 4 chains, hdc, sc. (7 more, 17 total)

That uses the remaining 7 chains. The return side has no single crochet at the tip end because the tip already has three.

Sl st into the first sc. Fasten off with a long tail for sewing.

**Count check.** If the return side runs out of chain early, a stitch on the way out landed in the wrong chain. If it has chains left over, one was skipped.

## The larger leaf

The same plan, with a longer middle.

**Chain 13.**

**First side.** Starting in the 2nd chain from the hook: sc, hdc, dc in each of the next 8 chains, hdc, sc. (12)

**Tip.** 2 more sc in the last chain. (14 so far)

**Return side.** In the free loops: hdc, dc in each of the next 8 chains, hdc, sc. (11 more, 25 total)

Sl st into the first sc. Fasten off.

The check is the same: 1 + 1 + 8 + 1 + 1 = 12 on the first side, 11 on the return, and 2 extra at the tip.

## The stem

Join the stem color at the base, the end opposite the tip. Ch 6. Slip stitch in the 2nd chain from the hook and in each chain back to the leaf. (5 slip stitches) Fasten off and weave that short end into the stem.

## Veins (optional)

With a contrasting color or a darker shade, work surface slip stitch from the base to the tip, down the middle: hold the yarn behind the leaf, insert the hook from front to back, pull up a loop, and pull it through the loop on the hook. Work along the line of the chain. One center vein is enough on the small leaf. On the large leaf, add two short side veins angled toward the tip.

Alternatively, embroider the veins with a yarn needle and backstitch.

## Mark the turning tip of the chain

For the small leaf, mark the first chain made: it is the far tip, reached after the eight first-side stitches. All three tip sc go into that same chain. Rotate and begin the return side in the next unused chain loop, not the tip chain again. Seven return stitches give seventeen total. The skipped chain nearest the hook remains unworked. For the larger leaf the corresponding totals are twelve outward stitches, two additional tip sc, and eleven back: twenty-five. The stem attaches at the joined base, opposite the marked tip, so the leaf and stem do not share the wrong end.

## Troubleshooting

**It twists.** The return side went into the front of the first-side stitches, not the free loops of the chain. Undo to the tip and use the underside loops.

**The tip is blunt.** The 3 sc are spread across two chains. All three go in the end chain.

**It cups.** The chain is tighter than the stitches. Chain with a hook one size larger.

**One side is fatter.** A stitch height is different between the sides. Compare the list.

**The stem curls into a corkscrew.** The slip stitches are tighter than the chain. Chain more loosely, or use a larger hook for the stem.

## Customizing

Change the heights to change the shape. More doubles in the middle make a longer, slimmer leaf, like the larger version. Treble crochet in the middle makes it wider. Single crochet all the way along makes a narrow willow-like strip. Two colors of leaf on one pumpkin read as fall without any other decoration.

For a garland, chain a cord long enough for the window and sew the leaves on, spacing them evenly.

## Frequently asked

### Should I block it?

If it cups, yes. Pin it flat and steam lightly, or wet-block cotton. [Blocking](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

### Can I use it on a baby item?

Sew it on firmly all the way round and check that the stem cannot pull free. A loose appliqué is a poor idea on anything a baby can mouth.

### What should I make next?

The same sewn-on idea is the [simple flower](/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower/). A flat motif worked in rounds instead of around a chain is the [snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/).


# How to Crochet a Gnome, Step by Step

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-gnome.md



A crochet gnome is a body, a hat, a nose, and a beard. None of those pieces is hard. Two things make it work. The first is proportion: **the hat comes down far enough to cover where the eyes would be, and the brim touches the top of the nose.** Stop the brim higher and you have a person in a cap.

The second is direction. A gnome is wider at the bottom than at the top, and it has to stand. The body is worked **from the top down**: a flat top, then a few spaced increase rounds so it widens, then a flat base made last, with a weight inside before it closes. Starting at the base and increasing upward makes the opposite shape, a cup that is widest at the top.

US terms. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations of the finished gnome, which has not been made up in yarn here, so its size depends on your yarn, hook, and stuffing.

![A finished crochet Christmas gnome with tall hat and beard](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-gnome-result.jpg "Illustration. The intended look.")

## How it fits together

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gnome-proportions.svg" alt="Side view of a gnome. The body widens from 30 stitches at the top to 36 and 42, then a back-loop round and decreases make a flat base with a weight bag inside. The hat runs from a floppy tip down to 42 stitches, slightly wider than the 36-stitch body it covers, with its brim touching the top of the nose. The beard hangs below the nose." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The body widens toward the base. The hat covers the top half, and its brim sits on the nose.</figcaption>
</figure>

## What you need

- **Worsted-weight yarn** in a body color, a hat color, and a skin tone for the nose. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **Something pale and fluffy for the beard**: bouclé, a fuzzy yarn, or cut strands of worsted. [Yarn for Christmas crochet](/yarn-for-christmas-crochet/).
- **A hook one or two sizes smaller than the label**, so stuffing does not show. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Stuffing, a yarn needle, a stitch marker.
- **A weight in a closed bag**: plastic stuffing pellets or clean aquarium gravel in a small sealed bag, or a scrap of fabric sewn shut. Loose grains work out through the stitches. Rice and lentils can be used, but they are food, and food attracts pests and does not survive a wash.

## Step 1: the body, top down

![Hands crocheting a round gnome body in the round](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-gnome-1.jpg "Illustration. The body is worked in the round.")

Body color. Work in a spiral and mark the first stitch of each round.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1 starts in a magic ring, pulled tight so the top has no hole.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 4:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)

**Round 5:** (Sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (30)

That flat circle is the top of the head. The hat will cover it.

**Rounds 6–7:** Sc in each stitch. (30)

**Round 8:** (Sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (36)

**Rounds 9–10:** Sc in each stitch. (36)

**Round 11:** (Sc 5, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (42)

**Rounds 12–14:** Sc in each stitch. (42)

One increase round every two or three rounds makes the gentle widening. Increasing every round would make a flat circle, not a body. [Why six increases behave this way](/crochet-increase-decrease/).

Shift where each increase round starts by a stitch or two, so the increases do not stack into six visible ridges.

## Step 2: the flat base

**Round 15:** Sc in the back loop only of each stitch. (42)

The back-loop round makes a fold line, so the base turns under at a crisp edge and sits flat.

**Round 16:** (Sc 5, sc2tog) 6 times. (36)

**Round 17:** (Sc 4, sc2tog) 6 times. (30)

**Round 18:** (Sc 3, sc2tog) 6 times. (24)

Stuff the body firmly, then lay the weight bag flat on top of the stuffing, against the base. Keep it centered. An off-center weight makes the gnome lean.

**Round 19:** (Sc 2, sc2tog) 6 times. (18)

**Round 20:** (Sc, sc2tog) 6 times. (12)

**Round 21:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6) Fasten off and pull the hole closed.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gnome-body-profile.svg" alt="Gnome body, top down, as bars sized by stitch count. Round 1: 6, Round 2: 12, Round 3: 18, Round 4: 24, Round 5: 30, Rounds 6 to 7: 30, Round 8: 36, Rounds 9 to 10: 36, Round 11: 42, Rounds 12 to 14: 42, Round 15: 42, Round 16: 36, Round 17: 30, Round 18: 24, Round 19: 18, Round 20: 12, Round 21: 6. Round 15 (terracotta) is back loops only at 42. Rounds 16 to 21 remove 6 each to close a flat base." width="480" height="693" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The body widens in steps with even rounds between, then the back-loop round 15 turns the base flat.</figcaption>
</figure>

Stand it on a table. If it rocks, the base domed: press it flat with your palm while it is still easy to adjust the weight.

## Step 3: the hat

Hat color. Worked from the tip down.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

**Rounds 2–4:** Sc in each stitch. (6) This thin tube is the floppy tip.

**Round 5:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (9)

**Rounds 6–7:** Sc in each stitch. (9)

**Round 8:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 3 times. (12)

**Rounds 9–10:** Sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 11:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 12:** Sc in each stitch. (18)

**Round 13:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)

**Round 14:** Sc in each stitch. (24)

**Round 15:** (Sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (30)

**Round 16:** Sc in each stitch. (30)

**Round 17:** (Sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (36)

**Round 18:** Sc in each stitch. (36)

**Round 19:** (Sc 5, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (42)

**Rounds 20–21:** Sc in each stitch. (42) Fasten off with a long tail.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gnome-hat-profile.svg" alt="Gnome hat, tip down, as bars sized by stitch count. Rounds 1 to 4: 6, Round 5: 9, Rounds 6 to 7: 9, Round 8: 12, Rounds 9 to 10: 12, Round 11: 18, Round 12: 18, Round 13: 24, Round 14: 24, Round 15: 30, Round 16: 30, Round 17: 36, Round 18: 36, Round 19: 42, Rounds 20 to 21: 42. A thin tip first: 3 added every third round to 12. Then 6 every other round to 42." width="480" height="633" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The hat ends at 42, the same as the body, so it sits over the top instead of perching on it.</figcaption>
</figure>

The hat opening, 42, is slightly wider than the body's 36-stitch middle where it will sit, so it drops over instead of perching. Do not stuff the hat. A stuffed hat stands stiff and loses the flop.

## Step 4: the nose

Skin tone.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Rounds 3–4:** Sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 5:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6)

Stuff lightly before round 5. After round 5, fasten off with a sewing tail, thread it through the six remaining front loops, pull closed, and secure it. Pin the nose in place before sewing; its size is a design choice.

## Step 5: the beard

Cut strands about twice the finished length you want. Fold each in half, pull the fold through a stitch with the hook, then pull the ends through the fold. Work an upside-down arc across the front of the body, starting about round 10. Use more strands than you think. Fringe looks full in your hand and sparse on the gnome. Trim straight or to a point.

## Step 6: assembly

![Sewing a pointed hat onto a crochet gnome](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-gnome-2.jpg "Illustration. The hat goes on last so it covers the joins.")

1. **Beard first**, so the nose can cover the top of it.
2. **Nose** centered on the top of the beard.
3. **Hat** pulled down until the brim touches the top of the nose. Pin it all the way round, look from a few feet away, then sew around the brim.

## Check the base and hat as separate fittings

After body round 18, redistribute stuffing while you can still reach inside. Keep the weighted insert centered against the base and the lower closure flexible enough to lie flat. After round 21, run the tail through all six front loops, pull closed and secure it. Pin the empty hat on the body before adding the beard: its forty-two-stitch rim must reach the intended position without tugging. Different hat and body yarns can have different gauge even with the same hook. Mark the front, add beard, sew the closed nose, then sew the hat brim through body surface stitches, removing every pin.

## Troubleshooting

**It is wider at the top than the bottom.** It was worked from the base up with increases. Work top down.

**It tips over.** No weight, an off-center weight, or a domed base.

**It looks like a cone in a hat.** The brim is above the nose. Pull it down.

**The hat perches.** The opening is too small. Add a round of increases.

**It leans.** Increases stacked in one line, or the weight is off-center.

## Frequently asked

### Does it need eyes?

No. The hat covers where they would be. Eyes under the brim turn it into a different character.

### Can I skip the nose?

You can, but then it looks like a decorated cone. The nose is what makes it a gnome.

### Is it a toy?

With a sealed weight bag and firmly sewn parts it has no small parts, but it has not been safety tested. Keep weighted figures away from small children.

### What should I make next?

A one-piece body with a visible face is [Santa](/crochet-santa-amigurumi-for-beginners/). A cone with branches is the [Christmas tree](/how-to-crochet-a-christmas-tree/).


# How to Crochet a Granny Square Tote

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-granny-square-tote.md



A granny square tote is two flat panels of squares sewn together on three sides, with a firm edge around the opening and two straps. The squares are the easy part. The bag depends on counting: eight squares that match, seams with the same number of stitches on both layers, and straps sewn into something stronger than a chain space.

It is an open bag. A pen will drop through the holes, and rain will not stay out.

US terms. US double crochet is UK treble. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) and the [basic stitches](/basic-crochet-stitches/). The pictures are illustrations. No tote for this page was crocheted, loaded, or carried. Inches below are estimates from a gauge you still have to swatch.

![A cream cotton tote made of granny squares with two straps](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-granny-square-tote-result.jpg "Illustration. It draws a different square motif and a boxed base. This page makes two flat panels with no base. Not a finished sample.")


## How the bag is built

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/tote-assembly.svg" alt="A front panel and a back panel, each four squares in a 2 by 2 grid. Each panel edge is 19 plus 19, which is 38 single crochet. The two panels joined along the sides and bottom with a dashed seam, top open, with two straps. A bar shows one panel's 34-stitch top edge split 8, strap 5, 8, strap 5, 8." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Two flat panels and three seams. Every number on the drawing follows from the 4-round square.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. Make eight squares, four rounds each.
2. Join them into two panels, two squares by two.
3. Put the panels right sides together and join the sides and the bottom. Leave the top open.
4. Work two rounds of single crochet around the opening.
5. Make two straps and sew them through that edge.

The bag is flat. It has no base, so a bulky item takes height out of it. The rounded, boxed bag in the illustration would need a separate base strip, which this page does not cover. The size follows the square: each panel is about twice the side of one square. If your square is 5 inches, the panel is about 10 by 10 and the opening is about 20 inches around. If your square is 4 inches, every one of those numbers shrinks by a fifth.

## Materials

![Worsted cotton, a hook, and a yarn needle](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-granny-square-tote-1.jpg "Illustration. Worsted cotton and tools.")

- **Worsted cotton.** It gives crisp clusters and a firm fabric. Every yarn lengthens when a loaded bag hangs from it, and cotton does not spring back once stretched. That is why the straps are single crochet, not chain, and why the bag should not be overloaded. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A US H/8 (5.0 mm) hook**, or the hook that gives eight matching squares. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Yarn needle, scissors, a few clips or locking markers to hold squares together while you seam.

To estimate yarn, weigh your first finished square and multiply by eight. Then weigh 10 rows of strap and multiply by 22 for two 110-row straps. Add some for the seams and the edge.

## The square

The beginning ch-3 counts as a double crochet. Clusters go into chain spaces, never into the tops of stitches. The center is a chain ring. See [working in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/).

![A finished granny square beside a hook](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-granny-square-tote-2.jpg "Illustration. A granny square with an extra border. It is not this page's 4-round square. Measure the square you make.")

**Round 1:** Ch 4, slip stitch in the first chain to form a ring. Ch 3, 2 dc in the ring, ch 2. (3 dc in the ring, ch 2) 3 times. Slip stitch in the top of the beginning ch-3. (12 dc, 4 corner spaces)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-round-1.svg" alt="Granny square round 1. Four clusters of three double crochet worked into the ring, 12 double crochet in all, with a chain-2 corner between each pair. There are no side spaces yet." width="480" height="639" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1: four clusters with a ch-2 corner between each. The ch 3 counts as the first dc.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** Slip stitch in the next 2 dc and into the ch-2 space. Ch 3, (2 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the same space. *Ch 1, (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the next ch-2 space.* Repeat from * twice more. Ch 1. Slip stitch in the top of the beginning ch-3. (24 dc)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-round-2.svg" alt="Granny square round 2. 24 double crochet in all. Each of the four corners holds 3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc. Each side has 1 chain-1 space, still empty; round 3 fills them." width="480" height="667" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 2: each corner space gets two clusters, and a ch 1 sits between corners.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 3:** Slip stitch into the next ch-2 corner space. Ch 3, (2 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the same space. *Ch 1, 3 dc in the next ch-1 space, ch 1, (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the next corner space.* Repeat from * twice more. Ch 1, 3 dc in the next ch-1 space, ch 1. Slip stitch in the top of the beginning ch-3. (36 dc)

**Round 4:** Slip stitch into the next corner space. Ch 3, (2 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in that corner. *Ch 1, 3 dc in the next space, ch 1, 3 dc in the next space, ch 1, (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the next corner.* Repeat from * twice more. Ch 1, 3 dc in the next space, ch 1, 3 dc in the next space, ch 1. Join and fasten off. (48 dc)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-round-4.svg" alt="Granny square round 4. 48 double crochet in all. Each of the four corners holds 3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc. Each side has 3 chain-1 spaces and 2 clusters worked into the chain-1 spaces of the round below." width="480" height="746" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 4: the corner spaces (ch 2) and the side spaces (ch 1) are worked differently. Mixing them up is the usual reason a square twists.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Checkpoint each round:** round n has 12 × n double crochet, and n clusters along each side if you count the corner halves. A count that is not a multiple of 12 means a cluster was added or missed, often because one went into a stitch top instead of a space.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-side-space.svg" alt="One side of a granny square, round 3 worked over round 2. Correct: the side cluster of round 3 sits in the chain-1 space between round 2's two corner halves, with a chain-1 on each side of it. Common mistake: the cluster goes into the top of a double crochet instead. The gap closes, the side goes stiff, and the square starts to cup." width="480" height="721" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Clusters go in the gaps between the clusters below, never on top of them.</figcaption>
</figure>

Make **8 squares** and weave in the ends as you finish each one. Then stack them. Measure each one flat, without pulling the corners. If one is noticeably smaller, make it again rather than easing it into a seam. Some people turn the square over between rounds to reduce a slight twist. Whichever you choose, do it the same way on all eight.

## Counting the seam edge

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/granny-square-edge.svg" alt="One edge of a 4-round square from corner to corner: a corner chain-2 space, four 3 double crochet clusters with three chain-1 spaces between them, and the next corner space. Two single crochet in each corner space, one in each of the 12 double crochet, and one in each chain-1 space, for 2 plus 12 plus 3 plus 2, 19. The formula 4n plus 3 gives 19 for 4 rounds, 23 for 5, and 27 for 6." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Both squares in a seam have the same edge, so both layers take the same 19 stitches. That is what keeps the seam flat.</figcaption>
</figure>

Along one edge of a 4-round square, work 2 sc in each corner space, 1 sc in each of the 12 dc, and 1 sc in each of the 3 chain-1 spaces: **19**. For a square of n rounds, the edge is 4n + 3. That formula matters if you make bigger squares.

Hold two squares right sides together and single crochet through both layers, matching stitch to stitch. The DK *Crochet: The Complete Step-by-Step Guide* notes that a single-crochet seam forms a ridge, which is why it is worked on the wrong side. Working through only the outer loop of each layer makes that ridge flatter. Count each seam. If the first one is not 19, fix it before you copy it.

## Assembly

**Panels.** Join square A to square B along one edge (19). Join C to D the same way. Then join the AB pair to the CD pair along their long edge: 19 + 19 = **38**. Make the second panel the same.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/sc-seam-two-layers.svg" alt="Two steps. 1: hold the pieces wrong sides together with the edges lined up stitch for stitch. 2: insert the hook through both layers at once, matching stitch to stitch, and work one sc in each pair; work 3 sc in a corner stitch to turn a corner flat." width="480" height="559" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Squares are joined right sides together with one sc in each stitch and space along the edge, 19 per square.</figcaption>
</figure>

Where the long seam crosses the short seam in the middle, four corners meet. Keep to the count: 2 sc in the corner space of each square. Skip there and you leave a hole a finger can pass through. Add stitches and the center bunches.

**Checkpoint:** each panel lies flat and square, about twice the side of one square.

**Bag.** Put the front and back right sides together. Single crochet them together down one side (38), across the bottom (38), and up the other side (38). The two corner spaces at each bottom corner already give you 4 sc in the same place, which turns the corner. Leave the top open and turn the bag right side out. The seam ridges sit inside.

## The top edge

Join yarn at a side seam on the top edge. Single crochet around the opening: 1 sc in each dc, 1 in each chain-1 space, and 1 in each corner space. Each square's top edge takes 4n + 1 = **17**, so the round is 4 × 17 = **68**. Slip stitch to the first sc. Work a second round of 68.

This edge is the structural part of the bag. The straps are sewn into it, not into the squares. If the first round ruffles, you have more than 68. If it draws the opening in, you have fewer.

## Straps

The ch 1 on the straps does not count as a stitch. Make 2.

Chain 6.

**Row 1:** Sc in the second chain from the hook and across. Ch 1, turn. (5)

**Row 2:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (5)

Repeat row 2 until the strap measures about **22 inches** without stretching. At 5 rows to the inch that is about **110 rows**. A 22 inch strap is for carrying by hand. For a shoulder, measure from the bag at your side, up over the shoulder, and back down, and use that length. Make the second strap to the same row count, not the same tape reading.

**Placement.** Each panel's top edge has 34 of the 68 stitches. Leave 8, place a strap end over 5, leave 8, place the other end over 5, and leave 8: 8 + 5 + 8 + 5 + 8 = 34. That puts each strap end 8 stitches in from the side seam, about 2 inches only if the edge is 4 sc to the inch.

Overlap each strap end about an inch inside the bag. Sew it through both rounds of the edge, around the overlap and across it. A strap sewn only through a granny space will tear out.

<div class="warning">

A pen, a key, or a lipstick will fall through the holes. Use the [makeup pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch/) for those. Rain comes through too. A [book sleeve](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/) helps only on a dry day.

</div>

## Before you load it

1. All eight squares match.
2. Every square-to-square seam is 19, and every panel edge is 38.
3. The top edge is 68 on both rounds.
4. Both straps have the same number of rows.
5. Each strap end is sewn through both edge rounds, 8 stitches in from a side seam.

## Make the joins visible before sewing straps

For a four-round square, a complete side includes twelve dc and three side ch-1 spaces. The two corner allocations determine whether it has nineteen seam positions or seventeen top-edge positions; they are not interchangeable. Mark the center seam of each two-square top edge and divide the full opening into two thirty-four-position panels. At each strap overlap, sew through both solid edging rounds and several underlying crochet stitches without crossing the opening. A one-inch overlap is longer than two sc rounds at the example gauge, so its lower end must also be anchored to solid cluster stitches rather than dangling behind a chain gap.

## Troubleshooting

**Squares come out different sizes.** Your tension changed between them. Redo the odd one. A 4 inch edge eased into a 5 inch edge puckers the whole panel.

**A square cups.** Clusters went into stitch tops, or the hook is small for the yarn.

**A seam puckers or ripples.** The two layers took different counts. Pull it out and match 19 to 19.

**There is a hole where four corners meet.** You skipped the corner space on one square. Work 2 sc there.

**The opening ruffles or draws in.** The edge count is off. It should be 68.

**The straps stretch with use.** Chain straps, or a loose single crochet. Shorten them by rows rather than knotting them.

**The bag sags at the bottom.** It is a flat bag with no base, carrying weight on open fabric. Lighter loads, or a different bag design.

## Care

Empty the bag. Hand wash cool. Dry flat, including the straps. Wet cotton stretches at the seams, so do not load the bag until it is dry.

## Frequently asked

**Can I use two colors?**

Yes. Change color at the start of a round, and keep every yarn the same weight. A thinner cotton makes a smaller square, and it will not join cleanly.

**Will it hold books?**

It has not been load-tested, so this page makes no claim about how much it carries. Anything that fits through a granny space will fall out, and rain or a leaking bottle in the same bag will reach the covers.

**Can I make it from two big squares instead of eight?**

Yes. Work both squares to the same number of rounds, n. Seam three sides at 4n + 3 per edge. The top edge is 2 × (4n + 1). Work the strap spacing out from that number the same way as above.

**Can I sell totes?**

Finished bags, yes. Do not republish these rounds as your pattern.

## Next

The same square, stopped at four rounds and worked in Halloween colors, is the [coaster set](/free-pattern-halloween-coasters/). The same shell and corner worked in rows instead of rounds makes the [lace bandana](/how-to-crochet-a-lace-bandana/). A closed cotton pocket for the things this tote would lose is the [makeup pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch/).


# How a Hooded Scarf Is Folded

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-hooded-scarf.md



A hooded scarf joins a folded hood to the center of a long scarf. The hood has three different edges: the crown seam, the face opening, and the neck edge attached to the scarf. This guide sizes the rectangles from a hood that fits the wearer. It has not been crocheted or wear-tested; the numerical example is a planning example, not a graded adult size.

US terms. US half double crochet (hdc) is UK half treble. Ch 2 does not count as a stitch on this page. Work the first hdc into the first real stitch and the last into the last real stitch, ignoring the turning chain.

## Materials and measurements

Use a smooth yarn suitable for a scarf, its matching hook, a tape measure, a tapestry needle, removable markers, and a hood that already fits the intended wearer. Swatch and wash/dry according to the yarn label before measuring. Record hdc stitches per inch and rows per inch separately. Estimate yarn from the combined hood and scarf area using a weighed swatch in the same stitch; no finished sample yardage is available.

Lay the reference hood sideways, folded at the center back. Measure **D**, from its center-back fold to the face edge, and **H**, from neck to crown. A rectangle hood is box-shaped; a shaped coat hood may not be reproduced by these two numbers alone. Measure the reference face-edge length and check that it is compatible with about 2H for this construction. Include any desired seam allowance in your planned rectangle. Baste and try the new hood rather than assume the coat measurements guarantee fit.

## The fold and the three edges

Let W be the rectangle width in the stitch direction and H its height in the row direction. Choose W = 2D. Fold W in half by bringing its two short H-length edges together. The folded piece is D deep and H tall. The fold is center back.

- **Crown:** the two upper half-width edges are sewn together from the fold to the face. The seam is D long.
