- **Face:** the two original short edges remain unsewn. Together their exposed length is 2H. This is an open front, not a closed hat brim that must be pulled over the head.
- **Neck:** the two lower half-width edges remain unsewn and open out to a W-length neck edge. This edge attaches to the scarf.

Adding stitches changes W and therefore depth D. Adding rows changes H and therefore face-edge length 2H. Head circumference alone does not determine either dimension. The original article confused the neck edge W with the face edge 2H; do not use that earlier diagnosis to resize a hood.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/hood-fold-corrected.svg" alt="Flat rectangle W wide and H tall folds down its center. Folded hood is W divided by two deep and H tall. Sew the crown edge only; leave the two H-length face edges and W-length neck edge open." width="800" height="480" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Construction diagram: crown seam, open face, and scarf attachment edge are different edges.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Calculate your hood rectangle

Stitches across = 2 × D × your stitches per inch. Foundation chains = stitch count + 2. Rows = H × your rows per inch, rounded to whole rows. Measure the actual fabric as you go; estimates are not more authoritative than the tape.

**Planning example only:** if a suitable reference hood measures D = 10 inches and H = 12 inches, and the washed swatch is 3 hdc and 2.5 rows per inch, W = 20 inches, so work 60 hdc starting with ch 62, for about 30 rows. Folded depth is 10 inches, face-edge length about 24 inches, and neck edge about 20 inches. These are derived dimensions, not a tested fit or an adult size recommendation.

## Crochet the hood

**Row 1:** Chain your calculated stitch count plus 2. Hdc in the third chain from the hook and each chain across. (Your calculated count; 60 in the example.)

**Row 2:** Ch 2, turn. Hdc in each real stitch across. (Same count.)

**Every later row:** Repeat row 2 until the relaxed fabric measures your planned H. Mark the first and last real stitch and count each row. Fasten off with a sewing tail long enough for the crown seam, or use a separate yarn length for sewing.

Lay it flat and mark the midpoint of both W-length edges. Bring the H-length short edges together without twisting, public faces together. The midpoint becomes center back. Baste one paired W/2 edge as the crown, matching stitch to stitch. Leave the other paired W/2 edge open at the neck and both H edges open at the face. Turn it right side out and try it on before permanent sewing. The crown should cover the top of the head without tugging and the neck should reach its intended position. If depth is wrong, revise the stitch-direction width; if height is wrong, revise the row count. Neither change guarantees a fit until tried.

## Crochet the scarf

Choose a scarf width and length with a tape. For an illustrative 8 × 60 inch scarf at 3 hdc and 2.5 rows per inch, work 24 hdc across from ch 26 for about 150 rows. Substitute your own washed gauge and intended dimensions.

**Row 1:** Ch 26 for the example; hdc in the third chain and across. (24)

**Row 2 and later:** Ch 2, turn, hdc in each real stitch. (24) Stop at the intended relaxed length; fasten off and weave the tails.

For a different width, calculate width × stitches per inch and add two foundation chains. For a different length, calculate length × rows per inch as a forecast, then measure. Do not seam the scarf into a loop.

## Attach the hood to the scarf

Once the hood fits and the crown is permanently sewn, open its neck edge out: it has the original W length. Mark its center-back fold and the center of the scarf length. Lay the scarf flat and match those centers along **one long scarf edge**. Distribute half the hood neck to each side of the scarf-center marker. The hood's face ends are the two ends of this attachment span.

Baste the hood neck to the scarf's long edge without gathering or stretching either piece. This matches hood stitch-direction fabric to scarf row ends, so the counts need not be identical. At the example gauges, 20 inches of hood neck contains 60 stitches and 20 inches of scarf edge contains 50 row ends. Distribute five row ends across six hood stitches rather than trying a one-to-one match. Keep both ends of the scarf free and check nothing is twisted.

Try the basted assembly on, then whipstitch with relaxed tension through the paired edges. Weave ends in separate directions and inspect the seam from both sides. No drawcord, button, or ornament is needed.

## Check the result before finishing

1. Crown seam closes the top only; the face remains open.
2. Center back matches the scarf center and both scarf ends hang evenly.
3. Hood depth and height suit the wearer without pulling the scarf upward.
4. Attachment seam lies flat without gathering the scarf or leaving loose loops.
5. All markers and basting threads are removed and ends secured.

## Troubleshooting

**It is too shallow front to back.** W was too short; more rows only increase H. Remake or redesign the width after checking the reference.

**It is too short neck to crown.** H was too short; add rows before closing the crown.

**The face was sewn shut.** The seam followed the H-length short edges rather than the top D-length crown edge. Open it and identify the marked edges again.

**The scarf bunches below the hood.** The attachment was stretched or pulled tight, or the two edge lengths differ. Unpick and distribute equal measured lengths.

**It sits over one ear.** The center-back fold was not matched to the scarf center, or the scarf twisted during sewing.

## Care and next steps

Follow the yarn label. Support the wet piece and dry flat with the hood opened out. Recheck fit after washing. This is a sizing and construction guide; a sample must still be made and worn before reporting a confirmed size or yardage.

For the scarf stitch alone, use the [beginner scarf](/how-to-crochet-a-beginner-scarf/). For a smaller fitted project, see the [ear warmer](/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer/) or [chunky beanie](/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie/).


# How to Crochet a Lace Bandana

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-lace-bandana.md



This bandana is a granny triangle: rows of three-double-crochet shells separated by chain spaces, with one corner that becomes the point. What surprises most people is where it starts. The first stitches sit in the middle of the long edge, not at the point. Every row wraps around the one before it like a V, so the long edge grows at both ends while the point moves away from it. Once that picture is clear, the row counts, the sizing, and most of the mistakes make sense.

US terms. US double crochet is UK treble. Names are on the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. The 16 inch long edge below is a common stopping point for the math, not a fitted size, so keep going until the triangle fits you.

![A cream crochet lace bandana worn tied at the nape](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-lace-bandana-result.jpg "Illustration. A worn cotton bandana with ties at the nape. The drawn mesh is not a stitch map of this pattern, and no sample was made.")


## How the triangle is built

Row 1 is a tiny V: two shells with a ch-2 corner between them, all worked into a single chain. That chain is the center of what becomes the long edge.

Every later row starts at one end of the long edge, works down one side to the corner, puts a new corner in the corner space, climbs the other side, and ends at the other end of the long edge. Then you turn. Each row adds one shell to each side and pushes the corner one row further away. The ends of all the rows line up into one straight edge. The corners line up into the point.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bandana-construction.svg" alt="Diagram of nested V-shaped rows. The first row sits at the middle of a straight top edge. Each larger V has its ends on that edge and its corner further down, and the corners stack into the point. A note says that with a square point the depth is half the long edge, so 16 inches gives 8." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The start is the middle of the long edge. The point is the last corner you work, not the first.</figcaption>
</figure>

The corner here, (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in a ch-2 space, is the same corner a granny square uses, and on a square it makes a right angle. If your point blocks square, the triangle is half a square cut corner to corner. The long edge is that square's diagonal, and the depth from the long edge to the point is half of it. A 16 inch long edge gives a point about **8 inches** deep, and each short side is about 16 ÷ 1.414 = **11.3 inches**.

That has a consequence the earlier version of this page missed. You cannot stop at a 16 inch long edge and get a shallow 5 inch bandana. With this corner, depth follows the long edge. If the point would hang too far down your neck, make the long edge shorter and the ties longer.

## Yarn, hook, and tools

![Cream yarn and a hook](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-lace-bandana-1.jpg "Illustration. DK cotton and a hook.")

- **DK-weight cotton.** It keeps the shells crisp and does not fuzz into the spaces. It also does not spring back after it is pulled, so block it to size rather than tugging it there. Worsted makes a stiff triangle that sits like a napkin. Thread needs a much smaller hook and many more rows. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook** as a starting point for DK, or the size that keeps the shells open without the fabric looking like a net. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One stitch marker.** Move it into the ch-2 corner every row. Finding the corner is the only hard part of this project, and the marker removes the guess.
- Tape measure, yarn needle, rust-proof pins for blocking.

No gauge is given. The long edge is measured on the work instead, so a different gauge changes the row count, not the fit.

## Measure the long edge you need

Tie a scrap of yarn around your head where the long edge will sit, across the forehead or over the crown, and mark the two points where you want the triangle to end and the ties to begin. Measure the scrap between those marks. That is the long edge.

About 16 inches with 12 inch ties is a common stopping point. Hair volume and placement change it. Before you start, also hold the tape from the long-edge line toward the back of your neck: the point will hang about half the long edge, 8 inches for a 16 inch edge. Decide now whether that suits you.

## Stitches and where they go

- **Shell:** 3 dc in the same place.
- **Corner:** (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the ch-2 corner space.
- **Turning chain:** ch 3 counts as the first dc of the edge shell. You add 2 dc in the same stitch to finish that shell, and the row's last shell goes into the top of the previous row's turning chain. [The turning chain](/crochet-turning-chain/) explains why counting it and also skipping it makes one edge grow faster.

Inside the row, every shell goes into a chain space, never into the top of a double crochet. Only the two edge shells go into stitches.

![Close view of cream granny clusters](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-lace-bandana-2.jpg "Illustration of the cluster texture. Not a stitch map. In this pattern every inner shell sits in a chain space.")

## The triangle, row by row

**Row 1:** Ch 4. In the 4th chain from the hook, work 2 dc, ch 2, 3 dc. Turn. (6 dc counting the skipped chains as the first, 1 ch-2 corner)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bandana-step-1.svg" alt="A chain of 4. Every stitch of row 1 goes into the 4th chain from the hook: 2 double crochet, a chain-2 corner, then 3 double crochet. The 3 skipped chains stand up as the first double crochet, so the row has 6 double crochet and one corner." width="480" height="514" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. All of row 1 goes into one chain. The 3 skipped chains count as the first dc on this page.</figcaption>
</figure>

Mark the ch-2. You should see two shells and one corner. A third shell means extra stitches went into the chain.

**Row 2:** Ch 3, 2 dc in the same stitch. Ch 1. (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc) in the corner. Ch 1. 3 dc in the top of the turning chain. Turn. (12 dc, 2 ch-1, 1 corner)

The earlier version of this page gave 9 dc for this row. Four shells are 12.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bandana-row-count.svg" alt="Row 2 drawn as four 3 double crochet shells with chain-1 dots after the first and before the last, and a chain-2 corner in the middle, for 12 double crochet and two chain-1 spaces. A table gives row 1 as 6 double crochet, row 2 as 12, row 3 as 18, row 4 as 24, and row n as 6n with 2 times n minus 1 chain-1 spaces." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Each row adds 6 double crochet: one shell at each end of the long edge. The corner only moves.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Row 3:** Ch 3, 2 dc in the same stitch. Ch 1, 3 dc in the next ch-1 space, ch 1. Corner in the ch-2. Ch 1, 3 dc in the next ch-1 space, ch 1. 3 dc in the top of the turning chain. Turn. (18 dc, 4 ch-1)

**Row 4 and after:** Ch 3, 2 dc in the same stitch, ch 1. Work (3 dc in the next ch-1 space, ch 1) in each space up to the corner. Corner. Ch 1. Work (3 dc in the next ch-1 space, ch 1) in each remaining space. 3 dc in the top of the turning chain. Turn.

Row n has n shells on each side of the corner, counting the corner halves, 6n double crochet, and 2(n − 1) chain-1 spaces. Row 10 is 60 dc and 18 spaces. Count the shells on each side of the marker before you turn. They should be equal, and equal to the row number.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bandana-step-2.svg" alt="The bandana drawn as nested V shapes hanging from the long edge. Each row is a V with its corner at the point. Row 4 is drawn in full: four shells of three double crochet on each arm, counting the corner halves, one ch-2 corner at the point, and six ch-1 spaces. Row n has 6n double crochet: 6, 12, 18, 24." width="480" height="504" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. Every row adds one shell to each arm and keeps one corner, so the counts go 6, 12, 18, 24.</figcaption>
</figure>

### Forecasting rows and yarn from your own swatch

Lay the triangle flat after row 6 and measure the long edge, tip to tip, without stretching. Each row adds about the same width at the two ends, so the long edge grows roughly in step with the row count.

Suppose six rows measure 7 inches. That 7 is an invented example, not a measurement. A 16 inch edge would need about 6 × 16 ÷ 7 = 13.7 rows, so plan on 14 and start measuring after row 12. Treat the forecast as a reason to measure, not as a count to trust over the tape.

Yarn grows faster than width, because every row is longer than the last. Through row N you have worked 3 × N × (N + 1) double crochet: 126 through row 6, and 630 through row 14. So 14 rows need about five times the yarn of the first six. Weigh the triangle at row 6, multiply by N × (N + 1) ÷ 42, and compare with the weight on the label. Leave extra for the ties and the chain spaces, which that count leaves out.

## Ties

Join yarn at one tip of the long edge, working through the last stitch and the turning chain together so the tie is not hanging from one strand. Chain until the chain measures about 12 inches without stretching. Slip stitch in the second chain from the hook and in each chain back to the tip. Fasten off and weave the end into the edge. Repeat at the other tip.

The slip-stitch pass thickens the tie and stops it from stretching and twisting like a bare chain. If you want longer ties because you shortened the long edge, change the chain, not the triangle.

## Block and check the shape

Wet the triangle, press out the water in a towel, and pin it flat with the long edge straight and at your measured length. Use the corner of a sheet of paper to check that the point is square. Measure both short sides. They should match, because each has the same number of shells. Let it dry pinned. Do not iron acrylic. [Weaving ends and blocking](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/) has the method.

Before you call it finished:

1. The shells on each side of the corner are equal, and equal to the row count.
2. The two short sides measure within a small fraction of each other.
3. The long edge lies straight when unpinned, not curved inward.
4. The point is a corner, not a rounded tip.

## Mark the long-edge ends and the single point

Use one marker in the ch-2 point and a different marker in the top of the beginning ch 3 at the long-edge end. At the next row start, ch 3 and two dc in the first stitch make the first edge cluster. At the far end, three dc go into the marked turning-chain top. All interior clusters belong in chain spaces. Row 4 has twenty-four dc, six ch-1 spaces and one ch-2 point; compare the two halves before adding row 5. Remove markers before washing, then block gently to the measured head fit without forcing a different angle into the point.

## Troubleshooting

**One side has more shells than the other.** You skipped a space or worked a shell into the top of a stitch. Count back to the row where the sides last matched and pull out to there.

**The long edge draws into a curve.** The ch-3 is shorter than your double crochet, so each row end is short and the edge cannot reach its full width. Work the turning chain looser. Pinning hides the curve only until the next wash.

**The triangle leans.** The corner moved into a ch-1 space on some row. The corner belongs only in the ch-2 space. The marker prevents this.

**The point is blunt or rounded.** The corner chain grew, or you worked extra stitches there. Keep exactly (3 dc, ch 2, 3 dc). A small rounding is normal before blocking.

**The point hangs into your collar.** That is the geometry, not a mistake: depth is about half the long edge. Make the next one with a shorter long edge and longer ties. Do not cut the point off.

**The shells look crowded.** Go up one hook size and start again. Pulling a finished triangle to loosen it distorts the point.

## Wearing and care

Put the long edge at the forehead or over the crown, the point toward the nape, and tie the ends. The open stitches catch earrings and small hair clips, so take those out first.

Hand wash cool. Dry flat, pinned if the edge needs straightening again. Do not machine dry. Cotton can shrink, and a shorter long edge means ties that no longer meet comfortably.

## Frequently asked

**Why does my triangle start in the middle of the top edge?**

Because that is how this construction works. The first chain is the center of the long edge, and the point is the last corner you make.

**Can I make it deeper without making it wider?**

Not with this corner. A square point fixes the depth at about half the long edge. A deeper triangle with the same long edge needs a sharper corner, which is a different pattern.

**Is this a kerchief or a hair scarf?**

It is a triangle you tie on. The name depends on where you put the long edge. The rows do not change.

**Can I sell finished bandanas?**

Yes. Do not republish the rows.

## Next

The same shell and corner, worked in rounds with four corners, is a granny square. The [granny square tote](/how-to-crochet-a-granny-square-tote/) counts those edges and joins the squares. For a flat piece that must keep points crisp, try the [snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/).


# How to Crochet a Makeup Pouch

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch.md



Makeup at the bottom of a bag picks up crumbs, and an open tube leaves a mark on whatever it touches. A small drawstring pouch keeps everything together so you can lift it out in one go. This one is a single-crochet rectangle, folded and seamed, with an eyelet round and a cord at the top.

The construction is simple. The sizing is where most pouches go wrong, in two ways. A flat pouch has to be wider than the widest item, because thick items take up width too. And a drawstring needs spare fabric above the contents to gather. A pouch cut to the exact height of the tallest tube will not close over it.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No pouch for this page was crocheted or packed. The item sizes below are an invented example.

![A small cotton drawstring pouch](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch-result.jpg "Illustration. A closed rectangle with a tie at the top. Not a tested pouch.")


## How the pouch is built

Crochet a rectangle twice as long as the pouch is tall. Fold it in half so the fold is the bottom. Seam both sides with single crochet through both layers, on the wrong side. Turn it right side out. Around the opening, work an eyelet round and a round above it. Thread a cord through the eyelets and pull.

Seaming on the wrong side matters. A single crochet seam leaves a ridge. Turned inside, the ridge is hidden, and the outside shows only a neat fold line at each side.

## Measure what goes in

Lay out what you will carry and find three numbers:

1. **The widest item**, lying flat. Example: a palette, 5.75 inches.
2. **The thickest item.** Example: a compact, 0.75 inch.
3. **The tallest item**, standing. Example: a brush or tube, 3.5 inches.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/pouch-item-sizing.svg" alt="A folded pouch, 28 stitches wide and 25 rows tall per side, with items inside and a drawstring near the top. Width: 5.75 widest plus 0.75 thickest plus 0.5 ease is 7 inches, 28 stitches. Height: 3.5 tallest plus 1.5 for the gather is 5 inches per side, 50 rows in all." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Thickness counts as width. Gathering needs height. Both come from the contents.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Width.** Widest item + thickest item + about half an inch of ease. A flat pouch with a 0.75 inch compact inside bulges, and the bulge takes fabric from the width. The same rule sizes the [phone case](/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case/).

**Height.** Tallest item + about 1.5 inches. The eyelets sit near the top, and gathering pulls the fabric above the contents into the middle. Without that spare height, the cord closes on top of the tallest tube and leaves a gap.

## Yarn, hook, and gauge

![Worsted cotton, a hook, and a short crochet chain for a tie](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch-1.jpg "Illustration. Cotton. The tie is a chain.")

- **Smooth worsted cotton.** It washes. It also stains: makeup oil and dark lipstick will not come out of pale cotton just because it is cotton. Pick a color that can wear a mark. Fuzzy yarn grabs powder and pills. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the hook that gives dense fabric. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Yarn needle, scissors.

Single crochet is the whole fabric. Taller stitches leave holes, and a pressed powder will dust through them. Swatch and measure; [gauge](/crochet-gauge/) turns inches into stitches. The example uses 4 stitches and 5 rows per inch.

## The worked example

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Width | 5.75 + 0.75 + 0.5 | 7.0 in, 28 sc, chain 29 |
| Height per side | 3.5 + 1.5 | 5.0 in |
| Rows | 5.0 × 2 sides × 5 rows | 50 rows |
| Opening | 28 + 28 | 56 sts, even |
| Cord | 14 in around + 2 tails of 5 in | 24 in |

## Stage 1: the rectangle

Chain 29. Keep the chain loose. The foundation edge becomes half of the opening, and a tight chain draws it in.

**Row 1:** Sc in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Ch 1, turn. (28)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/makeup-pouch-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 29. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 28 sc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Chain 29 for 28 sc. The foundation edge becomes half of the opening, so keep it loose.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rows 2–50:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (28)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The first sc of each row goes into the edge stitch, not the ch 1. That keeps 28 stitches, and the opening count of 56.</figcaption>
</figure>

The ch 1 does not count as a stitch. Count every 10 rows. An extra stitch makes one side seam longer than the other, and the pouch twists.

**Checkpoint:** fold the rectangle in half and set the contents on it. The fabric should close around them with the edges meeting and about an inch and a half above the tallest item. If not, add rows now.

## Stage 2: seam

Fold so row 1 meets row 50, right sides together. The fold is the bottom. Join yarn at the fold on one side and single crochet through both layers, one stitch in each row end, up to the opening. Fasten off. Repeat on the other side. Start each seam in the fold itself, not one row above it, or powder leaks out of the corner. Turn the pouch right side out.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/makeup-pouch-step-2.svg" alt="Drawn to scale. A flat piece 28 sc, 7 in wide and 50 rows, 10 in tall, folded at the dashed line into a pocket. Right sides together. Sc through both layers up each side, one sc per row end." width="480" height="455" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. Drawn to scale: a 7 by 10 inch rectangle folds into a 7 by 5 inch pouch. Row 1 and row 50 together make the 56-stitch opening.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Stage 3: eyelets and the top round

Join yarn at one side seam at the opening. You will work around both top edges: 28 stitches along row 50 and 28 along the foundation chain, 56 in all.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/sc-seam-two-layers.svg" alt="Two steps. 1: hold the pieces wrong sides together with the edges lined up stitch for stitch. 2: insert the hook through both layers at once, matching stitch to stitch, and work one sc in each pair; work 3 sc in a corner stitch to turn a corner flat." width="480" height="559" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Each side seam is one sc in each row end, through both layers, starting in the fold itself.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Eyelet round:** Ch 1, (sc in the next stitch, ch 1, skip 1) 28 times. Slip stitch to the first sc. (28 sc, 28 ch-1 spaces)

**Top round:** Ch 1, sc in each sc and in each ch-1 space. Slip stitch to the first sc. (56) Fasten off.

The eyelet round needs an even count, because each repeat uses two stitches. Work the counted fifty-six-stitch setup round described below before making the eyelets; do not add a stitch halfway through the eyelet repeat. The [drawstring bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/) shows what an odd count does to the last hole.

## Stage 4: the cord

The cord goes once around the opening, 14 inches, and leaves two tails to knot, about 5 inches each: 24 inches. Chain to 24 inches without stretching, then slip stitch back along the chain so it does not stretch in use.

Thread it in and out of the eyelets, starting and finishing at the center front. Knot each tail so it cannot pull back through.

![A chain woven through eyelets at the top of the pouch](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch-4.jpg "Illustration. The tie starts and ends at the front.")

**Checkpoint:** pack the pouch and pull the cord. The top should close above the tallest item with no gap you can see into.

<div class="warning">

Do not put nail polish, a leaking bottle, or a wet brush in this pouch. Cotton holds the stain and passes the liquid on to whatever it touches. This is a dry organizer, not a wash bag.

</div>

## Establish the opening count before making holes

After seaming and turning, mark the two side folds. Join yarn and work a plain setup round: one sc in each of the twenty-eight top-row stitches and one in each of the twenty-eight used foundation-chain positions. Exclude the skipped foundation chain and do not add stitches into the side-seam ridges. Join to the first sc (56). Work the eyelet round over these fifty-six stitch tops, then restore fifty-six in the top round. If the setup count differs, fix it before the eyelets rather than inserting an extra corner stitch partway through the hole repeat. Yarn is porous: keep loose powder and liquids in their own sealed containers.

## Troubleshooting

**The top will not close over the items.** Not enough height for the gather. Add rows, or carry fewer tall things.

**It bulges and the seams strain.** The width did not include the thickness of the thickest item.

**The pouch twists.** The rows gained or lost stitches, so one side seam is longer.

**One spot will not gather.** An odd count on the eyelet round left a missing hole.

**The cord stretches and the top reopens.** Slip stitch back along the chain.

**Powder escapes at a bottom corner.** A seam started above the fold. Close the corner with a few stitches.

## Care

Empty it. Hand wash cool. A makeup stain may not come out. Dry flat with the cord pulled loose so the eyelets do not dry crushed. Refill only when dry.

## Frequently asked

**Can I line it?**

A cloth lining stops crumbs and some stains. Cut it from the finished pouch, not from these numbers. This page does not include a lining pattern.

**What about a bigger pouch?**

Redo the table from your own three measurements. The method does not change.

**Can I sell pouches?**

Finished pouches, yes. Do not republish the rows as your pattern.

## Next

A pouch with a zipper instead of a cord is the [pumpkin zipper pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-zipper-pouch/). The same rectangle with a strap is the [strap bag](/how-to-crochet-a-strap-bag/).


# How to Crochet a Market Bag

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-market-bag.md



A plastic sack splits at the handle. A cloth tote with no base falls over in the cart. A crochet market bag sits between the two: a solid base that holds its shape, open mesh sides that weigh almost nothing and expand around what you put in, and a firm band at the top where the handles attach.

Three things decide whether it works. The base has to stop increasing at the right moment, or the bag turns into a cone. The mesh has to keep its count, or the sides flare. And the handles have to go into the solid band, spaced evenly, or one side carries all the weight. This page covers each.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. US double crochet is UK treble. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No bag for this page was crocheted, filled, or carried. Inches are estimates from the example gauge.

![A natural cotton market bag with mesh sides and two handles](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-market-bag-result.jpg "Illustration. Solid base, open mesh, two handles. Not a load test.")

<div class="warning">

There is no load rating for this bag, and this page gives no weight limit. Cotton mesh stretches. If the handles pull thin, the holes grow long, or the base pulls into a cone, take items out.

</div>


## How the bag is built

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/market-bag-profile.svg" alt="The bag in profile: a flat base of 96 single crochet, about 7.6 inches across, one even round, mesh rounds of 48 double crochet and 48 chain-1 spaces, and a top band of 96 single crochet. Beside it, half the band, 48 stitches, split 10, handle end 8, 12, handle end 8, 10, repeated on the other half." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Solid where it needs strength, open where it needs to be light. The handles go only into the solid band.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Base.** A flat circle grows by six stitches a round. Each round adds one stitch height to the radius, and a circle's edge is 2 × 3.14 times its radius, so the edge gains about 6.28 stitch heights. In single crochet that comes out near six stitches. The exact figure depends on how tall your stitches are for their width, which is why one person's circle lies flat and another's cups or ruffles slightly. The [coaster page](/free-pattern-halloween-coasters/) shows how to correct either. Sixteen rounds give 96 stitches. At 4 per inch that is 24 inches around and 24 ÷ 3.14 ≈ **7.6 inches** across.

**Turn.** One round with no increases. That is where the flat base starts to become a wall. Keep increasing into the mesh and the sides lean outward and the bag becomes a cone.

**Mesh.** Each repeat is a double crochet and a chain 1 over two stitches of the round below: 96 stitches make 48 mesh units. Double crochet posts are tall and thin, and the chain spaces between them open sideways under load. That is why the bag expands around a melon and why it stretches.

**Band.** Back to solid single crochet for three rounds. It stops the top from stretching and gives the handles something solid to hold.

## Yarn and hook

![Natural cotton yarn, a hook, and a yarn needle](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-market-bag-1.jpg "Illustration. Yarn, hook, and needle.")

- **Smooth worsted cotton.** Every fiber stretches under a load. Acrylic mesh tends to stay stretched once it has gone. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives the base gauge. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Stitch markers, yarn needle, scissors.

Swatch the single crochet and measure. [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/) sets the base diameter. Measure the real mesh as you go; the 2 rounds per inch figure is a planning number, not a measurement.

## The worked example

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Base | 6 × 16 rounds | 96 sc |
| Around | 96 ÷ 4 | 24 in |
| Across | 24 ÷ 3.14 | about 7.6 in |
| Mesh | 96 ÷ 2 | 48 dc and 48 ch-1 |
| Mesh height | about 12 in × 2 rounds per in | about 24 rounds, measured as you go |
| Band | 48 dc + 48 spaces | 96 sc |
| Handle placement, per half | 10 + 8 + 12 + 8 + 10 | 48 |

## Stage 1: the base

Joined rounds: slip stitch to the first stitch, ch 1 (does not count), then work the round.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The base starts in a magic ring.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (2 sc in next, sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 4:** (2 sc in next, sc 2) 6 times. (24)

Keep going, with one more plain stitch between increases each round. Round 8 is 48. Round 12 is 72. **Round 16 is 96.**

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/market-bag-step-1.svg" alt="Base: rounds 1 to 17, as bars sized by stitch count. Round 1: 6, Round 2: 12, Round 3: 18, Round 4: 24, Round 5: 30, Round 6: 36, Round 7: 42, Round 8: 48, Round 9: 54, Round 10: 60, Round 11: 66, Round 12: 72, Round 13: 78, Round 14: 84, Round 15: 90, Round 16: 96, Round 17: 96. Round 16 is 96, about 7.6 in across. Round 17 is even and starts the turn." width="480" height="668" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Sixteen rounds of six increases each. Round 16 is 96.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Checkpoint:** lay the base flat. It should be flat, not cupped or ruffled, and about 7.6 inches across. Set your usual shopping on it. If the footprint is bigger, keep increasing by six a round. The final count must be even.

To keep the edge round rather than hexagonal, move the increases: in alternate rounds, start the repeat with half the plain stitches, put the increase in the middle, and end with the rest. [Increase and decrease](/crochet-increase-decrease/) explains staggering.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/increase-stagger.svg" alt="Correct: the increase in each round is moved, for example by working half the plain stitches before the first increase on alternate rounds, so the edge stays round. Mistake: every increase lands directly above the last one, which builds six corners and the circle becomes a hexagon." width="480" height="679" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Sixteen rounds of stacked increases make a clear hexagon. Moving them keeps the base round.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Stage 2: the turn

**Round 17:** Sc in each stitch. (96)

Put a marker in this round. Measure the side height from here.

![A flat cotton circle in single crochet](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-market-bag-2.jpg "Illustration. The base is solid single crochet. Measure it before the mesh starts.")

## Stage 3: the mesh

Decide now whether the starting ch 3 counts as a double crochet. On this page it does. If you count the ch 3 and then also work a dc into the same place, you add a mesh unit every round.

**Mesh round 1:** Ch 3 (counts as dc), ch 1, skip the next stitch. (Dc in the next stitch, ch 1, skip the next stitch) around. Slip stitch to the top of the ch 3. (48 dc, 48 ch-1 spaces)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/market-bag-step-2.svg" alt="Mesh round 1 on 96, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 17: undefined, 96 stitches. Mesh round 1: (dc in next, ch 1, skip 1) around: 48 dc, 48 stitches. Count the dc every few rounds. 47 or 49 means a unit was lost or added." width="480" height="563" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 3. The mesh: 48 dc with a chain space over every skipped stitch. The ch 3 counts as the first dc.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Mesh round 2:** Slip stitch into the first ch-1 space. Ch 3 (counts as dc), ch 1. (Dc in the next ch-1 space, ch 1) around. Slip stitch to the top of the ch 3. (48 dc, 48 ch-1 spaces)

Repeat mesh round 2 until the side, measured from the marker, is the height you want. The example is about 12 inches. Count the double crochet every few rounds. 47 or 49 means a unit was dropped or added, and the bag will twist or flare.

If the mesh rim comes out much wider than the base, switch to a hook one size smaller for every remaining mesh round.

A key or a loose berry will find a hole. Put small things inside something larger.

## Stage 4: the band

**Round 1:** Ch 1, (sc in the next dc, sc in the next ch-1 space) around. Slip stitch to the first sc. (96)

**Rounds 2–3:** Ch 1, sc in each stitch. Slip stitch to the first sc. (96)

## Stage 5: the handles

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/market-handle-placement.svg" alt="Eight-stitch handles match the eight-position attachment spans." width="800" height="480" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Eight-stitch handles match the eight-position attachment spans.</figcaption>
</figure>


Make 2. Chain 9.

**Row 1:** Sc in the second chain from the hook and across. Ch 1, turn. (8)

**Next rows:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (8)

Work to about 18 inches, about 90 rows at 5 rows per inch. Pin one handle on, put some weight in the bag, and try it in your hand and on your shoulder before you decide the length.

**Placement.** Put a marker at the start of the band round and another at stitch 49. Each half has 48 stitches. On each half: skip 10, sew a handle end over 8, skip 12, sew the other end over 8, skip 10. The two handles sit opposite each other, and the four ends are spaced the same way on both sides, so neither handle pulls the band crooked.

Overlap each handle end on the inside of the band and sew through every one of its 8 stitches across all three band rounds, then back the other way. Keep the sewing out of the mesh. A handle sewn into a chain space pulls that space into a long tear.

![Handle ends sewn across the solid top band](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-market-bag-4.jpg "Illustration. Each handle end spans many stitches. There is no weight number on this bag.")

## Keep handle width consistent with its attachment span

Each revised handle is eight sc wide and starts with ch 9. On each forty-eight-stitch half of the band, mark 10 unworked positions, an eight-position attachment, 12 unworked positions, another eight-position attachment, and 10 unworked positions. The full round has four attachment spans. Overlap each end inside the bag and sew across all three solid band rounds plus solid stitches below where available; do not rely on a single chain space. Match the two handles by row count, then check both attachments lie at the same height. Test gently with light contents while supporting the base; this does not establish a load rating.

## Troubleshooting

**The bag is a cone.** Increases continued past the base, or the mesh grew. Stop increasing at round 16 and count the mesh.

**The bag twists as it grows.** That is common in joined mesh rounds, because each round starts a little to one side. Count the units. If the count is right, a slight lean is cosmetic.

**The base is a hexagon.** Increases stacked in the same place. Stagger them.

**One handle sits lower.** Placement counts differ between the halves. Recount from the markers.

**The handles thinned.** Cotton stretched under weight. Carry less, or make the handles wider.

## Care

Empty it. Hand wash cool. Dry flat on a towel with the handles straight. Drying it hanging by a handle stretches that handle.

## Frequently asked

**How much will it hold?**

This page does not say. There is no tested load rating. Watch the fabric and take items out if it distorts.

**Can I use acrylic?**

It will make a bag. Acrylic mesh stretches and tends to stay stretched, so cotton is the page's choice.

## Next

A flat bag made of squares is the [granny square tote](/how-to-crochet-a-granny-square-tote/). The rounds under the base are practiced in [crochet in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/).


# How to Crochet a Phone Case for iPhone 18 Pro Max

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case.md



A crochet phone sleeve fails in one predictable way: it is chained to the phone's width, seamed, and then the phone will not go in. The fabric does not only cover the front and back. It also wraps both edges, so the flat sleeve has to be wider than the phone. This page works out by how much, using one specific phone, and then makes the sleeve.

The iPhone 18 Pro Max body is **3.07 inches wide (78.0 mm)**, **6.43 inches tall (163.4 mm)**, and **0.34 inch thick (8.75 mm)**, per [Apple's tech specs](https://support.apple.com/en-us/148591). Those are the bare phone. No sleeve for this page was crocheted or fitted on a phone, and the pictures are illustrations. The method works for any phone. Put your own three numbers into the table.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/).

![Five illustrated crochet phone sleeves in different colors](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case-result.jpg "Illustration. Five colorways of the same rectangle. Not a sleeve measured on a phone.")


## How the sleeve is built

One rectangle of single crochet. The foundation chain sets the width. The rows build the length. Fold it in half so the fold is the bottom, seam the two sides, and leave the top open. There is no shaping and no separate base.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/phone-sleeve-width.svg" alt="Looking down into a sleeve with a phone inside. The fabric runs around the phone's 3.07 inch face and its 0.34 inch edges, with a seam at each side. Notes: fabric all the way round is 2 times 3.41, which is 6.82 inches, so each flat face is 3.41 inches before seams, about 3.9 inches with seams and ease, 16 single crochet at 4 per inch rather than 13." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The sleeve has to cover the phone's edges as well as its face. That is the 0.34 inch most first sleeves forget.</figcaption>
</figure>

Wrap a strip of paper around the phone the short way and mark where it meets. That length, about 6.82 inches here, is what the sleeve has to go around. A flat sleeve has two faces, so each face must be at least half: **3.41 inches**. The side seams then take up roughly one stitch of the width, and you want a little ease so the phone slides. That makes the fabric about 3.9 inches wide.

The same thing happens at the bottom. The fold wraps the bottom edge, so each face starts about half the depth, 0.17 inch, above the fold. The pocket needs about 6.6 inches of depth on each side before the phone is covered.

## Yarn and hook

![An iPhone 18 Pro Max beside a tape measure, yarn, and a hook](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case-1.jpg "Illustration. Measure against the phone you own, not a smaller one.")

- **Smooth worsted cotton.** Cotton slides on the phone's glass and frame. Fuzzy yarn grips it, sheds lint into the ports, and pills inside. [Yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A hook that makes dense fabric**, often US G/6 (4.0 mm). Drop a size if you can see light through the stitches. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- The phone, without its case. If you keep a case on, measure the phone in its case instead.
- Yarn needle, scissors.

Crochet a swatch at least 4 inches wide and measure stitches and rows per inch. A [gauge](/crochet-gauge/) swatch is worth it here, because the width has almost no room for error.

## The worked example

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Around the phone | 2 × (3.07 + 0.34) | 6.82 in |
| One flat face | 6.82 ÷ 2 | 3.41 in |
| Fabric width | 3.41 + about 0.25 seams + about 0.25 ease | about 3.9 in |
| Stitches | 3.9 × 4, rounded up | 16 sc, chain 17 |
| Pocket depth per side | 6.43 + 0.17 at the fold + about 0.4 collar | about 7.0 in |
| Rows | 2 × 7.0 × 5 | 70 rows |

The earlier version of this page said to chain "just over 3.07 inches". At 4 stitches per inch that is about 13 stitches. Seamed, it leaves roughly 3.0 inches inside a sleeve that needs 3.41.

## Stage 1: chain and first row

Chain 17. Single crochet in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Ch 1, turn. (16)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/phone-case-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 17. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 16 sc. Checkpoint: the first row lying flat measures about 3.9 in." width="480" height="524" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. The chain next to the hook is skipped and does not count, so 17 chains give 16 sc.</figcaption>
</figure>

Keep the chain looser than the rows, or use a hook one size larger for the chain only. A tight chain pulls the bottom edge in, and the fold will pucker.

**Checkpoint:** lay the first row flat. It should measure about 3.9 inches. Fold a strip of paper of that width around the phone. It should close with a little overlap.

## Stage 2: the rectangle

Single crochet in each stitch across. Ch 1, turn. The ch 1 does not count as a stitch. (16)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Where the first stitch of each row goes. Working into the ch 1 is the usual reason a 16-stitch sleeve grows to 17.</figcaption>
</figure>

Count the stitches every 10 rows. Single crochet edges often gain a stitch when the first stitch goes into the turning chain, and each gained stitch is a quarter inch of looseness at that row.

Stop at about 70 rows, or when the rectangle folded in half covers the phone with a little collar above the top edge. Measure it lying flat, not hanging.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/phone-case-step-2.svg" alt="Rows 2 to 70: 16 sc every row. Rows of 16 sc drawn one symbol per stitch and worked back and forth, each starting with a turning chain (ch 1, not a st). Row 36 is highlighted. Every row: 16 sc. Count every 10 rows. Row 35 is the middle. With 70 rows, the fold lands between rows 35 and 36." width="480" height="585" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. The same 16 stitches on every row. The two halves of the sleeve meet at the middle row.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Checkpoint:** fold the rectangle with the phone inside, without seaming. The two long edges should meet beside the phone with no strain. If they gap, the sleeve is too narrow. Start again with more stitches. Do not stretch it.

## Stage 3: fold and seam

Fold the rectangle in half with the right side inside. Whipstitch each side from the fold to the top, through the outer loop of each edge stitch only. Going deeper into the stitch takes width from the inside. Turn it right side out so the seams are inside.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/phone-case-step-3.svg" alt="Drawn to scale. A flat piece 16 sc, 3.9 in wide and 70 rows, 14 in tall, folded at the dashed line into a pocket. Right side in. Whipstitch each side from the fold up, through the outer loops only. Top stays open. Each side ends up about 7 in deep: the phone's 6.43 in plus the fold and a collar." width="480" height="678" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 3. Drawn to scale: a 3.9 by 14 inch strip folds into a 3.9 by 7 inch pocket.</figcaption>
</figure>

Slide the phone in before you weave the tails. It should go in without force and stay put when you tip the sleeve. If the opening fights you, the last rows are tighter than the rest. Redo them more loosely. Weave ends on the inside, where they cannot catch on the buttons. See [weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/whipstitch-outer-loops.svg" alt="Two edges laid side by side, right sides together. Correct: the needle passes through the outer loop of each edge stitch only, so the seam is flat and takes almost no width. Mistake: the needle goes through the whole stitch on both sides, which builds a thick ridge and takes width from the inside." width="480" height="580" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>A seam through the outer loops keeps the inside width the sleeve was sized for.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Color

![Joining terracotta yarn on the last loop of a cream stitch](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case-4.jpg "Illustration. Change color on the last yarn-over so the new row starts clean.")

**Solid.** Best first sleeve. You can see every stitch you need to count.

**Two-tone.** Work 35 rows in color A, [change color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/), and finish in color B. The color change lands on the fold.

**Stripes.** Change every 4 rows. On a piece this small, two-row stripes read as noise. Cut the old color unless it returns within two rows. A strand carried up the side is on the inside of the seam, where it snags on the phone.

Keep every color the same yarn weight. A thinner yarn makes a narrower stripe, and the phone sticks there.

## Make the optional flap a complete separate stage

For a straight eight-stitch flap, mark the middle eight stitches of the back rim and join at the first marked stitch. Row 1: ch 1, sc in each marked stitch (8). Rows 2–6: ch 1, turn, sc across (8). With the phone inside for marking, check it reaches the intended button location; add full rows if needed. Final row: ch 1, turn, sc 3, ch 3, skip 2, sc 3 (six sc and a loop). Test your button through the loop, remove the phone, then sew the button through the front wall alone. This replaces the unspecified optional shaping.

## Troubleshooting

**The phone will not go in.** The sleeve was sized to the face, not around the phone. Recheck the width against 2 × (width + depth) ÷ 2, plus seams.

**It goes in but sticks at the top.** The camera plateau. Add a few loose rows to the collar, or work the last 4 rows with a larger hook.

**The phone slides out when tipped.** Too much ease, or a loose fabric. Redo with one fewer stitch or a smaller hook.

**One side is longer than the other.** Gained or lost stitches. Count back to the row where you still had 16.

**The bottom corners stick out.** The fold is not square. Refold along a single row.

## Frequently asked

**Does this work for a different phone?**

Yes. Measure width, height, and depth, or look them up on the maker's spec page, and redo the table.

**Should I cut a hole for the camera?**

No. The plateau sits at the top, and the open collar clears it. A hole in a single-crochet sleeve stretches and runs.

**Does the sleeve protect the phone from a drop?**

No. It keeps scratches and lint off the glass. It does not cushion a fall.

**Can I sell sleeves I make?**

A plain sleeve sized from published measurements is yours to make. Do not copy someone else's charted motif onto it.

## Next

The same "fabric goes around, not across" rule sizes every folded sleeve in the [device case guide](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/), from a book to a laptop. A round tube sized for a smaller object is the pouch method on that same page.


# How to Crochet a Pumpkin, Step by Step

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step.md



A crochet pumpkin is a stuffed ball, slightly squashed, with grooves pulled in from the outside and a short stem on top. The ball is the same basic shape behind most amigurumi, which is why this is a good first one. The grooves are a finishing trick, not a stitch.

The lesson here is the shape. Every amigurumi ball has three parts: rounds that increase, rounds that stay even, and rounds that decrease. The increases set how wide it gets. The even rounds set how tall. Change only the number of even rounds and the same ball becomes a pumpkin, an egg, or a cylinder. Once you see that, most stuffed toys stop looking mysterious.

If you can work [in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/) and make a [basic increase and decrease](/crochet-increase-decrease/), you can make this. US terms. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations of the finished pumpkin, which has not been made up in yarn here, so no finished size is given.

![A finished orange crochet pumpkin with vertical grooves and a short brown stem](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step-result.jpg "Illustration. The intended shape.")

## How the shape works

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/sphere-profile.svg" alt="Side view of a stuffed shape in three bands. Rounds 1 to 6 increase 6 stitches a round to 36. Even rounds at 36 set the height. Decrease rounds go back to 6. About 6 even rounds makes a near ball, about 4 makes a squat pumpkin. A top view shows 6 yarn wraps making 6 grooves." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Increase for width, work even for height, decrease to close. The grooves come later, from the outside.</figcaption>
</figure>

Six increases per round keeps a single crochet circle growing evenly. Six decreases per round closes it the same way. Between them, each even round adds one stitch height of wall. A ball of 36 stitches usually wants about six even rounds to look round. A pumpkin wants fewer, because it is wider than it is tall, and cinching the grooves will squash it further.

## What you need

- **Worsted-weight yarn** in orange, plus a scrap of brown or green for the stem. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A hook one or two sizes smaller than the yarn label suggests**, often US E/4 (3.5 mm) or F/5 (3.75 mm) for worsted. Amigurumi uses tighter fabric than a garment so the stuffing does not show through. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Polyester stuffing, a yarn needle, a stitch marker.

## Step 1: increase to 36

![Hands crocheting an orange sphere in the round, the sides curving upward](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step-1.jpg "Illustration. Increase until the circle is wide enough, then the sides turn up.")

Work in a spiral. Mark the first stitch of every round and move the marker up.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1 starts in a magic ring, pulled tight so the top has no hole.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 4:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)

**Round 5:** (Sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (30)

**Round 6:** (Sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (36)

Each repeat covers one more old stitch than the last round's repeat and makes one more new stitch. If your count is not on the list, find the error now. A miscount here shows as a lump later.

## Step 2: a short even section

**Rounds 7–10:** Sc in each stitch. (36)

Four even rounds is squat on purpose. For a rounder pumpkin, work five or six. For a flatter one, three.

## Step 3: decrease and stuff

![An orange crochet sphere being stuffed and closed with a yarn needle through the last stitches](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step-4.jpg "Illustration. Stuff firmly while the opening is still wide.")

**Round 11:** (Sc 4, sc2tog) 6 times. (30)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/invisible-decrease.svg" alt="Three steps. 1: insert the hook into the front loop only of the next stitch. 2: without pulling through, insert into the front loop of the stitch after it, so three loops are on the hook. 3: yarn over and pull through the two front loops, then yarn over and pull through the two loops on the hook. Two stitches become one." width="480" height="752" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Through the front loops only, the decrease disappears into the fabric.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 12:** (Sc 3, sc2tog) 6 times. (24)

**Round 13:** (Sc 2, sc2tog) 6 times. (18)

Stuff firmly now, while you can still reach in. Push stuffing into the edges, not just the middle.

**Round 14:** (Sc, sc2tog) 6 times. (12)

Top up the stuffing.

**Round 15:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6)

Fasten off with a long tail. Thread it through the front loops of the last 6 stitches and pull the hole closed.

<div class="tip">

Stuff firmer than seems necessary. The grooves compress the body. A softly stuffed pumpkin turns into a dented blob when you pull them in.

</div>

## Step 4: cinch the grooves

![A length of yarn wrapped around a stuffed orange ball and pulled tight to form a groove](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step-2.jpg "Illustration. The grooves are cinched from the outside, not crocheted in.")

Thread a long strand of the body color, or a slightly darker orange, onto a yarn needle.

1. Bring the needle up from the bottom center through the middle of the pumpkin and out at the top center.
2. Take the yarn down the outside to the bottom center, and pass the needle up through the middle again.
3. Pull until a groove forms. That is one wrap.
4. Turn the pumpkin and repeat. Six wraps, evenly spaced, make six sections. Eight look a little more detailed.

Pull each wrap enough to show a clear groove but not so tight that the top and bottom pucker into points. Pull every wrap to about the same tension. Tie off at the bottom and bury the ends inside.

## Step 5: the stem

![A small crocheted brown tube and a green leaf shape lying beside a tapestry needle](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step-3.jpg "Illustration. Stem and optional leaf, made separately and sewn on.")

With brown or green:

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

**Rounds 2–4:** Sc in each stitch. (6)

Fasten off with a tail. Leave it hollow or stuff lightly. Sew it to the top center so it covers the point where the wraps meet.

## Close the body and anchor the shaping strand

After round 15, pass the tail through the six front loops, draw closed and secure it. Start a separate long shaping strand under several stitches at the bottom center so it has an anchor before the first groove is pulled. Guide each wrap through the same top and bottom centers, spacing the external strands evenly around the body. Keep tension gentle enough not to break the yarn or split the center stitches. Once all six grooves are balanced, secure and bury both ends, then sew the stem around its six-stitch open edge to the top center. The stem remains hollow unless lightly stuffed.

## Troubleshooting

**A wavy disc instead of a cup.** The increases were uneven or skipped. Count each round.

**A tall tube with grooves.** Too many even rounds. Use four.

**Dents and soft spots.** Under-stuffed. Stuff before cinching, not after.

**A pointed top.** The last rounds were not stuffed, or the wraps were pulled too tight at the top.

**Stuffing shows through.** The fabric is too loose. Use a smaller hook.

**Visible lines of increases.** Six increases stacked in the same place every round make a hexagon. Shift the start of each repeat by a stitch or two from round to round.

## Frequently asked

### Why is no finished size given?

Hook, yarn, your tension, and how firmly you stuff all change the size, and no pumpkin was measured for this page. Make one, measure it, and write the number down.

### Can I make it bigger?

Keep increasing past 36 by adding a round: (sc 5, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 42. Add an even round or so, then decrease from 42 with (sc 5, sc2tog) first.

### Can I use blanket yarn?

With a larger hook, yes, but the fabric is soft and the grooves show less. Worsted or aran holds grooves better.

### Is it safe for a baby?

A stuffed toy for a baby needs securely closed seams and no small parts. This pumpkin has none, but it has not been safety tested. Treat it as decoration.

### What should I make next?

The same increase, even, decrease logic with an open hem is the [ghost amigurumi](/crochet-ghost-amigurumi-for-beginners/). A small oval body with flat wings is the [bat](/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears/).


# How to Crochet a Pumpkin Zipper Pouch

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-zipper-pouch.md



Wrapped candy and lip balm disappear in a tote. A small zipped pouch keeps them together and stops them walking out when the bag tips. This one is orange, with a green stem, so it reads as a pumpkin in October and as a small orange pouch the rest of the year.

The crochet is a flat circle with straight sides. The real lesson is the zipper. A zipper is straight, the top of the pouch is round, and crochet stretches where zipper tape does not. This page explains how long the zipper should be, where its ends go, and how to sew it by hand so it opens without catching.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No pouch for this page was crocheted or zipped. Sizes are estimates from the example gauge.

![A small orange crochet pumpkin zipper pouch with a green stem](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-zipper-pouch-result.png "Illustration. An orange pouch with a stem and a zipper across the top.")


## How the pouch is built

Rounds 1 to 8 make a flat circle of 48 stitches. Rounds 9 to 23 are even, so the edge turns up into a cup about 3 inches tall. That cup is the pouch. A zipper is sewn inside the top edge, and a short green chain on the outside is the stem.

It is not stuffed. For a solid round pumpkin, see the [pumpkin pattern](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/). The drawstring version is the [drawstring bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/).

### How a straight zipper meets a round top

Pinch the rim so two opposite stitches meet, and it flattens into a straight opening. Its length is half the circumference. Forty-eight stitches at 4 per inch is 12 inches around, so the flattened opening is about **6 inches**.

The zipper's teeth should be a little shorter than that. A 5 inch zipper leaves about half an inch at each end. You close those ends with a few stitches joining front to back, which also keeps the zipper stops from sitting on the folds, where they would strain. A zipper longer than the opening will not lie flat. The extra has to fold somewhere, and it buckles the rim.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/zipper-round-opening.svg" alt="A circular rim of 48 stitches, 12 inches around, and the same rim pinched flat into a 6 inch opening. A 5 inch zipper sits along it with its tape behind the crochet and teeth just below the edge. About half an inch at each end is closed by whipstitching front to back." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Half the circumference is the longest zipper that fits. A little shorter, with the ends closed, sits flatter.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Yarn, zipper, and tools

![Orange yarn, green yarn, a hook, and a short zipper](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-zipper-pouch-1.png "Illustration. Yarn, hook, and zipper.")

- **Worsted cotton**, orange, and a few yards of green for the stem. Cotton holds its edge against the zipper tape. Fuzzy yarn gets caught in the teeth. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives firm fabric. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **A closed-end zipper**, about 5 inches. Nylon coil is light and flexible. A metal zipper is heavier than the pouch.
- **Sewing needle and thread** that matches the zipper tape, pins or clips, a yarn needle, scissors.

## The worked example

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Around | 48 ÷ 4 | 12 in |
| Across the base | 12 ÷ 3.14 | about 3.8 in |
| Wall | 15 rounds ÷ 5 | 3 in |
| Flattened opening | 12 ÷ 2 | 6 in |
| Zipper | a little under 6 in | 5 in, about 0.5 in closed at each end |

If your circle has a different count, the opening is always half of it. At 4 stitches per inch, 42 stitches gives a 5.25 inch opening, which wants a zipper of about 4.5 inches.

## Stage 1: the base

These counts are for joined rounds: slip stitch to the first stitch, then ch 1, which does not count. To spiral instead, skip the join and the ch 1, and mark the first stitch.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a [magic ring](/crochet-in-the-round/). (6)

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 4:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)

**Round 5:** (Sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (30)

**Round 6:** (Sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (36)

**Round 7:** (Sc 5, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (42)

**Round 8:** (Sc 6, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (48)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/zipper-pouch-step-1.svg" alt="Base: rounds 1 to 8, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 1: 6 sc in a magic ring, 6 stitches. Round 2: 2 sc in each st, 12 stitches. Round 3: (sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 18 stitches. Round 4: (sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 24 stitches. Round 5: (sc 3, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 30 stitches. Round 6: (sc 4, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 36 stitches. Round 7: (sc 5, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 42 stitches. Round 8: (sc 6, 2 sc in next) 6 times, 48 stitches." width="480" height="509" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Round 8 ends on 48 stitches, 12 inches around at 4 sc per inch.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Checkpoint:** lay it flat. About 3.8 inches across at the example gauge. A cup means the tension is tight; a ruffle means extra increases. Fix it now. A wavy base rocks on the table.

## Stage 2: the sides

**Rounds 9–23:** Sc in each stitch. (48)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/zipper-pouch-step-2.svg" alt="The pouch by stitch count, as bars sized by stitch count. Round 1: 6, Round 2: 12, Round 3: 18, Round 4: 24, Round 5: 30, Round 6: 36, Round 7: 42, Round 8: 48, Rounds 9 to 23: 48. The zipper is sewn behind the top of round 23. Flattened, 48 stitches make a 6 in opening." width="480" height="453" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. Fifteen even rounds turn the flat base up into a 3 inch wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

Fifteen even rounds, about 3 inches. Count once in the middle. A gained stitch makes the rim longer than the zipper expects. Fasten off and weave the end down the inside.

Optional ribs: with a darker orange, work a line of surface slip stitch from the center of the base up the side to the rim, six times, starting above each increase line. It is decoration and does not change the size.

## Stage 3: the zipper

1. **Close the zipper.** A closed zipper lies straight and keeps the two sides level.
2. **Find the ends of the opening.** Pinch the rim flat. Mark the two folds with pins. The opening between them is about 6 inches.
3. **Close the ends.** Center the zipper. About half an inch of rim remains at each end. Whipstitch front edge to back edge across that half inch, with yarn.
4. **Pin the tape behind the crochet.** On each side, the tape goes on the inside, with the teeth just below the top edge of the crochet. The two crochet edges should almost meet over the teeth, with a little space so they do not get caught.
5. **Sew with thread.** Backstitch through the tape and the top round of crochet, about a quarter inch from the teeth. Catch the tape, never the teeth. Space the stitches evenly. The crochet edge may be slightly longer than the tape, so ease it on rather than stretching the tape.
6. **Tack the tape ends.** Fold each tape end down at an angle inside and stitch it so it does not scratch.
7. **Open and close it.** If the pull drags on a stitch, that section is too close to the teeth. Unpick and move it out.

Do not crochet into the zipper tape. Its weave is not made for a hook, and a crocheted join usually ripples.

![A zipper being sewn into the orange pouch](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-zipper-pouch-4.png "Illustration. Sew the tape to the fabric. Do not crochet into the teeth.")

## Stage 4: the stem

With green, join on the outside of the pouch, right next to one end of the zipper. Chain 8. Slip stitch in the second chain from the hook and in each chain back down. Slip stitch into the pouch to anchor it. Fasten off. If the stem flops into the zipper pull, it is too long. Remake it with ch 6.

## Baste the zipper before permanent backstitching

Mark twenty-four rim stitches on each side of the flattened forty-eight-stitch opening. Center the actual closed zipper between the side-fold marks and baste each tape independently to its own wall. Open it fully: no basting stitch should cross both tapes or front and back through the usable opening. Close it again and check the two rims are level. Only then close the short rim gaps outside the zipper stops and replace the basting with backstitch. Keep the needle out of the teeth. A fabric lining, if added, needs its own seam allowance and must not obstruct the pull.

## Troubleshooting

**The rim waves along the zipper.** The crochet was stretched onto the tape, or the zipper is too long for the opening.

**The zipper bulges at the ends.** The ends of the opening were not closed, so the stops sit on the folds.

**The pull catches.** Stitches went too close to the teeth.

**The zipper will not open.** You sewed both tapes together. Open it every few stitches as you sew.

**The pouch rocks.** The base ruffles. Rework it with fewer increases.

## Care

Empty it. Hand wash cool if the zipper is nylon. Dry flat with the zipper open so the tape does not dry curled. Do not iron a nylon coil.

## Frequently asked

**Can I skip the zipper?**

Yes. Work an eyelet round near the top on the even count of 48 and use a cord, as in the [drawstring bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/).

**Will it hold a phone?**

No. Twelve inches around and 3 inches tall is for candy and small things. A phone needs its own measurements: [phone case](/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case/).

**Can I use a sewing machine?**

Crochet stretches and shifts under a presser foot. Hand sewing is easier to control on fabric this thick.

## Next

A drawstring version, sized from what goes inside, is the [makeup pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-makeup-pouch/). A larger round drawstring version is the [drawstring bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/).


# How to Crochet a Scrunchie (Beginner, One Evening)

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-scrunchie.md



A crochet scrunchie is a fabric tube gathered on a hair elastic. It uses scrap yarn, and the only measurement that matters is the elastic you already own.

You crochet a flat strip, fold it around a covered hair tie, seam it into a tube, and join the ends. If you can [single crochet](/basic-crochet-stitches/) in rows, you can make one. The picture is an illustration of the finished scrunchie.

There is a second method, working single crochet directly over the elastic. It is faster for some people. The strip method is easier to size, easier to undo, and gives a fuller scrunchie, so it is the one written out here. The other is described at the end.

![A soft yarn crochet scrunchie in warm terracotta on a wooden vanity beside a hairbrush](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-scrunchie-result.jpg "Illustration. A gathered crochet tube on a regular hair elastic.")

## What you need

- Smooth worsted-weight yarn, cotton or acrylic. A small scrap ball is enough. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- A hook matched to the yarn, often US G/6 (4.0 mm) or H/8 (5.0 mm). [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One covered elastic hair tie**, the soft fabric-wrapped kind, not a bare rubber band.
- Yarn needle and scissors.

US abbreviations throughout. [Abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/).

## Which yarn behaves best

Cotton gives crisp stitches and a firm scrunchie that holds its gathers, but it is heavier and grips hair more. Acrylic is lighter and softer and slides more easily. A cotton-acrylic blend sits between them. Very fuzzy or bouclé yarn hides the stitches, which makes it hard to see where to seam, so save it for your second or third. Whatever you pick, a thinner yarn makes a lighter scrunchie, and lighter is easier to wear all day.

## Size it from your elastic

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/scrunchie-gather.svg" alt="An elastic hair tie measuring 7 inches around, beside a crochet strip twice as long, 14 inches, and 2.5 to 3 inches wide. The strip folds lengthwise around the elastic and is seamed along the long edge, then the short ends are joined. At about 5 single crochet rows per inch, 2.5 to 3 inches is 12 to 15 rows." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Length comes from the elastic. Width comes from the rows.</figcaption>
</figure>

Measure around the elastic as it sits, not stretched. The strip has to be longer than that loop or there is nothing to gather. **About twice the elastic** is a sensible starting point. A longer strip makes a fuller, bulkier scrunchie. A shorter one gives thin gathers.

The width is the number of rows. It folds in half around the elastic, so a 2.5 to 3 inch strip makes a tube a bit over an inch across.

| Your numbers | Example |
| --- | --- |
| Elastic, relaxed | 7 in |
| Strip length, 2 × elastic | 14 in |
| 56 sc at 4 per inch | ch 57; verify actual strip length |
| Strip width | 2.5 to 3 in |
| Rows at about 5 per inch | 12 to 15 |

The example numbers are typical for worsted single crochet. They are not from a measured swatch. Measure your own after a few rows.

## Step 1: chain the length

![Hands chaining a long foundation for a crochet scrunchie strip](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-scrunchie-1.jpg "Illustration. The chain is the length of the strip.")

Chain until the chain, held relaxed, is about twice the elastic. Do not stretch it to measure. Write the count down so the next one matches.

Sc in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. That stitch count stays the same every row.

<div class="tip">

A foundation chain is usually tighter than the rows above it, which makes the strip cup. If yours does, chain with a hook one size larger, then switch back.

</div>

## Step 2: work the rectangle

![A flat crochet rectangle in progress for a hair scrunchie](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-scrunchie-2.jpg "Illustration. Rows of single crochet until the strip is wide enough.")

Ch 1 and turn at the end of each row. The ch 1 does not count as a stitch. Keep the count identical.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Keeping the count identical means starting each row in the edge stitch, not the ch 1.</figcaption>
</figure>

Stop when the strip is 2.5 to 3 inches wide. In worsted that is usually 12 to 15 rows. The fabric should be soft. If it feels stiff, go up a hook size on the next one.

For a stripe, work the last two rows in a second color. [Changing yarn color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/).

## Step 3: seam around the elastic

![A tapestry needle seaming a crochet strip into a tube around a hair elastic](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-scrunchie-3.jpg "Illustration. Fold the long edges together around the elastic and whipstitch closed.")

Lay the elastic along the middle of the wrong side. Fold the long edges together over it. Whipstitch the long edges closed along the full length, catching both edges but not the elastic. Push the fabric along the elastic as you go, because the strip is twice as long.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/scrunchie-step-3.svg" alt="Three cross-section steps. 1: lay the elastic along the middle of the strip's wrong side. 2: fold both long edges up over it so they meet. 3: whipstitch the two long edges together along the full length, catching only the crochet and never the elastic, pushing the fabric along as you go because the strip is twice as long as the elastic." width="480" height="731" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 3 in cross-section. The seam closes the fabric around the elastic without piercing it.</figcaption>
</figure>

Do not sew through the elastic. If you catch it, it will not stretch.

Join the two short ends with a few firm stitches to close the ring. Bury the tails in the seam. [Weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

## Step 4: even the gathers
