![Hands sliding and evening gathers on a finished crochet scrunchie](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-scrunchie-4.jpg "Illustration. Slide the fabric around until the gathers are even.")

Slide the fabric around the elastic until the gathers are even. Twist it twice, as you would a store-bought scrunchie. If it fights you, the strip was too short or the seam too tight.

## The other method: crochet over the elastic

Join yarn to the elastic with a slip stitch around it. Work single crochet around the elastic itself, packing the stitches tightly, until it is covered. Join with a slip stitch. Then work two or three rounds of increases into those stitches (for example 3 dc in each stitch) to make a ruffle. It is quick, but the ruffle grows fast, and too many stitches on the base round stretch the elastic so it never fully relaxes. Pack them firmly, but stop before the elastic is stretched.

## Keep the elastic free inside the tube

For a fourteen-inch strip at four stitches per inch, use fifty-six sc, so the foundation is ch 57, not ch 56. Measure the completed rows rather than trusting chain length. Before closing the long seam, bring the short ends near each other around the elastic; gather the extra fabric along the ring as you sew. Match the foundation edge to the final row stitch by stitch and catch fabric only. Keep both short-end openings accessible, then sew those openings together to close the fabric ring. Stretch the elastic gently before weaving tails; any section that will not move may be caught by the seam.

## Troubleshooting

**No stretch.** The seam caught the elastic. Unpick and resew.

**A skinny yarn doughnut.** The strip is too narrow. Use at least 2.5 inches.

**Stiff and bulky.** Too tight, or too thick a yarn. Go up a hook size, or use a thinner yarn.

**Tails catching in hair.** Bury them in the seam, not on the outside.

**The elastic snaps quickly.** A thin, cheap elastic. Use a soft covered tie you would wear on its own.

## Frequently asked

### How long does it take?

That depends on your speed, so there is no honest single number. Time ten rows of your strip and multiply by the number of rows you need, then add the seam.

### Can I use velvet or fur yarn?

Yes, but not for a first one. Stitches are hard to see and seaming becomes guesswork. Make one in smooth worsted first.

### Will it damage hair?

A soft covered elastic with no knots on the outside is the same idea as any fabric scrunchie. Avoid metal-joined hair ties.

### Can kids make these?

If they can keep an even single crochet for a dozen rows, yes, with help on the seam so the needle stays off the elastic.

### What should I make next?

A [simple flower](/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower/) sewn onto a second scrunchie, or a [mug cozy](/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners/) for a gift.


# How to Crochet a Simple Flower (Beginner Appliqué)

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower.md



This flower is an appliqué: a center of 5 single crochet, then one petal in each of those stitches. It is sewn onto something else. It is not a stem, a rose, or a measured blossom.

US terms. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). You need a [magic ring](/crochet-in-the-round/) or the chain substitute below, plus [double crochet](/basic-crochet-stitches/). The pictures are illustrations of the finished flower, which has not been made up in yarn here, so its size depends on your yarn and hook.

![A flat five-petal crochet flower in soft pink and yellow on a linen cloth](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower-result.jpg "Illustration. A small flat flower.")


## Step 1: Five stitches in the center

Worsted in a center color and a petal color. A US G/6 (4.0 mm) or H/8 (5.0 mm) is a common match for worsted. Use the hook that lets you see 5 separate stitches. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/). [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).

**Round 1.** Magic ring, 5 single crochet, slip stitch to the first single crochet to join. (5)

Pull the ring shut before you start petals. A loose ring is a hole in the middle of the flower.

If the ring will not close, chain 2 and work 5 single crochet into the second chain, then join. That center has a small hole. The petals cover some of it. They do not change the count.

![Hands crocheting a small yellow magic-ring center for a flower](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower-1.jpg "Illustration. Close the ring before the petals. The hands are not a gauge.")

Fasten off the center color if you want fewer carried strands. Leave enough tail to weave.

## Step 2: One petal in each stitch

Join the petal color in any center stitch.

**Petal.** In that same stitch: chain 2, 3 double crochet, chain 2, slip stitch into the same stitch.

Slip stitch into the next center sc to move the hook there, then repeat, until there are 5 petals. Slip stitch to the base of the first petal if you want a tidy close. Fasten off and leave a long tail for sewing.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/flower-petals.svg" alt="Diagram of five center stitches, with one petal in each. The petal is chain 2, three double crochet, chain 2, and a slip stitch in that same stitch." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Five centers, five petals. The next petal starts in the next stitch, not in the one you just used.</figcaption>
</figure>

Checkpoint: five lobes, and you can still find five center stitches under them. Two petals in one stitch leave another stitch empty. That empty stitch reads as a gap. An extra petal has to steal space, and the circle stops being round.

The slip stitch that closed round 1 is not a sixth stitch. If you put a petal in it, you get a sixth lobe and the first petal gets crowded. Petals go in the five single crochets only.

If a petal looks like a tight knot, the doubles were pulled short. If the petals cup into a bowl, the doubles are too tall for the ring or the hook is small. Go up a hook and make another center. Do not block acrylic with an iron. The petals can melt.

![A crochet hook forming open petals around a small flower center](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower-2.jpg "Illustration. Each petal is a short group of taller stitches in one center stitch.")

## Step 3: The leaf is one pass, if you want it

Chain 7 in green. Single crochet in the second chain from the hook, half double in the next, double in the next 2, half double in the next, single crochet in the last.

That is 6 stitches on the 6 chains that remain after the skip: 1 + 1 + 2 + 1 + 1.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/flower-leaf.svg" alt="Diagram of chain 7 with the first chain skipped, then single, half double, double, double, half double, single. Six stitches, one side of the chain only." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The two doubles are the wide part of the strip. There is no second side.</figcaption>
</figure>

![A small green crochet leaf beside a finished flower appliqué](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower-3.jpg "Illustration. A tapered strip, not a measured leaf.")

Sew it under one side of the flower so part of it shows. Skip it if you only need the flower. A leaf with stitches down both sides of the chain is a second pass. This page does not give that count, because it was not checked.

## Step 4: Sew the center and the petal bases

Thread the petal tail. Sew the center to the fabric, then catch the base of each petal once. A flower sewn only through the center can flip. Glue is not the method here. It is not a wash test, and it is not a substitute for the stitches.

![A tapestry needle sewing a crochet flower onto fabric](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-simple-flower-4.jpg "Illustration. Sew the center and the base of each petal.")

Do not sew this onto the [baby blanket](/how-to-crochet-a-baby-blanket/). A flower that can be pulled off does not belong where a child mouths fabric. A [scrunchie](/how-to-crochet-a-scrunchie/) or the flap of a [mug cozy](/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners/) is a place where an appliqué is decoration, not a loose part on a blanket.

## Change petal color before building the lobes

After joining the five-sc center, fasten off its color and attach the petal color with a slip stitch in any of the five sc. Ch 2, three dc, ch 2 and sl st all go into that same base sc. Slip stitch into the next sc to move, then make the next complete petal there. After five, fasten off without adding a petal in the joining slip stitch. The small optional leaf is one row, so it is a curved strip rather than the two-sided leaf on the fall-leaf page. Sew it under the flower before attaching the complete motif.

## Mistakes

**The center is a hole.** The ring was not pulled shut. Close it before the petals, or the petals lock the hole open.

**Four petals, or six, on five stitches.** Match the counts. Six petals need six center stitches, which is a different round.

**The flower is wider than the strap you sewed it to.** Worsted on a narrow strap looks out of scale. A lighter yarn is a new swatch, not the same five stitches squeezed.

**Petals flipped after washing.** Only the center was sewn. Catch the five bases.

## Frequently asked

### Is this a rose?

No. A rose is a long ruffle rolled up. This is one flat round of petals.

### Do I have to know double crochet?

Yes for this petal. The three doubles are what make the lobe. Single crochet in the same places makes a bump, not this petal. The stitch is in [basic stitches](/basic-crochet-stitches/).

## Next

The ring that starts the center is practiced on [crochet in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/). A flat motif with a different count, six points instead of five petals, is the [snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/).


# How to Crochet a Snowflake (and Make It Stay Flat)

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake.md



The crochet part of a snowflake is three short rounds. The part that decides whether it looks like an ornament or a limp doily is the finishing: soaking it in stiffener, pinning every point, and letting it dry hard. This page covers both, and spends more time on the second, because that is where most snowflakes go wrong.

The crochet is ordinary [work in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/), with the stitches spread apart on purpose. The finished size depends on your thread and how you block it, so the inch sizes here are a guide rather than a measured result. The pictures are illustrations of the intended shape.

![Hands working a white thread snowflake with a fine steel crochet hook](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake-1.jpg "Illustration. The crochet is quick. The blocking decides how it looks.")

![A finished white crochet snowflake with open stiff points](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake-result.jpg "Illustration. Flat points after stiffening.")

## What you need

- **Size 10 crochet thread** in white, or worsted yarn for a chunky version
- A **steel hook** around 1.65–1.75 mm for thread, or a 3.5–4 mm hook for yarn. Steel numbers run backwards: a higher number is a smaller hook. The [hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/) has the table
- A yarn needle, rustproof pins, and a board you can pin into
- A stiffener, covered below

Abbreviations below are US terms.

## Why six points

Real snowflakes have six-fold symmetry, from the way water molecules lock as ice. Eight-point crochet flakes exist and look fine. Six reads as a snowflake at once. Five reads as a flower or a starfish. Build in multiples of six and the shape does most of the work.

![A white thread motif with five chain-loop petals radiating from a closed center ring](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake-4.jpg "Illustration. The same construction with five loops instead of six. Nobody would call this a snowflake, which is why the count is the first thing to get right.")

## Three rounds

Every flake, however fancy, is a center, then spokes, then points.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/snowflake-rounds.svg" alt="A ring of 12 double crochet, six chain-5 loops each using 2 of the 12 stitches, and a picot point in each loop: six arms." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Twelve stitches, six loops, six points. Each round is a multiple of six.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 1, the center.** Magic ring. Ch 3, which counts as the first dc, then 11 dc into the ring. Join with a slip stitch to the top of the ch 3. (12)

If the magic ring is still awkward, chain 6, slip stitch into the first chain, and work the 12 dc into that loop. The magic ring closes tighter. A gappy center is the first thing the eye hits.

**Round 2, the spokes.** The hook is at center stitch 1 after the round-1 join. Work (ch 5, skip the next dc, sc in the following dc) five times; anchors land in center stitches 3, 5, 7, 9, and 11. Ch 5, skip center stitch 12, and sl st into the base at center stitch 1 to close the sixth loop. Do not make an additional sc there. (6 ch-5 loops, 5 sc anchors and the closing sl st)

**Round 3, the points.** Slip stitch into the first ch-5 loop. In that loop work 3 sc, ch 3, sl st in the third chain from the hook (picot), 3 sc. Sl st into the next anchor sc. Repeat in each of the five remaining loops, using the closing join as the last anchor. Join to the first sc and fasten off. (36 sc in the loops, 6 picots; connecting slip stitches do not count as sc) Count six loops before starting and six points before finishing.


For a lacier arm, replace each picot with ch 5, sc into the loop, ch 5, sc into the same loop, ch 5. Each arm ends in three spikes. The count stays six.

<div class="tip">

Make one plain flake and stiffen it before you make six ornate ones. Stiffening changes the look more than the stitch choice does.

</div>

Leave a tail and run it along the top of a few stitches. Openwork has no fabric to hide an end. More is in [weaving in ends and blocking](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/). Weave before you stiffen. A hard tail will not thread.

## Stiffening

Off the hook the flake is soft and slightly cupped. Saturate it, pin it, and let it dry hard.

**Commercial fabric stiffener** dries clear and hard, does not attract insects, and does not yellow the way some household mixes do. Use it when the flake will stay on a tree for years. Follow the dilution on the bottle. Products differ.

**White school glue (PVA)**, roughly equal parts glue and warm water, stirred smooth. It dries mostly clear. Test a spare flake. Some glues leave a sheen on white thread.

**Sugar water**, roughly equal parts, heated until the sugar dissolves, then cooled. It dries very hard. Sugar can attract insects, and it softens in humid air. Fine for one season. Poor for a box in the attic.

**Spray starch** is the lightest hold. It will not survive a damp room. Redo it each year.

All four work, and they fail differently. If the flake matters, use commercial stiffener. For thirty on a garland, most people use glue and water.

## Blocking

![A wet white snowflake pinned out flat on a foam blocking board](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake-3.jpg "Illustration. Every point pinned, pulled to full length, and left until bone dry.")

1. Soak until the solution is through the thread, not just on the surface.
2. Squeeze gently. Do not wring, or the arms dry twisted.
3. Lay it on wax paper or plastic wrap so it does not stick to the board.
4. Pin **every point** at full length. Six arms means at least six pins. Pin the picots for sharper tips.
5. Match opposite arms before you leave.
6. Leave it bone dry: overnight, longer if the room is humid. Unpinning early leaves it half flat.

<div class="warning">

**Use rustproof pins.** Ordinary dressmaking pins sit in a wet solution for hours and can leave orange marks on white thread that will not wash out. Stainless steel or plastic-headed quilting pins are the safe choice.

</div>

## Label six spokes before stiffening

Lay the unstiffened flake flat and mark its six ch-5 loops mentally as A–F. Each gets exactly one complete point; opposite pairs are A/D, B/E, and C/F. Spread those pairs to comparable distances from the center when pinning. Check the pin metal is rust-resistant; a plastic head does not guarantee a rust-resistant shaft. Test any stiffener on a spare motif and follow its own label. Do not assume every commercial product stays clear or washproof. Finish weaving the center and outer tails before applying stiffener, then wait until the whole piece is dry before removing pins or adding the hanger.

## Mistakes

**Uneven arms.** A short arm was pinned short. Wet the flake and block it again.

**A tight, lumpy center.** Too many stitches in the ring, or the ring closed before they were spread. Twelve double crochets sit comfortably in a magic ring. Sixteen do not.

**Ends stiffened in place.** The tail dries at that angle and will not thread.

**Tension that is too tight.** The holes are the design. Dense thread reads as a small white blob at three feet. Loosen up, or go up a hook size.

**No test flake.** The result changes with the brand, the thread, and the humidity. Treat the first one as a test.

## Frequently asked

**Can I use yarn instead of thread?** Yes. A worsted flake comes out large and soft, closer to a coaster, and it needs more stiffener. Cotton holds a blocked shape better than acrylic, which springs back.

**How do I hang it?** Clear fishing line through one picot disappears on a tree. Thread stays visible. Add the hanger after stiffening, or the loop dries flat against the arm.

**Why does it cup?** Too many stitches in the outer round force a ruffle. Too few pull it into a dome. A cup means that round is too tight. A ruffle means it is too loose. Check the unstiffened flake before you blame the pins.


# How to Crochet a Spider (and Web)

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-spider-and-web.md



A crochet spider usually fails in one of two ways: the body is a featureless blob, or the legs are different lengths and stick out at random. The stitches are easy. The anatomy and the consistency are the skill.

Two facts fix most of it. A spider's body is in two parts, a larger rear section (the abdomen) and a smaller front section (the cephalothorax). **All eight legs come from the smaller front section**, four on each side. Many crochet spiders put the legs around a single ball, which reads as a starfish or a sun. Put them on the front section and it reads as a spider from across a room.

The web has its own rule: it is a flat mesh worked in rounds, and each round's chain loops have to be longer than the last, because each round goes around a bigger circle. Get that wrong and the web cups or ruffles.

You should be comfortable with [magic rings and spirals](/crochet-in-the-round/). US terms. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations of the finished spider and web, which have not been made up in yarn here, so their size depends on your yarn and hook.

![A finished black crochet spider beside a pale gray flat crochet web](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-spider-and-web-result.jpg "Illustration. The intended look.")

## The plan

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/spider-web-plan.svg" alt="A spider from above with a larger rear body and a smaller front body. All eight legs attach to the front body, four on each side, front pairs angled forward and back pairs angled back, with eyes at the front. A web in rounds: a ring of 8 single crochet, then 8 loops of chain 5, 7, 9, and 11." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Legs belong on the smaller front body. Web loops grow by two chains a round.</figcaption>
</figure>

## What you need

- **Worsted-weight yarn**: black or dark brown for the spider, white or gray for the web. Smooth cotton gives the crispest web. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A hook one or two sizes smaller than the label** for the body, and the label size for the web. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Stuffing, a yarn needle, stitch markers, a scrap of white or red for eyes.

## Step 1: the rear body (abdomen)

![Hands crocheting a small black sphere in the round](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-spider-and-web-1.jpg "Illustration. Each body section is a small ball.")

Black. Work in a spiral and mark the first stitch of each round.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Round 1 starts in a magic ring, pulled tight so the top has no hole.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (Sc, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 4:** (Sc 2, 2 sc in next) 6 times. (24)

**Rounds 5–7:** Sc in each stitch. (24)

**Round 8:** (Sc 2, sc2tog) 6 times. (18)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/invisible-decrease.svg" alt="Three steps. 1: insert the hook into the front loop only of the next stitch. 2: without pulling through, insert into the front loop of the stitch after it, so three loops are on the hook. 3: yarn over and pull through the two front loops, then yarn over and pull through the two loops on the hook. Two stitches become one." width="480" height="752" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Through the front loops only, the decrease disappears into the fabric.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 9:** (Sc, sc2tog) 6 times. (12) Stuff firmly.

**Round 10:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6) Fasten off and pull closed.

## Step 2: the front body (cephalothorax)

**Rounds 1–3:** As the rear body. (18)

**Rounds 4–5:** Sc in each stitch. (18)

**Round 6:** (Sc, sc2tog) 6 times. (12) Stuff firmly.

**Round 7:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6) Fasten off with a long tail.

Sew the front body to one end of the rear body, with the seam hidden underneath. Sew all the way round the joint, not at one point, or the front body nods.

## Step 3: eight matched legs

![Eight thin black crocheted cords laid out in four matched pairs](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-spider-and-web-2.jpg "Illustration. Eight legs, all the same length.")

Pick one leg length and use it for all eight. Write the number down.

**Chain legs (fast).** Join black at the side of the front body. Ch 14. Slip stitch in the 2nd chain from the hook and in each chain back to the body. Fasten off and weave the end into the body. That leg is 13 slip stitches long. Make every leg 13.

**Tube legs (sturdier).** 4 sc in a magic ring. Work even for 10 rounds. Fasten off with a long tail and sew on. Every leg 10 rounds.

**Placement.** Four on each side of the front body, in a short row from front to back. Angle the front two pairs forward and the back two pairs backward. Pin all eight before attaching any, then look from above. The two sides should mirror each other.

<div class="warning">

Some makers slide a chenille stem (pipe cleaner) into tube legs so they can be posed. The wire ends can poke through. That is fine for a shelf decoration, and not for anything a child will handle.

</div>

Attach the legs before embroidering the face. Eight legs means a lot of needle traffic through the front body.

## Step 4: eyes

![A tapestry needle stitching two small black eyes onto a crochet spider body](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-spider-and-web-4.jpg "Illustration. Two eyes near the front of the head section.")

Most spiders have eight eyes, but at this size two French knots in white or red, close together at the front of the front body, read better. Embroidering all eight on a small spider looks like a texture rather than a face.

## Step 5: the web

![A pale gray crochet web being worked flat, with the hook still in the work](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-spider-and-web-3.jpg "Illustration. The web is worked flat in rounds of chain loops.")

White or gray, label-size hook.

**Round 1:** 8 sc in a magic ring. Join with a slip stitch to the first sc. (8)

**Round 2:** (Ch 5, sl st in next sc) 8 times. (8 loops)

For each later round, label the eight loops A–H clockwise or counterclockwise in your working direction.

**Round 3:** Sl st into loop A and sc in that same loop to establish the first anchor. (Ch 7, sc in the next loop) 7 times, reaching H. Ch 7 and sl st into the first anchor sc. (8 ch-7 loops, 8 sc anchors)

**Round 4:** Sl st into the first new loop and sc there. (Ch 9, sc in the next loop) 7 times. Ch 9 and sl st to the first sc. (8 loops)

**Round 5:** Sl st into the first new loop and sc there. (Ch 11, sc in the next loop) 7 times. Ch 11 and sl st to the first sc. (8 loops) Fasten off.


**Why two more chains each round.** Each round sits further from the center, so it has a longer distance to cover around. Adding two chains a round is a starting point, not a rule. Lay the web flat after each round. If it cups into a bowl, the loops are too short: add a chain. If it ruffles, they are too long: take one away. Your yarn and tension decide.

**Radial threads (optional).** With a yarn needle, run a strand from the center straight out to the edge, tacking it at each round. Four lines crossing the center, like the diagram, make it read as a web.

Block it flat if the chains curl. [Blocking](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

## Step 6: put them together

![A black crochet spider being sewn slightly off-center onto a pale gray crochet web](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-spider-and-web-5.jpg "Illustration. Off-center looks placed. Dead center looks stamped.")

Sew the spider off-center on the web through the underside of the rear body. Add a hanging loop at the top edge of the web. Do not hang it from a leg.

## Close both body pieces before making the legs

The front body ends at six stitches just like the abdomen. After stuffing it, thread the tail through the six front loops, pull closed and secure it, retaining enough tail to join the two closed pieces. Pin their contact surfaces together and sew around the join rather than attaching one loose point. For tube legs, do not stuff the four-stitch tube; flatten its open end and sew it to the front body. All four pairs attach to that front section. For chain legs, slip stitch back through thirteen chains, then slip stitch into the original attachment stitch before fastening off.

## Troubleshooting

**It looks like a starfish.** Legs around one ball, or on the rear body. Move them to the front section.

**Legs of different lengths.** Count every leg against the number you wrote down.

**The front body droops.** Sewn at one point. Sew around the whole joint.

**The web cups.** Loops too short. **The web ruffles.** Loops too long.

**Floppy abdomen.** Under-stuffed.

## Frequently asked

### Is this beginner-friendly?

The bodies are beginner work. Eight identical legs and a flat web push it to intermediate, because consistency is the skill.

### Can I skip the web?

Yes. The spider stands alone as an ornament or appliqué.

### What should I make next?

Another small stuffed body with flat sewn parts is the [bat](/bat-amigurumi-wings-body-and-ears/). A flat lacy motif blocked stiff is the [snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/).


# How to Crochet a Bag with a Strap (Shoulder or Crossbody)

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-strap-bag.md



A strap is what turns a crochet sleeve into a bag you can wear. The body is the easy part — it is a folded rectangle, the same construction as any flat sleeve. The strap is where bags fail: sewn through one stitch it tears out, and made to the length that looked right on the table it hangs too low once the bag is full, because cotton stretches under weight and does not spring back. This page covers both the shoulder length and the crossbody length, how to attach a strap so it holds, and how to set its length after it has carried a load.

US terms throughout. US single crochet is UK double crochet. See the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) if a term is new. The sizes are worked out from an example gauge rather than from a finished, worn bag, so swatch, and wear it loaded before you fix the strap.

![A dusty rose crochet shoulder bag with a long strap](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-strap-bag-thumb.png)

## The body: a folded rectangle, sized to what you carry

The body is a single-crochet rectangle folded in half and seamed up the sides, open at the top, with one round worked around the opening to firm it. That full method — measuring the contents, adding the stack's thickness to the width, and keeping the stitch count honest over many rows — is the folded-sleeve method in the [device case guide](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/). Work the body at the size you need:

- **A phone crossbody** uses a small sleeve sized around one phone: each face covers the phone's width plus its thickness, plus a little ease and the seams. For a 3.0 x 6.0 x 0.35 in phone at 4 stitches to the inch, that is about 15 stitches wide and 70 rows, folded to a sleeve about 7.0 inches deep.
- **A shoulder bag** uses a wider body sized around a small stack — a phone, a slim wallet, keys. Measure the widest item and the whole stack's thickness. For a 4.5 in wallet in a 1.5 in stack, that is about 27 stitches wide and 66 rows.

After seaming, join yarn at a side seam and work one round of single crochet around both top edges. On the shoulder bag that is 27 along the last row and 27 along the foundation chain, 54 stitches. This round keeps the opening from stretching and gives the strap something solid to anchor to.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/shoulder-bag-step-2.svg" alt="Drawn to scale. A flat piece 27 sc, 6.75 in wide and 66 rows, 13.2 in tall, folded at the dashed line into a pocket. Seam both sides, then work 27 sc along row 66 and 27 along the foundation chain." width="480" height="547" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The body is a folded rectangle; the top round joins both edges and firms the opening.</figcaption>
</figure>

## The strap: a worked strip, never a bare chain

A strap carries the whole weight of the bag at two small points, so it has to be fabric, not a chain. A bare chain stretches without limit and the bag ends up at your knee. Work a narrow single-crochet strip instead:

Chain 5 or 6, single crochet across (4 or 5 stitches), then single crochet every row, turning with a chain 1 that does not count, until the strip reaches the length you worked out. Four stitches is enough for a light bag; five for a phone that swings.

### How long: shoulder versus crossbody

**Over one shoulder.** The strap sits on one shoulder and the bag hangs at your hip on the same side, about 26 inches of strap in the example. Hold the strip on your shoulder and let it hang to where you want the top of the bag, then add or remove rows.

**Across the body.** A crossbody strap is a loop all the way around your torso, so measuring shoulder-to-hip on the front gives you only about half of it. Put on the clothes you will wear it over, hold a string at the hip where the bag's top should sit, run it across your chest, over the opposite shoulder, down your back, and to the other side of the bag, then measure.



Measure the string path directly from one planned attachment point, across the chest and opposite shoulder, down the back, to the other attachment point. That path is the exposed strap length; add the overlap sewn inside the bag at both ends. Do not subtract the bag width a second time from a path already measured between attachment points.

## Attaching the strap so it holds

Lay one strap end inside the bag at the top of a side seam, overlapping by about an inch. With a yarn needle, sew a box around the overlap and an X across it, going through the strap, the top round, and the bag wall. A strap held by a single corner stitch pulls that stitch out under load; the box-and-X spreads the pull across many stitches and both layers.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/strap-attachment.svg" alt="The strap end overlapping the inside of the bag by about an inch at the side seam, sewn with a box and an X through strap and wall. A single corner stitch is marked as the version that tears out. A test list: fill the bag, pin the second end, hang it for a day, remeasure, then sew." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The box-and-X spreads the load; the strap's final length is decided after it has carried weight.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Fit it after it stretches, not before

1. **Sew the first end** with the box-and-X.
2. **Pin the second end** inside the opposite side seam with two safety pins, and check the strap is not twisted.
3. **Baste the second end securely before checking it.** Do not carry a loaded bag supported only by safety pins. Use several temporary sewing passes over the overlap, then check with light contents while supporting the base. Remove basting to alter the length and sew the final box-and-X before normal use.
4. **Remeasure and sew.** If it now hangs lower than you wanted, take out rows before sewing the second end the same way as the first.

Every row of a single-crochet strap gives a little under steady weight, and over a hundred-plus rows that adds up to inches that cotton will not take back. Setting the length on the second day is what keeps the bag where you want it.

## Optional closure

An open top is quick to reach but a hard swing can throw the contents. For a shoulder bag, chain a loop at the center of the back top edge and sew a button on the front where it reaches with the bag full. For a phone crossbody, work a short flap over the middle stitches of the back opening with a chain loop on its last row, and button it. Place the button only with the contents inside, so the loop reaches.

<div class="warning">

Single crochet is not waterproof and a bag is not padding. Rain and spilled drinks go through, and a dropped phone is not protected. This keeps your things at your side, nothing more.

</div>

## Write out the folded body before making the strap

For the phone example at the stated four-stitch/five-row gauge: ch 16, sc in the second chain and across (15). Continue ch 1, turn, sc across until seventy rows make two equal thirty-five-row faces, about seven inches deep each; check against your phone before seaming. For the shoulder example: ch 28 for twenty-seven sc, work sixty-six rows for two thirty-three-row faces, then fit your actual contents. Fasten off, fold with equal faces and seam both sides only. The seventy- and sixty-six-row totals replace odd-row examples that could not split into equal numbered faces. Make the strap only after this fit check.

## Troubleshooting

**The bag bulges and the seams strain.** The body width left out the stack's thickness. Start wider.

**The contents sit above the top edge.** Too few rows in the body.

**The strap got longer after a week.** It was sewn to length before the wearing test. Unpick one end, take out rows, and resew.

**A strap end tore out.** It was sewn through too few stitches. Resew with the box-and-X through both layers.

**The strap twists on the shoulder, or the bag tilts.** The strap was sewn with a twist, or at different heights on the two sides. Unpick one end and lay it flat.

## Care

Empty the bag. Hand wash cool and dry flat with the strap laid straight. Do not hang it wet by the strap — that stretches it the most.

## Frequently asked

**Shoulder or crossbody — which is easier to make?**

Both are the same work; only the strap length differs. The crossbody just needs the loop measured around your body rather than a drop from the shoulder.

**Can I make the strap adjustable?**

Not with this plain strip. Sew it to the fitted length, which is why the wearing test matters.

**Can I sell what I make?**

Finished bags, yes. Do not republish these instructions, and do not stitch a brand name onto a bag as if the company made it.

## Next

For the folded-rectangle body in full detail, see the [device case guide](/how-to-crochet-a-device-case/). For a phone sleeve with no strap, the [phone case](/how-to-crochet-a-phone-case/). For a larger open carry with no strap sewing, the [granny square tote](/how-to-crochet-a-granny-square-tote/) or the [market bag](/how-to-crochet-a-market-bag/).


# How to Crochet a Tumbler Sleeve

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve.md



Most travel tumblers are not straight. They are wider at the top than at the bottom, and a straight crochet strip around one gaps at the top or slides down. A plain rectangle works on a straight mug, which is what the [mug cozy](/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners/) makes. This page handles the taper.

For a handled tumbler, the sleeve is a strip worked sideways, the same direction as the mug cozy, with a short wedge of partial rows that makes the top edge longer than the bottom. For a tumbler with no handle, it is a tube worked in rounds from the bottom up, with a few single increases spread along the way.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No sleeve for this page was crocheted or fitted on a cup. The cup in the example is invented. It is not a named brand.

![A sage crochet sleeve on a plain handled tumbler](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve-result.jpg "Illustration. The handle stays outside the fabric. Not a tested fit.")


## Measure the cup

1. **Height** of the wall you want covered, below the lid's threads and above the base. Example: 4.5 inches.
2. **Around the top** of that area. Example: 9.3 inches.
3. **Around the bottom** of that area. Example: 8.5 inches.
4. **Handle base**, the width where the handle meets the wall. Example: 1.5 inches.

If the top and bottom measurements match, your cup is straight. Use the [mug cozy](/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners/) instead.

## How the handled version is shaped

The foundation chain is the sleeve's height. Each row runs from the top edge to the bottom edge, and the rows stack around the cup. So the length along the top edge is the number of rows at the top, and the same for the bottom.

To make the top edge longer, you work some rows that stop partway down. These short rows add length to the top part only. Two short rows over the top 12 stitches add two rows there. Two more over the top 6 add two more at the very top. The top edge now has four more rows than the bottom, the middle has two more, and the strip curves to follow the taper.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/tumbler-short-rows.svg" alt="A sideways strip 18 stitches tall with a wedge of short rows in the middle. The top edge has 35 plus 4, 39 rows, 7.8 inches; stitches 7 to 12 get 2 extra rows; the bottom edge has 35 rows, 7.0 inches. The wedge is worked from the top edge after row 17: sc 12, turn, sc 12, turn, sc 6, turn, sc 6, turn." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Four extra rows at the top, two in the middle, none at the bottom. That is a taper of 0.8 inch.</figcaption>
</figure>

## The worked example

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Height | 4.5 × 4 sts | 18 sc, chain 19 |
| Top edge | (9.3 − 1.5) × 5 rows | 39 rows |
| Bottom edge | (8.5 − 1.5) × 5 rows | 35 rows |
| Difference | 39 − 35 | 4 rows: one wedge |

The old version of this page multiplied the cup's circumference by the stitch gauge and then called the result rows. That is the same error the mug cozy page had: the strip runs sideways, so its length comes from rows per inch.

## Yarn and hook

![Sage cotton, a hook, a button, and a plain tumbler](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve-1.jpg "Illustration. Cotton, a button, and a hook.")

- **Smooth worsted cotton.** A cold cup sweats, and acrylic stays damp and smells. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives firm fabric. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One button**, about 3/4 inch, and thread.
- Two stitch markers, yarn needle, scissors.

Measure both stitches and rows per inch on a swatch. [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/) explains how.

## Stage 1: the strip

Chain 19.

**Row 1:** Sc in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Ch 1, turn. (18)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/tumbler-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 19. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 18 sc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. The chain is the height of the sleeve, 18 sc for a 4.5 inch cup. Rows run around the cup.</figcaption>
</figure>

Put a marker in the last stitch of row 1. That end is the **top edge**. Every odd row ends there, and every even row starts there.

**Rows 2–17:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (18)

Row 17 ends at the top edge.

## Stage 2: the wedge

Work these from the top edge. Each short row turns partway down.

**Short row 1:** Sc in the next 12 stitches. Ch 1, turn, leaving 6 unworked.

**Short row 2:** Sc in each of the 12 stitches back to the top edge. Ch 1, turn.

**Short row 3:** Sc in the next 6 stitches. Ch 1, turn.

**Short row 4:** Sc in each of the 6 stitches back to the top edge. Ch 1, turn.

You are at the top edge again, ready for a full row.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/tumbler-step-2.svg" alt="The sleeve strip drawn with each row as a column 18 stitches tall and the top edge at the top. Between rows 17 and 18 sit four short rows worked from the top edge: two of 12 stitches and two of 6. The top edge gains four rows and the bottom edge none, which is what makes the top 39 rows long and the bottom 35." width="480" height="585" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. The short rows add length only to the top edge, which is what lets a straight strip follow a wider rim.</figcaption>
</figure>

At each turning point the fabric steps, and a small gap can open. When you work the next full row past it, put the stitch firmly into the stitch below the step. A tiny gap is normal in single crochet short rows.

## Stage 3: the rest of the strip

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/tumbler-wedge-return.svg" alt="The first full row crosses three exposed levels without adding stitches." width="800" height="480" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The first full row crosses three exposed levels without adding stitches.</figcaption>
</figure>


**Full row 18:** Starting at the top, sc into the six stitches of short row 4, then the six exposed stitches of short row 2, then the six still-unworked stitches of full row 17. Do not work a turning chain as an extra stitch. Ch 1, turn. (18)

**Full rows 19–35:** Sc in each of the 18 stitches. Ch 1, turn. (18)

Count the bottom edge: 35 rows. The top edge has 39.

**Checkpoint:** wrap the strip on the cup with the top edge at the top. Both short ends should sit beside the handle, and the fabric should lie against the wall all the way down. Add or remove full rows at the end if the ends do not reach.

## Stage 4: the closure

The closure works exactly as it does on the [mug cozy](/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners/). Sew the button on the foundation-chain end, at mid-height. On the last-row end, join yarn at mid-height and chain a loop long enough to pass through the gap between the handle and the wall and reach the button. Slip stitch back to close the loop. Putting the loop on the last row means you can pull out rows to adjust the fit and remake the loop. The foundation end cannot be shortened.

![The wrap buttoned with the handle outside](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve-5.jpg "Illustration. The handle is not inside the crochet.")

## For a tumbler with no handle

Work a tube in rounds, from the bottom up, and spread a few single increases along it.

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bottom | 8.5 × 4 | 34 sts |
| Top | 9.3 × 4 | 37 sts |
| Rounds | 4.5 × 5 | 22 rounds |
| Increases | 37 − 34 | 3, one at rounds 6, 12, and 18 |

Chain 34 and join into a ring with a slip stitch, without twisting the chain. Work single crochet in rounds, using a marker. On rounds 6, 12, and 18, work 2 sc in one stitch, and put each increase in a different place around the tube so they do not stack into a ridge. Stop after round 22.

This sleeve has no ease. It grips the cup. If it will not go on, start again with 36 stitches and three increases to reach 39; measure both edges and adjust the whole tube rather than only enlarging the bottom.

## Heat and water

The sleeve is for holding a cup comfortably. It does not make a cup that is too hot to hold safe to hold, and it does not go in a microwave. A sweating cold cup wets the cotton. Take the sleeve off and dry it.

## Follow the exposed stitches when the wedge ends

Use a marker at each short-row turn. The first full row after the wedge travels through six stitches from the latest short-row pair, six exposed stitches from the earlier pair, then six exposed stitches from the full row below. That is eighteen actual stitches across three levels, not eighteen stitches in the latest six-stitch row. Do not fill turning-chain holes with extra sc. After the full row, remove the turn markers and count eighteen again on the return. Test on an empty, cool cup with the long edge at the larger circumference. Do not add a top wedge when the top is already loose.

## Troubleshooting

**The top edge is loose while the bottom fits.** Too many top-only short rows or too much top-edge length. Remove a wedge or reduce it. If the top edge is too tight while the bottom fits, add top-only short rows in pairs and try the strip again.

**The bottom bunches.** The wedge was worked from the bottom edge. Check which end the marker is on.

**There are holes at the turns.** Work the next full row's stitch firmly into the stitch below each step.

**The strip is an inch short.** The length was worked from the stitch gauge. Use rows per inch.

**The rounds version spirals into a ridge.** The increases stacked. Move them.

## Care

Take it off the cup. Hand wash cool. Dry flat. A damp sleeve left on a steel cup can mark it.

## Frequently asked

**Will it fit every travel cup?**

No. Measure yours. The method works on any cup you measure.

**Can I sell them?**

Finished sleeves, yes. Do not republish the rows, and do not sew a brand name on a sleeve as if the cup company made it.

## Next

The straight-mug version, without short rows, is the [mug cozy](/crochet-mug-cozy-for-beginners/). Rounds with spaced increases are explained in [increase and decrease](/crochet-increase-decrease/).


# How to Crochet a Wine Bottle Bag

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-a-wine-bottle-bag.md



A bottle in a grocery sack knocks against everything, and a paper bag's handle tears before you reach the car. A crochet bottle bag is a drawstring tube: a flat base, straight sides, and a cord that gathers around the neck. It is a carrying sleeve and a gift wrap. It will not stop glass breaking on a hard floor.

The pattern is simple, but it is tall, and two things in it are easy to get wrong. The flat base adds no height, so the side rounds have to be counted from where the base ends. And a drawstring is not a handle. A bottle lifted by its cord hangs its whole weight on the small chain loops of one round.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No bag for this page was crocheted or put on a bottle. The bottle measurements below are an invented example.

![A sage crochet drawstring bag around a plain wine bottle](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-wine-bottle-bag-result.jpg "Illustration. A tube and a drawstring. Not a tested fit on a real bottle.")


## Measure the bottle

- **Around the widest part.** Usually the body, sometimes the shoulder. Example: 9.4 inches, about 3 inches across.
- **Height to the cord point.** From the table to a little above the shoulder, where the neck begins to narrow. Example: 9 inches.

Add about half an inch of ease to the circumference. Glass that has to be forced through crochet is glass that slips.

## How the bag is built

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/bottle-bag-plan.svg" alt="A bottle in a straight tube. The flat base of 40 stitches sits under the bottle and adds no height. The sides rise from round 8 for 9 inches, 45 rounds, to just above the shoulder, where the eyelet round and two more rounds gather around the neck. An optional handle is sewn to solid rounds below the eyelets." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Count side rounds from the end of the base. The base lies under the bottle.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Base.** A flat circle that stops growing at the circumference you need. It lies under the bottle. The old version of this page counted its rounds as part of the height, which made the bag about an inch and a half short at the example gauge.

**Sides.** Even rounds. Because the count never changes, the tube's width at the eyelets is the same as at the base, so the bottle's widest part passes through without stretching.

**Eyelets.** A round of holes just above the shoulder, then two more rounds. The cord gathers the top around the neck.

## Yarn and hook

![Sage cotton yarn, a hook, and a plain bottle](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-a-wine-bottle-bag-1.jpg "Illustration. Yarn and a hook.")

- **Smooth worsted cotton.** A cold bottle sweats; cotton handles that better than acrylic. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives firm fabric. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Stitch marker, yarn needle, scissors.

Your base is your swatch. At 4 stitches per inch, 40 stitches should measure 10 inches around the edge of the flat circle.

## The worked example

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Around | 9.4 + 0.5 ease | 9.9 in, 40 sts (10 in) |
| Base across | 10 ÷ 3.14 | about 3.2 in, a little wider than the bottle |
| Side rounds | 9 in × 5 | 45 rounds, rounds 8–52 |
| Eyelets | 40 ÷ 2 | 20 holes |
| Cord | 10 in around + 2 tails of 8 in | 26 in |

## Stage 1: the base

Joined rounds: slip stitch to the first stitch and ch 1 (does not count) at the start of each round. To spiral instead, skip both and use a marker.

**Round 1:** 6 sc in a magic ring. (6)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/magic-ring.svg" alt="Four steps. 1: wrap the yarn around two fingers so the tail crosses over the working yarn. 2: insert the hook under the ring, catch the working yarn, and pull up a loop, then ch 1; the ch 1 is not a stitch. 3: work the stated number of sc into the ring, over both strands of the ring and the tail. 4: pull the tail to close the center, then count the stitches." width="480" height="1011" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The base starts in a magic ring, closed tight so the bottle cannot push through.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 2:** 2 sc in each stitch. (12)

**Round 3:** (2 sc in next, sc in next) 6 times. (18)

**Round 4:** (2 sc in next, sc 2) 6 times. (24)

**Round 5:** (2 sc in next, sc 3) 6 times. (30)

**Round 6:** (2 sc in next, sc 4) 6 times. (36)

**Round 7:** (2 sc in next, sc 8) 4 times. (40)

Round 7 adds only four stitches, to land on 40. Stand the bottle on the circle. The base should be a little wider than the bottle. If your bottle needs a different count, stop increasing when you reach it, keeping the count even for the eyelets.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/wine-bag-step-1.svg" alt="Base: rounds 1 to 7, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 1: 6 sc in a magic ring, 6 stitches. Round 2: 2 sc in each st, 12 stitches. Round 3: (2 sc in next, sc in next) 6 times, 18 stitches. Round 4: (2 sc in next, sc 2) 6 times, 24 stitches. Round 5: (2 sc in next, sc 3) 6 times, 30 stitches. Round 6: (2 sc in next, sc 4) 6 times, 36 stitches. Round 7: (2 sc in next, sc 8) 4 times, 40 stitches." width="480" height="509" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Rounds 2 to 6 add 6 each. Round 7 adds 4 to land on 40, an even count for the eyelets.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Stage 2: the sides

**Rounds 8–52:** Sc in each stitch. (40)

Slide the bottle in every 10 rounds. Stop when the fabric reaches the cord point you chose, just above the shoulder. Forty-five rounds is the example. Count the stitches now and then. A gained stitch turns the tube into a slow cone.

## Stage 3: eyelets and top

**Eyelet round:** (Sc in next, ch 1, skip 1) 20 times. (20 sc, 20 ch-1 spaces)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/wine-bag-step-2.svg" alt="Eyelet round on 40, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 52: undefined, 40 stitches. Eyelet round: (sc in next, ch 1, skip 1) 20 times: 20 sc, 20 stitches." width="480" height="509" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. The eyelet round keeps the stitch positions: 20 sc and 20 chain spaces, back to 40 sc on the next round.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Next round:** Sc in each sc and in each ch-1 space. (40)

**Last round:** Sc in each stitch. (40) Fasten off.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/wine-bag-step-3.svg" alt="The whole bag by stitch count, as bars sized by stitch count. Round 1: 6, Round 2: 12, Round 3: 18, Round 4: 24, Round 5: 30, Round 6: 36, Round 7: 40, Rounds 8 to 52: 40, Round 53: 20+20, Round 54: 40, Round 55: 40. R53 to R55 are the pattern's eyelet round (20 sc and 20 chain spaces), next round, and last round." width="480" height="541" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 3. The whole bag by stitch count. The 7 base rounds lie flat and add no height.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Cord:** chain to 26 inches, then slip stitch back along the chain so it does not stretch. Thread it in and out of the eyelets, starting and ending at the front. Knot each end.

**Checkpoint:** with the cord loose, the bottle should slide in and out. With the cord pulled, the top should close around the neck.

## Stage 4: a carry handle

Do not lift the bag by the cord. The cord passes through 20 chain-1 loops in a single round, and lifting by it hangs the full bottle from those loops. They stretch, and the eyelet round can tear.

For a handle, chain 6 and work 5-stitch rows of single crochet to about 12 inches. Sew one end to each side of the bag, opposite each other, over the two or three solid rounds just below the eyelet round. Overlap about an inch and sew a box with an X through it, through both layers.

## Support the bottle while checking the seams

Place the filled bottle into the sleeve while it stands on a table. Mark the shoulder and eyelet height without lifting it by the cord. The forty-five side rounds are counted after the seven base rounds, not including them. Sew any handle with the bottle removed and keep stitches on opposite walls rather than sewing through the whole tube. A handle is not tested merely because its overlap looks secure; support the bottle from underneath when handling or gifting it. Leave the cord loose when inserting or removing the glass, and do not force a too-tight opening.

## Troubleshooting

**The bottle will not go in.** The count is too small for the widest point. Add stitches at the base.

**The bag is short of the shoulder.** The base rounds were counted as height. Add side rounds.

**The bottle rocks inside.** The base is much wider than the bottle. Stop increasing sooner next time.

**The tube flares toward the top.** Gained stitches. Count back.

**The top will not close.** The eyelets are below the shoulder, where the bottle is still wide. Move the eyelet round higher.

**The eyelet round stretched.** It was used as a handle. Add a handle.

## Care

Take the bottle out. Hand wash cool. Dry flat with the cord loose.

## Frequently asked

**Will this fit a standard 750 ml bottle?**

Bottles of that volume come in different shapes. Measure the one you have.

**Is it safe to drop?**

No. Crochet is not padding.

**Will it fit other bottles?**

Any bottle you measure. Redo the table from its widest point and its height.

## Next

The same base and eyelet round, smaller, is a [drawstring bag](/how-to-crochet-a-drawstring-bag/). A sleeve for a cup is the [tumbler sleeve](/how-to-crochet-a-tumbler-sleeve/).


# How to Crochet an Ear Warmer

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer.md



An ear warmer is a ribbed strip joined into a loop. The starting chain is the width of the band. The rows are the distance around the head. Adding rows does not make it wider. Adding chains does not make it longer. That swap is the whole project.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. The rib is single crochet in the back loop only. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. This band has not been worn.

![An oatmeal crochet ear warmer headband](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer-result.jpg "Illustration. A wide ribbed loop.")


## Step 1: Measure the head, then decide the ease

Tape the path the band will take. Write that number down.

A shorter band than the head is negative ease. It lets a stretchy rib hug. Two inches shorter is the old example: a 22 inch path becomes a 20 inch strip. That 2 inches was not tested on a head. A stiff yarn, or cotton, may need less of a cut, or none. A very elastic wool may tolerate more. The check is the strip around the head before you seam it, not the subtraction.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ear-warmer-directions.svg" alt="Diagram of a short ribbed strip. The starting chain is the width. The rows run out to the side and become the circumference. More chains widen the band. More rows lengthen the loop." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Width and circumference are different edges. Do not add rows to fix a narrow band.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Step 2: Swatch both directions

Planning stitch gauge: **3.5 back-loop single crochet = 1 inch.** Work 14 of them. If those 14 are not about 4 inches, change the hook. [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/). [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).

Then count the rows in one inch of that same swatch. Do not copy 3.5 into the row gauge because the stitches were 3.5. Single crochet rows are often a different number per inch. The old 70-row target is 20 × 3.5, which is right only when the rows really are 3.5 per inch.

Twelve stitches at 3.5 per inch are 12 ÷ 3.5 = about **3.4 inches** wide. That width was not tried on an ear. It is only the paper width of the example.

## Materials

Worsted yarn that will stretch a little after you join it. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/). The example hook is a US H/8 (5.0 mm) only if it hits your swatch. Yarn needle, scissors, tape. No weighed yardage.

![Worsted yarn, a hook, and a tape measure](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer-1.jpg "Illustration. Yardage is not a weighed band.")

## Step 3: Rib until the relaxed strip matches the tape

Chain-1 does not count.

The 12-stitch start is the example width. For a wider band, chain more at the beginning. Chain 16 gives 15 stitches if you still start in the second chain. The circumference math does not change when you do that.

Chain 13.

**Row 1:** Single crochet in the second chain from the hook and across. Chain 1, turn. (12)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ear-warmer-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 13. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 12 sc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Chain 13 for 12 sc. The chain is the band's width, about 3.4 inches at 3.5 per inch.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Row 2:** Single crochet in the back loop only across. Chain 1, turn. (12)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/blo-rib.svg" alt="Two steps. 1: the top of each stitch has a front loop, nearer you, and a back loop, farther away. For the rib, insert the hook under the back loop only. 2: the front loops left behind form a raised ridge on every row. The fabric stretches across the ridges, in the direction the rows stack up, so a band worked sideways stretches around the head." width="480" height="593" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Every row from row 2 goes into the back loop only. The unworked front loops become the ribs.</figcaption>
</figure>

![Back-loop single crochet in oatmeal yarn](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer-2.jpg "Illustration. The rib is the back loop. The strip is still flat.")

Repeat row 2 until the strip, lying relaxed, matches the length you chose in step 1. Do not stretch it up to 70 rows to hit the example. If your row gauge is 3.5 and you did choose 20 inches, 70 rows is that arithmetic. Otherwise stop at the tape.

![The flat band beside a tape measure](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer-3.jpg "Illustration. Measure the relaxed strip. Do not stretch it to the example.")

Checkpoint: the strip is still 12 stitches wide, or your wider count. A gain at the edge makes one side longer than the other, and the seam will not lie flat.

## Step 4: Seam without a twist

Lay the strip flat. Both short ends should face the same way. Sew through both layers. If one end is flipped, the rib spirals.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/ear-warmer-seam.svg" alt="Diagram of two short ends sewn in line, beside a join where one end is flipped so the rib spirals." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Match the ends, then sew. A twist is a flipped end, not a short row count.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The short ends sewn into a loop](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer-4.jpg "Illustration. Sew the short ends. Do not twist the band.")

Put the loop on before you weave the ends. This page cannot tell you it will stay put when you look down. It can tell you what to change: if the band is loose, the strip was long; if it will not go on, the strip was short or the ease cut was too deep. Open the seam and add or remove rows. Weaving the ends into the seam, rather than across the ribs, keeps them from reading as a stripe.

A crossed front, where the ends overlap in an X, uses up some length. Measure the loop after you pin the overlap. Do not assume the overlap eats one inch. That inch was a guess.

The seam can sit at the nape so the bump is not on the forehead. That is a comfort preference, not a tested fit.

![The ear warmer next to a beanie](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer-5.jpg "Illustration. The beanie is a separate project, not part of this band.")

## Match the two short ends stitch by stitch

The short ends have twelve positions in the example: twelve used foundation-chain links at one end and twelve sc tops at the other. Hold them together without twisting and sew each matched pair, or slip stitch through both edges. Do not include the foundation's skipped chain or the unused turning chain as a thirteenth position. Try the loop before securing the final tail. If it is too small, open the seam and add whole rows; if too large, remove rows. A crossed-front version needs separate assembly instructions and is not defined by merely overlapping these ends.

## Mistakes

**The band is a spiral.** One short end was flipped. Open the seam.

**It will not go over the ears.** The strip is short, or a stiff yarn was cut 2 inches under the head. Use a smaller ease cut, or a yarn that stretches, and measure again before you sew.

**You added rows because it looked narrow.** Rows lengthen the loop. Width is the chain. Start over with more chains if you want more forehead coverage.

**Chunky yarn with 12 stitches and 70 rows.** Those counts are a worsted example, and the 70 already depends on an untested row gauge. Swatch the new yarn in both directions.

## Care

Hand wash cool. Dry flat. Read the label before a dryer. Some wool blends felt, and a felted band does not return to the length you measured.

## Frequently asked

### Will it clear a ponytail?

The band does not cover the crown, so hair gathered on top of the head is out of its path. A low ponytail can sit on the seam. Move the seam, or wear the hair down. This was not tried on a head.

### Is it a first project?

The stitch is a beginner stitch. The first project is still easier as a flat [scarf](/how-to-crochet-a-beginner-scarf/), where a twist does not have to pass over a head. Back loop, if it is new, is in [basic stitches](/basic-crochet-stitches/).

## Next

Hands are the [fingerless gloves](/how-to-crochet-fingerless-gloves/). A crown, which this band does not have, is the [chunky beanie](/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie/).


# How to Crochet Fingerless Gloves

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-fingerless-gloves.md



A fingerless glove is three parts. A ribbed cuff is worked flat and sewn into a ring. A tube of single crochet is worked in rounds from the cuff's edge. One round leaves a gap for the thumb. There is no shaping. The fit comes from five measurements and two conversions: turning cuff rows into hand stitches, and bridging the thumb gap without changing the stitch count.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No pair for this page was crocheted or worn. The hand numbers below describe one example hand so the arithmetic has values. They are not a size chart.

![A pair of oatmeal crochet fingerless gloves](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-fingerless-gloves-result.jpg "Illustration. It draws a short thumb. This page makes a thumb opening only. Not a fitted pair.")


## How the glove is built

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gloves-construction.svg" alt="Three steps. A flat ribbed cuff strip whose 9-stitch chain is the depth and whose rows go around. The strip sewn into a ring, with the seam marking where rounds join. The hand worked as a tube upward from the cuff, with a thumb gap on one side. A note says to pick up 4 stitches for every 5 row ends, so 30 rows become 24 stitches, and 28 by round 2." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The cuff is sideways: its chain is the depth along the arm, and its rows go around the wrist. The hand's first round is made from the ends of those rows.</figcaption>
</figure>

The cuff is worked sideways for a reason. Back-loop single crochet makes ridges along the rows. Turned on its side, those ridges run up the arm, and the fabric stretches around the wrist, which is the direction a cuff needs to give. The 4-H Crocheting Handbook builds its mitten cuffs on the same principle: rows of back-loop single crochet, ends joined, hand worked from the row ends. No pattern from that book is used here.

The hand is a straight tube. It does not get wider over the knuckles, because the knuckle count is reached on round 2 and held. The thumb is not cased in. A thumb opening is the whole design.

## Measure five places

Write these down for the hand you are fitting. The example values are one hand on paper.

| Measure | Example | Becomes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Around the wrist, where the cuff sits | 6.5 in | Cuff length |
| Around the knuckles, thumb tucked out of the tape | 7.5 in | Hand stitch count |
| Wrist crease to the web of the thumb | 1.5 in | Rounds below the thumb gap |
| Wrist crease to the base of the fingers | 3.5 in | Total hand rounds |
| Around the thumb at its base | 2.5 in | Width of the thumb gap |

The example takes half an inch off the wrist and the knuckles so the glove stays put: 6 inches and 7 inches. Single crochet has less stretch than the rib, so that half inch is more likely to be too much on the hand than at the wrist. If you are unsure, plan the hand at the full knuckle measurement and try it on at round 3.

## Yarn, hook, and gauge

![Worsted yarn and a hook for a pair of gloves](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-fingerless-gloves-1.jpg "Illustration. Worsted yarn and a hook.")

- **Smooth worsted yarn**, ideally with some wool. Wool recovers after it stretches. Cotton has little spring, so a cotton glove that fits in the morning can be loose by the evening. Fuzzy yarn hides the stitches you need to count. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **A US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook** as a starting point, or whatever hook matches your swatch. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **Two stitch markers:** one for the first stitch of the round, one to mark the thumb round.
- Yarn needle, scissors, tape measure.

Make two small swatches, because the cuff and the hand are different fabrics. In plain single crochet, measure stitches and rows per inch. In back-loop single crochet, measure rows per inch. A back-loop rib does not have to match the plain fabric. The example assumes 4 stitches and 5 rows per inch in plain single crochet, and 5 rows per inch in the rib. [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/) explains how to measure without stretching.

## The worked example

Every number below follows from the five measurements and the gauge. Change either, and redo this table before you chain.

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cuff depth | 2 in × 4 sts | 8 sts, chain 9 |
| Cuff length | (6.5 − 0.5) in × 5 rows | 30 rows |
| Round 1 | 6 in of cuff × 4 sts | 24 sts, 4 for every 5 row ends |
| Hand | (7.5 − 0.5) in × 4 sts | 28 sts from round 2 |
| Thumb gap position | 1.5 in × 5 rounds | Gap on round 8, after 7 rounds |
| Thumb gap width | 2.5 in ÷ 2 × 4 sts | Skip 5, chain 5 |
| Hand length | 3.5 in × 5 rounds | 17 rounds, 3.4 in |

The earlier version of this page had two counting errors. It called 28 cuff rows 7 inches at 4 rows per inch, while its own gauge said 5 rows per inch, which makes 5.6 inches. Its thumb round chained 6 over 4 skipped stitches, which quietly turns 28 stitches into 30.

## Stage 1: the cuff

Chain 9.

**Row 1:** Sc in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. Ch 1, turn. (8)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gloves-step-1.svg" alt="A foundation chain of 9. The chain nearest the hook is skipped and the first sc goes into the 2nd chain from the hook, so row 1 has 8 sc." width="480" height="466" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 1. Chain 9 for an 8-stitch cuff, about 2 inches deep. The cuff is worked sideways.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Row 2:** Sc in the back loop only of each stitch across. Ch 1, turn. (8)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/blo-rib.svg" alt="Two steps. 1: the top of each stitch has a front loop, nearer you, and a back loop, farther away. For the rib, insert the hook under the back loop only. 2: the front loops left behind form a raised ridge on every row. The fabric stretches across the ridges, in the direction the rows stack up, so a band worked sideways stretches around the head." width="480" height="593" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Back loop only from row 2 gives the cuff its stretch around the wrist.</figcaption>
</figure>

Repeat row 2 until you have 30 rows. The ch 1 does not count as a stitch.

Record rows as you work; use ridges as a cross-check, not a replacement for the row count. In back-loop rows, each raised ridge is two rows, so 30 rows show 15 ridges on each side. Count the stitches every few rows too. A ninth stitch makes the cuff deeper at one end, and the seam will not meet.

**Checkpoint:** relaxed, the strip is about 6 inches long. Wrap it around the wrist. It should close with a little stretch. If it overlaps, take out rows. If it gapes, add them.

Fold the strip so row 30 meets row 1, right sides together, and slip stitch through both layers across the 8 stitches. Do not twist the strip. Turn it right side out. That seam is where every round of the hand will start and end. Wear it on the little-finger side of the wrist.

## Stage 2: the hand, up to the thumb

**Round 1:** Join yarn at the seam on one row-end edge. Ch 1. Work 24 sc around the edge by working 1 sc in each of 4 row ends, then skipping 1, all the way round. Slip stitch to the first sc. (24)

The cuff edge is 6 inches. At 4 stitches per inch, 6 inches holds 24 stitches. One stitch in every row end would make 30, which is 7.5 inches of fabric on a 6 inch edge, and the glove flares above the cuff like a bell. The [bowl cozy](/how-to-crochet-a-bowl-cozy/) uses the same 4-for-5 ratio to join row ends to stitch tops.

**Round 2:** Ch 1. (Sc in the next 5 stitches, 2 sc in the next) 4 times. Slip stitch to the first sc. (28)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gloves-step-2.svg" alt="Round 2: 24 to 28, drawn as rings from the center out. Round 1: undefined, 24 stitches. Round 2: (sc 5, 2 sc in next) 4 times, 28 stitches." width="480" height="509" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Step 2. Four increases take the hand from the wrist's 24 stitches to the knuckles' 28.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Rounds 3–7:** Ch 1, sc in each stitch, slip stitch to the first sc. (28)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/join-round-slst.svg" alt="Rounds shown unrolled as strips, with the join of each round marked. Correct: slip stitch into the top of the first sc, ch 1, and work the first sc of the next round into that same stitch; the joins stack in a straight line. Mistake: the first sc goes into the next stitch instead, so every join moves one stitch sideways and the seam slants." width="480" height="584" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>These rounds are joined. Starting each round in the same stitch as the join keeps the seam straight and the count at 28.</figcaption>
</figure>

Put the marker in the first stitch of each round. The join of a joined round tends to drift a little to one side. That is normal. Count from the marker, not from where the seam seems to be.

**Try it on after round 3.** The tube should close around the palm without gaps and without straining. If it is tight, rework round 2 with six increases for 30 stitches. If it slides around, work round 2 with two increases for 26.

**If you changed the hand count.** Recalculate the thumb round rather than reusing 11 + 5 + 12. With a five-stitch gap, 26 stitches use sc 10, ch 5, skip 5, sc 11; 30 stitches use sc 12, ch 5, skip 5, sc 13. On the next round work one sc into every sc and each of the five chains. For a six-stitch gap at 28, use sc 11, ch 6, skip 6, sc 11, then restore all 28 stitch positions on the following round.

## Stage 3: the thumb gap

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/gloves-thumb-round.svg" alt="Round 8 opened into a line of 28 stitch boxes: 11 single crochet, 5 skipped boxes under an arched chain of 5, then 12 single crochet. The gap is centered on stitch 14, opposite the join. The next round is 11 plus 5 plus 12, which is 28. A note shows that a chain of 6 over 4 skipped stitches gives 30." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The chain replaces the skipped stitches one for one. That is why the next round still has 28.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Round 8:** Ch 1. Sc in the next 11 stitches, ch 5, skip the next 5 stitches, sc in the last 12. Slip stitch to the first sc. (23 sc and a ch-5)

**Round 9:** Ch 1. Sc in the next 11 stitches, sc in each of the 5 chains, sc in the last 12. Slip stitch to the first sc. (28)

Why skip half the thumb's circumference: the hole is the 5 skipped stitches on one side and the 5 chains on the other, so its edge runs about 1.25 inches each way, about 2.5 inches around, plus the height of the round. That is an estimate. Try it on before you go further.

The gap sits directly opposite the seam, centered on stitch 14 of 28. With the seam on the little-finger side, the gap lands on the thumb side, and the left and right gloves are identical. Turn one over for the other hand. Two "right" gloves only happen when the gap is placed somewhere else.

If the edge of the hole cuts into the web of your thumb, pull out rounds 8 and 9 and skip 6 with a chain of 6. If you ever chain more than you skip, decrease the difference on the next round, or the hand gets wider from there up.

## Stage 4: the upper hand and the edge

**Rounds 10–17:** Ch 1, sc in each stitch, slip stitch to the first sc. (28)

Try it on at round 14. Stop when the fabric reaches the base of the fingers and the fingers still bend freely. Seventeen rounds is the example's 3.5 inches at 5 rounds per inch. Your round count is whatever reaches your knuckles.

Fasten off. If the top edge flips outward, work one loose round of slip stitch to firm it. For a neater thumb hole, join yarn at one side and work about 12 sc around it: 5 along the skipped stitches, 5 along the chain, and 1 at each side.

## The second glove

Write down four numbers from the first glove: cuff rows, the round 1 count, the round with the thumb gap, and the total rounds. Work the second to the same numbers. Before you weave in ends, lay the two gloves side by side. The holes should be at the same height and the tops should line up.

## Recenter the thumb gap after a fit adjustment

The example hand is twenty-eight stitches. Its gap row uses eleven sc, five skipped stitches and twelve sc: twenty-eight base positions, with five chains replacing the skipped span. If you altered the hand count, use the recalculated gap counts above. On the following round, insert into each chain link, not five sc all into one chain space, unless you deliberately choose that alternative and recount. Keep twenty-eight positions through the upper hand. Before making the second glove, record the final hand count and thumb span as well as the cuff rows and total rounds; a resized first glove needs a matching second.

## Troubleshooting

**The glove flares just above the cuff.** Round 1 has too many stitches for the cuff edge. Rework it at 4 stitches for every 5 row ends, or whatever your gauge gives.

**The fabric puckers just above the cuff.** Too few stitches on round 1. Pick up more evenly.

**The glove rotates until the hole is on the back of the hand.** The tube is loose. Fewer stitches on round 2.

**The thumb hole is on the palm, not the side.** The gap was not opposite the join, or you counted from a drifting seam. Count from the marker.

**The cuff stretches out over the day.** The rib is loose, or the yarn has no spring. Work the cuff on a hook one size smaller, or use a yarn with some wool.

**One glove is longer.** Different round counts. Compare the notes before blaming gauge.

**Double crochet for speed.** The gaps let cold through and catch on zippers. Stay with single crochet.

## Care

Wash the pair together so they wear the same way. Dry flat. Do not hang them by the thumb hole, because the chain stretches.

## Frequently asked

**Can I add a real thumb?**

A thumb sleeve is a short tube worked in rounds around the opening. It is not specified on this page, and no thumb was tested.

**What about a child?**

Measure the child and redo the table. Do not scale the example by eye. A thumb gap that is too small matters more on a small hand.

**Can I use the rib for the whole glove?**
