You can, but the hand stitch count then comes from the rib's row gauge, and the pickup step disappears. That is a different pattern.

## Next

The same back-loop rib, as a band around the head, is the [ear warmer](/how-to-crochet-an-ear-warmer/). [Increases and decreases](/crochet-increase-decrease/) explains how to spread the round 2 increases evenly, and [crochet in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/) covers joined rounds versus spirals.


# How to Crochet Slippers

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-crochet-slippers.md



Socks slide on a wood floor, and a bought slipper is often a size you do not have. A crochet slipper can follow the foot you measured. It will still slide on tile, wood, and any hard floor. These are indoor slippers for a rug or carpet. They are not shoes, and they are not safe on stairs.

The shape is a clog: a flat sole, a cap over the toes, and a low wall around the heel. Each part is a rectangle. The part most simple slipper patterns get wrong is the cap. A foot has height. If the cap is the same width as the sole and is sewn down flat on top of it, there is no room for toes inside. The cap has to be as wide as the distance from one edge of the sole, **over** the foot, to the other edge. This page shows how to measure that.

US terms. US single crochet is UK double crochet. [Abbreviations](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). The pictures are illustrations. No pair for this page was crocheted or worn. The example measurements are invented to show the arithmetic.

![A pair of simple crochet slippers with a toe cap and a heel tab](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-slippers-result.jpg "Illustration. A flat sole, a toe cap, and a heel.")

<div class="warning">

Crochet soles are slippery on hard floors. Tile, wood, laminate, and a wet bathroom floor are all a fall risk, and stairs are worse. Wear these on carpet or a rug. A sock underneath does not add grip. This page does not include a non-slip sole.

</div>


## How it is built

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/slipper-plan.svg" alt="Top view of a sole 14 stitches wide and 48 rows long. A toe cap 24 stitches wide and 16 rows deep is sewn to the first 16 rows of each side and gathered onto the 14 toe stitches. A heel wall 26 stitches long and 8 rows high wraps the heel end and 8 rows up each side. 24 rows of each side stay open. An inset shows a tape from one edge of the sole, over the foot, to the other edge: about 6 inches." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The cap's width is the over-the-top measurement, not the sole width. Its extra stitches gather at the toe.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Measure the foot

Stand on paper and measure three things:

1. **Length**, heel to longest toe.
2. **Width**, across the widest part.
3. **Over the top.** About 3 inches back from the tip of the longest toe, run a tape from the floor on one side of the foot, over the top, to the floor on the other side. That is the width the cap needs.

The example foot is 9 inches long, 3.5 inches wide, and 6 inches over the top.

## Yarn and hook

![Worsted yarn, a hook, and a tape measure](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-slippers-1.jpg "Illustration. Yarn and tools. The sole is still slippery.")

- **Worsted yarn.** Wool or a wool blend is warmer. Cotton washes more easily and feels cooler. Both slide on hard floors. [Yarn weights](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that makes dense fabric. A larger hook makes a floppy sole. [Hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- Yarn needle, scissors, a scrap of yarn to mark the toe.

Single crochet is used throughout because it is the densest of the [basic stitches](/basic-crochet-stitches/). Swatch 14 sc for 20 rows. In the example it would be 3.5 inches wide and 4 inches tall. [Gauge](/crochet-gauge/).

## The worked example

| Part | Arithmetic | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sole width | 3.5 in × 4 | 14 sts |
| Sole length | 9 in + about 0.6 in, × 5 | 48 rows (9.6 in) |
| Cap width | 6 in over the top × 4 | 24 sts |
| Cap depth | about 3.2 in × 5 | 16 rows |
| Heel wall | 3.5 in across + 1.6 in up each side | about 6.5 in, 26 sts |
| Heel height | 1.6 in × 5 | 8 rows |
| Opening each side | 48 − 16 − 8 | 24 rows, about 4.8 in |

The sole is a little longer than the foot so the toes do not press against the end.

## Stage 1: the soles (make 2)

Ch 1 does not count as a stitch.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/slipper-step-1.svg" alt="Sole: 14 sc, 48 rows. Toe cap: 24 sc, 16 rows. Heel wall: 26 sc, 8 rows." width="480" height="486" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>The three pieces of one slipper, drawn to the same scale from the example counts.</figcaption>
</figure>

Chain 15.

**Row 1:** Sc in the 2nd chain from the hook and across. Ch 1, turn. (14)

**Rows 2–48:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (14)

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/turn-first-stitch.svg" alt="Two rows of single crochet. Correct: the turning ch 1 sits beside the edge and the first sc of the new row goes into the first stitch, the last one made on the row below, so the count stays the same. Mistake: the first sc goes into the ch 1, outside the edge, which adds a stitch and makes the edge flare." width="480" height="551" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>All three pieces are plain sc rows. Start each row in the edge stitch so the widths stay at 14, 24, and 26.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Checkpoint:** Stand on it. The longest toe should sit just inside the far end. Add rows if not. If the width is tight, restart with more stitches.

Mark one short end with yarn as the toe. Both ends look alike. Make the second sole and count its rows. The two must match.

## Stage 2: the toe caps (make 2)

Chain 25.

**Row 1:** Sc in the 2nd chain from the hook and across. Ch 1, turn. (24)

**Rows 2–16:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (24)

**Sewing:**

1. Sew the side edges of the cap to the first 16 rows of each side of the sole, row end to row end, starting at the toe. Both use 5 rows per inch, so they match.
2. Run a length of yarn through the cap's chain edge. Gather it until it fits the 14 stitches of the toe end, then sew it down. The gathers make room for the toes.
3. The last row of the cap stays open. That is the instep edge.

![A toe cap sewn to one end of a rectangular sole](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-slippers-3.jpg "Illustration. The marked end is the toe. The last row of the cap stays open.")

**Checkpoint:** Put the foot in. The toes should fit under the cap without pressing it tight. If they do not, the cap needs more stitches. If the cap gapes loosely, it has too many.

## Stage 3: the heel walls (make 2)

Chain 27.

**Row 1:** Sc in the 2nd chain from the hook and across. Ch 1, turn. (26)

**Rows 2–8:** Sc in each stitch. Ch 1, turn. (26)

**Sewing:** Find the middle of the chain edge. Pin it to the middle of the heel end. Sew 14 stitches across the heel end, then 6 stitches up each side over the last 8 rows of the sole. The wall stands up around the heel. The top edge stays open.

![A short heel tab on the unmarked end of the sole](../../assets/articles/how-to-crochet-slippers-4.jpg "Illustration. The heel is the unmarked end.")

## Stage 4: finishing

Weave the ends in on the inside. [Weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/). Sew without pulling hard, because a puckered seam cups the sole and makes it rock when you stand.

Left and right slippers are identical, so the second is made to the same counts.

## Baste one slipper before making the second

The toe cap's twenty-four-stitch foundation is gathered to the sole's fourteen-stitch toe edge, while its sixteen row-end positions match the first sixteen sole rows on each side. Baste both side seams first, then ease the foundation edge evenly rather than putting all gathers at one corner. On the heel wall, allocate fourteen chain positions to the heel end and six to each side; distribute those six over the final eight sole row ends. Leave both wall short ends unsewn unless a separate design calls for joining them to the toe cap. Try the foot in on carpet before repeating the counts for the second slipper.

## Troubleshooting

**Toes will not fit under the cap.** The cap is too narrow. Measure over the top again and add stitches.

**The foot will not go in.** The cap is sewn too far down the sides. Sew fewer rows.

**The heel walks out.** The heel wall is too low or too loose. Add rows, or ease it in.

**The sole rocks.** A seam was pulled tight and cupped the sole.

**The pair do not match.** Different row counts. Count, do not compare by eye.

**It slides.** It is a crochet sole. Keep it on a rug.

## Care

Hand wash cool. Dry flat. A damp sole is slicker. Do not wear them wet.

## Frequently asked

**Will this fit every foot?**

Only if you measure. The example counts suit only the example foot.

**Can I add a non-slip sole?**

People do, using products sold for that purpose. This page has not tested any, and grip depends on the product and your floor. Follow the product's instructions and test it carefully on a rug first.

**Can I sell them?**

Finished slippers, yes. Do not republish the pattern. Tell the buyer they slide on hard floors.

**Are they warm enough?**

Wool is warmer than cotton. Neither replaces a shoe outdoors.

## Next

A winter project that stays off the floor is the [chunky beanie](/how-to-crochet-a-chunky-beanie/). The same measure-then-calculate approach to a hand is the [fingerless gloves](/how-to-crochet-fingerless-gloves/).


# How to Read a Crochet Pattern, Line by Line

Source: src/content/posts/how-to-read-a-crochet-pattern.md



Crochet patterns are shorthand. The line below is a whole row, and the rest of a pattern uses the same pieces.

> **Row 4:** Ch 3 (does not count as a st), *2 dc in next st, sk 1 st, rep from * across, turn. (24 sts)

![A printed crochet pattern beside yarn and a hook on a desk](../../assets/articles/how-to-read-a-crochet-pattern-result.jpg "Illustration. Read the materials and gauge notes before the first stitch line.")

## Materials, hook, and gauge

Read the pattern once before you chain. Check for a special-stitches section, for US or UK terms, and for the construction: flat and seamed, or in one piece in the round, brim first or crown first.

**Yarn.** Weight (a number from 0 to 7 on the Craft Yarn Council scale, or a name such as worsted or DK), total yardage, and the yarn used. Buy by yards or meters, plus ten percent. Ball sizes differ by brand.

**Hook.** Trust the millimeters, not the letter. Letters differ by maker. The [hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/) converts them.

**Gauge.** Often `16 sc and 18 rows = 4 inches`, in the pattern stitch. The same yarn and hook can still differ by an inch or two, because one person holds the yarn tighter. Swatch a bit larger than 4 inches square, lay it flat, and count the middle 4 inches. Too many stitches: go up a hook size. Too few: go down.

<div class="tip">

For an unfitted practice piece, work to a measurement instead of matching an exact gauge. Swatch for a target-size blanket, a fitted bag or wallet, and any project with a specific size requirement. Do not skip it for hats, sweaters, mittens, socks, gloves, or amigurumi. Loose amigurumi lets the stuffing show.

</div>

**Notions and size.** Markers, a tapestry needle, safety eyes, buttons. A hat listed at 21 inches will not become 23 inches after you finish it. Check the finished size first.

## US or UK terms

US and UK names are shifted by one. A US single crochet is a UK double crochet. If `sc` appears, the pattern is US. UK terms have no single crochet. If you see `htr`, it is UK. The table is in the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/). If you cannot tell, check the designer's stitch definitions or ask before starting. Do not assume a terminology system from the photograph.

## Rows and rounds

**Rows** are flat. You turn, and the next row starts with a turning chain.

**Joined rounds** end in a slip stitch and a chain up, which leaves a seam. **Spiral rounds** never join. Amigurumi usually works this way. Mark the first stitch and move the marker each round. Without it, you end up counting from the beginning.

## Reading a single line

![Finger pointing at crochet pattern abbreviations on paper](../../assets/articles/how-to-read-a-crochet-pattern-1.jpg "Illustration. One pattern line is a recipe — abbreviations, counts, and punctuation.")

The sample row, in order:

- **`Row 4:`** This row only. `Rows 4–12:` means the same instruction for each of those rows.
- **`Ch 3 (does not count as a st)`** Chain 3 for height only. The first repeat starts in the first stitch. When a pattern says `ch 3 (counts as dc)` instead, the chain is the first double crochet: you skip the first stitch, and the chain is included in the count.
- **`*` through `rep from *`** The part you repeat.
- **`2 dc in next st`** Two double crochets in one stitch. That is an increase.
- **`sk 1 st`** Skip the next stitch.
- **`rep from * across`** Keep alternating those two instructions to the end of the row.
- **`turn`** Flip the work.
- **`(24 sts)`** A checkpoint. You should have 24 stitches. It is not another stitch to make.

**Punctuation.** Parentheses group stitches that all go in one place: `(2 dc, ch 1, 2 dc) in next st`. Parentheses at the end of a row are the count instead. Brackets repeat a set number of times: `[sc, ch 1] x 6`. An asterisk marks a repeat that runs to the end of the row or round. A number before a stitch is how many to make (`3 dc`). A number inside the name is one combined stitch (`dc3tog`, a decrease made from three stitches).

One line, expanded. The sample row starts on 24 stitches, and its chain-3 is not counted. Each repeat uses 2 stitches of the old row (one that receives two double crochets, one that is skipped) and makes 2 new double crochets. Twelve repeats use 24 old stitches and make 24 new ones. The count stays 24. If you finish with 26, an extra increase landed in a stitch that should have been skipped. If you finish with 25, you probably counted the chain-3 as a stitch.

## Count your stitches

![Finger counting stitches on a cream swatch beside a printed pattern](../../assets/articles/how-to-read-a-crochet-pattern-2.jpg "Illustration. Count after every row or round until the numbers stick.")

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/count-stitches.svg" alt="Diagram of four chain-top Vs numbered 1 through 4, and a loop still on the hook marked as not a stitch." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The number in parentheses is how many finished Vs you should have. The loop on the hook is not an extra stitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

Use the number at the end of the row. Most drift comes from three places. The turning chain, if you sometimes count it and sometimes do not. The last stitch, easy to miss in double crochet, where the previous chain looks like fabric. Losing one stitch every row is usually that. And `ch-2 sp`, which means the hole, not the chain stitches. The wrong choice changes the count and the gauge.

If you are off by a stitch or two, undo to the last row you trusted. Compensating on the next row does not fix it.

## A line small enough to check by hand

This example is only a count. It is not a project.

**Foundation:** chain 11.

**Row 1:** single crochet in the second chain from the hook and in each chain across. (10 sc)

Eleven chains, skip the one nearest the hook, 11 − 1 = 10. The loop still on the hook is not an eleventh stitch.

**Row 2:** chain 1, turn, single crochet in each stitch across. (10 sc)

For this exercise the chain-1 does not count. Another pattern may say its turning chain does count. Use that pattern's note, not this one.

A bracket works the same way. `[sc in next st, 2 sc in next st] 3 times` means one single crochet, then two single crochets in the following stitch, and do that pair three times. Each repeat uses 2 old stitches and makes 3 new ones. Three repeats use 6 old stitches and make 9. If the row does not have those 6 stitches left, the repeat does not fit.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/pattern-repeat.svg" alt="Diagram of the repeat single crochet, then two single crochets in the next stitch, worked three times. It uses six old stitches and makes nine new stitches." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Count what the repeat uses, then what it makes, then multiply by how many times it says.</figcaption>
</figure>

The same rules are on the [pattern practice page](/crochet-pattern-practice/), five questions with the reason after each answer.

## Several sizes, and repeats

Garment patterns stack sizes in one line:

> Ch 60 (66, 72, 78)

The first number is the smallest size. The rest follow the size list at the top. Before row one, circle every number that belongs to your size.

`Worked in multiples of 6 + 2` tells you how to change the width yourself. The starting chain must be a multiple of six, plus two. Chains of 20, 26, 32, and 38 work. A chain of 25 does not, and the pattern breaks at the end of the first row. Some designers state the multiple for the stitches and add the turning chain separately, so read the note under the foundation row before you chain.

## Make a row-by-row worksheet

Before starting, write four columns on paper: row/round, previous count, stitches consumed by one repeat, and resulting count. For (sc 2, inc) six times on eighteen stitches, write eighteen, three, twenty-four. For (sc 2, sc2tog) six times on twenty-four, write twenty-four, four, eighteen. A foundation chain and a turning chain are accounted for separately. If a pattern offers several sizes, highlight one complete size through every step before chaining. Do not silently repair an impossible repeat by adding stitches: check the designer's correction or resolve the construction first.

## Mistakes

Skipping the notes, where the designer warns that the piece runs small, the first round is awkward, or the yarn splits. Guessing a special stitch instead of reading the definition at the top. Mixing size numbers. And redoing a row that cannot add up before you search the designer's name plus "errata."

Some lace and Japanese patterns use a diagram. The symbols draw the shape.

## Frequently asked

**Do I need every abbreviation first?** No. Keep the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) open. About fifteen of them stick within a month.

**Why does it get narrower or wider?** The count is drifting, usually at the turning chain or the last stitch. Count each row until you see which.

**What is a sound first pattern?** Flat, one color, worsted, in rows of one stitch. A dishcloth or a simple scarf. Leave shaping and color changes for later.


# Left-Handed Crochet: How to Follow Any Pattern

Source: src/content/posts/left-handed-crochet.md



Roughly one person in ten is left-handed, and most crochet tutorials are filmed right-handed. The usual workaround used to be holding a printed diagram up to a mirror, or tracing it and flipping the tracing.

You can do better than that now. Here is how to follow any pattern, diagram, or video left-handed without translating anything by hand.

![Two pairs of hands mirroring each other, one crocheting left-handed and one right-handed](../../assets/articles/left-handed-crochet-1.jpg "Illustration. Same stitches, same fabric, opposite direction of travel.")


![A left hand crocheting a cream swatch with an aluminum hook](../../assets/articles/left-handed-crochet-result.jpg "Illustration. Same stitches, opposite direction of travel.")
## The good news first

**Crochet is not a handed craft the way writing is.** Both hands work the whole time: one holds the hook and one manages the tension and the fabric. Left-handed crochet is a genuine mirror image of right-handed crochet, and every stitch works the same way. Nothing is off limits. The stitches are not harder. Following a right-handed video without flipping it is harder, and that is covered below.

There are only three real differences, and they are all mechanical.

## Difference 1: you work in the opposite direction

Right-handers work a row from right to left. You work from left to right.

![A left hand working a row of crochet from left to right across the fabric](../../assets/articles/left-handed-crochet-2.jpg "Illustration. The finished stitches of the row sit to the left of the hook, and the fabric still to be worked runs off to the right.")

That is the entire change for flat work in rows. Chain, turn, work back the other way. Your stitches are identical, your fabric is identical, and your finished piece is identical.

**In the round**, right-handers work counterclockwise. You work clockwise. Again, the result is the same object.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/left-handed-direction.svg" alt="Diagram comparing direction of travel. Right-handed rows go right to left and rounds counterclockwise. Left-handed rows go left to right and rounds clockwise." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The only change in the hands is the direction of travel.</figcaption>
</figure>

## Difference 2: written patterns say "right" and "left"

Mirror directions that describe hook travel. Keep garment labels such as the wearer's right front tied to the wearer; do not swap every use of “right” and “left.” Resolve each asymmetric placement from the intended finished orientation.

This matters in exactly three situations, and is irrelevant everywhere else:

- **Shaping instructions in garments** — "work to the right front edge" needs swapping.
- **Directional colorwork or motifs** — anything where the design has a definite left and right.
- **Anything asymmetric** — a cardigan's buttonband, a bag's side pocket, an animal's ears if they differ.

For a scarf, a blanket, a hat, a dishcloth, a granny square, or a plain amigurumi body, there is nothing to swap. The overwhelming majority of beginner patterns need no translation at all.

<div class="tip">

**Before starting a garment, read the pattern through once with a pencil** and label the intended garment sides and your starting edge before changing any directional instruction. Ten minutes up front, and you never have to think about it again while your hands are busy.

</div>

## Difference 3: your fabric is a mirror image

Your finished piece is the mirror of the photograph in the pattern.

For nearly everything, this is invisible and irrelevant — a mirror-image granny square is a granny square. It becomes visible in two cases:

**Directional stitch patterns.** Anything that leans, spirals, or slants — chevrons, spike stitches, some cables — will lean the other way. Usually this looks perfectly good and nobody but you will notice.

**Written text and lettering.** If you are crocheting letters or a graphgan with words, they *will* come out backwards. For these, keep the lettering layout in its intended orientation and read right-side rows in your working direction. A mirrored photograph of text is not the chart to follow. Check a small letter sample before committing to a graphgan.

## How to use right-handed videos

This is the practical problem, and there are three solutions that all work better than tracing paper.

**1. Search for left-handed tutorials directly.** Add "left handed" to the search. For the basic stitches and the magic ring, left-handed videos are easy to find. For a specific designer's project, they usually are not.

**2. Mirror the video on your device.** Some video players and apps can flip playback horizontally, and on a desktop there are browser extensions that mirror video. A mirrored right-handed tutorial shows exactly what your hands should do. Check what your own device offers, because the options change.

**3. Use an actual mirror.** Prop a mirror beside your screen and watch the reflection. Low-tech, free, and it works on any screen.

Do not bother learning to mirror the movements in your head while also learning the stitch. Flip the video and learn the stitch once.

## Reading stitch diagrams

Stitch diagrams — the symbol charts common in lace and Japanese patterns — are read bottom to top by everyone. The horizontal direction is what changes.

| | Right-handed | Left-handed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Right-side rows | right to left | left to right |
| Wrong-side rows | left to right | right to left |
| Rounds | counterclockwise | clockwise |

Circular motif diagrams need nothing at all if the motif is symmetrical, which most are.

## Should you just learn right-handed?

Some left-handers do, and it genuinely works for a fair number of people — because both hands are already working, the hook hand is doing less fine motor work than you might assume. If you have a right-handed friend or relative teaching you in person, learning their way can be the path of least resistance.

But do not force it. If it feels wrong after a few sessions, switch. Working left-handed costs you nothing except occasionally flipping a video, and a craft you resent is a craft you quit.

## Practical notes

**Hooks are symmetrical.** Standard aluminum and plastic hooks work identically in either hand, so there is nothing special to buy. The one exception is some ergonomic handles with a thumb rest shaped for a specific grip — check that the rest is symmetrical, or use a plain hook.

![A plain aluminum crochet hook held in the left hand, working a swatch of ivory yarn](../../assets/articles/left-handed-crochet-4.jpg "Illustration. An ordinary hook off the shelf. Both hands are working either way — the only thing that changes is which one holds the hook.")

**Seaming and joining are unaffected.** Whipstitching, slip stitch joins, and weaving in ends have no handedness.

**Gauge swatches are unaffected.** Your gauge is your gauge. Nothing about being left-handed changes how gauge works or how to measure it.

## Separate movement directions from garment labels

Mirror hook movements and the direction you travel along stitches. Keep garment names tied to the wearer: a “right front” panel remains the wearer's right front when a left-handed person makes it. For asymmetric pieces, identify both the starting edge and the finished orientation before swapping any instruction. A chart containing letters must remain readable on the finished public face; change the direction you read the row rather than automatically flipping the entire lettering image. Mark the intended public face with a removable strand and compare the first few chart rows before working the full motif.

## Frequently asked

**Will my stitches look different from the pattern photo?** Only mirrored, and only visibly so on directional stitch patterns. The fabric quality, tension, and structure are identical.

**Can I work on a project a right-hander started?** Yes. You will be working in the opposite direction from them, but the stitches you meet are the same stitches. Collaborative blankets get made this way all the time.

**Are there left-handed patterns?** A few designers publish them, but you do not need them. Standard patterns work with the swaps above, and for most beginner projects there is nothing to swap.

**Why was I told to trace the diagrams?** A printed diagram cannot flip itself, so tracing or a mirror was the only way to see it reversed. A mirrored video or a mirror beside the screen does the same job with less effort.


# Pumpkin Crochet Wallet: Free Jack-o'-Lantern Card Pouch Pattern

Source: src/content/posts/pumpkin-crochet-wallet.md



Most crochet pumpkins are round, which is a poor shape for cards. This one is a flat pouch that still reads as a jack-o'-lantern, because the ribs run up and down the way a real pumpkin's do. You get those ribs for free by working sideways in the back loop only. No color changes, no counting ribs, and one fold makes the pocket.

It is one of five patterns in the [Halloween crochet wallet collection](/halloween-crochet-wallets/), and the most forgiving one for a first project.

![An orange crochet pumpkin pouch with a green stem, a black face, and a zipper](../../assets/articles/pumpkin-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

The finished size and card fit here are worked out from the example gauge rather than measured from a finished wallet, so swatch in back loops (those rows run shorter) and slip a real bank card in before you fold. A standard US bank card is 3.370 by 2.125 inches, the ISO/IEC 7810 ID-1 size.

## Materials

- **Worsted weight (CYC 4) yarn** in orange. Cotton shows the ribs most crisply. [Yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **Scraps** of black for the face and green for the stem and tab.
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives the gauge. [Hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One sew-on snap**, 3/8 in (10 mm), and thread.
- Tapestry needle, scissors, a stitch marker, one card.

**How much yarn.** Not weighed from a made wallet. The pouch is about 24 square inches, roughly 1.5 times a 4 inch swatch. Weigh your back-loop swatch, multiply by 1.5, and add 20 percent.

## Abbreviations and stitches

US terms. See the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/).

| Abbreviation | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| ch | chain |
| sc | single crochet |
| BLO | back loop only: insert the hook under the loop farther from you |
| sc3tog | single crochet three together: pull up a loop in each of 3 stitches, yarn over, pull through all 4 loops |
| st(s) | stitch(es) |

**Turning chain rule.** Every row: ch 1, turn. The ch 1 does not count as a stitch.

## Size and gauge

**Gauge:** 16 sc and 20 rows = 4 in in single crochet, as an example. Work your swatch in BLO, because back-loop row gauge can differ from plain single crochet. The [gauge guide](/crochet-gauge/) shows how to measure.

The piece is worked sideways. The 20 stitches in each row set the height before folding: 5 inches, which folds to 2.5. The number of rows sets the width. You work rows until the piece measures 4.25 inches, which is about 22 rows at the example gauge, whatever your back-loop row gauge turns out to be. After seaming, the inside is about 3.9 inches wide and 2.4 deep against a 3.37 by 2.125 inch card.

## The pattern

### Stage 1: the ribbed piece

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/pumpkin-wallet-sideways.svg" alt="Diagram. The piece laid flat with vertical orange ribs. Stitch 1 at the top is the front rim, stitch 20 at the bottom is the back rim, and a dashed fold line runs between stitches 10 and 11. Rows run up and down from row 1 at the left to row 22 at the right." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Rows run up and down. The fold runs across the middle of every row.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/pumpkin-wallet-ribbing.svg" alt="Diagram. A close-up of the top of a single crochet, with the hook in the back loop and the front loop left as a ridge. Three swatch options: back loop every row for 11 ribs over 22 rows, both loops for a flat face, or two shades of orange changing every 2 rows." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. One raised rib for every two rows, on one face of the fabric.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The orange piece laid flat, with the ribs running vertically, before the face or the fold](../../assets/articles/pumpkin-crochet-wallet-ribs.jpg "Illustration.")

With orange:

**Row 1:** Ch 21. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and in each ch across. (20)

**Row 2 and every row after:** Ch 1, turn. Sc BLO in each st across. (20)

Work until the piece measures 4.25 inches from the chain edge to the top of the current row, ending on an even-numbered row. At the example gauge that is about 22 rows. Fasten off.

The side with the raised ribs is the right side. Mark it. Then pick one long edge to be the front rim. From here on, stitches are counted from that edge: stitch 1 at the front rim, stitch 10 next to the fold, stitch 20 at the back rim.

<div class="tip">

Count 20 at the end of every few rows. Back-loop rows are easy to miscount at the turn, because the first stitch's back loop hides under the turning chain.

</div>

### Stage 2: the rims

With the right side facing, turn the piece so the back rim edge, the long edge of row ends at stitch 20, is at the top.

**Back rim:** Join orange at the right-hand corner. Ch 1. Work 22 sc along the edge, one in each row end. (22, or one per row if you worked a different number) Fasten off.

Join orange at the other long edge.

**Front rim:** Ch 1. Work 22 sc along the edge, one in each row end. Fasten off.

These two edges become the opening. One sc per row end keeps them from flaring. If the rim ripples, you added stitches; if it puckers, you skipped row ends.

### Stage 3: the face, before folding

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/pumpkin-wallet-face.svg" alt="Diagram. A grid of the front half with rows 1 to 22 left to right and stitches 1 to 10 down from the rim. The leaf tab covers rows 10 to 13 on stitches 1 to 5. Triangle eyes span rows 5 to 7 and 16 to 18 on stitches 3 to 5. The nose covers rows 11 and 12 on stitches 6 and 7. A zigzag mouth runs from row 6 to row 17 on stitches 8 and 9." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Row k mirrors row 23 minus k. Stitch 1 is at the front rim.</figcaption>
</figure>

On the right side of the front half, stitches 1 to 10:

1. **Eyes.** With black, a triangle on rows 5 to 7 over stitches 3 to 5, point toward the rim or toward the mouth, your choice. Outline in backstitch, then fill with close straight stitches. Repeat on rows 16 to 18.
2. **Nose.** A small triangle on rows 11 and 12 over stitches 6 and 7.
3. **Mouth.** A zigzag in backstitch from row 6 to row 17, moving between stitch 8 and stitch 9 every two rows. Following the ribs makes the points even.

Keep rows 10 to 13 on stitches 1 to 5 clear; the leaf tab covers that spot. Never pass the needle past stitch 10, or you will stitch the fold shut.

### Stage 4: fold and seam

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/pumpkin-wallet-fold.svg" alt="Diagram. Left, the flat piece with a 22 single crochet rim on each long edge and a fold line between stitches 10 and 11, with the two short sides marked as seams. Right, the folded pouch to scale, about 4.25 by 2.5 inches outside, with an ID-1 card inside and the inside about 3.9 by 2.4 inches on paper." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Fold right side out so the ribs and face stay outside.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. Fold between stitch 10 and stitch 11, right side out, so the two rims meet at the top.
2. Seam the foundation-chain edge: whipstitch with orange through both layers, stitch 10 to stitch 11, stitch 9 to stitch 12, and so on up to the rims. Catch the rim stitch at the top.
3. Seam the last-row edge the same way.
4. Slide a card in. It should go in flat. If it binds at the seams, the whipstitch was pulled tight; loosen it.

### Stage 5: stem, leaf tab, and snap

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/pumpkin-wallet-stem-tab.svg" alt="Diagram. The stem, a green strip 6 single crochet by 3 rows rolled into a small cylinder. The leaf tab, 3 stitches wide for 6 rows with a point on row 7. A side view of the tab sewn inside the back rim, rising over both rims and down the front, with a snap under the tip and the stem standing on the back rim." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The tab is the closure. The stem only decorates.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The closed orange wallet. The leaf tab folds over the front; sew the stem on the back rim, behind that tab](../../assets/articles/pumpkin-crochet-wallet-result.jpg "Illustration. The stem in the picture sits on the tab. The pattern puts it on the back rim.")

**Stem**, green: Ch 7. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and across. (6) Rows 2–3: ch 1, turn, sc in each st. (6) Fasten off with a 10 inch tail. Keep the six-stitch edges horizontal. Roll the six-stitch width around itself, bringing the short three-row ends together, and sew those ends together. The cylinder height is the three-row direction, about 0.6 inch at the example row gauge; do not roll the three-row height into the circumference.

**Leaf tab**, green: Ch 4. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and across. (3) Rows 2–6: ch 1, turn, sc in each st. (3) Row 7: ch 1, turn, sc3tog. (1) Fasten off with a 10 inch tail. The tab is about 0.75 inch wide and 1.4 inches long.

**Assembly:**

1. Sew the tab's foundation edge to the inside of the back rim at the center, over the middle 3 rim stitches.
2. Fold the tab over both rims and down the front. Mark where the tip lands, usually stitches 3 to 4 of the front, between rows 11 and 12. Mark it with a card inside.
3. Sew the ball half of the snap under the tab at row 6, and the socket on the front at the mark. Doubled thread, four or five passes per hole, through the front layer only.
4. Sew the stem standing upright on the back rim, right over the tab's base, catching the tab too. That double layer is what takes the pull every time the tab opens.

Weave in ends inside the pocket along the seams. See [weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

## Make it yours

- **Two oranges.** Change color every 2 rows on the last yarn over, as in [changing yarn color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/). Carry the unused color up the edge, under the rim.
- **No ribs.** Work both loops. Row gauge changes, so still work to 4.25 inches.
- **A friendly face.** Round eyes and a curved smile instead of triangles.
- **A white or green pumpkin.** Same counts, different yarn.
- **Lining.** Cut fabric 4.25 by 5 inches plus a 1/4 inch fold all around, sew it to the wrong side before folding, and fold the two together.

## Label the two rims before embroidery

Lay the twenty-stitch rows horizontally and the row-count direction vertically. Choose one row-end edge as the front rim; label the stitch positions from that edge as 1–20 on every row, regardless of the direction in which that row was worked. The fold is between positions 10 and 11, and both row-end edges become the opening. Pair positions 10/11, 9/12, continuing to 1/20 when seaming the foundation and last-row edges. Work each rim into exactly one row end per completed row and use that actual rim count to center the tab. If it is not twenty-two rows, recenter the face from the measured midpoint rather than copying rows 11 and 12.

## Common mistakes and fixes

**The ribs show on both sides.** That is normal in turned BLO rows. If the texture changes partway through, check that every row after row 1 was worked into the back loop only.

**The piece slants.** The first or last stitch of rows got skipped. Count 20.

**The rims ripple.** More than one sc went into some row ends. One per row end.

**The face is on the wrong half.** Stitches were counted from the wrong edge. Stitch 1 is at the front rim.

**The tab will not reach.** The tab tip should land 3 to 4 stitches below the front rim. Add a row to the tab before row 7 if your rims came out tall.

**Cards fall out.** Normal if the tab is open. The pouch is a card sleeve, not a coin purse.

## Care

Hand wash cool, dry flat, and shape the ribs with your fingers while damp. Test the black and green for color running on a white paper towel before the first wash.

## Frequently asked

**Will a bank card fit?** On paper, yes: about 3.9 by 2.4 inches inside against a 3.37 by 2.125 inch card, so test with a real card before you fold.

**Why sideways?** Sideways back-loop rows make ribs that run up and down, like a pumpkin. Worked bottom to top, the ribs would run sideways.

**Can a beginner make this?** Yes. It is single crochet, a fold, and two short seams. The embroidery is the slowest part.

**Do I need the snap?** Not strictly. Without it, the tab lies over the opening and cards stay in if the pouch stays upright.

**Can I sell the finished pouches?** Yes. Link here rather than reposting the pattern.

## More Halloween wallets

- [Black cat crochet wallet](/black-cat-crochet-wallet/): a seamless tube with ears
- [Ghost crochet wallet](/ghost-crochet-wallet/): shaped panels and a scalloped hem
- [Witch hat crochet wallet](/witch-hat-crochet-wallet/): two pockets and a buttonhole
- [Spiderweb crochet wallet](/spiderweb-crochet-wallet/): an embroidered web on a pointed flap

For round pumpkins, see [how to crochet a pumpkin](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-step-by-step/). For a zipped pumpkin pouch, see the [pumpkin zipper pouch](/how-to-crochet-a-pumpkin-zipper-pouch/).

The pumpkin depends on two habits: back loops on every row, and one single crochet per row end on the rims. Work to 4.25 inches, embroider the face before you fold, and let the stem anchor the tab.


# Spiderweb Crochet Wallet: Free Pattern With an Embroidered Web

Source: src/content/posts/spiderweb-crochet-wallet.md



A spiderweb on a crochet wallet usually means a lot of fiddly white color changes. This one skips that. You crochet a plain black wallet with a pointed flap, then draw the web on top with backstitch, which you can pull out and redo until it looks right. A tiny crocheted spider sits on one strand.

It is one of five patterns in the [Halloween crochet wallet collection](/halloween-crochet-wallets/), and the one that teaches surface embroidery and a button loop.

![A black crochet wallet with a white spiderweb, a small spider, and a side button](../../assets/articles/spiderweb-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

The pocket size, flap drop, and loop length here are worked out from the example gauge rather than measured from a finished wallet, so swatch first and slip a real bank card in before you join. A standard US bank card is 3.370 by 2.125 inches, the ISO/IEC 7810 ID-1 size.

## Materials

- **Worsted weight (CYC 4) yarn** in black. Smooth cotton makes the white embroidery look crisp; fuzzy yarn blurs it. [Yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **White** worsted or a thinner white yarn, or six-strand embroidery floss, for the web.
- **Purple** scraps for the spider.
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook** for the wallet, and a size smaller for the spider if you have one. [Hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One button**, 5/8 in (15 mm), flat with holes.
- Tapestry needle, a sharp embroidery needle if using floss, chalk or a white pencil, stitch markers, one card.

**Yardage.** There is no weighed sample to quote. The wallet covers about 26 square inches, so weigh a 4 inch square swatch and allow 1.6 times that weight, then a fifth more for seams and the spider.

## Abbreviations and stitches

All terms are American; the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) lists the UK equivalents.

| Abbreviation | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| ch | chain |
| sc | single crochet |
| sl st | slip stitch |
| sc2tog | decrease: draw up a loop in each of the next 2 stitches, then yarn over and pull through every loop on the hook |
| sc3tog | as sc2tog, over 3 stitches, removing 2 |
| inc | 2 sc in the same stitch |
| MR | magic ring; see [crochet in the round](/crochet-in-the-round/) |
| rnd | round |
| st(s) | stitch(es) |

**Turning.** Start each row with ch 1 and turn; that chain is never counted, and the first sc goes into the very first stitch.

**Backstitch.** Bring the needle up one stitch length ahead, go down at the end of the previous stitch, and repeat. It makes a solid line that can follow any direction.

## Size and gauge

**Gauge:** 16 sc and 20 rows to 4 inches. Measure the middle of a 5 inch swatch before starting; the [gauge guide](/crochet-gauge/) explains why the edges lie.

Seventeen stitches are 4.25 inches; after the side seams, the pocket is about 3.9 inches wide inside against a 3.37 inch card. The front and back are 13 rows, 2.6 inches, so the pocket is about 2.5 inches deep, enough for a 2.125 inch card with room to grip it. The flap is 8 rows, about 1.6 inches to the point, and the loop hangs about 0.4 inch past it.

## The pattern

### Stage 1: the rectangle and pointed flap

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/spiderweb-wallet-zones.svg" alt="Diagram. A black rectangle 17 stitches wide. Rows 1 to 13 are the front, rows 14 to 26 the back, and rows 27 to 34 a pointed flap that decreases at both ends of each row, 15, 13, 11, 9, 7, 5, 3, then a three-together decrease. A chain-8 loop sits at the point." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The flap loses one stitch at each end of every row until it comes to a point.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The black pointed flap before any embroidery](../../assets/articles/spiderweb-crochet-wallet-flap.jpg "Illustration.")

With black:

**Row 1:** Ch 18. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and in each ch across. (17)

**Rows 2–26:** Ch 1, turn. Sc in each st across. (17)

Mark the end of row 13 and row 26; those are the bottom and top folds.

**Row 27:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 13 sts, sc2tog. (15)

**Row 28:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 11 sts, sc2tog. (13)

**Row 29:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 9 sts, sc2tog. (11)

**Row 30:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 7 sts, sc2tog. (9)

**Row 31:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 5 sts, sc2tog. (7)

**Row 32:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 3 sts, sc2tog. (5)

**Row 33:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next st, sc2tog. (3)

**Row 34:** Ch 1, turn. Sc3tog. (1)

**Loop:** Ch 8, sl st in the same st. Hold the button against the loop. It should pass with a little resistance. Adjust between 7 and 10 chains. Fasten off and weave in.

<div class="tip">

The flap edges will be a little bumpy where each row decreases. A round of slip stitch along both slanted edges, worked loosely, smooths them. Do it before the embroidery.

</div>

### Stage 2: the web

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/spiderweb-wallet-web.svg" alt="Diagram. The closed flap with five white spokes running from the point at row 34 to row 27 at stitches 2, 5, 8, 11, and 14, and three rings at rows 29, 31, and 33, each made of four segments that dip toward the point. A purple spider sits between the rings at rows 29 and 31 on the fourth spoke." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Spokes first, inner ring first, spider last.</figcaption>
</figure>

Fold the front up and the flap down, so you see the flap the way it will sit. Mark the spoke ends on row 27 with chalk or pins.

1. **Spokes.** With white, backstitch 5 straight lines, each starting at the point (row 34) and ending at row 27: one at stitch 2, then stitches 5, 8, 11, and 14. The middle spoke runs straight up stitch 8.
2. **Rings, inner first.** At row 33, take one stitch from each spoke to the next, so the ring has 4 segments. Pull each one only until it dips slightly toward the point. Then the ring at row 31, then row 29.
3. Secure the white on the back with running stitches under the existing strands, not with a knot. The back of the flap rubs the front of the wallet.

Work through the flap only. If a line comes out crooked, unpick it; backstitch comes out cleanly.

### Stage 3: the spider

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/spider-applique.svg" alt="Diagram. Left, the abdomen: magic ring of 6, round 2 increases to 12, round 3 even, round 4 decreases to 6. The head: magic ring of 6 and one even round. Right, the head sewn to the abdomen with eight two-segment legs, four per side, bent at the knee." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Two tiny balls and eight stitched legs.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The purple spider on the right side of the web, with the loop and button at the point](../../assets/articles/spiderweb-crochet-wallet-spider.jpg "Illustration. The pattern uses five spokes.")

With purple, on the smaller hook if you have one:

**Abdomen. Rnd 1:** MR, 6 sc in the ring. (6)

**Rnd 2:** Inc in each st. (12)

**Rnd 3:** Sc in each st. (12)

**Rnd 4:** Sc2tog 6 times. (6) Stuff with purple yarn scraps. Fasten off, thread the tail through the 6 front loops, and pull closed. Keep the tail.

**Head. Rnd 1:** MR, 6 sc in the ring. (6)

**Rnd 2:** Sc in each st. (6) Fasten off and close the same way; it cups into a small bead.

**Assembly:** Sew the head to one end of the abdomen. Sew the pair to the flap on the fourth spoke from the left, the one ending at stitch 11, between the rings at rows 29 and 31. Then make 8 legs in purple, 4 per side, each two straight stitches: one out from the body, one shorter one bent toward the point. The spider is about 0.5 inch across before the legs.

### Stage 4: seam and button

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/spiderweb-wallet-closure.svg" alt="Diagram. The loop at the point of the flap, chain 8 slip stitched to the same stitch. The closed wallet to scale, about 4.25 by 2.6 inches, with the flap about 1.6 inches below the fold, the loop 0.4 inch more, and the button near row 10 of the front at stitch 9. A dashed ID-1 card fits inside on paper." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Close the flap with cards inside before marking the button.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. **Fold.** Fold rows 1 to 13 up against rows 14 to 26, right sides out. The chain edge is the front rim.
2. **Side seams.** Join black at the bottom fold. Ch 1. Work 13 sc up the side, one in each row end, through both layers. Fasten off. Repeat on the other side.
3. **Button.** Put two cards in and close the flap. Mark where the loop lands, at the marked position, centered across the front; row 1 is at the opening and row 13 is nearest the bottom fold. Sew the button through the front layer only.
4. **Ends.** Weave in along the seams. See [weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

## Make it yours

- **Purple wallet, black web.** Reverse the colors. Black backstitch on purple shows less, so use two strands.
- **More spokes.** Seven spokes at stitches 1, 3, 5, 8, 11, 13, 15 look closer to a real web but crowd near the point. Stop the outer ones at row 31.
- **No spider.** Embroider a small one instead: a French knot body and eight straight-stitch legs.
- **Snap closure.** Skip the loop. Sew a 3/8 inch snap under the flap point and on the front.
- **Lining.** Line the back only, so the pocket keeps its width. Cut fabric 4.25 by 2.6 inches plus a 1/4 inch fold.

## Pair the folded pocket rows before marking the button

Fold between rows 13 and 14. Pair front row 13 with back row 14, row 12 with 15, continuing to front row 1 with back row 26. Row 1 is now the top front rim, not the bottom. Sew only those thirteen row-end pairs on each side. The flap starts at row 27 and ends at row 34, so it remains free. With cards inside, lower the flap and mark the loop's actual landing place; ignore any estimated front-row number if your fit differs. Embroider only after fixing the flap's closed orientation, and keep the needle out of the pocket.

## Common mistakes and fixes

**The flap is off center.** One decrease row missed a decrease at one end. Count each row: 15, 13, 11, 9, 7, 5, 3, 1.

**The spokes pull the flap into a cup.** The backstitch was too tight. Keep each stitch the length of one crochet stitch and do not tug.

**The rings look like straight lines.** They need a little slack. Leave each segment slightly long, then nudge it toward the point with the needle.

**The white strand shows gray.** Black fiber traveled with the needle. Use a sharp needle and go into the center of each stitch.

**The loop is too loose.** Crochet loops stretch. Redo with one chain fewer.

**The spider wobbles.** It is sewn only at one point. Tack it at both the head and the back of the abdomen.

## Care

Wash by hand in cool water and lay it flat to dry, spider up. Before the first wash, press a damp scrap of the black yarn on a white paper towel; if color transfers, the web will gray.

## Frequently asked

**Will cards fit?** On paper, the pocket is about 3.9 by 2.5 inches inside against a 3.37 by 2.125 inch card, so test the fit with a real card before you join.

**Can I crochet the web instead of embroidering it?** Possible, with surface slip stitch, but it is harder to control on a small triangle. Backstitch can be undone.

**Is the spider safe for a child?** It is sewn on, but small parts on any handmade item can come loose. For a young child, embroider the spider instead.

**Why a loop, not a buttonhole?** A buttonhole cannot go into the last few stitches of a point. The loop does the same job at the tip.

**May I sell wallets made from this?** Go ahead. Share a link to this page instead of copying the instructions.

## More Halloween wallets

- [Black cat crochet wallet](/black-cat-crochet-wallet/): a seamless tube with ears
- [Ghost crochet wallet](/ghost-crochet-wallet/): shaped panels and a scalloped hem
- [Pumpkin crochet wallet](/pumpkin-crochet-wallet/): back-loop ribs and a leaf tab
- [Witch hat crochet wallet](/witch-hat-crochet-wallet/): two pockets and a worked buttonhole

For a standalone spider and web, see [how to crochet a spider and web](/how-to-crochet-a-spider-and-web/). Decreases are explained in [increases and decreases](/crochet-increase-decrease/).

The spiderweb wallet is straight crochet followed by drawing. Count every flap row down to 1, put the spokes in first, and keep the backstitch loose enough that the flap stays flat.


# Finishing Crochet: Weaving In Ends and Blocking

Source: src/content/posts/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking.md



Finishing is most of the difference between a project that looks handmade and one that looks homemade. Three jobs: secure the ends, block if the fiber will respond, and join the pieces.


![A blocked crochet square pinned flat on a foam mat](../../assets/articles/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking-result.jpg "Illustration. Weave ends, then block — that order keeps the finish clean.")
## Fastening off

Cut the yarn, leaving at least a six-inch tail. Leave eight to ten inches if you will seam with it. Yarn over, pull the cut end all the way through the last loop, and tug gently. That secures the stitch. The tail is the next job.

## Weaving in ends

![Tapestry needle weaving a yarn end into crochet fabric](../../assets/articles/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking-1.jpg "Illustration. Weave through several stitches on the wrong side, then reverse direction once.")

A tail threaded straight through the fabric works loose with wear and washing. You want friction and a change of direction.

Use a blunt tapestry needle so it slides between the plies instead of splitting them.

1. On the wrong side, run the needle through the backs of four or five stitches, following the stitch bumps.
2. Change direction. Weave back through several more stitches, on a different path.
3. Split a ply once on the way back if you want it permanent.
4. Trim close, then stretch the piece gently in both directions so the cut end pulls inside.

The direction change is what holds. A straight pass can slide back out. A pass that reverses on itself cannot.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/weave-reverse.svg" alt="Diagram comparing a tail woven straight through a row of stitches with a tail that runs out through several stitches and turns back on a different path." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. The turn is what holds the end, not the distance.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Lace and open fabric.** There is no solid wrong side to hide a tail in. Weave along a line of solid stitches, such as the posts of a cluster or a row of single crochet, rather than across the holes, where it shows from both sides.

**Slippery yarn.** Cotton, bamboo, and silk blends slide more than wool. Split a ply on every pass instead of once, and leave the tail a little longer before you trim.

**Reversible pieces.** A blanket or scarf seen from both sides has no wrong side either. Weave through the middle of the stitch posts, where the tail is covered on both faces.

<div class="tip">

Run dark tails through dark stitches and light tails through light. A navy end through a cream stripe shows a shadow, and it gets worse after the first wash.

</div>

**Amigurumi.** Weave across the inside, catching stitches, then bring the needle out and back in through the same spot before you trim. Toys are handled hard, and once the piece is stuffed there is no wrong side to check.

On a striped piece, weave as you go. Leaving every end until the last row is where projects stall.

## Blocking

![A cream granny square pinned flat on a foam blocking mat](../../assets/articles/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking-2.jpg "Illustration. Pin to shape while damp — corners first, then mid-sides.")

Blocking means wetting or steaming a finished piece, shaping it, and letting it dry that way. Stitches relax, tension evens out, lace opens, and edges lie flat. Whether it works depends on the fiber.

| Fiber | Does blocking help? | Method |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Wool and wool blends | Dramatically | Wet block |
| Cotton and linen | Yes, noticeably | Wet block |
| Acrylic | Barely, unless steamed | Steam, with care |
| Superwash wool | Yes, but it can grow | Wet block, do not stretch far |
| Novelty and fuzzy yarns | No | Skip it |

### Wet blocking

For wool and cotton:

1. Soak in cool water with a little wool wash for fifteen to twenty minutes, until the piece is fully wet.
2. Lift it supporting the whole weight. A wet piece that hangs will stretch and stay stretched.
3. Press the water out between towels. Do not wring or twist.
4. Lay it on a flat padded surface and pin it to the measurements you want, with rustproof pins.
5. Let it dry completely before you unpin. A day, sometimes two. Unpinning early undoes the work.

This is what turns a curling granny square into a flat one, and it changes lace.

### Steam blocking

Acrylic does not respond to water. Heat changes it permanently. Hold a steam iron above the piece, in short bursts, and keep it moving. Do not let the iron touch the fabric. Acrylic melts, flattens, and goes shiny. Some people do this on purpose for drape. It is often called killing acrylic, and you cannot undo it.

Bobbles, popcorns, and puffs lose their dimension if pressed. Steam them from farther away, or skip them.

Skip blocking for acrylic blankets, dishcloths, amigurumi, bags, and anything that goes through the washing machine often. Toys should stay firm.

## Joining pieces

**Whipstitch.** Wrong sides together, sew over the edge through the matching stitches. It lies flat and shows as small slanted stitches on the right side. Working through the back loops only makes it less visible.

**Mattress stitch.** Right sides facing you, pieces side by side, sew under the matching stitch on one piece, then the other, and pull snug every inch or so. The seam closes from behind and is the least visible option. Use it where a seam should disappear, such as garment side seams.

**Slip stitch.** Right sides together, slip stitch through one loop of each piece. Faster, with a small firm ridge. Good for blankets made of squares, and for bags.

**Single crochet the edges together** when you want a raised seam on purpose, as on some granny-square blankets.

Block before you seam. Flat pieces line up. Curling ones do not.

1. Weave in ends on each piece as you finish it.
2. Block the pieces.
3. Seam them.
4. Add the border or edging.
5. Weave in the last ends.

## Make a seam sample before joining the project

Use two ten-stitch rectangles in the actual yarn. Clip their corresponding edges together and try the chosen seam on just a few positions. Pull them open gently to see which face shows the ridge. For an inside seam on a bag, hold public faces together, sew or crochet the paired edges, then turn it out. For a visible raised join, hold backs together. Weave a tail through several stitches, change direction on a separate path, and inspect both faces before trimming. Blocking can even tension but cannot replace missing corner stitches or correct a mathematically wrong stitch count.

## Common mistakes

**A straight weave, or a tail under six inches.** Without a turn, the end slides out. A knot holds, then comes to the surface as a hard bump.

**A tail through the wrong color.** Navy through a cream stripe shows a shadow after washing.

**The iron touching acrylic.** Wool and cotton can be blocked again after every wash. Acrylic that has been flattened by heat stays that way.

**Seaming before blocking.** Curled edges will not line up, and the seam locks the curl in. A granny square with missing corner stitches needs those stitches corrected first. Blocking cannot supply them.

## Frequently asked

**How long do woven ends need to be?** Six inches minimum. Longer, eight to ten, if that tail will seam a piece.

**Can I use a knot instead?** It holds, then works its way to the surface as a hard bump. Woven ends are barely slower.

**Do I have to block?** No. Skip it for acrylic blankets and toys. For wool garments, lace, and granny squares, it is the largest improvement you can make in the finishing.


# What You Need to Start Crocheting: One Hook, One Yarn

Source: src/content/posts/what-you-need-to-start-crocheting.md



Start with a **5 mm aluminum hook, US size H/8**, and a **medium worsted acrylic in a light solid color**. Both are among the cheapest things in a craft store, and the light color is what lets you see your stitches.

![A single aluminum crochet hook beside one ball of ivory worsted-weight yarn](../../assets/articles/what-you-need-to-start-crocheting-1.jpg "Illustration. This is genuinely the whole shopping list.")

<div class="tip">

**Disclosure:** this guide contains affiliate links where products are named, which means a purchase may earn this site a small commission at no extra cost to you. No company sent anything for review and no commission influences what is recommended here. See the [disclosures page](/disclosures/).

</div>

![Beginner crochet kit flat lay with hook yarn needle and markers](../../assets/articles/what-you-need-to-start-crocheting-result.jpg "Illustration. This is genuinely the short shopping list.")

## The hook

A 5 mm hook is large enough to see and matches the worsted yarn most beginner patterns assume. A 4 mm feels fiddly. A 6.5 mm exaggerates every tension wobble. Buy one hook until you know which sizes you use.

### Head shape

![Two crochet hook heads side by side, one with a pointed tapered tip and one with a flat inline head](../../assets/articles/what-you-need-to-start-crocheting-2.jpg "Illustration. Tapered on the left, inline on the right. Most people develop a firm preference within a week.")

**Tapered** heads (Boye) have a pointed tip and a throat that narrows gradually. They enter tight stitches easily, and the loop can slip, so size varies a little. **Inline** heads (Susan Bates) sit flush with the shaft, so stitches stay more even. The blunter tip is harder to insert. Buy one of each in 5 mm and keep the shape you prefer.

**Aluminum**, from 2.25 mm through about 10 mm, is the hook to learn on. **Steel** hooks are the small sizes used for thread, and their numbers run backward: a higher number is a smaller hook. The [hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/) lists them. **Plastic and resin** are mostly above about 10 mm. Cheap small plastic hooks can snag. **Wood and bamboo** grip silk, feel slower with acrylic, and are best from about 4 mm up, because smaller wood hooks snap. **Ergonomic** handles cost several times more and help on long sessions. Clover Amour and Tulip Etimo are the mid-priced ranges. Furls is the premium end.

## The yarn

Learn on **medium worsted, category 4**, in a **light solid color** such as cream, light gray, pale blue, or soft yellow, so the loops show. The [yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/) lists the categories. **Smooth plied acrylic** is springy, machine washable, and cheap to rip out.

The label's hook size is a starting point. The pattern's gauge wins when the finished size matters. If your swatch has more stitches than the pattern in 4 inches, try a larger hook. If it has fewer, try a smaller hook. Millimeters matter more than the US letter, because letters are not identical across brands. The [hook chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/) and the [gauge lesson](/crochet-gauge/) are the two pages for that check. You do not need a full set of hooks to start.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/hook-gauge-choice.svg" alt="Diagram. More stitches than the pattern in four inches means try a larger hook. Fewer stitches means try a smaller hook. Measure again after the change." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Change one hook size, then measure the middle of a new swatch. The first swatch does not become the right size by stretching it.</figcaption>
</figure>

| You are making | What to check first |
| --- | --- |
| A practice rectangle | Smooth light yarn, and a hook you can see the loops on |
| A dishcloth | Cotton, and the care label |
| A fitted hat | The pattern gauge, then your own swatch |
| A stuffed toy | A dense fabric with no gaps for stuffing |
| A scarf where length can vary | Drape, not an exact width |

Common US worsted acrylics: **Red Heart Super Saver** (stiff until washed), **Lion Brand Basic Stitch** (smoother), **Bernat Super Value**, and **Loops & Threads Impeccable** (Michaels). Dishcloths need worsted cotton, **Lily Sugar'n Cream** or **Peaches & Creme**. Acrylic can melt on a hot pan. Cotton is less forgiving, so make it the second project.

### Yarns that make learning harder

![Four balls of yarn in a row: smooth pale solid, very dark, variegated, and fuzzy](../../assets/articles/what-you-need-to-start-crocheting-3.jpg "Illustration. Only the first one shows you where to put the hook.")

None of these are bad yarns. They are poor teachers. **Black and very dark colors** hide the loops. Save them for your fifth project. **Variegated and self-striping** yarn makes a color change look like a mistake. **Chenille and velvet** split, hide stitches, and can worm into loops that will not lie flat. Wait until your tension is steady. **Fuzzy yarn** (mohair, boucle, eyelash, blanket yarn) covers the loops, and the fibers cling when you undo them. **Sock or fingering weight** is tiny and slow. A scarf can become a three-month project.

## Cotton, acrylic, and wool

**Acrylic** is machine washable, pills, and does not breathe well. It suits blankets, children's items, and amigurumi. **Cotton** stays stretched: right for dishcloths, market bags, coasters, and summer tops. A beanie becomes a bucket. **Wool** blocks into shape for garments and hats. Most is hand wash. Superwash is machine washable and less springy. Cotton in place of acrylic in a garment usually disappoints.

## How much yarn to buy

Buy **yardage, not a skein count.** One brand's large skein can hold more than double another's. Divide the pattern's yards by the yards on the label, add ten percent, and buy it all at once so the dye lots match.

When there is no pattern yardage, weigh instead of guessing. This page used to print a table of typical amounts. None of those numbers was weighed from a finished piece, so it is gone.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/yarn-estimate-weigh.svg" alt="Three steps. A yarn label reading 200 yards per 100 grams gives 2 yards per gram. One finished piece weighs 14 grams on a kitchen scale. 14 times 2 is 28 yards, plus 10 to 20 percent. The numbers are examples." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Yards per gram from the label, grams from the scale. The example numbers are invented.</figcaption>
</figure>

For a big piece like a blanket, make one square or a measured strip, weigh it, and scale up by area. A strip that is a tenth of the blanket's area uses about a tenth of the yarn. Bobbles, puffs, and popcorns use more yarn than plain stitches, which is one more reason to weigh a sample in the real stitch.

## If your hands hurt

Loosen the yarn hand. Tight tension is what makes you grip. Work about twenty minutes, then stretch. Pencil grip is like a pen. Knife grip is like cutting food. If one hurts, use the other for a full session. A thicker handle, a larger hook, or thicker yarn eases the pinch.

<div class="warning">

**Persistent numbness, tingling, or pain that wakes you at night is not a hook problem.** Take those symptoms to a doctor. Repetitive hand work can contribute to or aggravate symptoms; a handle change is not a diagnosis or treatment.

</div>

## The rest of the kit

Buy a **hook gauge** early, a metal plate with holes. It gives the millimeter size when a hook is unlabeled or the print has worn off. Patterns mean millimeters. Letters are not standard between makers.

![A hook gauge plate, a blunt tapestry needle, split-ring stitch markers and a tape measure](../../assets/articles/what-you-need-to-start-crocheting-4.jpg "Illustration. The hook gauge is the one accessory worth buying early.")

Add a blunt tapestry needle, split-ring markers, and a tape measure. Keep the ball band pinned to a swatch, in a labeled bag with the leftovers, so a repair still has a fiber and a wash method.

## Use the kit on one counting exercise

With a smooth light yarn and a comfortable hook, chain sixteen, sc in the second chain and across (15). Ch 1, turn and work fifteen sc again; make four rows and count after each. Mark the first and last stitch if they disappear into the edge. The goal is consistent stitches you can identify, not a particular speed. Keep the yarn label and note the hook's millimeter size. A larger hook or a different grip is a comfort experiment, not a reason to continue through pain. Stop and seek medical advice if symptoms persist.

## Mistakes

A 20-piece set before you know tapered from inline. Three sizes cover the first six months, and the wrong head is twenty unused hooks. Lighted and interchangeable hooks are novelties. The light points the wrong way. A plain aluminum hook is enough unless you crochet long hours or your hands hurt. Buy yardage in one dye lot.

## Frequently asked

**Does the hook brand change gauge?** A little. Head shape changes the loop, so finish on the hook you started with.

**Are there left-handed hooks?** Ordinary hooks work in either hand. Some ergonomic thumb rests do not, so check the shape or use a plain hook. More is in the [left-handed guide](/left-handed-crochet/).

**What hook and yarn suit amigurumi?** Worsted acrylic or cotton, with a hook smaller than the label suggests, around 3.5 mm or 4 mm, so the stuffing does not show through.


# Where to Find Free Crochet Patterns

Source: src/content/posts/where-to-find-free-crochet-patterns.md



If you are searching for where to find free crochet patterns, the useful answer is a short list of databases, not another board of unsourced photos. A free crochet pattern database either indexes other people's patterns or publishes its own. Those are different jobs, and mixing them up is how a download turns into a dead page.

**Every external link on this page goes to the publisher.** This site does not copy their instructions. The [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) is here when a pattern uses a term you do not know yet.

![Laptop and tablet beside yarn and a crochet hook on a desk](../../assets/articles/where-to-find-free-crochet-patterns-result.jpg "Illustration. Start with trusted libraries — then check the pattern still opens.")

## The best free crochet pattern databases

![Laptop open beside a printed crochet pattern yarn and hook](../../assets/articles/where-to-find-free-crochet-patterns-2.jpg "Illustration. Start with known libraries, then verify the pattern page still opens.")

Use the table to pick a starting point. "Volume" is a description of the library, not a ranking score. Counts move. Where a site publishes a number, that number is theirs, and it includes whatever crafts they count.

| Site | What it is | Free pattern volume | Filters | Sign-up needed | Best for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ravelry | Aggregator database | Indexes designers, publishers, and yarn brands | Craft, free, weight, hook, category | A free account for the full search | Checking that other people have actually made it |
| Lion Brand | Yarn brand | Large free crochet catalog on their pattern pages | Project type and yarn | Account not required to read the pattern | Beginner accessories in their yarns |
| Yarnspirations | Yarn brand | Shared catalog for Red Heart, Bernat, Caron, and Patons | Craft, including crochet | Not required for the pattern download | Blankets and labeled skill levels |
| DROPS Design | Yarn brand | Large combined knit and crochet catalog; their browse page shows the current total | Category and yarn | Not required to open a pattern | Garments, if you can read European terms |
| LoveCrafts | Shop that hosts designers | Mixed free and paid | Free filter | Account for some downloads | Finding an independent designer in one catalog |
| Moogly | Designer site | One designer's long free catalog | By project on the site | No | Photo tutorials with a consistent format |

**[Ravelry](https://www.ravelry.com/patterns/search#craft=crochet&availability=free)** is an aggregator. It does not design the patterns it lists. It stores records that point at the designer's page or a yarn company's page, plus projects other crocheters have logged. Filter by crochet and by free. A free account is what unlocks that search. Before you buy yarn, open the project pages. If several people report the same wrong count, believe the notes.

**[Lion Brand](https://www.lionbrand.com/collections/crochet-patterns)** publishes its own free crochet patterns. Link the crochet pattern collection, not the store homepage. The patterns are written to sell Lion Brand yarn, which is a fine reason for a yarn company to give the pattern away. You can still substitute another yarn of the same weight if you swatch. How to do that is in [crochet gauge](/crochet-gauge/).

**[Yarnspirations](https://www.yarnspirations.com/collections/patterns?filter.p.m.global.skill_type=Crochet)** is the pattern library for Red Heart, Bernat, Caron, and Patons. One crochet filter covers those brands. The old habit of starting at a brand homepage drops you in a shop. Start on the pattern collection.

**[DROPS Design](https://www.garnstudio.com/search.php?action=browse&sort=default)** publishes free knitting and crochet patterns on Garnstudio. The browse page states a combined total, and that total changes, so read the number there instead of trusting a blog that copied it. The patterns are translated and use European hook sizes and, often, UK or international stitch names. Check the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) and the [hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/) before you start a DROPS garment. A US single crochet is not the stitch a UK pattern means by "double crochet."

**[LoveCrafts](https://www.lovecrafts.com/en-us/l/crochet/crochet-patterns)** is a shop with a large mixed library. Use the free filter. The designer is named on the pattern. That name is who you go back to when a round does not add up.

**[Moogly](https://www.mooglyblog.com/free-crochet-patterns/)** is a designer site: one person, a long catalog, photo steps, the same format from one pattern to the next. A designer site is not a database of everyone. It is a good place once you know you like that writer's explanations.

Individual designers also post free patterns on their own domains, with a paid PDF beside them. Quality is uneven. If the explanations suit you, stay with that designer rather than hopping to a random pin.

"Free" on these sites means you do not pay for the PDF. It does not mean the page is free of ads, email offers, or a shop beside the download. That is normal for a yarn brand. Read the pattern. Skip the cart until you know the yardage.

Filters are the point of a database. On Ravelry, set craft to crochet and availability to free before you set a category, or you will be looking at knitting. On Yarnspirations, the crochet skill filter is what keeps knit patterns out of the list. On DROPS, the browse page mixes crafts until you narrow it. A search box alone is not a database. The filters are.

When a filter offers yarn weight, use it. A "free blanket" search that ignores weight is how people buy super-bulky yarn for a worsted pattern. The names of those weights are on the [yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/). The hook that matches is on the [hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/). The pattern's own hook in millimeters wins over either chart when they disagree.

Free patterns are enough to crochet for years. Paying is worth it when a garment is graded in many sizes and you want that editing, and when you want to pay the person whose work you use.

## What a pattern page has to include

![Notebook project list beside yarn and a hook](../../assets/articles/where-to-find-free-crochet-patterns-1.jpg "Illustration. Clear materials, gauge, and photos beat a pretty cover shot alone.")

A pattern missing several of these will waste the yarn.

- **A gauge statement.** On a garment, no gauge means the size was not planned. A dishcloth can skip it. A sweater cannot. The method is in [crochet gauge](/crochet-gauge/).
- **Total yardage**, not only "3 skeins." Skein lengths differ by brand. The [yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/) is the language those labels use.
- **Stitch counts** at the end of rows or rounds, written so you can check before row forty.
- **US or UK terminology**, stated. If it is not, look for clues. UK terms have no single crochet, so a pattern that uses `sc` is in US terms. A pattern whose basic stitches are `htr` and `tr` is in UK terms.
- **A special-stitches note** when the pattern says bobble, cluster, or shell. Those words are not standard across designers.
- **Finished measurements.**
- **More than one photo**, from more than one angle, of an object that matches the written fabric.

<div class="tip">

Search the pattern title on Ravelry and read project notes before you commit a weekend. Repeated complaints about the same round are a correction, not a mood.

</div>

## How to spot a fake or AI-scam pattern

Pinterest and Facebook are full of crochet pictures that were never made. The link goes nowhere, or the "pattern" is a paragraph that does not produce the object in the photo.

**Look at the stitches.** Real crochet repeats. In a generated picture, stitches melt, change direction for no reason, or turn into the table.

**Look at the hands, if any are shown.** Generated hands are where these pictures fail. A real pattern does not need a hand in the frame to be credible. It needs a gauge, a yarn weight, a hook, and a yardage.

**Check the byline.** A real pattern names a person or a yarn company. A scam names a vibe. If the only credit is the account that posted the pin, leave.

**Open the page, do not stop on the pin.** A pattern page has materials, a hook size in millimeters, and counts. A page that is only an email box is collecting addresses.

**Check the aggregator.** A pattern that has been on the internet will usually have Ravelry projects. Zero projects does not prove a fake. A brand-new designer can have zero. Zero projects plus a picture with impossible stitches is enough to skip it.

**Do not pay a stranger for a "secret" PDF** that the pin describes as free. The databases above are the free ones.
