This site's own project pictures are labeled as illustrations. That label is the opposite of a scam: the words say the picture is not a finished object. A scam hides that.

## Patterns on this site

Crochet Explained publishes its own tutorials and some written patterns. Those pages are not an aggregator. They do not list every free pattern on the internet, and they do not copy a designer's text. If a page here is a technique, it explains the stitch. If it is a project, the instructions are written for this site and the photos are labeled illustrations. Start at [free patterns](/free-patterns/) if you want what is hosted here, and use the databases above when you want a designer's catalog.

A pattern that has not been crocheted and measured will say so. Treat the inch sizes on those pages as gauge estimates. The stitch counts are still there for you to check as you work.

## How to read the pattern once you have it

Pick one terminology and stay there. The [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/) is the lookup. [How to read a crochet pattern](/how-to-read-a-crochet-pattern/) walks a line from hook size to the first repeat.

Write the stitch count at the end of each round in the margin. When your count and the pattern's count disagree, stop. Ripping three rounds is cheaper than ripping thirty.

Save the PDF before the tab closes. Yarn-company pages redesign, and a pattern you meant to make in November is annoying to hunt for in December. Put the designer name in the file name. "blanket-final.pdf" is useless six months later. Ravelry project pages are the other copy: if you log the project, you still have the link when the pin disappears.

For a first project, choose something flat, one color, one stitch, in worsted-weight yarn. A dishcloth or a simple scarf teaches you to follow a row. Shaping and color can wait. The [basic stitches](/basic-crochet-stitches/) page is the order to learn them.

## Check one pattern before buying its yarn

Open the original publisher's full instructions, not just a social image. Identify the terminology, finished size, stitch key, gauge, yarn amount, assembly instructions, and any correction notes. Then read an increase or decrease row aloud and check that its repeat consumes the previous count. Project notes from other makers can reveal missing steps but do not substitute for instructions. A free download may require an account, and those access rules can change. Follow the publisher's current page. Do not treat a professional byline, many images, or a lack of Ravelry projects as proof of whether a pattern works.

## Frequently asked

### Can I sell what I make from a free pattern?

Often yes, and the answer is on that pattern page, not in a general rule. Yarn-company patterns usually allow finished objects. Selling the pattern text, or posting it as your own, is not allowed.

### What is a good first project from these databases?

Filter for beginner, crochet, and a flat project. Avoid a fitted garment until you have made a [gauge](/crochet-gauge/) swatch on purpose.

### A pattern seems mathematically impossible. Is it me?

Not always. Search the designer's name plus "errata," and read the Ravelry project notes. A wrong count is often already documented.

### Do I need a Ravelry account?

You can browse some pages without one. The filters that make it useful as a free-pattern database ask for a free account. Lion Brand, Yarnspirations, and DROPS Design do not require an account to open the pattern.

### Are AI pictures always a scam?

No. A labeled illustration next to a written pattern is a picture. An unlabeled generated object with no pattern, no designer, and a link that asks for your email is the scam.


# Witch Hat Crochet Wallet: Free Two-Pocket Card Wallet Pattern

Source: src/content/posts/witch-hat-crochet-wallet.md



A wallet with one pocket fills up fast, and the card you want is always at the back. This witch hat wallet has an inner divider, so there are two pockets the same size: cards in one, folded cash or a transit pass in the other. The flap closes with a buttonhole you crochet into a row, and the hat sits right above it.

It is one of five patterns in the [Halloween crochet wallet collection](/halloween-crochet-wallets/). The new skills here are a worked buttonhole and seaming through three layers.

![A black crochet witch-hat pouch with a purple band, an orange buckle, and a zipper](../../assets/articles/witch-hat-crochet-wallet-thumb.png)

The pocket sizes and the 5/8 inch button fit here are worked out from the example gauge rather than measured from a finished wallet, so swatch first and test with a real bank card and your button before you sew up. A standard US bank card is 3.370 by 2.125 inches, the ISO/IEC 7810 ID-1 size.

## Materials

- **Worsted weight (CYC 4) yarn** in purple. A cotton or cotton blend keeps the buttonhole from stretching. [Yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).
- **Scraps** of black and orange for the hat.
- **US G/6 (4.0 mm) hook**, or the size that gives the gauge. [Hook size chart](/crochet-hook-size-conversion-chart/).
- **One button**, 5/8 in (15 mm), flat, with holes rather than a tall shank. A shank button pushes into the cards.
- Purple thread or yarn for the button, a tapestry needle, stitch markers, one card.

**Estimating yarn.** No sample has been weighed. The rectangle and divider together are about 39 square inches, which is 2.4 four-inch squares. Weigh your swatch, scale it up, and keep a fifth extra for seams.

## Abbreviations and stitches

US terms. See the [abbreviations chart](/crochet-abbreviations-chart/).

| Abbreviation | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| ch | chain |
| sc | single crochet |
| sk | skip |
| sp | space |
| sc2tog | single crochet two together: pull up a loop in each of 2 stitches, yarn over, pull through all 3 loops |
| sc3tog | single crochet three together: the same with 3 stitches |
| st(s) | stitch(es) |

**Turning.** Each row opens with ch 1 and a turn. Do not count that chain.

**Color change.** Work the last stitch of the old color until two loops remain on the hook, then pull the new color through. [Changing yarn color](/changing-yarn-color-crochet/) shows it step by step.

## Size and gauge

**Gauge:** 16 sc and 20 rows = 4 in. Measure a swatch first, as in the [gauge guide](/crochet-gauge/).

Seventeen stitches make 4.25 inches; after the side seams, each pocket is about 3.9 inches wide against a 3.37 inch card. The front and back are 12 rows each, 2.4 inches, so each pocket is about 2.3 inches deep inside, enough for a 2.125 inch card. The flap is 9 rows, about 1.8 inches. The buttonhole is 3 stitches, about 0.75 inch, which is the usual starting point for a 5/8 inch button, since crochet holes stretch.

## The pattern

### Stage 1: the main rectangle

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/witch-wallet-zones.svg" alt="Diagram. A rectangle 17 stitches wide, one grid square per stitch. Rows 1 to 12 are the front, rows 13 to 24 the back, rows 25 to 33 the flap. Row 31 has a buttonhole of 3 chains over 3 skipped stitches. Row 33 decreases at both ends to 15. Dashed lines mark the folds at rows 12 and 24." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Three zones in one piece. The chain edge becomes the front rim.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The purple rectangle before it is folded, with the buttonhole worked into the row](../../assets/articles/witch-hat-crochet-wallet-rectangle.jpg "Illustration. In the pattern the hole is centered.")

With purple:

**Row 1:** Ch 18. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and in each ch across. (17)

**Rows 2–30:** Ch 1, turn. Sc in each st across. (17)

Place a marker at the end of row 12 and row 24; those are the two folds.

**Row 31 (buttonhole):** Ch 1, turn. Sc in the next 7 sts, ch 3, sk 3 sts, sc in the last 7 sts. (14 sc and a ch-3 space)

**Row 32:** Ch 1, turn. Sc in the next 7 sts, 3 sc in the ch-3 sp, sc in the last 7 sts. (17)

**Test the button now.** Push it through the hole. It should pass with a little resistance. If it falls through, redo row 31 as sc 7, ch 2, sk 2, sc 8, and row 32 as sc 7, 2 sc in the space, sc 8. If it will not pass, redo row 31 as sc 6, ch 4, sk 4, sc 7, and row 32 as sc 7, 4 sc in the space, sc 6.

**Row 33:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next 13 sts, sc2tog. (15)

Fasten off and weave in.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/witch-wallet-buttonhole.svg" alt="Diagram. Row 31 is 7 single crochet, chain 3, skip 3, 7 single crochet. Row 32 is 7, then 3 into the chain space, then 7, back to 17. The closed wallet to scale, about 4.25 by 2.4 inches, with the button near row 7 of the front at stitch 9, about 1.3 inches below the top fold." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Count 17 after row 32. The hole sits over stitches 8 to 10.</figcaption>
</figure>

### Stage 2: the divider

**Row 1:** With purple, ch 18. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and across. (17)

**Rows 2–11:** Ch 1, turn. Sc in each st across. (17)

Fasten off. The divider is 11 rows, about 2.2 inches, one row shorter than the front, so its top edge stays below the rim and does not show.

### Stage 3: the hat appliqué

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/witch-hat-applique.svg" alt="Diagram. The cone in 5 rows: row 1 in orange, 5 stitches, then black, 5, 3, 3, and 1. A separate black brim strip of 8 stitches. On the closed flap, the brim lies on row 30 centered on stitch 9, the tip reaches row 25 at the top fold, and the buttonhole on row 31 sits just below the brim." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Hat band in orange, the rest in black. Keep row 31 clear.</figcaption>
</figure>

![The small black hat with an orange band, beside a ball of purple yarn](../../assets/articles/witch-hat-crochet-wallet-hat.jpg "Illustration.")

**Cone:**

**Row 1:** With orange, ch 6. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and across, changing to black on the last yarn over. (5) Cut orange, leaving a tail.

**Row 2:** Ch 1, turn. Sc in each st. (5)

**Row 3:** Ch 1, turn. Sc2tog, sc in the next st, sc2tog. (3)

**Row 4:** Ch 1, turn. Sc in each st. (3)

**Row 5:** Ch 1, turn. Sc3tog. (1) Fasten off with a 12 inch tail.

**Brim:** With black, ch 9. Sc in the 2nd ch from the hook and across. (8) Fasten off with a 10 inch tail.

The hat is about 1.25 inches wide at the band and about 1.2 inches tall with the brim.

**Sew it on before seaming,** while the flap is a single layer. Fold the flap down over the front so you can see where it lands. Place the brim on row 30, centered on stitch 9, and the cone's orange row on top of the brim, with the tip reaching row 25 at the fold. Whipstitch around the edges through the flap only. Do not cover or sew into row 31; the button has to come through.

### Stage 4: assembly

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/witch-wallet-divider.svg" alt="Diagram. A side view with the front folded up against the back and the divider between them, its lower edge at the fold and its top one row below the rim, making two pockets. A side seam of single crochet passes through front, divider, and back, one per row end, 12 per side." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Diagram. Two pockets of the same width, sealed by the same side seams.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. **Fold.** Fold rows 1 to 12 up against rows 13 to 24, right sides out. The chain edge is now the front rim.
2. **Divider.** Slide it between the front and back, its lower edge in the fold. Clip all three layers at both sides.
**Secure the divider bottom first.** Whipstitch all 17 foundation-chain positions of the divider to the inside bottom fold, using shallow stitches that do not show on the outside. The divider must meet the bottom continuously so a card cannot pass under it from one compartment to the other. Match divider row ends 1–11 to the first 11 side-seam positions; front and back have one additional position at the top.



3. **Side seams.** Join purple at the bottom fold on one side. Ch 1. Work 12 sc up the side, one in each row end, through front, divider, and back. The 12th stitch catches only front and back, because the divider is one row shorter. Fasten off. Repeat on the other side.
4. **Button.** Put two cards in, close the flap, and mark the front through the buttonhole. It should land near row 7, stitch 9, about 1.3 inches below the fold. Sew the button there through the front layer only, so it does not stitch the pocket shut.
5. **Ends.** Weave them along the seams, as in [weaving in ends](/weaving-in-ends-and-blocking/).

<div class="tip">

Check the continuous divider-bottom seam before closing the sides. Sparse tacks can leave openings between the compartments.

</div>

## Make it yours

- **One pocket.** Skip the divider. The seams stay the same.
- **Snap instead of button.** Skip the buttonhole: work rows 31 and 32 as plain sc. Sew a 3/8 inch snap under row 31 at the center and on the front at the mark.
- **Edged flap.** Work one round of slip stitch around the flap edges for a firmer outline.
- **Other hat colors.** A green or purple band, or a black hat with a silver band. Keep the band a color that shows against the flap.
- **Lining.** Line the back only, so the divider still fits. Cut fabric 4.25 by 2.4 inches plus a 1/4 inch fold.

## Keep the hat above the buttonhole and the divider below the rim

The rectangle divides into front rows 1–12, back rows 13–24 and flap rows 25–33. Fold first to see the closed flap orientation, then pin the hat on the flap only, leaving row 31's hole clear. For a resized buttonhole, use the specified reversed left/right counts on its return row so the extra stitches do not shift the hole. Anchor the divider's entire lower edge inside the fold before seaming the eleven three-layer positions and final two-layer position. Test one card in each compartment separately; neither should pass underneath the divider.

## Common mistakes and fixes

**Row 32 has 16 or 18 stitches.** The ch-3 space got 2 or 4 sc. It takes 3, one for each skipped stitch.

**The buttonhole is off center.** Count 7 before the chain. The hole should be over stitches 8 to 10.

**The flap lifts at the corners.** The decrease row is what lets it lie flat. Make sure row 33 has 15.

**The hat covers the buttonhole.** The brim drifted below row 30. Unpick and move it up a row.

**The divider shows above the rim.** It has 12 rows instead of 11. Pull out the last row.

**The button pulls the front out of shape.** It was sewn without cards inside. Mark with cards in place, and add a small flat button on the inside as a backer if the fabric is soft.

## Care

Wash by hand, cool, and dry it flat. Orange and black scraps sometimes bleed onto purple, so blot a wet scrap of each on paper towel first. Wash it on its own; the button can scratch.

## Frequently asked

**Will cards fit both pockets?** On paper, each pocket is about 3.9 by 2.3 inches inside, against a 3.37 by 2.125 inch card, so test both pockets with a real card before you sew up.

**Why 3 stitches for a 5/8 inch button?** Three stitches measure about 0.75 inch at the gauge, and crochet stretches around a button. The test after row 32 is the real check.

**Can I add more pockets?** A second divider makes three, but each pocket then holds only one or two cards, and the side seams become very thick.

**Is the hat sewn or crocheted on?** Sewn. It is crocheted separately so you can position it exactly.

**Can I sell wallets I make from it?** Yes. If you share the design, link to this page; do not republish the rows.

## More Halloween wallets

- [Black cat crochet wallet](/black-cat-crochet-wallet/): a seamless tube with ears
- [Ghost crochet wallet](/ghost-crochet-wallet/): shaped panels and a scalloped hem
- [Pumpkin crochet wallet](/pumpkin-crochet-wallet/): back-loop ribs and a leaf tab
- [Spiderweb crochet wallet](/spiderweb-crochet-wallet/): an embroidered web and a button loop

The decreases in row 33 and the hat cone are explained in [increases and decreases](/crochet-increase-decrease/).

The witch hat wallet depends on two tests: the button through the hole after row 32, and the cards inside before you mark the button. Do both, and the rest is straight single crochet.


# Yarn for Christmas Crochet: Sparkle, Thread and What Actually Works

Source: src/content/posts/yarn-for-christmas-crochet.md



Christmas crochet is mostly small: ornaments, gnomes, snowflakes, coasters, a garland. Drape stops mattering. Stitch definition matters more, and two questions decide the yarn: whether the piece must hold a blocked shape, and whether it will sit near a candle.

**Worsted acrylic for anything stuffed, cotton or thread for anything that must hold a shape, and sparkle as an accent rather than the main yarn.**

<div class="tip">

**Disclosure:** this guide contains affiliate links where products are named, which means a purchase may earn this site a small commission at no extra cost to you. No company sent anything for review and no commission influences what is recommended here. See the [disclosures page](/disclosures/).

</div>


![Basket of red green and cream Christmas crochet yarns](../../assets/articles/yarn-for-christmas-crochet-result.jpg "Illustration. Match yarn to the job — ornaments, gnomes, and trees ask for different things.")
## Match the yarn to the job

### Stuffed things — gnomes, Santas, baubles

**Worsted acrylic.** Springy, widely available in red and green, and forgiving of uneven tension on a dense fabric. The elasticity helps the fabric close around stuffing instead of gaping.

Use a hook a size or two smaller than the label suggests, so stuffing cannot show through.

### Things that must hold a shape — snowflakes, stars, flat ornaments

**Cotton, or size 10 crochet thread.** Cotton has no memory. Block it flat and it stays flat. Acrylic springs back, so a stiffened acrylic snowflake slowly curls again over a season. Blocking is covered in [how to crochet a snowflake](/how-to-crochet-a-snowflake/).

### Coasters, placemats, potholders

**Worsted cotton.** Absorbent, washable, and it handles a hot mug. Lily Sugar'n Cream and Peaches & Creme are the two widely sold US worsted cottons, and both come in reds, greens, and whites. Acrylic is the wrong fiber for anything that meets heat.

### Garlands and bunting

Either fiber. A garland is a string of small pieces, so leftovers that are useless elsewhere are perfect. Mixing fibers is invisible from across a room.

## The sparkle question

![Smooth worsted yarn beside sparkly Christmas yarn](../../assets/articles/yarn-for-christmas-crochet-1.jpg "Illustration. Sparkle photographs well and fights you on small stitches.")

Metallic yarn is usually a fine polyester or nylon filament plied with a normal acrylic strand. That filament is the problem.

**It splits.** The two plies give differently, so the hook slides between them.

**It hides stitch definition.** Sparkle scatters light, so you cannot see where the last stitch ended.

**It can be scratchy.** Fine on an ornament nobody touches. Poor on something worn.

Hold a fine metallic thread together with ordinary worsted. The worsted carries the structure and the stitch definition, and you still get the shimmer. Go up a hook size for the extra thickness. If you work metallic alone, keep it to the last round of an edging.

## Colors that make the work harder

![Matte red and green Christmas yarn beside a sparkly metallic skein](../../assets/articles/yarn-for-christmas-crochet-2.jpg "Illustration. Matte worsted is easier for small stitches than glitter yarn.")

**Deep forest green and dark burgundy swallow stitches.** They photograph well and they are tiring under warm indoor light. For bobbles, cables, or any texture you want to see, go a shade lighter.

**Bright red is hard for phone cameras.** It blows out into a flat shape with no visible stitches. A slightly muted or darker red photographs better.

**White and cream show every mark.** Fine for a snowflake handled twice. Think harder before using them for a coaster.

## Heat and acrylic

<div class="warning">

**Acrylic melts.** It does not char and go out like wool or cotton. It softens, melts, and can stick to skin. Anything going near a candle, a fireplace, a hot dish, or an oven glove should not be acrylic.

Wool is safer near heat: it has a high ignition point and tends to self-extinguish. Cotton burns but does not melt.

</div>

Candle rings, advent wreaths, mantel garlands, and potholders are the projects people make in cheap acrylic without thinking about it. Keep all yarn decorations away from flames, hot lamps, fireplaces, and heaters. Cotton and wool are not a reason to place crochet near a flame; never put a crocheted piece around a lit candle.

## How much to buy

This page does not print a yardage table. None of the projects on this site has been weighed, and copied tables are exactly the kind of number that gets passed around wrong. A small kitchen scale gives you a better answer than any table.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/yarn-estimate-weigh.svg" alt="Three steps. A yarn label reading 200 yards per 100 grams gives 2 yards per gram. One finished piece weighs 14 grams on a kitchen scale. 14 times 2 is 28 yards, plus 10 to 20 percent. The numbers are examples." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Yards per gram from the label, grams from the scale. The example numbers are invented.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. **Read the label.** Divide the yards by the grams. That is yards per gram for that yarn.
2. **Make one piece and weigh it.** One ornament, one gnome, one coaster.
3. **Multiply** the weight by yards per gram, then by the number of pieces, and add 10 to 20 percent for swatches and mistakes.

Most small Christmas pieces use a fraction of a skein, so shop leftovers first. For a matched set, buy in one go so the dye lots match. A mismatch is invisible on one ornament and obvious when several hang on the same branch. Weights are in the [yarn weight chart](/yarn-weight-chart/).

## Keep fiber choice separate from heat protection

Read the fiber percentage and care instructions on every scrap before combining it with a project. A cotton coaster is not automatically a potholder, and no crochet decoration belongs near an open flame or a heater regardless of fiber. For a mixed-color stuffed ornament, compare fabric density in each yarn: a thinner color may show stuffing through the same stitch count. For a stiffened motif, test the thread with the actual stiffener, let it dry completely, and record the result. Weigh one finished piece for batch yarn planning rather than applying another project's yardage.

## Mistakes worth skipping

**Printed novelty yarn.** A yarn printed to make a motif is spaced for one particular stitch and width, usually knitting. Crochet uses yarn at a different rate, so you tend to get fragments of the motif rather than the picture on the label.

**Chenille or velvet in dark green.** Velvet already hides stitches and worms into loops that will not sit flat. A dark color on top of that leaves you working blind.

**A full Christmas color pack bought on sale in January.** The red and green you need depend on projects you have not chosen yet.

## Frequently asked

### Can I substitute cotton for acrylic in a stuffed ornament?

Yes. Cotton is heavier and has no give, so the fabric is firmer, the piece hangs lower, and uneven tension shows more clearly.

### Is wool worth it?

For hats and garments, wool blocks beautifully and keeps its spring. For a stiffened snowflake or star, cotton or thread is still the usual choice, because it takes stiffener well and does not spring back. For a stuffed gnome that lives in a box most of the year, acrylic is the sensible choice, and moths have no interest in it.

### Can I wash a crocheted ornament?

Acrylic and cotton, yes, gently by hand. Anything stiffened with sugar or glue, no. Washing dissolves the stiffener.


# Yarn Weight Chart: All 8 Categories, Plainly Explained

Source: src/content/posts/yarn-weight-chart.md



"Yarn weight" means how thick the strand is, not how much the ball weighs. It is the thing to get right when you substitute one yarn for another.

Every country and brand has its own name for the same thickness. What Americans call worsted, the British call aran, the Australians call 10-ply, and the label might just say "medium." The Craft Yarn Council's numbers, 0 through 7, mean the same thing everywhere. That number is usually printed inside a yarn-skein symbol on the ball band.

<div class="warning">

**If you have an older chart, it may list only six categories.** The standard now includes lace as category 0 and jumbo as category 7, which makes the eight in the table below. Charts showing six groups predate that. The linked Craft Yarn Council chart currently presents categories 0–7. Check that source for future changes rather than relying on an undated announcement.

</div>


![Yarn balls from thin lace weight to bulky lined up by size](../../assets/articles/yarn-weight-chart-result.jpg "Illustration. Weight categories tell you thickness — not brand quality.")
## The chart

Gauge below is single crochet over 4 inches, which is how the Craft Yarn Council expresses crochet gauge for yarn weight. The categories, names, hooks, and gauges follow the Council's Standard Yarn Weight System.

| # | Category | Also called | Hook | Gauge (sc / 4 in) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | Lace | fingering, 10-count crochet thread | Steel 1.6–1.4 mm, or regular 2.25 mm | 32–42 double crochet* |
| 1 | Super Fine | sock, fingering, baby, 4-ply | 2.25–3.5 mm | 21–32 sts |
| 2 | Fine | sport, baby, 5-ply | 3.5–4.5 mm | 16–20 sts |
| 3 | Light | DK, light worsted, 8-ply | 4.5–5.5 mm | 12–17 sts |
| 4 | Medium | worsted, afghan, aran, 10-ply | 5.5–6.5 mm | 11–14 sts |
| 5 | Bulky | chunky, craft, rug, 12-ply | 6.5–9 mm | 8–11 sts |
| 6 | Super Bulky | super chunky, roving | 9–15 mm | 7–9 sts |
| 7 | Jumbo | jumbo, roving, arm-knitting weight | 15 mm and up | 6 sts or fewer |

\* The lace row is given in double crochet, not single, and the Council notes that lace is usually worked on larger hooks for an open fabric, so a gauge range is hard to pin down. Follow the pattern's gauge.

The hook ranges are starting points. Learn on category 4, worsted: blankets, hats, scarves, amigurumi, dishcloths. Lace is a poor first yarn because you cannot see the stitches. DK is the most useful garment weight. A super-fine blanket is a several-month commitment, and super bulky gets expensive at blanket size.

![Thin fingering yarn strand beside worsted yarn for comparison](../../assets/articles/yarn-weight-chart-1.jpg "Illustration. Thinner yarns need more stitches and usually a smaller hook.")

<div class="tip">

**Unlabeled yarn:** wrap it around a ruler for one inch, side by side, snug but not stretched, and count the wraps. That is wraps per inch. Then do the same with a yarn whose label you have, and compare. Published wraps-per-inch charts disagree with each other, and the count changes with how hard you pull, so a side-by-side comparison with a known yarn is more reliable than a number from a table.

</div>

## Substituting one yarn for another

![Four cream yarn cakes lined up from thin to bulky](../../assets/articles/yarn-weight-chart-2.jpg "Illustration. Match thickness first — then check yardage and fiber.")

The yarn a pattern names is often discontinued. Match these, in order.

**1. The weight number.** Substituting a category 3 for a category 4 changes the finished size.

**2. Total yardage, not the number of balls.** "3 skeins" means nothing on its own, because skein sizes differ between brands. One brand's 7 oz skein can hold more than double another's 3.5 oz. Find the total yards or meters, divide by the yardage on the yarn you are buying, and add ten percent. Buy it all at once so the dye lots match.

**3. The fiber.** Acrylic is springy, machine washable, and holds its shape: blankets, kids' items, amigurumi. Cotton has no memory, so a cotton hat stretches and stays stretched. It suits dishcloths, bags, and summer tops. Wool is warm, elastic, and blocks well, and most of it needs hand washing. Acrylic is the right yarn for a blanket a toddler will drag around. For a garment you will wear for years, the fiber is most of what you are paying for.

**4. The gauge.** Two yarns in the same category can still differ by a stitch or two over 4 inches. Swatch after you substitute.

For amigurumi, use a smaller hook than the label so stuffing does not show through: worsted on a 3.5 mm or 4 mm hook, rather than the suggested 5.5 mm. For blankets and shawls, go one size up. At the label's gauge they often come out stiff.

## How much yarn a project needs

A pattern's own yardage is the number to buy. When there is none, weigh a sample instead of trusting a table. This page used to print typical amounts, but none was weighed from a finished piece, so they are gone.

<figure class="diagram">
<img src="/diagrams/yarn-estimate-weigh.svg" alt="Three steps. A yarn label reading 200 yards per 100 grams gives 2 yards per gram. One finished piece weighs 14 grams on a kitchen scale. 14 times 2 is 28 yards, plus 10 to 20 percent. The numbers are examples." width="640" height="420" loading="lazy" decoding="async" />
<figcaption>Yards per gram from the label, grams from the scale. The example numbers are invented.</figcaption>
</figure>

For a large piece, crochet a measured square in the real stitch, weigh it, and scale by area. A 6 inch square is 36 square inches. A 30 by 40 inch blanket is 1,200, about 33 times as much.

Dense single crochet can use half again as much yarn as an open double crochet mesh over the same area. Bobbles, puffs, and popcorns use the most. Buy one extra ball. Returning it is easier than matching a dye lot three weeks later.

## Calculate a substitution from yardage and gauge

If a project needs six hundred yards and the replacement yarn has one hundred sixty-five yards per ball, 600 ÷ 165 is about 3.64: round up to four balls before adding any separate reserve. Check the replacement's fiber, washed gauge and fabric feel, not only its category number. A category is a range, and ply names vary between markets. The [Craft Yarn Council weight system](https://www.craftyarncouncil.com/standards/yarn-weight-system) provides guideline ranges, not guaranteed interchangeability. For an unmeasured project, weigh a swatch in the actual stitch and scale by area or stitch count, then allow extra for edging and tails.

## Common mistakes

**Buying "3 skeins," or using a six-category chart.** Match the weight number and the total yardage. Current charts run from lace at 0 through jumbo at 7.

**Changing the fiber and skipping the swatch.** A cotton hat stretches and stays stretched. Two yarns in one category can still differ by a stitch or two over 4 inches.

## Frequently asked

**What does "4-ply" or "8-ply" mean?** It is the Australian and New Zealand system, and it means thickness, not the literal number of strands. 4-ply is roughly fingering, 8-ply is roughly DK, 10-ply is roughly worsted.

**Why is my hat too big when I followed the pattern exactly?** Almost always gauge. Your tension is looser than the designer's, so go down a hook size. [Gauge is covered here](/crochet-gauge/).

**Can I hold two thin yarns together to make a thicker one?** Yes. Two strands of DK held together behave roughly like a chunky. Swatch it, because the result is never exactly what the arithmetic suggests.
